Its weakness is that the picture depends on the bin width you pick. The same 12 values look quite different with 1-wide bins and 0.5-wide bins (p.55-56), which is why the slide says "cell size is critical!" (p.54).

A scatter plot answers a different question. It shows whether two paired variables are related, like CPU time against I/O time for the same jobs (p.57-58). It says nothing about whether two samples come from the same distribution. For that you want a Q-Q plot (p.53).

What to know

Divide the range into equal-sized cells, count the points in each, and plot relative frequency (count divided by \(n\)) against the cells (Summ p.54). Sturges' rule picks the number of bins.

\[ k = \lfloor 1 + \log_2 n \rfloor \]

The scatter plot on Summ p.58 has 20 jobs, and what it shows is three separate clusters, not one trend. Six jobs use about 0.02 s of CPU and only 0.04 to 0.14 s of I/O. Six use 0.145 to 0.170 s of CPU and 1.48 to 1.74 s of I/O, so I/O climbs steeply with CPU there. Eight use 0.45 to 0.60 s of CPU and 0.90 to 1.24 s of I/O, a much gentler rise. So the middle group does the most I/O even though the right group uses three times the CPU. A single mean or a single correlation over all 20 jobs would hide that there are three kinds of job, which is exactly what the picture is for.

Worked example

Sixteen values 0, 3, 5, 6, 7, 7, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11, 12, 13, 15, 17, 20.

Sturges' rule is \(k = 1 + \log_2 n\), where \(k\) is the number of bins and \(n\) is the number of values. Plug in \(n = 16\). \(\log_2 16 = 4\) because \(2^4 = 16\), so \(k = 1 + 4 = 5\) bins.

Bin width is range over \(k\). The range is \(20 - 0 = 20\), so each bin is \(20/5 = 4\) wide. Start at the minimum 0 and step by 4 to get the edges 0, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20. Relative frequency is count over \(n\).

BinValuesCountRelative frequency
[0, 4)0, 322/16 = 12.5%
[4, 8)5, 6, 7, 744/16 = 25%
[8, 12)9, 9, 10, 10, 1155/16 = 31.25%
[12, 16)12, 13, 1533/16 = 18.75%
[16, 20]17, 2022/16 = 12.5%

Counts add to 16 and percentages add to 100%. The shape is single-peaked and roughly symmetric.

For HW3 Problem 2(c), \(n = 1000\). Plug in, \(k = 1 + \log_2 1000\). Without a calculator, use \(2^{10} = 1024\), which is just over 1000, so \(\log_2 1000\) is just under 10 (it's 9.97). Then \(k = 1 + 9.97 = 10.97\), which is 10 bins with the slide's floor and 11 if you round.

Easy to get wrong

  • The slide puts a floor on Sturges (p.54), which gives 10 bins for \(n = 1000\). HW3 rounded and used 11 for both files (the script does round(1 + log2 n)), and the study guide also says "about 11". On the exam, show \(1 + \log_2 n\) and say which way you rounded.
  • Plotting raw counts when the slide asks for relative frequency. Divide by \(n\). The p.55 example has 12 values, so one value is \(1/12 = 8.3\%\) and three are 25%. Its end bins are open (\(\le 0\) and \(> 5\)), so it doesn't literally follow the p.54 recipe of equal cells across the whole range. Equal cells from −3.0 to 5.5 would leave the left bins nearly empty because of the single value −3.0.
  • Using a scatter plot to compare two distributions. Scatter plots are for paired variables.
  • Drawing a firm conclusion about shape from one histogram. Check it with a bin-free method like a Q-Q plot.

Quantile plots and Q-Q plots

The idea

A quantile plot is \(Q(p)\) against \(p\), so it's just the sorted data plotted at the plotting positions (Summ p.59-60). A Q-Q plot puts the quantiles of one distribution against the quantiles of another. If the two have the same shape, the points fall on a straight line, because "equal shape" is the same as "linearly related quantile functions" (p.61). A difference in scale changes the slope and a difference in location changes the intercept, so straightness tests shape alone (Q-Q plots handout).

There are two versions. A two-sample Q-Q plot compares two data sets. A theoretical Q-Q plot compares one data set to a distribution you suspect, using its inverse CDF to produce the theoretical quantiles (p.63). Unlike a histogram there are no bins to choose.

What to know

The quantile plot example on Summ p.60 has \(n = 10\), so the plotting positions are 0.05, 0.15, up to 0.95, and the sorted values 10.5, 24.0, 28.0, 29.0, 34.0, 36.5, 40.3, 44.5, 50.3, 55.3 sit at those positions. You can read quantiles straight off it. \(Q(0.25) = x_3 = 28.0\) and \(Q(0.75) = x_8 = 44.5\) because 0.25 and 0.75 are plotting positions. The median falls between 0.45 and 0.55, so \(Q(0.5) = (34.0 + 36.5)/2 = 35.25\). The steep first segment (10.5 to 24.0 over one step) shows a stretched-out lower tail, and the flat stretch from 28.0 to 29.0 shows values bunched together.

Two-sample plot. Plot \((Q_1(p), Q_2(p))\) with \(p = (i-0.5)/n\), where \(n\) is the size of the smaller data set, and interpolate in the larger one (Summ p.61). The example on p.62 fits \(y = 1.0957x + 0.4712\) with \(R^2 = 0.94\), reasonably linear, so the shapes are similar.

Theoretical plot. Plot \(\big(F^{-1}((i-0.5)/n),\, x_i\big)\) with \(x_i\) the \(i\)-th sorted data point (p.63). Inverses from p.64, with the exponential parameterized by its mean \(a\).

DistributionCDF \(F(x)\)Inverse at \(u\)
Exponential\(1 - e^{-x/a}\)\(-a\ln(1-u)\)
Pareto\(1 - x^{-a}\)\(1/(1-u)^{1/a}\)
Geometric\(1 - (1-p)^x\)slide \(\lceil \ln u / \ln(1-p) \rceil\), exact \(\lceil \ln(1-u) / \ln(1-p) \rceil\)

The study guide writes the same inverses with different parameters. Exponential with rate \(\lambda = 1/a\) is \(-\ln(1-p)/\lambda\), and Pareto with scale \(k\) is \(k(1-p)^{-1/a}\), which is the slide's version when \(k = 1\). Read which parameter a question gives you, a mean or a rate.

The geometric row needs care. Solving \(1 - (1-p)^x = u\) gives \(x = \ln(1-u)/\ln(1-p)\), rounded up because \(x\) is a whole number. The slide's \(\ln u\) version comes from random-variate generation, where \(u\) and \(1-u\) are both uniform so it doesn't matter. For a Q-Q plot it does matter, because the \(\ln u\) form returns the \(1-u\) quantile. With \(p = 0.5\) and \(u = 0.75\), the exact form gives \(\lceil \ln 0.25/\ln 0.5 \rceil = \lceil 2 \rceil = 2\), which is right since \(F(1) = 0.5\) and \(F(2) = 0.75\). The slide's form gives \(\lceil \ln 0.75 / \ln 0.5 \rceil = \lceil 0.415 \rceil = 1\), the 0.25 quantile.

The 1000-value exponential example gives a fitted line \(y = 0.9642x + 0.016\) with \(R^2 = 0.9988\), confirming the guess (p.65-68). Its table on p.67 uses a hand-picked set of percentiles (0.1 through 0.99, plus the maximum) instead of every \((i-0.5)/n\), which is fine for big data. The theoretical column matches an exponential with mean \(a = 2\), since \(-2\ln(0.5) = 1.39\) and \(-2\ln(0.1) = 4.61\).

The normal has no closed-form inverse, so use a z table or the approximation \(z_i = 4.91[q_i^{0.14} - (1-q_i)^{0.14}]\). For \(N(\mu, \sigma)\) rescale to \(\mu + \sigma z_i\) before plotting (p.69). If you plot the data against unscaled \(z\), the line's intercept estimates the mean and its slope estimates the standard deviation (p.70). The slide's fit \(y = 1.0505x + 0.0301\) says mean about 0.03 and standard deviation about 1.05. HW3 instead rescaled with \(x = m + s z\) using the sample mean and standard deviation, so normal data should fall on the 45° line.

Reading a normal probability plot with data on the vertical axis and \(z\) on the horizontal (p.71-73).

What the ends doMeaning
Low end bends below the line and high end bends above it (steeper at both ends)Longer tails than normal (p.71)
Low end flattens above the line and high end flattens below itShorter tails than normal (p.72)
The whole curve bows one way, both ends on the same side of the lineAsymmetric, skewed (p.73). Bowed up at the right end (convex) is right skew, bowed down is left skew

What the Q-Q plots handout adds. Papers use Q-Q plots for four jobs. They check normality before a parametric method, check that a model's residuals behave as assumed, check that a fitted stochastic model reproduces the data (the Hawkes paper plots rescaled residuals against an exponential, not a normal), and check that a test statistic is calibrated (GWAS p-values against the uniform). Systems and networking papers rarely show Q-Q plots. They use CDF or log-log CCDF overlays plus a number like the K-S distance. Other lessons from the handout follow.

  • The correlation of the plotted points can serve as a one-number fit score, with 1 meaning perfect agreement (the D-band paper). HW3 reported exactly this, 0.9993 and 0.904.
  • In the GWAS paper the Q-Q plot tests the method, not the data. Observed p-values are plotted against the uniform. A curve that lifts off the diagonal along its whole length means the test is inflated, and a lift only at the far right end is the real signal the study wants.
  • Showing a diagnostic only where data are plentiful (the Hawkes paper plots its five largest groups) says nothing about fit where data are thin.
  • The K-S test and Q-Q plots can disagree on the same small data set, and an honest paper says so.
  • A z-score transform only shifts and rescales, so it can't change shape. Its Q-Q plot looks like the raw one with new axis labels.
  • A P-P plot resolves the center and squeezes the tails, while a Q-Q plot does the opposite. For a p99 claim only the Q-Q plot can answer.
  • Writing that assumptions "could be verified" with Q-Q plots and then showing none is claiming a check you didn't do.

Feitelson, Workload Modeling, section 4.5.1 "Using Q-Q Plots" (book p.166-169). The handout points here as further reading, and it's the only part of the distribution-fitting chapter the course points to. The rest of the chapter (moments, maximum likelihood, K-S and chi-square tests) falls under the goodness-of-fit material Summ p.52 defers. The section's main points are these.

  • Same recipe as Setia. Sort, give sample \(i\) the position \(p_i = (i-0.5)/n\), and compute the model value \(F^{-1}(p_i)\). Using \(i/n\) would tie the last sample to 1 and no sample to 0, which is lopsided.
  • With huge data you can subsample, for example sort 1000 values and keep every tenth so each point is one percentile.
  • His axes are flipped. He plots \((X_{(i)}, F^{-1}(p_i))\), data on the horizontal axis and model on the vertical. A matching model gives a line of slope 1. A straight line with another slope means the right shape but the wrong scale. Slope steeper than 1 means the data are more condensed than the model, so the model's scale should shrink.
  • With data on the horizontal axis, a plot that tapers off to the right means the data's tail is longer than the model's, so you need a heavier-tailed model. A plot that shoots straight up means the model's tail is too long.
  • Q-Q plots are sensitive to the tails, which is why workload modelers prefer them to P-P plots. Rare extreme values can dominate the picture, though. Dropping the extreme percentiles hides the tail, so a log scale is the better compromise.
  • When \(F^{-1}\) has no closed form, you can draw many samples from the model and compare two empirical distributions instead.

Worked example

Is 2, 4, 4, 5, 10 plausibly normal? From the dispersion topic, \(\bar{x} = 5\) and \(s = 3\).

The recipe has three formulas. The plotting position is \(p_i = (i - 0.5)/n\), where \(i\) is the rank of the sorted value and \(n = 5\). The standard normal quantile \(z_i\) is the z whose table area \(\Phi(z_i)\) equals \(p_i\). The rescaled theoretical value is \(\bar{x} + s z_i = 5 + 3z_i\), which puts z into the data's units. You plot each pair \((5 + 3z_i,\ x_i)\) and compare to the line \(y = x\).

Positions. \(p_1 = 0.5/5 = 0.1\), \(p_2 = 1.5/5 = 0.3\), \(p_3 = 2.5/5 = 0.5\), \(p_4 = 3.5/5 = 0.7\), \(p_5 = 4.5/5 = 0.9\).

\(i\)\(x_i\)\(p_i = (i-0.5)/5\)\(z\) from table\(5 + 3z\)Data vs line
120.1−1.285 − 3.84 = 1.16above
240.3−0.525 − 1.56 = 3.44above
340.505below
450.70.525 + 1.56 = 6.56below
5100.91.285 + 3.84 = 8.84above

Table lookup. In the standard normal table, look inside the body for the area closest to your target and read z off the row and column. For 0.9, row 1.2 and column 0.08 give \(\Phi(1.28) = 0.8997\), the closest entry, so \(z = 1.28\). For 0.7, row 0.5 and column 0.02 give \(\Phi(0.52) = 0.6985\), closer than \(\Phi(0.53) = 0.7019\), so \(z = 0.52\). For 0.5, \(z = 0\). The lower ones follow by symmetry, \(z_{0.1} = -z_{0.9} = -1.28\) and \(z_{0.3} = -0.52\).

Rescale. For \(i = 1\), \(5 + 3(-1.28) = 5 - 3.84 = 1.16\). The rest are in the table the same way. Then compare. The data value 2 is bigger than 1.16, so that point is above the line.

Plot the pairs \((5+3z, x_i)\) against the 45° line. The low end sits above the line, the middle dips below, and the top point is above again. That's a convex bow, so the data are right-skewed. With only five points this is weak evidence, but the pattern is the right one to describe.

This is exactly HW3 Problem 2(e). For not-normal.txt the points sat above the line at both ends and below it in the middle (correlation 0.904). The right end was too high because the largest values were far bigger than a normal allows, and the left end was too high because a normal fitted to that data would need values near −0.5, while the data never go below zero. For normal.txt the points hugged the line with correlation 0.9993, drifting only in the sparse tails.

Exponential Q-Q plot (study guide E1). Are 0.4, 1.1, 1.9, 3.2, 6.8 exponential with \(\lambda = 0.5\), that is mean \(a = 2\)? The formula for the exponential quantile is \(F^{-1}(p) = -\ln(1-p)/\lambda\), where \(p\) is the plotting position and \(\lambda\) is the rate. Plug in \(\lambda = 0.5\). Dividing by 0.5 is multiplying by 2, so \(F^{-1}(p) = -2\ln(1-p)\). The positions are \(p_i = (i - 0.5)/5\), so 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9 as before. For \(i = 1\), \(1 - 0.1 = 0.9\), \(\ln 0.9 = -0.1054\), and \(-2 \times (-0.1054) = 0.2107\). Bring a small ln table into the exam, since you can't compute these by hand.

\(i\)\(x_{(i)}\)\(p_i\)\(\ln(1-p_i)\)\(-2\ln(1-p_i)\)
10.40.1−0.10540.2107
21.10.3−0.35670.7133
31.90.5−0.69311.3863
43.20.7−1.20402.4079
56.80.9−2.30264.6052

Plot theory on \(x\) and data on \(y\). The points are close to a straight line, and least squares gives slope 1.45, intercept −0.02 and \(R^2 = 0.995\). So the exponential shape fits. By hand, use the slope formula \(\text{slope} = (y_5 - y_1)/(x_5 - x_1)\) through the two end points, with data on \(y\) and theory on \(x\). Plug in \((6.8 - 0.4)/(4.6052 - 0.2107)\). The top is 6.4 and the bottom is 4.3945, and \(6.4/4.3945 \approx 1.46\), close to the fitted value.

The slope isn't 1, so the guessed parameter is off, not the family. The data are stretched 1.45 times relative to Exp(0.5). The model's mean is \(1/\lambda = 1/0.5 = 2\), so the data's mean is about \(2 \times 1.45 = 2.9\), and the rate is \(\lambda \approx 1/2.9 \approx 0.345\) (the same as \(0.5/1.45\)). Linearity identifies the shape, and slope and intercept identify scale and location.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using the larger sample's \(n\) for the plotting positions of a two-sample plot. Use the smaller one.
  • Confusing which axis is which. The slides put theory on \(x\) and data on \(y\). If you swap them, "above the line" flips meaning.
  • Calling a straight line with slope 2 "not the same distribution". A straight line at any slope or intercept means the same shape with a different scale or location.
  • The Q-Q plots handout warns that a Q-Q plot ignores the order of the data, so it can't show independence, and that normal residuals don't prove a model is accurate.
  • Reading Feitelson's slope rules with Setia's axes. Feitelson puts data on \(x\), so his "steeper than 1" is the same situation as a slope below 1 on Setia's plot. Always check which axis holds the data before you interpret a slope or a tail.
  • Copying the slide's geometric inverse \(\lceil \ln u/\ln(1-p) \rceil\) into a Q-Q plot. It returns the \(1-u\) quantile, so your points come out in reverse order. Use \(\ln(1-u)\).
  • Mixing up the exponential's mean \(a\) (slides) and rate \(\lambda = 1/a\) (study guide). \(-a\ln(1-u)\) and \(-\ln(1-u)/\lambda\) are the same thing.
  • Small samples wiggle off the line even when the model is right. Judge the overall pattern, especially in the tails where points are sparse.

Reporting variability

The idea

The slides' summary is "avoid means if possible" because a mean loses information (Summ p.32). The reporting variability handout turns that into practice. The number that describes how much the measurements varied is the one that tells a reader how far to trust the headline number, and it's the one most often left out.

The handout's main rule is to report a spread whose meaning matches the claim. If you're claiming something about a typical run, report the spread of the data itself, like the standard deviation, IQR or percentiles. If you're claiming something about how precisely you estimated a mean, report a confidence interval for that mean. A figure labelled only "error bars" tells the reader neither.

What to know

MeasureAnswersFails when
Mean ± SDHow far individual runs sit from the meanSkewed or heavy-tailed data, where mean − SD can go below possible values
Standard error \(s/\sqrt{n}\)How precisely the mean is estimatedPresented as if it described the data, since it shrinks with \(\sqrt{n}\) and the data's spread doesn't
Confidence intervalWhich values of the summary fit the dataRead as containing 95% of the data
Percentiles (p95, p99)What the tail looks likeToo few samples (a p99.9 needs thousands)
IQR, quartilesWhere the middle half sits, robust to outliersThe tail is the actual finding
Min, max, rangeWorst case seenUsed as a stability claim, since the range grows with sample size
COVSpread relative to size, across different scalesMean near zero, or heavy tails
Median absolute deviationTypical deviation, robust to contaminationRarely fails, mostly just underused
Interquartile meanA center that ignores both tails, good with few runs and wild outliersThe tails are what you're studying
Box, violin, raincloud plots, CDF or CCDFThe whole shape, more than any single numberBox plots with unstated whisker rules, CCDFs without log axes
Number of runsHow much evidence there isOmitted, which makes every other measure uninterpretable

Hoefler and Belli's rules quoted in the handout. Rule 5 says to report whether values are deterministic and give confidence intervals when they aren't. Rule 6 says not to assume normality without a diagnostic check, which is what the HW3 Q-Q plots are. Rule 7 says to compare nondeterministic data soundly, e.g. with non-overlapping confidence intervals or ANOVA. Rule 8 says to ask whether a mean or median is even the right summary, because a worst-case latency claim needs a high percentile.

Good examples from the handout include Parendi, which prints the maximum next to its 2.8× geomean and gives the full per-benchmark table, and the Aurora paper, which uses a harmonic mean for a rate (TEPS) and an arithmetic mean for times, stating the number of repetitions each time.

Paper in the handoutWhat it shows you how to report
Agarwal et al., deep RL (NeurIPS 2021)Means over 3 to 5 seeds can reverse rankings. Use the interquartile mean, performance profiles and stratified bootstrap CIs, which hold up with few runs
Georges, Buytaert and Eeckhout, Java (OOPSLA 2007)JIT, GC and runtime noise make a single best or mean time indefensible. Use CIs for comparisons and violin plots to show bimodality and skew
Serverless variability (2023)Variability is the subject. Show the empirical CDF first, then time series with CIs, then seasonal decomposition
Post-quantum timing side channels (2025)Pair pictures (histograms with KDE, ECDFs, violins) with numbers (Welch t, K-S distance, Cliff's delta)
Miller, error bars for LLM evals (2024)Standard errors from the CLT, clustered SEs when questions come in related groups, and power analysis for sample size
Arcuri and Briand, randomized SE tools (ICSE 2011)Many independent runs, a non-parametric test, and the Vargha-Delaney \(\hat{A}_{12}\) effect size, the chance one tool's run beats the other's

When a paper gives you only a geomean speedup, the study guide (F2) says to ask for three things. Ask for the per-benchmark raw times, so you can recompute any summary including total time. Ask for a spread (range, COV or a box plot), since 1.3× spanning 0.4× to 9× is a different result from 1.3× spanning 1.2× to 1.4×. Ask how benchmarks were chosen and whether any were dropped. Two systems with the same mean can still differ in spread (SD or COV), in the tail (p90, max, box plot) or in shape, such as unimodal against bimodal (histogram or Q-Q plot), which is study guide F3.

Worked example

You time a request 25 times and get \(\bar{x} = 40\) ms and \(s = 10\) ms.

The standard error formula is \(\text{SE} = s/\sqrt{n}\), where \(s\) is the sample standard deviation and \(n\) is the number of runs. Plug in \(10/\sqrt{25}\). \(\sqrt{25} = 5\), so \(\text{SE} = 10/5 = 2\) ms. That's how far the sample mean itself is likely to wander, not how far one request wanders. If you ran 100 times instead and \(s\) stayed near 10, the SE would be \(10/\sqrt{100} = 10/10 = 1\) ms. The SE halved because you know the mean better, but individual requests are just as spread out. Writing "40 ± 2 ms" without saying it's an SE makes the system look four times steadier than it is.

Now a latency set with mean 4 ms and \(s = 6\) ms. One SD below the mean is \(\bar{x} - s = 4 - 6 = -2\) ms, an impossible latency. That tells you the data are right-skewed, so "mean ± SD" is the wrong summary. Report the median with the IQR or SIQR, and the p95 or p99 if the claim is about the tail. This matches the slide rule on p.49 for unbounded, skewed data.

Easy to get wrong

  • Unlabelled error bars. Always say SD, SE or CI, and give \(n\).
  • Reporting a bare geomean speedup with no spread. Give the max or min beside it, or the per-benchmark table.
  • Using the range to argue a system is stable. More runs make the range grow on their own.

Check yourself

1. A researcher adds two features, each built on the previous one. The first improves quality by 20% and the second by 87.5%. Which mean gives the improvement per feature, and what is it?

Use the geometric mean, because each gain applies on top of the last, so the factors multiply (Summ p.30). Same setup as HW3 1(a).

Formula. \(x_G = \big(\prod_{i=1}^{n} x_i\big)^{1/n}\), where \(x_i\) is each growth factor (1 plus the percent as a decimal) and \(n\) is the number of features.

Plug in. Convert the percents first, 20% becomes 1.2 and 87.5% becomes 1.875. With \(n = 2\), \(x_G = (1.2 \times 1.875)^{1/2}\).

Arithmetic. \(1.2 \times 1.875 = 1.875 + 0.2 \times 1.875 = 1.875 + 0.375 = 2.25\). Then \(\sqrt{2.25} = 1.5\), since \(1.5 \times 1.5 = 2.25\). Subtract 1 to get back to a percent, \(1.5 - 1 = 0.5 = 50\%\).

Result. 50% per feature. Two 50% gains in a row give the real 2.25× total. The arithmetic mean of the percents, \((20 + 87.5)/2 = 53.75\%\), would overstate it.

2. Five compiler runs on similar-sized inputs take 100, 140, 120, 160 and 180 ms. Which mean, and what value?

Use the arithmetic mean, because these are times and their sum is the total time, so the mean should be directly proportional to it (Summ p.25, p.27). Same setup as HW3 1(b).

Formula. \(\bar{x} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i\), where \(x_i\) is each run's time and \(n\) is the number of runs.

Plug in. \(\bar{x} = (100+140+120+160+180)/5\).

Arithmetic. \(100+140 = 240\), \(240+120 = 360\), \(360+160 = 520\), \(520+180 = 700\). Then \(700/5 = 140\).

Result. 140 ms, the average compile time.

3. A database runs the same workload three times and measures 40, 60 and 120 transactions per second. Which mean, and what value?

Use the harmonic mean, because it's a rate and the mean should be inversely proportional to total time (Summ p.28). Same setup as HW3 1(c).

Formula. \(x_H = \dfrac{n}{\sum_{i=1}^{n} 1/x_i}\), where \(x_i\) is each rate and \(n\) is the number of runs.

Plug in. \(x_H = \dfrac{3}{1/40 + 1/60 + 1/120}\).

Arithmetic. Use the common denominator 120. \(1/40 = 3/120\) since \(120/40 = 3\). \(1/60 = 2/120\) since \(120/60 = 2\). \(1/120\) stays. The sum is \(3/120 + 2/120 + 1/120 = 6/120 = 1/20\). Then \(x_H = 3/(1/20) = 3 \times 20 = 60\).

Result. 60 tps, the throughput you'd get from total transactions over total time. The arithmetic mean, \((40+60+120)/3 = 220/3 = 73.3\), would overstate it.

4. A program spends 60 s in each of two phases. An optimization makes phase 1 take 20 s and leaves phase 2 alone. Find the arithmetic, geometric and harmonic means of the two speedups and the true overall speedup.

The harmonic mean, 1.5, is the one that matches the true overall speedup, because the two phases had equal baseline time (study guide B2, and Eeckhout's equal-time speedup in the means handout).

Speedups first. Speedup is old time over new time. Phase 1 is \(60/20 = 3\). Phase 2 is \(60/60 = 1\).

Arithmetic mean. \(x_A = \frac{1}{n}\sum x_i = (3 + 1)/2 = 4/2 = 2\).

Geometric mean. \(x_G = (\prod x_i)^{1/n} = (3 \times 1)^{1/2} = \sqrt{3} \approx 1.73\).

Harmonic mean. \(x_H = n/\sum (1/x_i) = 2/(1/3 + 1/1)\). Write \(1 = 3/3\), so the bottom is \(1/3 + 3/3 = 4/3\). Then \(2/(4/3) = 2 \times 3/4 = 6/4 = 1.5\).

True speedup. Old total time over new total time. Old is \(60 + 60 = 120\) s. New is \(20 + 60 = 80\) s. \(120/80 = 1.5\).

Result. AM 2, GM 1.73, HM 1.5, true 1.5. Only the harmonic mean gets the real answer, and the geometric mean still overstates it.

5. For 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, compute the sample variance, standard deviation and COV.

Formulas. \(\bar{x} = \frac{1}{n}\sum x_i\), \(s^2 = \dfrac{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2}{n-1}\), \(s = \sqrt{s^2}\), and \(\text{COV} = s/\bar{x}\). Here \(x_i\) is each value, \(n = 5\) is the count, \(\bar{x}\) is the mean, and \(s\) is the standard deviation (Summ p.39-41).

Mean. \(\bar{x} = (3+5+7+9+11)/5 = 35/5 = 7\).

Deviations \(x_i - 7\) are \(-4, -2, 0, 2, 4\). Squares are 16, 4, 0, 4, 16. Their sum is \(16+4+0+4+16 = 40\).

Plug in. \(s^2 = 40/(5-1) = 40/4 = 10\). Then \(s = \sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\), since \(3.16^2 = 9.99\). Then \(\text{COV} = 3.16/7 \approx 0.45\).

Result. \(s^2 = 10\), \(s \approx 3.16\), COV \(\approx 0.45\). A typical value sits about 3.16 from the mean, which is 45% of the mean. Divide by \(n-1\), not \(n\).

6. Sorted data 4, 7, 8, 10, 12, 15, 16, 18, 21, 30. Find Q1, the median, Q3, the IQR and the SIQR with the course convention.

Formula. The position is \(r = np + 0.5\), where \(n\) is the count and \(p\) is the quantile. With \(j\) the whole part of \(r\), \(Q(p) = x_j + (r - j)(x_{j+1} - x_j)\), where \(x_j\) is the \(j\)-th sorted value (Summ p.59).

Plug in \(n = 10\). The values are \(x_1 = 4\), \(x_2 = 7\), \(x_3 = 8\), \(x_4 = 10\), \(x_5 = 12\), \(x_6 = 15\), \(x_7 = 16\), \(x_8 = 18\), \(x_9 = 21\), \(x_{10} = 30\).

Q1. \(r = 10(0.25) + 0.5 = 2.5 + 0.5 = 3\). Whole number, so \(Q_1 = x_3 = 8\).

Median. \(r = 10(0.5) + 0.5 = 5.5\). So \(j = 5\) and \(Q_2 = 12 + 0.5(15 - 12) = 12 + 1.5 = 13.5\), the same as \((12+15)/2\).

Q3. \(r = 10(0.75) + 0.5 = 7.5 + 0.5 = 8\). Whole number, so \(Q_3 = x_8 = 18\).

IQR \(= Q_3 - Q_1 = 18 - 8 = 10\). SIQR \(= 10/2 = 5\).

Result. Q1 = 8, median 13.5, Q3 = 18, IQR 10, SIQR 5. The middle half of the data spans 10 units (Summ p.45). Excel's PERCENTILE would put Q1 at position \(1 + (n-1)p = 1 + 9(0.25) = 3.25\), which gives \(8 + 0.25(10 - 8) = 8.5\), so don't copy the method from p.43.

7. How many histogram bins does Sturges' rule give for 64 points? For 1000?

Formula. \(k = 1 + \log_2 n\), where \(k\) is the number of bins and \(n\) is the number of points (Summ p.54).

For 64. \(\log_2 64 = 6\) because \(2^6 = 64\). So \(k = 1 + 6 = 7\) bins.

For 1000. \(2^{10} = 1024\) is just over 1000, so \(\log_2 1000\) is just under 10, about 9.97. So \(k = 1 + 9.97 = 10.97\).

Result. 7 bins for 64 points. For 1000 it's 10 with the floor written on Summ p.54 and 11 with the rounding HW3 and the study guide used. Say which you're doing. Bin width matters because the same data can look different with different cells (p.56).

8. Data set 1 is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and data set 2 is 7, 9, 11, 13, 15. What does their Q-Q plot look like, and do they have the same distribution shape?

The points lie exactly on a straight line, so yes, the two sets have the same shape. Both sets have \(n = 5\), so the \(i\)-th sorted value of each is its quantile at \(p_i = (i-0.5)/5\), and you pair them as (1, 7), (2, 9), (3, 11), (4, 13), (5, 15). Each \(y\) is \(2x + 5\), for example \(2(1) + 5 = 7\) and \(2(3) + 5 = 11\). Linear quantile functions mean equal shape (Summ p.61). The slope 2 shows set 2 is twice as spread out and the intercept 5 shows a shift.

9. On a normal probability plot (data against z), the lowest points fall below the line and the highest points rise above it. What does that mean? What if the curve bows the same way at both ends?

It means the data have longer tails than a normal. Points below the line at the low end are smaller than a normal would predict, and points above at the high end are larger, so the data reach further out in both directions (Summ p.71). If both ends sit on the same side of the line and the middle on the other, the plot is bowed, which means the data are asymmetric, or skewed (p.73). Bowed upward like HW3's not-normal.txt means right skew.

10. Pick an index of dispersion for (i) a cache hit ratio over many intervals, (ii) response times on a loaded server, (iii) repeated timings that look symmetric and unimodal.

(i) the range, (ii) percentiles or SIQR, (iii) COV. The rule on Summ p.49 goes by the shape of the data. Hit ratios are bounded between 0 and 1, and for bounded data the range is meaningful. Response times are unbounded and right-skewed, so a quantile-based measure like percentiles or SIQR isn't thrown off by the long tail. Timings that are unbounded, unimodal and symmetric are the case where the standard deviation behaves, so COV works.

11. A paper reports latency as "40 ms ± 2 ms" from 25 runs with no other detail. What two things would you ask, and why?

Ask whether the 2 ms is an SD, an SE or a CI half-width, and ask for the distribution or high percentiles (reporting variability handout, Hoefler and Belli rule 8).

The first matters because the three answer different questions. If 2 ms is an SE, use \(\text{SE} = s/\sqrt{n}\), where \(s\) is the SD and \(n\) is the run count, and solve for \(s\). That gives \(s = \text{SE} \times \sqrt{n} = 2 \times \sqrt{25} = 2 \times 5 = 10\) ms. So individual runs would vary five times more than the figure suggests.

The second matters because latency is usually right-skewed, and a mean ± SD can describe a distribution no user sees.

12. Why does the HW3 Q-Q plot rescale each z with \(m + s z\), and what would the line look like without that step?

Rescaling puts the theoretical quantiles in the data's units, so normal data land on the 45° line \(y = x\) (Summ p.69). Here \(m\) is the sample mean and \(s\) the sample standard deviation, so \(m + s z\) is where a normal with your data's center and spread would put that quantile. Without it you plot data against standard z, and a normal sample falls on a line whose intercept is about the mean and whose slope is about the standard deviation (p.70). Either way the test is straightness.

13. A box plot's five-number summary is 4.26, 6.08, 7.35, 8.33, 12.74. Find the IQR, SIQR and midhinge, and say whether the maximum is an outlier by Tukey's rule.

The five numbers are min, \(Q_1\), median, \(Q_3\), max, so \(Q_1 = 6.08\) and \(Q_3 = 8.33\). This is the Summ p.50 data (study guide D1).

Formulas. \(\text{IQR} = Q_3 - Q_1\), \(\text{SIQR} = \text{IQR}/2\), \(\text{Midhinge} = (Q_1 + Q_3)/2\), and Tukey's upper fence is \(Q_3 + 1.5\,\text{IQR}\).

Plug in and compute. IQR \(= 8.33 - 6.08 = 2.25\). SIQR \(= 2.25/2 = 1.125\). Midhinge \(= (6.08 + 8.33)/2 = 14.41/2 = 7.205\). Fence \(= 8.33 + 1.5(2.25) = 8.33 + 3.375 = 11.705\).

Result. The maximum 12.74 is above 11.705, so yes, it's an outlier. The midhinge 7.205 is below the median 7.35, which hints at left skew in the middle half.

14. A theoretical Q-Q plot against Exp(\(\lambda = 0.5\)), with theory on \(x\) and data on \(y\), is a clean straight line through the origin with slope 2. What do you conclude?

The data look exponential with mean about 4, that is \(\lambda \approx 0.25\). The straight line means the exponential shape fits (Summ p.63). The slope tells you the scale. Exp(0.5) has mean \(1/\lambda = 1/0.5 = 2\). A slope of 2 means each data quantile is twice the model's, so the data's mean is \(2 \times 2 = 4\) and the rate is \(1/4 = 0.25\). A wrong parameter changes the slope, not the straightness (study guide E1).

15. For a geometric distribution with \(p = 0.5\), what is the 0.75 quantile, and what goes wrong if you use the slide's inverse?

Formula. The CDF is \(F(x) = 1 - (1-p)^x\), where \(p\) is the success probability and \(x\) is a whole number. The \(u\) quantile is the smallest \(x\) with \(F(x) \ge u\), which works out to \(x = \lceil \ln(1-u)/\ln(1-p) \rceil\).

Plug in \(p = 0.5\) and \(u = 0.75\). \(x = \lceil \ln(1 - 0.75)/\ln(1 - 0.5) \rceil = \lceil \ln 0.25/\ln 0.5 \rceil\).

Arithmetic. \(0.25 = 0.5^2\), so \(\ln 0.25 = 2\ln 0.5\) and the ratio is exactly 2. \(\lceil 2 \rceil = 2\). Check, \(F(2) = 1 - 0.5^2 = 1 - 0.25 = 0.75\), and \(F(1) = 1 - 0.5 = 0.5\) is too small.

Result. The 0.75 quantile is 2. The slide's \(\lceil \ln u/\ln(1-p) \rceil\) on Summ p.64 gives \(\lceil \ln 0.75/\ln 0.5 \rceil = \lceil 0.415 \rceil = 1\), which is the 0.25 quantile. That form is fine for generating random values but reverses a Q-Q plot.

16. Hit ratios at three cache levels are 0.90, 0.80 and 0.50. Which mean, what is it, and what does the product mean?

Use the geometric mean, because effects across levels multiply (Summ p.30, study guide B4).

Formula. \(x_G = \big(\prod_{i=1}^{n} x_i\big)^{1/n}\), where \(x_i\) is each level's hit ratio and \(n = 3\).

Plug in. \(x_G = (0.90 \times 0.80 \times 0.50)^{1/3}\).

Arithmetic. \(0.90 \times 0.80 = 0.72\) and \(0.72 \times 0.50 = 0.36\). For the cube root, try values. \(0.71^3 = 0.71 \times 0.71 \times 0.71 = 0.5041 \times 0.71 = 0.358\), just under 0.36. \(0.72^3 = 0.373\), too big. So the root is just above 0.71, about 0.711.

Result. \(x_G \approx 0.711\). The product 0.36 is the compounded pass-through fraction, the share that gets through all three levels, and 0.711 is the per-level ratio that gives the same product.

Confidence intervals

Slides: CI (Lecture 6, Confidence Intervals), plus the confidence-intervals study guide, the Confidence Intervals in the Wild handout and the Monte Carlo handout (Law and Kelton p.90-91). Homework: HW4 (Problems 1 and 2 are the mechanics, Problem 3 is the paper audit).

Errors, accuracy, precision and resolution

The idea

Every measurement you take is the true value plus some error. A confidence interval exists to put a number on that error, but it can only see one kind of it. So before any formula, you need to know which errors repetition can fix and which it can't.

Systematic errors push every measurement the same way, like a clock that drifts with temperature or forgetting to clear the cache before each timing run. Random errors go up or down with equal chance and come from the tool's limits, the person reading it, or random processes inside the system. Repeating the experiment averages random error away and leaves systematic error exactly where it was.

The study guide's one-line summary is worth memorising. A narrow interval is a claim about precision only, so you can be very precise about a wrong number.

What to know

Systematic error comes from an experimental mistake, gives a constant or slowly varying bias, and is controlled by the experimenter's skill (CI p.7). Random error is unpredictable and unbiased, usually can't be controlled, and is what statistics characterises (p.8).

Timer quantization is the standard example of random error. The same event can be reported as 13 or 14 clock ticks depending on where it starts relative to the tick, so a measurement is really \(X \pm \Delta\) (p.9-10).

The error model (p.11-15). Suppose each of \(n\) independent error sources adds \(+E\) or \(-E\) with probability \(\tfrac12\) each. The chance of a given measured value equals the number of paths to it divided by \(2^n\), which is a binomial distribution. As \(n\) grows the binomial turns into the normal, which is why measurements form a bell curve and why the rest of the lecture assumes normal random errors (p.21).

TermCaused byMeans
AccuracySystematic errorsHow close the mean of the measurements is to the true value
PrecisionRandom errorsHow repeatable the measurements are
ResolutionThe toolSmallest increment between measured values

That table is (p.16-17). Accuracy is hard to know because you need an outside standard, resolution depends on the tool, and precision is the one you quantify with statistics (p.18). That last point is the whole reason for the rest of this section.

Why statistics at all (p.3-6). Statistics is the branch of maths that deals with collecting, analysing, interpreting and presenting masses of numerical data, and this course cares about the analysis and interpretation part (p.3). A statistic is a quantity computed from a sample, and it serves as an estimate of a population parameter. Your measurements are a sample of the process you're measuring, so you use statistics for two jobs. One is to infer the true value of the metric. The other is to quantify how much imprecision the random errors left in that estimate.

Turning systematic error into random error (study guide Q17). You can't average away a bias, but you can sometimes spread it out so it acts like noise. Randomize the order of the configurations instead of running all of A and then all of B, so any drift hits both equally. Interleave the runs of each configuration across hours and days, so time-of-day effects on a shared machine get averaged instead of confounded with the comparison. Stabilizer does the same thing to code and heap layout (Wild handout, entry 5). None of this fixes a bias that hits every configuration equally, like a clock source that always under-reports, because randomizing only turns differences between conditions into noise.

Warm-up is also systematic. The first iterations of a JIT or a simulation run are slow for a reason you know, so you delete them before computing anything (study guide Q18, Wild handout entries 4 and 15).

Worked example

Three independent error sources, each \(\pm E\) with probability \(\tfrac12\). What measured values can you get and how likely is each?

  1. Formula. \(\Pr(\text{value}) = \dfrac{\text{number of paths to that value}}{2^n}\), where \(n\) is the number of error sources and \(2^n\) is the number of equally likely ways the \(+E\)/\(-E\) choices can come out. The number of paths with exactly \(k\) minus signs is \(\binom{n}{k}\), the number of ways to pick which \(k\) sources went down (CI p.11-12).
  2. Plug in. \(n = 3\), so the bottom is \(2^3 = 8\). The possible values are \(x - 3E\) (3 minus), \(x - E\) (2 minus), \(x + E\) (1 minus) and \(x + 3E\) (0 minus).
  3. Work it out. \(x - 3E\) needs all three down, \(\binom33 = 1\) path. \(x - E\) needs two down and one up, and the up one can be any of the 3 sources, so \(\binom32 = 3\) paths. \(x + E\) needs one down, \(\binom31 = 3\) paths. \(x + 3E\) needs none down, \(\binom30 = 1\) path. Check the counts add to \(1 + 3 + 3 + 1 = 8\).
  4. Result. \[ \Pr(x-3E) = \tfrac18,\quad \Pr(x-E) = \tfrac38,\quad \Pr(x+E) = \tfrac38,\quad \Pr(x+3E) = \tfrac18 \] They add to \(\tfrac{1+3+3+1}{8} = 1\). The counts 1, 3, 3, 1 are row 3 of Pascal's triangle, so the shape is binomial and already peaks in the middle. With two sources you get the \(\tfrac14, \tfrac12, \tfrac14\) bars on (p.12-13).

Easy to get wrong

  • Calling a narrow interval "accurate". Narrow means precise. A biased setup gives a tight interval around the wrong value, and more runs only make it tighter (study guide, and the Mytkowicz entry in the Wild handout).
  • Mixing up resolution and precision. Resolution is a property of the tool. Precision is how much repeated readings scatter.
  • Thinking quantization is systematic. On (p.9) it is listed as random error because the start of the event relative to the tick is unpredictable.
  • Thinking randomization removes bias. It converts differences between conditions into random error, but an offset shared by every condition stays exactly where it was.

Sampling distribution, estimators and the central limit theorem

The idea

Your measurements are a sample, and the true value you want is a population parameter. A statistic like \(\bar x\) is your estimate of it (CI p.4-5). If you could repeat the whole experiment many times, each repeat would give a different \(\bar x\). Those values have their own distribution, called the sampling distribution of the mean, and it's much narrower than the spread of single measurements.

The central limit theorem says what that distribution looks like. For large samples of independent values it's normal, centred on \(\mu\), with standard deviation \(\sigma/\sqrt n\). Every interval in this lecture is built from that fact.

What to know

Point versus interval estimate (CI p.22-23). A point estimate is a single best guess, like \(\bar x\). An interval estimate \((a, b)\) comes with a stated confidence, the probability that the procedure correctly captures the parameter.

Point estimator properties (p.23). Unbiased means the average of the statistic over all possible samples of size \(n\) equals the parameter. Efficient means precise as an estimator. Consistent means it gets closer to the parameter as \(n\) grows.

The sample mean is unbiased (p.24) because \[ E[\bar X] = \frac{E\left[\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\right]}{n} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n E[X_i]}{n} = \frac{n\mu}{n} = \mu. \]

The tables on (p.25-26) draw samples from the same skewed population HW4 Problem 2 uses (mean 0.2083, variance 0.0440). At \(n = 15\) the three sample means average to 0.2643, an error of 26.9%. At \(n = 87\) they average to 0.2206, an error of 5.9%. That's consistency in action. The sample variance row shrinks the same way. The three variances average 0.0639 at \(n = 15\), which is 45.3% off the population's 0.0440, and 0.0459 at \(n = 87\), which is only 4.3% off. The row labelled "efficiency" there is really the relative error \(|\bar x - \mu|/\mu\) of each sample.

Central limit theorem (p.29). If the observations are independent and come from one population with mean \(\mu\) and standard deviation \(\sigma\), then for large \(n\) \[ \bar x \sim N\!\left(\mu,\ \sigma/\sqrt n\right) \] where the slide writes the second argument as a standard deviation. The standard deviation of the sample mean, \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), is the standard error.

On (p.30) the slide cuts \(\alpha/2\) off each tail of that curve. With \(z_{\alpha/2}\) the critical value of \(N(0,1)\) for an upper-tail area of \(\alpha/2\), \[ c_1 = \mu - \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} z_{\alpha/2}, \qquad c_2 = \mu + \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} z_{\alpha/2}. \] So \(\bar x\) lands in \((c_1, c_2)\) with probability \(1-\alpha\). Flip it around and you get the interval for \(\mu\).

How large is large? A later slide says the sum of a "large number" of values is normal, typically assumed to be more than about 6 or 7 values (p.64). That's a rule about how fast averages become normal for well-behaved data. It is a different rule from the \(n > 30\) cutoff for using \(z\) instead of \(t\) in the next topic.

Monte Carlo estimates are sample means too (Monte Carlo handout, Law and Kelton p.90-91). To estimate \(I = \int_a^b g(x)\,dx\), draw \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) independent and uniform on \([a,b]\) and set \(Y_i = (b-a)g(X_i)\). Then \(E(Y) = I\), so \[ \bar Y(n) = (b-a)\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n g(X_i)}{n} \] is an unbiased estimator of \(I\), and \(\mathrm{Var}[\bar Y(n)] = \mathrm{Var}(Y)/n\), so it's also consistent. The handout's table estimates \(\int_0^\pi \sin x\,dx = 2\) and gets 2.213 at \(n = 10\) but 1.993 at \(n = 160\). Since \(\bar Y(n)\) is just a mean of independent values, the usual mean interval gives its precision, and the sample-size formula tells you how many draws you need. HW4 Problem 2c part 5 is the same idea pointed at a method, because checking an interval's coverage means simulating many samples and counting hits.

Worked example

Response times have \(\sigma = 12\) ms. You take \(n = 36\) independent measurements. Where will \(\bar x\) fall 95% of the time?

  1. Formula. \(\bar x\) lands in \(\mu \pm z_{\alpha/2}\,\dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}\) with probability \(1 - \alpha\) (CI p.30). Here \(\mu\) is the true mean, \(\sigma\) is the population standard deviation, \(n\) is the number of measurements, \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) is the standard error, and \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the normal critical value with \(\alpha/2\) in the upper tail.
  2. Plug in. \(\sigma = 12\), \(n = 36\). For 95%, \(1 - \alpha = 0.95\), so \(\alpha = 0.05\) and \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\). In the normal table, the value with upper-tail area 0.025 is \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So the band is \(\mu \pm 1.96 \times \dfrac{12}{\sqrt{36}}\).
  3. Work it out. \(\sqrt{36} = 6\). Standard error \(= 12/6 = 2\) ms. Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 2 = 3.92\) ms.
  4. Result. \(\bar x\) lands within \(\pm 3.92\) ms of \(\mu\) with probability 0.95. In words, 95 out of 100 repeats of this 36-run experiment would give a mean within about 4 ms of the truth.

Now take \(n = 144\). Same formula, \(\sqrt{144} = 12\), so the standard error is \(12/12 = 1\) ms and the half-width is \(1.96 \times 1 = 1.96\) ms. Four times the data halved the band, because \(\sqrt 4 = 2\).

Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing \(s\) with \(s/\sqrt n\). The standard deviation describes single measurements. The standard error describes the mean.
  • Forgetting the CLT needs independence (p.29, p.65). Consecutive latencies from one run are not independent, and more of them doesn't fix it (p.66).
  • Reading \(N(\mu, \sigma/\sqrt n)\) on (p.29) as a variance. Here the second argument is a standard deviation. On (p.36) the slide writes \(N(\mu, \sigma^2)\) with a variance, so check which one each slide means.

Interval for the mean, z for large n and t for small n

The idea

The interval is the point estimate plus or minus a multiple of the standard error. The only decision is which multiple. If \(\sigma\) is known, or \(n\) is large enough that \(s\) is a good stand-in for \(\sigma\), you use a normal critical value. If \(n\) is small you also have to pay for having estimated \(\sigma\) from the same few values, and the \(t\) distribution's heavier tails are that payment.

This is exactly HW4 Problem 1. You used \(z\) for the sample of 50 and \(t\) with 15 degrees of freedom for the sample of 16.

What to know

Which distribution (CI p.27). With \(\sigma\) known, use the normal. With \(\sigma\) unknown and a large sample, \(s\) is a good estimate of \(\sigma\), so the normal is still fine. With \(\sigma\) unknown, a small sample, and normally distributed data, use \(t\) with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom.

Large samples, \(n > 30\) (p.31). The \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval is \[ \left(\bar x - z_{\alpha/2}\frac{s}{\sqrt n},\ \ \bar x + z_{\alpha/2}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}\right) \] where \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the \((1-\alpha/2)\)-quantile of \(N(0,1)\).

Small samples from a normal population (p.37). \[ \left(\bar x - t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\frac{s}{\sqrt n},\ \ \bar x + t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}\right) \] where \(t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\) is the critical value of \(t\) with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom for an upper-tail area of \(\alpha/2\).

Why \(t\) (p.35-36). For normal data \((\bar X-\mu)/(\sigma/\sqrt n)\) is \(N(0,1)\) and \((n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\) is chi-square with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom, so \((\bar X - \mu)/\sqrt{s^2/n}\) has a \(t\) distribution with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. Its pdf looks like \(N(0,1)\), and for \(v > 30\) degrees of freedom you can approximate it by \(N(0,1)\). That is where the \(n > 30\) rule comes from. The slide's definition is \[ t(v) \sim \frac{N(0,1)}{\sqrt{\chi^2(v)/v}} \] where \(\chi^2(v)\) is the sum of squares of \(v\) independent unit normals (p.35). The study guide puts a number on the rule. At 29 df the 95% value is 2.045 against 1.960, under 5% apart.

Confidence\(\alpha\)\(z_{\alpha/2}\)\(t\), df 3df 8df 9df 15df 24
90%0.101.6452.3531.8601.8331.7531.711
95%0.051.9603.1822.3062.2622.1312.064
99%0.012.5765.8413.3553.2502.9472.797

On (p.38) the slide gets 2.145 for \(n = 15\), which is the df 14 value at 95%. Remember df is \(n-1\), never \(n\). The same slide reruns the three \(n = 15\) samples from (p.25) with \(t\), and all three intervals contain the population mean 0.2083. For \(n = 87\) the table on (p.32) uses \(z\), and its interval sizes 0.0750, 0.0894 and 0.1175 at 90%, 95% and 99% show the width growing with confidence.

Worked example

Small sample. You time a request 16 times and get \(\bar x = 50\) ms and \(s = 8\) ms. Give the 90%, 95% and 99% intervals for the mean.

  1. Formula. \(\bar x \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\,\dfrac{s}{\sqrt n}\) (CI p.37). Here \(\bar x\) is the sample mean, \(s\) is the sample standard deviation, \(n\) is the number of runs, \(s/\sqrt n\) is the standard error, and \(t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\) is the \(t\) critical value with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom and \(\alpha/2\) in the upper tail. You use \(t\) because \(n = 16\) is small and \(\sigma\) is unknown.
  2. Plug in. \(\bar x = 50\), \(s = 8\), \(n = 16\), df \(= 16 - 1 = 15\). So it's \(50 \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,15]} \times 8/\sqrt{16}\).
  3. Work it out. Standard error \(= 8/\sqrt{16} = 8/4 = 2\) ms. Table lookups, all on the df 15 row. For 90%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.05\), column 0.05 gives 1.753. For 95%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\), column 0.025 gives 2.131. For 99%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.005\), column 0.005 gives 2.947. Multiply each by 2 for the half-width, then do \(50 \pm\) half-width.
    Level\(t_{[\alpha/2;\,15]}\)Half-width \(= t \times 2\)Interval
    90%1.7533.506\((50 - 3.506,\ 50 + 3.506) = (46.494,\ 53.506)\)
    95%2.1314.262\((50 - 4.262,\ 50 + 4.262) = (45.738,\ 54.262)\)
    99%2.9475.894\((50 - 5.894,\ 50 + 5.894) = (44.106,\ 55.894)\)
  4. Result. The 95% interval is \((45.738,\ 54.262)\) ms. In words, this procedure catches the true mean 95% of the time, and higher confidence costs a wider interval.

Large sample. Same mean and \(s\), but \(n = 64\).

  1. Formula. \(\bar x \pm z_{\alpha/2}\,\dfrac{s}{\sqrt n}\) (p.31), with \(z_{\alpha/2}\) the normal critical value. You can use \(z\) because \(n > 30\).
  2. Plug in. \(50 \pm 1.96 \times 8/\sqrt{64}\), where 1.96 is the normal value with upper-tail area 0.025.
  3. Work it out. \(\sqrt{64} = 8\), so the standard error is \(8/8 = 1\) ms. Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 1 = 1.96\). Lower end \(50 - 1.96 = 48.04\), upper end \(50 + 1.96 = 51.96\).
  4. Result. \((48.04,\ 51.96)\) ms. Four times the runs plus the smaller \(z\) value made it much narrower.

If the exam hands you raw data, compute \(\bar x\) and \(s\) first with the \(n-1\) divisor (HW4 did this in Python, but on the exam it's by hand). Check question 2 below walks through one.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(n\) instead of \(n-1\) degrees of freedom, as in reading 2.145 off the df 14 row when \(n = 14\).
  • Using the one-tailed column. The interval needs \(\alpha/2\) in each tail, so 95% means the 0.025 upper-tail value, 1.960 for \(z\).
  • Dividing by \(n\) instead of \(\sqrt n\). With \(n = 16\) you divide \(s\) by 4, not 16.
  • Using \(t\) for small skewed samples without comment. The \(t\) interval assumes the data are normal (p.37, p.67). For skewed data at small \(n\) see the last topic.
  • For a population file like HW4's, the population variance divides by \(N\). The sample \(s\) still divides by \(n-1\).

What the confidence level means and what sets the width

The idea

The study guide calls the meaning of "95% confident" the single most misstated idea in the lecture. The 95% describes the procedure. If you repeated the whole experiment many times and built an interval each time, about 95% of those intervals would contain \(\mu\). Your one interval either contains \(\mu\) or it doesn't, since both are fixed numbers once you've computed it.

Width is the other half of reading an interval. You control it through \(n\) and the confidence level, the data control it through \(s\), and the critical value changes with \(n\) when you use \(t\).

What to know

The picture on (CI p.33) is 100 samples, each with its own interval, and about \(100(1-\alpha)\) of them include \(\mu\). The summary slide words it as \(\Pr(\)population parameter within computed interval\()\) (p.74). Read that as the success rate of the method, not as a probability about one interval you already have.

The half-width of a mean interval is \[ h = (\text{critical value}) \times \frac{s}{\sqrt n}. \]

ChangeEffect on widthWhy
Larger \(n\)Narrower, like \(1/\sqrt n\)Standard error shrinks (p.39), and \(t\) drops toward \(z\) (p.35)
Larger \(s\)Wider, in proportionMore scatter in the data
Higher confidenceWiderBigger critical value (p.32)

To halve the width you need four times the data, and one more decimal place costs a hundred times the data. In general \(n_2 = n_1 (h_1/h_2)^2\) (study guide Q15). That question also prices it in time. Going from \(\pm 1.4\) to \(\pm 0.5\) ms takes 196 runs, and at 4 minutes a run that's 784 minutes, about 13 hours, against 100 minutes for the original 25.

A narrower interval at the same \(n\) is bought with confidence, not knowledge. A 90% interval is narrower than a 99% one only because you've accepted that 1 in 10 of them will miss \(\mu\) instead of 1 in 100 (study guide Q3).

To compare two systems, build one interval on the difference. The study guide's formula sheet lists the paired version \(\bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\, s_d/\sqrt n\) and the unpaired one, and Q5 and Q8 work them. Those belong to the comparing-alternatives section, which covers them in full.

Comparing two systems with intervals. If two 95% intervals don't overlap, the means differ at that level. If they do overlap, you've learned nothing, because the difference can still be significant (Wild handout, pitfall 1). The right object is an interval on the difference.

Error bars. At \(n = 5\) the standard error is \(s/\sqrt5 \approx s/2.24\) and the 95% half-width is \(2.776 \times s/2.24 \approx 1.24\,s\). So from narrowest to widest it's SE, then SD, then the CI. A caption that just says "mean of 5 runs" leaves a factor of nearly 3 unexplained (Wild handout, pitfall 6). The CI multiplier on \(s\) is \(t/\sqrt n\), which is about 1.05 at \(n = 6\) and about 0.92 at \(n = 7\), so from 7 runs on the CI is narrower than the SD. Careful with study guide Q21. Its first sentence orders them SE, CI, SD, which contradicts its own arithmetic two lines later. Trust the arithmetic.

Worked example

This mirrors HW4 Problem 1 part 4. Take \(s = 8\). Compare the 95% half-width at \(n = 16\) and at \(n = 64\).

  1. Formula. \(h = (\text{critical value}) \times \dfrac{s}{\sqrt n}\), where \(h\) is the half-width, the critical value is \(t_{[0.025;\,n-1]}\) for small \(n\) or \(z_{0.025}\) for \(n > 30\), \(s\) is the sample standard deviation and \(n\) is the number of runs.
  2. Plug in. At \(n = 16\), \(h_{16} = 2.131 \times 8/\sqrt{16}\), with 2.131 from the \(t\) table, df 15 row, 0.025 column. At \(n = 64\), \(h_{64} = 1.96 \times 8/\sqrt{64}\), with 1.96 from the normal table.
  3. Work it out. \(h_{16} = 2.131 \times 8/4 = 2.131 \times 2 = 4.262\). \(h_{64} = 1.96 \times 8/8 = 1.96 \times 1 = 1.96\). Ratio \(4.262/1.96 \approx 2.17\). Split the ratio into its two causes. The sample size gives \(\sqrt{64/16} = \sqrt4 = 2\). The critical value gives \(2.131/1.96 \approx 1.087\). Multiply them, \(2 \times 1.087 \approx 2.17\), which matches.
  4. Result. The 16-run interval is about 2.17 times wider. In words, most of the gap comes from \(\sqrt n\) and a little from using \(t\) instead of \(z\).

In your HW4 answer the 16-sample interval was about 2.3 times wider than the 50-sample one, with \(\sqrt{50/16} = 1.77\) from \(n\), the rest from \(t\) versus \(z\), and some from that sample happening to have a larger \(s\).

Now the reverse question. Your interval is \(\pm 1.4\) ms from 25 runs and you want \(\pm 0.5\) ms.

  1. Formula. \(n_2 = n_1 \left(\dfrac{h_1}{h_2}\right)^2\), where \(n_1\) and \(h_1\) are the runs and half-width you have and \(h_2\) is the half-width you want (study guide Q15). It comes from \(h \propto 1/\sqrt n\).
  2. Plug in. \(n_2 = 25 \times (1.4/0.5)^2\).
  3. Work it out. \(1.4/0.5 = 2.8\). \(2.8^2 = 7.84\). \(25 \times 7.84 = 196\).
  4. Result. About 196 runs. In words, a 2.8 times narrower interval costs \(2.8^2 = 7.84\) times the runs.

Easy to get wrong

  • Saying "there's a 95% probability \(\mu\) is in this interval". That's a Bayesian credible-interval statement and needs a Bayesian analysis (study guide). Even (p.53) slips into this phrasing with "95% certain".
  • Thinking more data narrows the interval around the true value. It narrows it around \(\bar x\), which only converges to \(\mu\) if there's no systematic error.
  • Treating overlap as "not significant". Non-overlap implies significance, overlap implies nothing.
  • Using one sample's hit or miss to judge a method. Coverage is a long-run rate, so you'd need many samples to check it, which is HW4 Problem 2c part 5.
  • Expecting 10 times the runs to give 10 times narrower. It gives \(\sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\) times narrower.

Interval for a proportion

The idea

Sometimes each observation is a category instead of a number, like whether a file is html, a request failed, or the PC was inside the OS when a timer interrupt fired. You want the fraction of the population in one category. A proportion is just the mean of a 0/1 variable, so the same machinery works with a standard error built from \(p(1-p)\).

What to know

If \(n_1\) of \(n\) observations are in the category, the sample proportion is \(p = n_1/n\) and the population proportion is \(\pi\) (CI p.46). The sampling distribution of \(p\) tends to a normal with mean \(\pi\) and standard error \(\sigma_p = \sqrt{\pi(1-\pi)/n}\) (p.47). This is a normal approximation to the binomial, so the slide requires \(n\pi \ge 10\). The study guide asks for both \(np\) and \(n(1-p)\) to be at least about 10.

The interval (p.48) is \[ \left(p - z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}},\ \ p + z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}\right). \]

The slide examples. A Web log with 650 gif files out of 1000 gives (0.625, 0.675) at 90% and (0.620, 0.680) at 95% (p.50). The OS-time example has 658 of 6000 interrupts in the OS, so \(p = 0.1097\) and the 95% interval is (0.1018, 0.1176), or 10.2% to 11.8% of the time (p.51-53).

The caveat (p.49). This is the Wald interval and it's known to be problematic. Use Wilson for \(n < 40\) and Agresti-Coull for \(n > 40\). The study guide adds that it breaks down when \(p\) is near 0 or 1 or \(n\) is small, and the symptom is an interval running below 0 or above 1. For 99 of 100 the Wilson interval is (0.946, 0.998), which is lopsided and stays inside [0, 1] (study guide Q7).

That failure zone is exactly where CS proportions live, like error rates, failure rates, coverage and benchmark accuracy near the ceiling (Wild handout, pitfall 4). A model scoring 98% on 200 questions has only \(200 \times 0.02 = 4\) misses, well under 10, so its Wald interval can't be trusted.

Worked example

80 of 400 requests hit a slow path. Give the 90% and 95% intervals for the true fraction.

  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\dfrac{p(1-p)}{n}}\) (CI p.48). Here \(p = n_1/n\) is the sample proportion, \(n_1\) is the count in the category, \(n\) is the total count, and \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the normal critical value. The condition is \(np \ge 10\) and \(n(1-p) \ge 10\) (p.47).
  2. Plug in. \(n_1 = 80\), \(n = 400\), so \(p = 80/400\). For 95% use \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), and for 90% use \(z_{0.05} = 1.645\), both from the normal table. So it's \(0.2 \pm z\sqrt{0.2 \times 0.8/400}\).
  3. Work it out. \(p = 80/400 = 0.2\), so \(1 - p = 0.8\). Condition check, \(np = 400 \times 0.2 = 80\) and \(n(1-p) = 400 \times 0.8 = 320\), both over 10. Inside the root, \(0.2 \times 0.8 = 0.16\). Split the root, \(\sqrt{0.16/400} = \sqrt{0.16}/\sqrt{400} = 0.4/20 = 0.02\). At 95%, half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.02 = 0.0392\), so \((0.2 - 0.0392,\ 0.2 + 0.0392) = (0.1608,\ 0.2392)\). At 90%, half-width \(= 1.645 \times 0.02 = 0.0329\), so \((0.2 - 0.0329,\ 0.2 + 0.0329) = (0.1671,\ 0.2329)\).
  4. Result. 95% interval \((0.1608,\ 0.2392)\), 90% interval \((0.1671,\ 0.2329)\). In words, somewhere between about 16% and 24% of all requests hit the slow path.

Splitting the square root into \(\sqrt{\text{top}}/\sqrt{\text{bottom}}\) is the trick that makes these hand-friendly when \(n\) is a perfect square.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(t\). Proportion intervals use \(z\) because they rest on the normal approximation to the binomial.
  • Skipping the \(np \ge 10\) check. With 99 of 100 correct the 95% Wald interval is \((0.9705, 1.0095)\), which goes past 1 (study guide Q7).
  • Forgetting the square root, or putting \(n\) outside it.
  • Comparing two models by whether their proportion intervals overlap. Build an interval on the difference instead.

How many measurements you need

The idea

Run the interval formula backwards. Pick the half-width you can live with, then solve for \(n\). The catch is that the formula needs \(s\) (or \(p\)) before you've collected the data. The fix is a small pilot run used only to estimate \(s\), which then feeds the formula (CI p.41).

The study guide's cultural point is that "we ran it three times" isn't a sample size. A justified \(n\) names the pilot, the target half-width and the confidence level.

What to know

Mean, relative error (p.40). Ask for \((c_1, c_2) = (1 \mp e)\bar x\), so \(z_{\alpha/2}\, s/\sqrt n = \bar x e\), which gives \[ n = \left(\frac{z_{\alpha/2}\, s}{\bar x e}\right)^2. \] The slide example (p.42-44) has \(\bar x = 7.94\) s, \(s = 2.14\) s, 90% confidence and "within 7%", which the slide reads as a total width of 7%, so \(e = 0.035\). Then \(n = (1.645 \times 2.14 / (0.035 \times 7.94))^2 = 160.5\), rounded up to 161.

Mean, absolute half-width \(e\) (study guide formula sheet). \(n = (z_{\alpha/2}\, s / e)^2\). Same idea with \(e\) in data units.

Proportion, relative error (p.54). Setting \((1-e)\bar p = \bar p - z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\bar p(1-\bar p)/n}\) gives \[ n = \frac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, \bar p(1-\bar p)}{(e\bar p)^2}. \] The OS example wants 95% and \(\pm 0.5\%\) with \(\bar p = 0.1097\), so \(e = 0.005\) is relative to \(\bar p\). That needs 1,247,102 interrupts, which at one per 10 ms is 3.46 hours (p.55-57).

Proportion, absolute half-width \(e\) (study guide). \(n = z_{\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)/e^2\). With no estimate of \(p\), use \(p = 0.5\) because it makes \(p(1-p)\) as large as it can be.

Always round up, because rounding down misses the target.

Worked example

Mean. A pilot of 10 runs gives \(\bar x = 20\) ms and \(s = 5\) ms. How many runs for a 95% interval of \(\pm 2\%\)?

  1. Formula. \(n = \left(\dfrac{z_{\alpha/2}\, s}{\bar x e}\right)^2\) (CI p.40). Here \(\bar x\) and \(s\) come from the pilot, \(e\) is the relative half-width as a fraction, \(\bar x e\) is that half-width in data units, and \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the normal critical value.
  2. Plug in. \(\bar x = 20\), \(s = 5\), \(e = 0.02\), and 95% gives \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So \(n = \left(\dfrac{1.96 \times 5}{20 \times 0.02}\right)^2\).
  3. Work it out. Bottom, \(20 \times 0.02 = 0.4\) ms. Top, \(1.96 \times 5 = 9.8\). Ratio, \(9.8/0.4 = 24.5\). Square, \(24.5^2 = 24^2 + 2(24)(0.5) + 0.5^2 = 576 + 24 + 0.25 = 600.25\). Round up to 601.
  4. Result. 601 runs. In words, that many runs puts the 95% interval within 2% of the mean, about \(\pm 0.4\) ms.

Proportion, relative error. A pilot says about 20% of requests hit the slow path. You want 95% confidence and \(\pm 10\%\) relative error.

  1. Formula. \(n = \dfrac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, \bar p(1-\bar p)}{(e\bar p)^2}\) (p.54). Here \(\bar p\) is the pilot proportion, \(e\) is the relative error as a fraction of \(\bar p\), so \(e\bar p\) is the half-width in proportion units.
  2. Plug in. \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), \(\bar p = 0.2\), \(e = 0.1\). So \(n = \dfrac{1.96^2 \times 0.2 \times 0.8}{(0.1 \times 0.2)^2}\).
  3. Work it out. \(1.96^2 = 3.8416\). \(0.2 \times 0.8 = 0.16\). Top, \(3.8416 \times 0.16 = 0.614656\). Bottom, \(0.1 \times 0.2 = 0.02\) and \(0.02^2 = 0.0004\). Dividing by 0.0004 is the same as multiplying by 2500, so \(0.614656 \times 2500 = 1536.64\). Round up to 1537. \[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.2 \times 0.8}{0.02^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.16}{0.0004} = \frac{0.614656}{0.0004} = 1536.64 \]
  4. Result. 1537 requests. In words, that pins the slow-path rate to \(0.2 \pm 0.02\) at 95%.

Proportion, absolute, no pilot (study guide Q13). You want a failure rate within \(\pm 3\) percentage points at 95% and know nothing about it.

  1. Formula. \(n = \dfrac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)}{e^2}\) (study guide), where \(e\) is now the absolute half-width in proportion units and \(p\) is your guess at the rate. With no guess, use \(p = 0.5\) because it makes \(p(1-p)\) as large as it can be.
  2. Plug in. \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), \(p = 0.5\), \(e = 0.03\). So \(n = \dfrac{1.96^2 \times 0.5 \times 0.5}{0.03^2}\).
  3. Work it out. \(1.96^2 = 3.8416\). \(0.5 \times 0.5 = 0.25\). Top, \(3.8416 \times 0.25 = 0.9604\). Bottom, \(0.03^2 = 0.0009\). \(0.9604/0.0009 = 1067.1\). Round up to 1068. \[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.25}{0.03^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.25}{0.0009} = \frac{0.9604}{0.0009} = 1067.1 \]
  4. Result. 1068 trials. In words, that's enough for \(\pm 3\) points whatever the true rate turns out to be.

If a pilot says the rate is about 5% (Q14), redo step 3 with \(p = 0.05\). Then \(p(1-p) = 0.05 \times 0.95 = 0.0475\), the top is \(3.8416 \times 0.0475 = 0.182476\), and \(n = 0.182476/0.0009 = 202.75\), so 203 trials. The catch is that \(\pm 3\) points around 5% is a relative error of \(0.03/0.05 = 60\%\), which is almost never what you wanted. For rare events, state the target as relative error, which is the slides' formula.

Easy to get wrong

  • Mixing up relative and absolute error. The slides' proportion formula divides by \((e\bar p)^2\) because their \(e\) is a fraction of \(\bar p\). The study guide's formula divides by \(e^2\) with \(e\) in percentage points. Read which one the question means.
  • Using the full width as \(e\). "Within 7%" on (p.42) became \(e = 0.035\), half of 7%.
  • Rounding 160.5 down to 160. Always round up.
  • Using \(t\). You don't know \(n\) yet, so you can't know the degrees of freedom. The slides use \(z\).
  • Forgetting to square. \(n\) grows with the square of \(z s/(\bar x e)\).
  • Trusting a low pilot estimate of \(p\). If the true rate is closer to 0.5 than the pilot said, \(p(1-p)\) is bigger and your interval comes out wider than planned, so round the pilot estimate toward 0.5 (study guide Q14).
  • Writing "we ran it three times" as a sample size. A justified \(n\) reports the pilot, the target half-width and the confidence level so a reviewer can check it (study guide Q16).

Interval for a variance

The idea

Sometimes the claim is about variability itself, like jitter or tail predictability. If the data are normal, \((n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\) follows a chi-square distribution, and you can invert that just like you inverted the \(t\) statistic. The chi-square isn't symmetric, so the interval isn't symmetric around \(s^2\) either.

What to know

The \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval for \(\sigma^2\) (CI p.59) is \[ \frac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_U} \le \sigma^2 \le \frac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_L} \] where \(\chi^2_L\) is the lower critical value (the \(\alpha/2\) quantile) and \(\chi^2_U\) is the upper one (the \(1-\alpha/2\) quantile), both with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. The big critical value gives the lower limit, and the two limits sit at different distances from \(s^2\) because the chi-square is not symmetric (p.59-60).

Slide example (p.61). For \(n = 100\) from \(N(3,2)\), \(s^2 = 4.71435\), the critical values with 99 df are 73.361 and 128.422, and the interval is \((3.6343, 6.362)\), which contains the true variance 4.

Only trust it for normal data. For non-normal populations, especially small samples, it isn't very accurate (p.62). For an interval on \(\sigma\), take square roots of both ends.

Worked example

Fifteen runs give \(s^2 = 4.0\) ms². Give the 95% interval for \(\sigma^2\) and \(\sigma\) (study guide Q6).

  1. Formula. \(\dfrac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_U} \le \sigma^2 \le \dfrac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_L}\) (CI p.59). Here \(n\) is the number of runs, \(s^2\) is the sample variance, \(\chi^2_U\) is the upper chi-square critical value (area \(\alpha/2\) above it) and \(\chi^2_L\) is the lower one (area \(\alpha/2\) below it), both with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom.
  2. Plug in. \(n - 1 = 14\), \(s^2 = 4.0\). For 95%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\). On the chi-square table's df 14 row, the value with 0.025 below it is \(\chi^2_L = 5.629\) and the value with 0.025 above it is \(\chi^2_U = 26.119\). So \(\dfrac{14 \times 4.0}{26.119} \le \sigma^2 \le \dfrac{14 \times 4.0}{5.629}\).
  3. Work it out. Numerator \(14 \times 4.0 = 56\). Lower limit \(56/26.119\). Since \(26.119 \times 2 = 52.238\), the remainder is \(56 - 52.238 = 3.762\), and \(3.762/26.119 \approx 0.144\), so about 2.144. Upper limit \(56/5.629\). Since \(5.629 \times 10 = 56.29\) is just over 56, the answer is just under 10, about 9.95. For \(\sigma\) take square roots, \(\sqrt{2.144} \approx 1.46\) (check \(1.46^2 = 2.13\)) and \(\sqrt{9.95} \approx 3.15\) (check \(3.15^2 = 9.92\)).
  4. Result. \(\sigma^2 \in (2.14,\ 9.95)\) ms² and \(\sigma \in (1.46,\ 3.15)\) ms. In words, fifteen runs leave the variance uncertain by more than a factor of 4, so they pin down a mean much better than a variance.

Easy to get wrong

  • Putting the small critical value under the lower limit. That gives limits in the wrong order.
  • Using \(n\) degrees of freedom or forgetting to multiply \(s^2\) by \(n-1\).
  • Writing the interval as \(s^2 \pm\) something. It's not symmetric.
  • Applying it to skewed data. It needs a normal population (p.59, p.62).

When normality or independence fails, batching, transformation and the bootstrap

The idea

Everything so far assumed normal errors and independent measurements (CI p.63, p.65). The CLT rescues normality for large \(n\), but not for small skewed samples, and nothing rescues you from dependence by adding more measurements from the same run (p.66-67). The deck gives three responses, and they are not interchangeable. HW4 Problem 2 had you run two of them on the skewed data.

What to know

The damage (p.67). With \(n = 16\) from a right-skewed population (skew 1.8), a nominal 95% \(t\) interval contains \(\mu\) only about 91% of the time. The error is systematic, since the interval is consistently too narrow.

Batching (p.68). Average groups of four or more observations and build the \(t\) interval from the batch means, which is the CLT applied by hand. With \(m\) batches the interval is \(\bar b \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,m-1]}\, s_b/\sqrt m\). The cost is degrees of freedom. 16 values become 4 batch means, so \(t\) goes from 2.13 to 3.18, about 50% wider, and you no longer have an interval for individual values. It's the only response that handles correlated observations (p.70). Batch in the order the data were collected, never after sorting.

Why batching works on dependence (study guide, Wild handout entry 15). Averaging within a batch breaks most of the correlation between batch means, so the batch means act like an independent sample. The standard error of the mean barely changes, since the mean of the batch means is the grand mean. What you lose is degrees of freedom. Batch size is a tradeoff. Batches too small leave correlation and the interval stays too narrow, and batches too large leave too few batches and the \(t\) multiplier explodes. Delete the warm-up before batching, because that's a systematic error.

Transformation (p.68). Apply log, square root or Box-Cox, then use the \(t\) interval. Choose the transform from the data. Back-transforming the endpoints gives an interval for the median, not the mean, and the centre \(e^{\text{mean}(\ln x)}\) is the geometric mean.

Bootstrap (p.69). Treat your sample as a stand-in for the population. Draw \(n\) values from it with replacement, compute the statistic, repeat \(B\) times (\(B = 10{,}000\) takes seconds), and read off the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles for a 95% interval. With replacement is essential so resamples differ like independent samples would. These percentile intervals under-cover at small \(n\), meaning the real coverage is below the promised level, so prefer BCa, SciPy's default. BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) reads off a shifted, lopsided pair of percentiles using a bias correction, from the fraction of resamples below the observed estimate, and an acceleration, from the jackknife (study guide). It works for medians, quantiles, ratios, correlations and differences of medians, which have no formula. Use a large \(B\) because it costs CPU, not data. With \(B = 40\) the tails can only move in steps of \(1/40 = 2.5\%\) and the endpoints jump around from run to run (study guide Q11).

SituationUse
Observations correlated (simulation output, consecutive requests)Batching
You want the median, or the process is multiplicativeTransformation
You want the mean and observations are independentBootstrap, the modern default and narrowest at equal coverage

That's (p.70). None of the three creates information, so at \(n = 16\) they all stay approximate and more data is the real fix. If your 16 values missed the tail, every resample misses it too. The slide ends by asking whether, with strongly skewed data, the mean is even the summary you want.

A geometric-mean speedup is the log transform done silently, since the log of the geometric mean is the mean of the logs (Wild handout, entry 16). So a paper that reports one has already moved to the median, whether it says so or not.

Worked example

Sixteen latencies in the order measured, in batches of four.

BatchValuesSumMean \(b_j\)
17, 9, 14, 1040\(40/4 = 10\)
215, 12, 13, 1656\(56/4 = 14\)
311, 13, 10, 1448\(48/4 = 12\)
418, 14, 17, 1564\(64/4 = 16\)
  1. Formula. \(\bar b \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,m-1]}\,\dfrac{s_b}{\sqrt m}\) (CI p.68). Here \(m\) is the number of batches, \(b_j\) is the mean of batch \(j\), \(\bar b\) is the mean of the batch means, and \(s_b = \sqrt{\sum (b_j - \bar b)^2/(m-1)}\) is the standard deviation of the batch means.
  2. Plug in. \(m = 4\), batch means 10, 14, 12, 16. For 95% the \(t\) table's df \(4 - 1 = 3\) row, 0.025 column gives \(t_{[0.025;\,3]} = 3.182\).
  3. Work it out. \(\bar b = (10+14+12+16)/4 = 52/4 = 13\), which equals the grand mean \(208/16 = 13\), as it always must. Deviations from 13 are \(-3, 1, -1, 3\). Squares are \(9, 1, 1, 9\), summing to 20. \(s_b^2 = 20/(4-1) = 6.667\) and \(s_b = \sqrt{6.667} \approx 2.582\) (check \(2.58^2 = 6.656\)). Standard error \(= 2.582/\sqrt4 = 2.582/2 = 1.291\). Half-width \(= 3.182 \times 1.291 \approx 4.108\). Ends \(13 - 4.108 = 8.892\) and \(13 + 4.108 = 17.108\).
  4. Result. \((8.892,\ 17.108)\). In words, treating the batch means as four independent values gives a wide interval because 3 df makes \(t\) large.

In your HW4 run the batch means were 0.4212, 0.2368, 0.0904 and 0.2139 with \(s_b = 0.1365\). Same formula, \(0.2406 \pm 3.182 \times 0.1365/2\). Here \(0.1365/2 = 0.06825\) and \(3.182 \times 0.06825 = 0.2172\), so \(0.2406 \pm 0.2172\) is (0.0234, 0.4578) by hand and (0.0233, 0.4578) in your table from the unrounded values. The 99% one used 5.841 (df 3 row, 0.005 column) and went below zero, which is impossible for that data. That's the price of only 3 degrees of freedom.

Your HW4 bootstrap on the same 16 values gave (0.1493, 0.3407) at 95%, narrower than the raw \(t\) interval (0.1319, 0.3493), which fits (p.70) calling it the narrowest. All nine HW4 intervals contained 0.2083, but one sample can't show coverage.

Log transform (study guide Q10). Sixteen skewed latencies have mean 18.394 ms, but in logs mean\((\ln x) = 2.7392\) and \(s = 0.5335\).

  1. Formula. Take \(y_i = \ln x_i\), build \(\bar y \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\, s_y/\sqrt n\) on the log scale, then exponentiate both ends (p.68). Here \(\bar y\) and \(s_y\) are the mean and standard deviation of the logs.
  2. Plug in. \(\bar y = 2.7392\), \(s_y = 0.5335\), \(n = 16\), and \(t_{[0.025;\,15]} = 2.131\) from the df 15 row. So \(2.7392 \pm 2.131 \times 0.5335/\sqrt{16}\).
  3. Work it out. Standard error \(= 0.5335/4 = 0.1334\). Half-width \(= 2.131 \times 0.1334 \approx 0.2842\). Log-scale interval \((2.7392 - 0.2842,\ 2.7392 + 0.2842) = (2.4550,\ 3.0234)\). Exponentiate (the exam would give you these), \(e^{2.4550} \approx 11.65\), \(e^{3.0234} \approx 20.56\), and the centre \(e^{2.7392} \approx 15.5\).
  4. Result. About \((11.65,\ 20.56)\) ms, an interval for the median centred on the geometric mean 15.5 ms. That sits below the 18.4 ms arithmetic mean because the two big values pull the arithmetic mean up. It's symmetric by ratio, \(15.5/11.65 \approx 1.33\) and \(20.56/15.5 \approx 1.33\), not by milliseconds.

Bootstrap bookkeeping (study guide Q11). With \(B = 10{,}000\) sorted resample means, the 95% percentile interval cuts 2.5% off each end. \(0.025 \times 10{,}000 = 250\), so the lower end is about the 251st value, and \(0.975 \times 10{,}000 = 9750\), so the upper end is about the 9750th. For 90%, \(0.05 \times 10{,}000 = 500\) and \(0.95 \times 10{,}000 = 9500\), so the 501st and 9500th. Conventions differ by one index.

Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting batching to narrow the interval. It almost always widens it because \(t\) jumps with fewer degrees of freedom. In HW4 it came out about twice as wide as the raw \(t\) interval at 95%.
  • Sorting before batching. Then the batches aren't draws from the same distribution (HW4 2a).
  • Believing the bootstrap "makes no assumptions". It still needs independent draws, and resampling an autocorrelated trace gives the same too-narrow interval (Wild handout, pitfall 7).
  • Reporting a back-transformed log interval as an interval for the mean. It's for the median.
  • Bootstrapping without replacement. Every resample would then be your original sample.

What an interval doesn't cover, reading intervals in papers

The idea

The whole lecture is machinery for one kind of uncertainty, the error that shrinks when you repeat a measurement. Bias, confounding, a sample that isn't representative and the wrong summary statistic all sit outside the interval, and they're usually the bigger problem (study guide, closing section). The Wild handout and HW4 Problem 3 ask you to audit a real paper with this in mind, and the study guide's interpretation questions (Q19 to Q26) are where people lose marks.

What to know

Sampling error only (Wild handout, pitfall 3). A user-initiated speed-test dataset is a selection effect, since people run tests when the connection feels slow. Ten times the tests narrows the interval and leaves the bias exactly where it was, so more data makes a biased estimate more confidently wrong.
