The fix is a design that breaks the link between outcome and inclusion, like scheduled tests from fixed vantage points (study guide Q23, Wild handout entry 12).

Right statistic (pitfall 5, study guide Q22). Latency is right-skewed and SLOs are written on the 99th percentile. A tight interval on the mean answers a question nobody asked, because a 42 ms mean fits a 400 ms p99. An interval on a quantile needs order statistics or the bootstrap, since the lecture's formulas cover means, proportions and variances. Raasveldt et al. report medians with quantile-based intervals for this reason (Wild handout, entry 14).

Experimental unit (pitfall 7, study guide Q19). Two hundred iterations inside one JVM share JIT state, heap layout and CPU frequency history, so they aren't 200 independent draws. Treating them as replicates is pseudo-replication and gives a far too narrow interval. Either make the invocation the unit (many JVM runs, one number each, then resample those) or delete warm-up and use batch means.

Randomized algorithms (study guide Q24). Intervals still apply. The population is the distribution of outcomes over random seeds on that input and machine, so the claim covers that input only. Generalizing to other inputs is a second, usually larger, source of variance.

Best of 3 (study guide Q25). The maximum of \(n\) runs is biased, since it grows with \(n\), and a reader who runs the method once lands near the mean, not your best. Comparing your best against a baseline's mean is rigged before any statistics. Report all runs, or a robust statistic with a bootstrap interval as Agarwal et al. do, and call the result preliminary if you can't support an interval.

Huge \(n\) (Wild handout, entry 9). With millions of users every difference is statistically detectable, so the question becomes whether the effect is big enough to matter.

The audit (Wild handout Part III, HW4 Problem 3). Find the interval, name the estimator, find \(n\) and whether it's justified, check the normality assumption and which response was used, check what the independent unit is, name one error source the interval leaves out, and check whether the conclusion quietly needs a credible interval.

Worked example

Your HW4 Problem 3 audit of the Puffer paper is the model. The stall ratio is total stall time over total watch time, a ratio with no lecture formula, so the authors bootstrap it (CI p.69). They randomized by session but resampled streams, and streams in one session share a connection, so the unit doesn't match the randomization and the intervals are probably too narrow. Resampling whole sessions would fix that. The intervals also say nothing about viewers who never found Puffer, which is error outside the interval. Finally, they compare schemes by whether error bars overlap, which isn't a significance test.

A quick number to keep the p99 point concrete. The count above the p99 is \((1 - 0.99) \times n\). With \(n = 10{,}000\) that's \(0.01 \times 10{,}000 = 100\) values. So a tail estimate rests on about 100 points even when the mean rests on 10,000. And if all 10,000 came from one run, the interval also says nothing about run-to-run variation (study guide Q22).

Easy to get wrong

  • Reading a narrow interval as a trustworthy result. Every measurement in a biased or confounded setup can carry a respectable interval (Wild handout, entry 14).
  • Counting measurements instead of units. Ask what was independently replicated.
  • Saying intervals don't apply to randomized algorithms. They do, with the seed distribution as the population.
  • Accepting undefined error bars. Check the caption for SD, SE or CI (pitfall 6).

Check yourself

1. True or false. A computed 95% interval of (45.7, 54.3) ms has a 95% probability of containing the true mean.

False. Once you've computed it, the interval and \(\mu\) are both fixed numbers, so it either contains \(\mu\) or it doesn't. The 95% is the long-run rate at which the procedure produces intervals that contain \(\mu\), which is the 100-samples picture on (CI p.33). A probability statement about this one interval would need a Bayesian credible interval.

2. Nine runs give 4, 6, 5, 7, 3, 5, 6, 4, 5 seconds. Find the 95% interval for the mean.
  1. Formula. \(\bar x \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\,\dfrac{s}{\sqrt n}\) (CI p.37), with \(\bar x = \sum x_i/n\) the sample mean and \(s = \sqrt{\sum (x_i - \bar x)^2/(n-1)}\) the sample standard deviation. You need \(t\) because \(n = 9\) is small.
  2. Plug in. \(n = 9\), so df \(= 8\). For 95%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\), and the \(t\) table's df 8 row, 0.025 column gives \(t_{[0.025;\,8]} = 2.306\).
  3. Work it out. Sum \(= 4+6+5+7+3+5+6+4+5 = 45\), so \(\bar x = 45/9 = 5\). Deviations from 5 are \(-1, 1, 0, 2, -2, 0, 1, -1, 0\). Squares are \(1, 1, 0, 4, 4, 0, 1, 1, 0\), summing to 12. \(s^2 = 12/8 = 1.5\) and \(s = \sqrt{1.5} \approx 1.225\) (check \(1.225^2 \approx 1.5\)). Standard error \(= 1.225/\sqrt9 = 1.225/3 \approx 0.408\). Half-width \(= 2.306 \times 0.408 \approx 0.941\). Ends \(5 - 0.941 = 4.059\) and \(5 + 0.941 = 5.941\).
  4. Result. \((4.06,\ 5.94)\) s. In words, the true mean run time is very likely between about 4.1 and 5.9 s.
3. In HW4 Problem 1 you used z for the sample of 50 and t for the sample of 16. Why, and why was the 16-sample interval so much wider?

You use \(z\) for 50 because \(n > 30\), and \(t\) for 16 because it's small. With \(n > 30\), \(s\) is a good estimate of \(\sigma\) and \(t\) with more than 30 df is close to \(N(0,1)\) (CI p.31, p.35). With 16 values you also have to pay for estimating \(\sigma\), so you use \(t\) with \(16 - 1 = 15\) df (p.37). The width grew for three reasons. The standard error has \(\sqrt n\) underneath, and \(\sqrt{50/16} = \sqrt{3.125} \approx 1.77\). The critical value went from 1.960 to 2.131, a factor of \(2.131/1.960 \approx 1.09\). And your sample of 16 happened to have a larger \(s\).

4. 90 of 900 test cases fail. Give the 95% interval for the failure rate and check the condition.
  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\dfrac{p(1-p)}{n}}\) (CI p.48), with \(p = n_1/n\) the failure fraction, \(n_1\) the failures, \(n\) the test cases. The condition is \(np \ge 10\) and \(n(1-p) \ge 10\) (p.47).
  2. Plug in. \(p = 90/900\), \(n = 900\), and \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\) from the normal table. So \(0.1 \pm 1.96\sqrt{0.1 \times 0.9/900}\).
  3. Work it out. \(p = 90/900 = 0.1\). Condition, \(np = 900 \times 0.1 = 90\) and \(n(1-p) = 900 \times 0.9 = 810\), both over 10. Inside the root, \(0.1 \times 0.9 = 0.09\). Split it, \(\sqrt{0.09}/\sqrt{900} = 0.3/30 = 0.01\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.01 = 0.0196\). Ends \(0.1 - 0.0196 = 0.0804\) and \(0.1 + 0.0196 = 0.1196\).
  4. Result. \((0.0804,\ 0.1196)\). In words, the true failure rate is very likely between about 8% and 12%.
5. 2 of 50 runs crash. Compute the 95% Wald interval. What's wrong with it and what would you use instead?
  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\dfrac{p(1-p)}{n}}\) (CI p.48), with \(p\) the crash fraction and \(n\) the runs.
  2. Plug in. \(p = 2/50\), \(n = 50\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So \(0.04 \pm 1.96\sqrt{0.04 \times 0.96/50}\).
  3. Work it out. \(p = 2/50 = 0.04\) and \(1 - p = 0.96\). \(0.04 \times 0.96 = 0.0384\). \(0.0384/50 = 0.000768\). \(\sqrt{0.000768} \approx 0.0277\) (check \(0.0277^2 \approx 0.000767\)). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.0277 \approx 0.0543\). Ends \(0.04 - 0.0543 = -0.0143\) and \(0.04 + 0.0543 = 0.0943\).
  4. Result. \((-0.014,\ 0.094)\). A negative crash rate is impossible, which tells you the method broke.

The problem is that the normal approximation fails here, because \(np = 50 \times 0.04 = 2\) is far below 10 (CI p.47). The slide's alternatives are Wilson for \(n < 40\) and Agresti-Coull for \(n > 40\), so with \(n = 50\) you'd use Agresti-Coull (p.49).

6. A pilot gives a mean of 40 ms and s = 8 ms. How many runs for a 90% interval within ±5% of the mean?
  1. Formula. \(n = \left(\dfrac{z_{\alpha/2}\, s}{\bar x e}\right)^2\) (CI p.40), with \(\bar x\) and \(s\) from the pilot and \(e\) the relative half-width as a fraction.
  2. Plug in. \(\bar x = 40\), \(s = 8\), \(e = 0.05\). For 90%, \(\alpha/2 = 0.05\), and the normal table gives \(z_{0.05} = 1.645\). So \(n = \left(\dfrac{1.645 \times 8}{40 \times 0.05}\right)^2\).
  3. Work it out. Bottom, \(40 \times 0.05 = 2\). Top, \(1.645 \times 8 = 13.16\). Ratio, \(13.16/2 = 6.58\). Square, \(6.58^2 = 43.30\). Round up to 44.
  4. Result. 44 runs. In words, that's enough to get the 90% interval within \(\pm 2\) ms of the mean. Round up, never down.
7. Your 95% interval from 20 runs is ±3 ms. Your advisor wants ±1 ms. Roughly how many runs?
  1. Formula. \(n_2 = n_1 \left(\dfrac{h_1}{h_2}\right)^2\), with \(n_1, h_1\) the runs and half-width you have and \(h_2\) the half-width you want. It comes from the half-width scaling like \(1/\sqrt n\) (CI p.39).
  2. Plug in. \(n_2 = 20 \times (3/1)^2\).
  3. Work it out. \(3/1 = 3\), \(3^2 = 9\), \(20 \times 9 = 180\).
  4. Result. About 180 runs. In words, a three-times-narrower interval costs nine times the runs. It's approximate because \(s\) and the critical value will shift a little.
8. Fifteen runs have s = 3 ms. Give the 95% interval for σ² using χ² values 5.629 and 26.119 (14 df).
  1. Formula. \(\dfrac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_U} \le \sigma^2 \le \dfrac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_L}\) (CI p.59), with \(\chi^2_U\) the big critical value and \(\chi^2_L\) the small one, both at \(n-1\) df.
  2. Plug in. \(n - 1 = 14\), \(s^2 = 3^2 = 9\), \(\chi^2_U = 26.119\) (0.025 above it on the df 14 row) and \(\chi^2_L = 5.629\) (0.025 below it). So \(\dfrac{14 \times 9}{26.119} \le \sigma^2 \le \dfrac{14 \times 9}{5.629}\).
  3. Work it out. Numerator \(14 \times 9 = 126\). Lower limit \(126/26.119 \approx 4.82\) (check \(26.119 \times 4.82 \approx 125.9\)). Upper limit \(126/5.629 \approx 22.38\) (check \(5.629 \times 22.38 \approx 126.0\)). For \(\sigma\), \(\sqrt{4.82} \approx 2.20\) and \(\sqrt{22.38} \approx 4.73\).
  4. Result. \(\sigma^2 \in (4.82,\ 22.38)\) ms² and \(\sigma \in (2.20,\ 4.73)\) ms. In words, the true spread could be anywhere from about 2 to almost 5 ms. It assumes normal data (p.62).
9. In HW4 the batched interval was the widest. Why does a "correction" for non-normality make the interval wider, and what did you give up?

It's wider because batching costs degrees of freedom, and you gave up those degrees of freedom plus any statement about individual values. Batching turns 16 observations into 4 batch means, so df drops from \(16 - 1 = 15\) to \(4 - 1 = 3\), and the 95% \(t\) value goes from 2.131 (df 15 row) to 3.182 (df 3 row), a factor of \(3.182/2.131 \approx 1.49\) (CI p.68). The standard error stays about the same, so the width grows with the multiplier. At 99% the df 3 value is 5.841, and your interval went below zero, a sign that 4 batches is too few at that level.

10. Which response would you use for (a) waiting times of 100,000 consecutive customers in one simulation run, (b) speedups on 12 benchmarks, (c) 40 independent skewed job times when you want the mean?

(a) Batching, because consecutive waits are correlated and batching is the only response that handles dependence. (b) Transformation, because speedups are multiplicative and the log scale gives the geometric mean, with the caveat that the back-transformed interval is for the median. (c) Bootstrap, because the data are independent, you want the mean, and it's the narrowest at equal coverage (CI p.70). BCa is better than plain percentiles here.

11. A colleague's interval is ±0.2 ms after 5,000 runs, so they call the result accurate. What's your reply?

It shows the result is precise, not that it's accurate. A narrow interval reflects small random error. Accuracy depends on systematic error, which repetition doesn't touch (CI p.17). A miscalibrated timer or a warm cache would give a tight interval around the wrong number. If the 5,000 values are consecutive requests from one run they also aren't independent, so the interval is too narrow anyway (p.66).

12. Two systems have 95% intervals (10, 14) and (13, 17). Can you conclude their means don't differ significantly?

No. Overlap tells you nothing. Non-overlap would imply a significant difference, but overlapping intervals are still compatible with a significant one, because the interval on the difference is narrower than the two separate intervals put end to end. Build an interval on the difference and see whether it contains 0 (Wild handout, pitfall 1, and study guide Q20).

13. A benchmark runs 10 times with mean 7.2 s and s = 0.8 s. Give the 95% interval. Then redo it for 100 runs with the same mean and s, and explain why the half-width doesn't shrink by a factor of 10.
  1. Formula. Small \(n\), \(\bar x \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\, s/\sqrt n\) (CI p.37). Large \(n\), \(\bar x \pm z_{\alpha/2}\, s/\sqrt n\) (p.31).
  2. Plug in. For \(n = 10\), df \(= 9\), and the df 9 row, 0.025 column gives 2.262. So \(7.2 \pm 2.262 \times 0.8/\sqrt{10}\). For \(n = 100\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), so \(7.2 \pm 1.96 \times 0.8/\sqrt{100}\).
  3. Work it out. At \(n = 10\), \(\sqrt{10} \approx 3.162\), standard error \(= 0.8/3.162 \approx 0.253\), half-width \(= 2.262 \times 0.253 \approx 0.572\), ends \(7.2 - 0.572 = 6.628\) and \(7.2 + 0.572 = 7.772\). At \(n = 100\), \(\sqrt{100} = 10\), standard error \(= 0.8/10 = 0.08\), half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.08 \approx 0.157\), ends \(7.2 - 0.157 = 7.043\) and \(7.2 + 0.157 = 7.357\). Ratio of half-widths \(0.572/0.157 \approx 3.6\). That splits into \(\sqrt{100/10} = \sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\) from \(n\) times \(2.262/1.96 \approx 1.15\) from dropping \(t\), and \(3.16 \times 1.15 \approx 3.6\).
  4. Result. \((6.63,\ 7.77)\) s at \(n = 10\) and \((7.04,\ 7.36)\) s at \(n = 100\). It isn't 10 times narrower because width goes like \(1/\sqrt n\) (p.39).

For the \(n = 10\) data at other levels (study guide Q3), keep the standard error 0.253 and change only the table value on the df 9 row. For 90%, column 0.05 gives 1.833, half-width \(1.833 \times 0.253 \approx 0.464\), interval \((7.2 - 0.464,\ 7.2 + 0.464) = (6.74,\ 7.66)\). For 99%, column 0.005 gives 3.250, half-width \(3.250 \times 0.253 \approx 0.822\), interval \((6.38,\ 8.02)\). The 90% one is narrower only because you accept that 1 in 10 such intervals misses \(\mu\) instead of 1 in 100. This is study guide Q1 to Q3.

14. You want a failure rate within ±3 percentage points at 95% and have no estimate of it. How many trials? What if a pilot says about 5%, and what's the catch?
  1. Formula. \(n = \dfrac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)}{e^2}\) (study guide), with \(e\) the absolute half-width and \(p\) your guess at the rate. With no guess use \(p = 0.5\), because it makes \(p(1-p)\) as big as possible.
  2. Plug in. \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), \(e = 0.03\). No estimate gives \(n = \dfrac{1.96^2 \times 0.5 \times 0.5}{0.03^2}\). The pilot gives \(n = \dfrac{1.96^2 \times 0.05 \times 0.95}{0.03^2}\).
  3. Work it out. \(1.96^2 = 3.8416\) and \(0.03^2 = 0.0009\). No estimate, \(3.8416 \times 0.25 = 0.9604\), and \(0.9604/0.0009 = 1067.1\), round up to 1068. Pilot, \(0.05 \times 0.95 = 0.0475\), \(3.8416 \times 0.0475 = 0.182476\), and \(0.182476/0.0009 = 202.75\), round up to 203.
  4. Result. 1068 trials with no estimate, 203 with the pilot. In words, knowing the rate is small saves a lot of trials.

The catch is that \(\pm 3\) points on a 5% rate is \(0.03/0.05 = 60\%\) relative error, too loose to be useful. State rare-event targets as relative error, which is the slides' form \(n = z_{\alpha/2}^2\,\bar p(1-\bar p)/(e\bar p)^2\) (CI p.54). This is study guide Q13 and Q14.

15. You estimate the integral of sin x from 0 to π by Monte Carlo. From 100 draws, Y = π sin X has mean 1.99 and s ≈ 0.97. Give a 95% interval for the integral and say why the method is legitimate.

It's legitimate because \(\bar Y(n)\) is a mean of independent values with \(E(Y) = I\), so it's unbiased and its variance is \(\mathrm{Var}(Y)/n\) (Monte Carlo handout, Law and Kelton p.90-91). That makes it an ordinary sample mean, so the usual interval applies.

  1. Formula. \(\bar Y \pm z_{\alpha/2}\, s/\sqrt n\) (CI p.31), with \(\bar Y\) the mean of the draws, \(s\) their standard deviation, \(n\) the number of draws. Use \(z\) because \(n = 100 > 30\).
  2. Plug in. \(1.99 \pm 1.96 \times 0.97/\sqrt{100}\).
  3. Work it out. \(\sqrt{100} = 10\), standard error \(= 0.97/10 = 0.097\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.097 \approx 0.190\). Ends \(1.99 - 0.190 = 1.80\) and \(1.99 + 0.190 = 2.18\).
  4. Result. \((1.80,\ 2.18)\), which contains the true value 2. To halve the width you'd need \(100 \times 2^2 = 400\) draws.
16. You simulate 1000 samples of size 16 from a skewed population and 910 of the nominal 95% t intervals contain μ. Is the method's coverage really below 95%?

Yes. The coverage estimate is a proportion, so put the proportion interval on it (CI p.48) and see whether 0.95 is inside.

  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\), with \(p\) the fraction of intervals that hit \(\mu\) and \(n\) the number of simulated samples.
  2. Plug in. \(p = 910/1000\), \(n = 1000\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So \(0.91 \pm 1.96\sqrt{0.91 \times 0.09/1000}\).
  3. Work it out. \(p = 0.91\), \(1 - p = 0.09\). \(0.91 \times 0.09 = 0.0819\). \(0.0819/1000 = 0.0000819\). \(\sqrt{0.0000819} \approx 0.00905\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.00905 \approx 0.0177\). Ends \(0.91 - 0.0177 = 0.892\) and \(0.91 + 0.0177 = 0.928\).
  4. Result. \((0.892,\ 0.928)\). It excludes 0.95, so the method really under-covers, which matches the 91% figure on (p.67). A single sample, as in HW4 Problem 2c part 5, could never show this.
17. A paper bootstraps an interval on mean throughput from 200 iterations inside one JVM run. What's wrong and how would you fix it?

The iterations aren't independent, so the interval is far too narrow. They share JIT state, heap layout and CPU frequency history. Treating them as replicates is pseudo-replication, and the bootstrap assumes independent draws (CI p.69, Wild handout pitfall 7). Fix it by running many JVM invocations, reducing each to one number, and resampling invocations. Or delete warm-up and use batch means, the only response that handles dependence (p.70). This is study guide Q19.

18. A model answers 348 of 400 benchmark questions correctly. Give the 95% Wald interval for its accuracy and check the condition.
  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\dfrac{p(1-p)}{n}}\) (CI p.48), with \(p\) the fraction correct and \(n\) the number of questions. Condition \(np \ge 10\) and \(n(1-p) \ge 10\) (p.47).
  2. Plug in. \(p = 348/400\), \(n = 400\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So \(0.87 \pm 1.96\sqrt{0.87 \times 0.13/400}\).
  3. Work it out. \(p = 348/400 = 0.87\), \(1 - p = 0.13\). Condition, \(np = 348\) and \(n(1-p) = 400 - 348 = 52\), both above 10. \(0.87 \times 0.13 = 0.1131\). \(0.1131/400 = 0.00028275\). \(\sqrt{0.00028275} \approx 0.0168\) (check \(0.0168^2 \approx 0.000282\)). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.0168 \approx 0.033\). Ends \(0.87 - 0.033 = 0.837\) and \(0.87 + 0.033 = 0.903\).
  4. Result. \((0.837,\ 0.903)\), or 83.7% to 90.3%. In words, the model's true accuracy on questions like these is very likely in that range.

This is study guide Q4. Its Q5, a second model at 89% on the same questions, needs an interval on the difference, which the comparing-alternatives section works through.

19. A new allocator is run against a baseline on 7 benchmarks. The differences (new minus baseline, in seconds) are −0.8, −1.2, +0.3, −0.5, −1.5, −0.2, −0.9. Is the improvement significant at 95%?

Yes. Both systems ran the same benchmarks, so the data are paired and you build one interval on the differences. It excludes 0.

  1. Formula. \(\bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\,\dfrac{s_d}{\sqrt n}\) (CI p.37, study guide formula sheet), with \(d_i\) the per-benchmark differences, \(\bar d\) their mean, \(s_d = \sqrt{\sum (d_i - \bar d)^2/(n-1)}\) their standard deviation, and \(n\) the number of pairs.
  2. Plug in. \(n = 7\), so df \(= 6\). The \(t\) table's df 6 row, 0.025 column gives \(t_{[0.025;\,6]} = 2.447\).
  3. Work it out. Sum \(= -0.8 - 1.2 + 0.3 - 0.5 - 1.5 - 0.2 - 0.9 = -4.8\), so \(\bar d = -4.8/7 \approx -0.686\). Deviations from \(-0.686\) are \(-0.114, -0.514, 0.986, 0.186, -0.814, 0.486, -0.214\). Squares are \(0.013, 0.264, 0.972, 0.035, 0.663, 0.236, 0.046\), summing to about 2.229. \(s_d^2 = 2.229/6 = 0.371\) and \(s_d = \sqrt{0.371} \approx 0.609\). Standard error \(= 0.609/\sqrt7 = 0.609/2.646 \approx 0.230\). Half-width \(= 2.447 \times 0.230 \approx 0.564\). Ends \(-0.686 - 0.564 = -1.250\) and \(-0.686 + 0.564 = -0.122\).
  4. Result. \((-1.25,\ -0.12)\) s. It excludes 0, so the improvement is significant, but it could be anywhere from 0.12 to 1.25 s (study guide Q8).

A faster way to get the 2.229 by hand is \(\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2\). Here \(\sum d_i^2 = 0.64 + 1.44 + 0.09 + 0.25 + 2.25 + 0.04 + 0.81 = 5.52\) and \(n \bar d^2 = (-4.8)^2/7 = 23.04/7 = 3.291\), so \(5.52 - 3.291 = 2.229\).

20. Sixteen latencies in measurement order (ms) are 11.2, 13.8, 9.6, 45.1 | 12.4, 10.9, 18.7, 11.8 | 14.6, 10.2, 62.3, 13.1 | 9.8, 16.4, 12.9, 21.5, with \(\bar x = 18.394\) and \(s = 14.501\). Give the 95% t interval and the 95% batch-means interval for the four marked batches, and explain the difference in width.

The plain \(t\) interval.

  1. Formula. \(\bar x \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\, s/\sqrt n\) (CI p.37).
  2. Plug in. \(\bar x = 18.394\), \(s = 14.501\), \(n = 16\), and the df 15 row, 0.025 column gives 2.131. So \(18.394 \pm 2.131 \times 14.501/\sqrt{16}\).
  3. Work it out. Standard error \(= 14.501/4 = 3.625\). Half-width \(= 2.131 \times 3.625 \approx 7.73\). Ends \(18.394 - 7.73 = 10.67\) and \(18.394 + 7.73 = 26.12\).
  4. Result. \((10.67,\ 26.12)\) ms.

The batch-means interval.

  1. Formula. \(\bar b \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,m-1]}\, s_b/\sqrt m\) (p.68), with \(m\) batches, batch means \(b_j\), their mean \(\bar b\) and their standard deviation \(s_b\).
  2. Plug in. \(m = 4\), so df \(= 3\), and the df 3 row, 0.025 column gives 3.182.
  3. Work it out. Batch sums are \(11.2 + 13.8 + 9.6 + 45.1 = 79.7\), \(12.4 + 10.9 + 18.7 + 11.8 = 53.8\), \(14.6 + 10.2 + 62.3 + 13.1 = 100.2\) and \(9.8 + 16.4 + 12.9 + 21.5 = 60.6\). Batch means are \(79.7/4 = 19.925\), \(53.8/4 = 13.450\), \(100.2/4 = 25.050\) and \(60.6/4 = 15.150\). Their average is \(73.575/4 = 18.394\), the grand mean. Deviations from 18.394 are \(1.531, -4.944, 6.656, -3.244\). Squares are \(2.344, 24.441, 44.306, 10.522\), summing to about 81.61. \(s_b^2 = 81.61/3 = 27.20\) and \(s_b = \sqrt{27.20} \approx 5.216\). Standard error \(= 5.216/\sqrt4 = 5.216/2 = 2.608\). Half-width \(= 3.182 \times 2.608 \approx 8.30\). Ends \(18.394 - 8.30 = 10.10\) and \(18.394 + 8.30 = 26.69\).
  4. Result. \((10.10,\ 26.69)\) ms.

The batched interval is only \(8.30/7.73 \approx 1.07\), about 7%, wider here even though the multiplier grew by \(3.182/2.131 \approx 1.49\). That's because this sample's batch standard error happened to come out smaller (2.608 against 3.625). That's luck. Batching keeps the standard error the same on average and costs degrees of freedom, so expect close to 50% wider in general (CI p.68, study guide Q9).

Hypothesis testing

Slides: HT (Lecture 9, Hypothesis Testing), with the hypothesis-testing study guide, the "Hypothesis Testing in the Wild" handout and the chi-square goodness-of-fit handout. Homework: HW5 (Problems 1 and 2, and Problem 3 on Spang et al., IMC 2021).

The two hypotheses and which side you test

The idea

A hypothesis test is an argument by contradiction. You assume a specific claim about the population, the null hypothesis \(H_0\), and then ask whether the data you got would be surprising if that claim were true. If they would be, you drop \(H_0\) and accept the alternative \(H_1\). If they wouldn't, you keep \(H_0\) for now, but you haven't shown it's true (HT p.4). So you're never asking "is this true?", only "is my data consistent with it?" (p.3).

That asymmetry decides how you set things up. A test can give you evidence for \(H_1\) by rejecting \(H_0\). It can never give you evidence for \(H_0\). So whatever you're trying to establish goes in \(H_1\), and its negation becomes \(H_0\), the thing you're hoping to knock down (p.4). If you put your claim in \(H_0\), a small noisy experiment would "support" it just by failing to reject, which is support through low power and not through evidence.

The second choice is the direction. If any difference counts, the test is two-sided. If only one direction supports the claim, it's one-sided. The research question decides this, and it has to be decided before you see the data (p.5). The slide's two examples are "Is A faster than B?", which is one-sided, and "Does the patch change latency?", which is two-sided.

What to know

PieceWhat it is
\(H_0\)Always contains the equality, \(\mu = \mu_0\). It's held until the data force you off it (p.4).
\(H_1\)\(\mu \ne \mu_0\) (two-sided), or \(\mu > \mu_0\) or \(\mu < \mu_0\) (one-sided) (p.4-5).
Two-sided ruleReject if \(|Z| > z[\alpha/2]\). The \(\alpha\) is split between two tails (p.5, p.9).
One-sided ruleReject if \(Z > z[\alpha]\) for \(H_1: \mu > \mu_0\), or \(Z < -z[\alpha]\) for \(H_1: \mu < \mu_0\). All of \(\alpha\) sits in one tail (p.5, p.9).

At \(\alpha = 0.05\) the two-sided cutoff is \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) and the one-sided cutoff is \(z[0.05] = 1.645\) (p.9). The one-sided test has the smaller cutoff, so it has more power in the direction you named and no power at all in the other one.

The trap is switching to one-sided after seeing which way the data went. You'd have picked the other tail if the data had gone the other way, so the real false-positive rate of that procedure is 0.10, not 0.05 (p.5).

Worked example

Setting up the hypotheses (HW5 Problem 2.1). A group suspects the mean time to the first sensor fault is less than 740 hours and wants to confirm it. The claim is "\(\mu < 740\)", so it goes in \(H_1\).

\[H_0: \mu = 740 \text{ (or } \mu \ge 740\text{)}, \qquad H_1: \mu < 740.\]

It's one-sided because only a mean below 740 supports the claim. A mean above 740 would just be "no support".

Same data, two directions. Take HW5 Problem 1, where \(n = 36\), \(\sigma = 0.8\), \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) and \(\bar{x} = 2.74\). The statistic is \(Z = 1.80\) (worked out step by step in the next topic).

Formula. Two-sided, reject when \(|Z| > z[\alpha/2]\). One-sided upper, reject when \(Z > z[\alpha]\). Here \(Z\) is your test statistic, \(\alpha\) is the false-positive rate you picked, and \(z[a]\) is the point on the standard normal with upper-tail area \(a\).

Plug in. \(\alpha = 0.05\), so two-sided needs \(z[0.025]\) and one-sided needs \(z[0.05]\).

Table lookup. In the normal table find the z whose upper-tail area is 0.025 (cumulative 0.975). That's row 1.9, column 0.06, so \(z[0.025] = 1.96\). For upper-tail area 0.05 (cumulative 0.95) the value sits between 1.64 and 1.65, so \(z[0.05] = 1.645\).

  • Two-sided, \(H_1: \mu \ne 2.5\). Compare \(|1.80| = 1.80\) with 1.96. Since \(1.80 < 1.96\), you fail to reject.
  • One-sided, \(H_1: \mu > 2.5\). Compare 1.80 with 1.645. Since \(1.80 > 1.645\), you reject.

Result. The same Z gives opposite verdicts, because the one-sided test puts all 5% in one tail and so has a lower cutoff.

The one-sided verdict is only legitimate if the question was "is training slower than 2.5 hours?" from the start. HW5 Problem 1 states \(H_0: \mu = 2.5\) with no direction, so the two-sided test is the right one there.

Easy to get wrong

  • Putting the claim you want to prove in \(H_0\). The equality always goes in \(H_0\) and the claim goes in \(H_1\).
  • Writing "we accept \(H_0\)". Say "fail to reject", because not rejecting isn't evidence for \(H_0\) (HT p.4).
  • Using \(z[\alpha/2]\) for a one-sided test, or \(z[\alpha]\) for a two-sided one. One-sided at 0.05 is 1.645, two-sided at 0.05 is 1.96, two-sided at 0.01 is 2.576, one-sided at 0.01 is 2.326.
  • For \(H_1: \mu < \mu_0\) the rejection region is the lower tail, so you reject when \(Z\) is more negative than \(-z[\alpha]\).

The test statistic, Z and t, and the link to the confidence interval

The idea

Every test in this lecture has the same shape. You take the estimate minus the value \(H_0\) claims, and divide by the standard error. The result tells you how many standard errors the data sit from what \(H_0\) predicts (HT p.8). If that distance is past the critical value, the data are "unlikely" under \(H_0\) and you reject.

When \(\sigma\) is known (or \(n\) is large, roughly over 30), you use the normal table and get a Z test. When you only have the sample standard deviation \(s\) and \(n\) is small, the extra uncertainty in \(s\) makes the distribution wider, so you use the t table with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. One degree of freedom is spent estimating the mean before you estimate the spread, which is why it's \(n-1\). Past roughly \(n = 30\) the t and Z distributions are indistinguishable, so the choice only matters for small samples (p.11). The t test is the one you'll actually use in practice, and the Z test is the teaching device.

A two-sided test and a confidence interval are the same arithmetic read two ways. You reject \(H_0: \mu = \mu_0\) at level \(\alpha\) exactly when \(\mu_0\) falls outside the \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval (p.8-9). That's why the lecture calls a test "a confidence interval with everything but the verdict thrown away" (p.3).

What to know

\[Z = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \qquad\qquad t = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}, \ \ n-1 \text{ degrees of freedom}\]
SituationStatisticTwo-sided cutoff
\(\sigma\) known, or \(n\) large (use \(s\) for \(\sigma\))\(Z\)\(z[\alpha/2]\), 1.96 at \(\alpha = 0.05\) (p.8)
\(\sigma\) unknown, \(n\) small, population roughly normal\(t\)\(t[\alpha/2,\,n-1]\), e.g. 2.776 for \(n = 5\) (p.12)
Small \(n\), clearly not normalA rank-based test instead (p.11). The study guide names Mann-Whitney U (two independent samples) and Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired).

The recipe on p.15 fixes the order. State \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) and the direction, choose \(\alpha\), choose \(n\) from a power calculation, choose the test, then collect the data and compute the statistic once, and report the verdict, p, the effect size with its interval, and the planned \(n\). Steps 1 to 4 happen before any data exist, and that ordering is what makes \(\alpha\) mean anything.

The study guide adds the paired case. When both systems are measured on the same units (same benchmarks, same seeds), take the per-unit differences first and run a one-sample t test of their mean against 0, with \(t = \bar{d}/(s_d/\sqrt{n})\).

The study guide's formula sheet also has three tests that aren't on the slides but follow the same "estimate minus null, over standard error" shape.

TestStatisticWhen
Two proportions\(Z = \dfrac{\hat{p}_1 - \hat{p}_2}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})(1/n_1 + 1/n_2)}}\)Two independent groups with a yes/no outcome (A/B conversion). \(\hat{p}\) is the pooled proportion, total successes over total trials, because under \(H_0\) both groups share one rate.
McNemar\(Z = \dfrac{b - c}{\sqrt{b + c}}\)Two classifiers on the same test items. \(b\) is items only A got right, \(c\) is items only B got right, and you want \(b + c \gtrsim 25\). Items they agree on carry no information about which is better, so they drop out. It's the paired design for yes/no outcomes.
Welch (two independent means)\(t = \dfrac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2}{\sqrt{s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2}}\)Degrees of freedom from the Welch-Satterthwaite formula, so in practice software. This one is developed in the comparing-alternatives lecture.

Worked example

Z test, HW5 Problem 1. \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) hours, \(\sigma = 0.8\) known, \(n = 36\), \(\bar{x} = 2.74\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), two-sided.

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\). Here \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, \(\mu_0\) is the mean that \(H_0\) claims, \(\sigma\) is the known population standard deviation, \(n\) is the number of observations, and \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\) is the standard error of the mean.

Plug in. \[Z = \frac{2.74 - 2.5}{0.8/\sqrt{36}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{36} = 6\). Standard error \(= 0.8/6 = 0.1333\). Top \(= 2.74 - 2.5 = 0.24\). \(Z = 0.24/0.1333 = 1.80\). The cutoff is \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) (normal table, row 1.9, column 0.06). Since \(1.80 < 1.96\), you fail to reject \(H_0\).

Check with the interval. The formula is \(\bar{x} \pm z[\alpha/2]\,\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.1333 = 0.2613\). Lower end \(= 2.74 - 0.2613 = 2.479\). Upper end \(= 2.74 + 0.2613 = 3.001\). The interval \((2.479, 3.001)\) contains 2.5, so the verdict matches.

Result. \(Z = 1.80\), fail to reject. The sample mean is 1.8 standard errors above 2.5, which isn't far enough to rule out 2.5 at the 5% level.

Smallest mean that rejects (HW5 Problem 1.3). Now \(\alpha = 0.01\), two-sided.

Formula. You reject when \(\dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} > z[\alpha/2]\). Multiply both sides by the standard error and add \(\mu_0\), which gives \(\bar{x} > \mu_0 + z[\alpha/2]\,\sigma/\sqrt{n}\).

Table lookup. \(\alpha/2 = 0.005\). The z with upper-tail area 0.005 (cumulative 0.995) is between 2.57 and 2.58 in the table, so \(z[0.005] = 2.576\).

Plug in and work it out. \[\bar{x} > 2.5 + 2.576 \times 0.1333 = 2.5 + 0.3435 = 2.8435 \approx 2.84 \text{ hours.}\]

If the alternative were one-sided (\(\mu > 2.5\)), all of \(\alpha\) goes in one tail, so the cutoff is \(z[0.01] = 2.326\) (upper-tail area 0.01, cumulative 0.99, between 2.32 and 2.33). Then \(\bar{x} > 2.5 + 2.326 \times 0.1333 = 2.5 + 0.3101 = 2.810\) hours.

Result. Two-sided at 1%, any sample mean above about 2.84 hours rejects. HW5 kept the two-sided version because \(H_0\) was stated without a direction.

Large-n test with s, HW5 Problem 2.2. \(n = 50\), \(\bar{x} = 706\), \(s = 96.1\), \(H_1: \mu < 740\), \(\alpha = 0.01\). Since \(n > 30\), use Z with \(s\) in place of \(\sigma\) (p.11).

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}\), where \(s\) is the sample standard deviation standing in for \(\sigma\).

Plug in. \[Z = \frac{706 - 740}{96.1/\sqrt{50}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{50} = 7.071\). Standard error \(= 96.1/7.071 = 13.59\). Top \(= 706 - 740 = -34\). \(Z = -34/13.59 = -2.50\). For \(H_1: \mu < 740\) the rejection region is the lower tail, so the cutoff is \(-z[0.01] = -2.326\). Since \(-2.50 < -2.326\), reject \(H_0\).

Result. Reject. The data support a mean time to first fault below 740 hours. (With the t table at 49 degrees of freedom the cutoff is about 2.405, and you still reject.)

t test where Z would mislead. Nine runs of a benchmark give \(\bar{x} = 10.6\) s and \(s = 0.9\) s. Is the mean different from 10 s at \(\alpha = 0.05\)?

Formula. \(t = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}\) with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom. You use t because \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small.

Plug in. \[t = \frac{10.6 - 10}{0.9/\sqrt{9}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{9} = 3\). Standard error \(= 0.9/3 = 0.3\). Top \(= 0.6\). \(t = 0.6/0.3 = 2.0\). Degrees of freedom \(= 9 - 1 = 8\). In the t table go to row df = 8 and the column for upper-tail area 0.025 (the 0.975 column in a cumulative table), which gives \(t[0.025,\,8] = 2.306\). Since \(2.0 < 2.306\), fail to reject.

Interval. \(\bar{x} \pm t[0.025,\,8]\,s/\sqrt{n} = 10.6 \pm 2.306 \times 0.3 = 10.6 \pm 0.692\). Lower \(= 10.6 - 0.692 = 9.908\), upper \(= 10.6 + 0.692 = 11.292\). It contains 10.

Result. Fail to reject. If you'd wrongly used 1.96 you would have rejected, because \(2.0 > 1.96\). With small \(n\) the t cutoff matters.

The slide's own t example (HT p.12). Five files, \(\bar{x} = 12.3\) KB, \(s = 0.5\) KB, sizes assumed normal, \(H_0: \mu = 12.35\), two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Formula. Same t formula, \(t = (\bar{x} - \mu_0)/(s/\sqrt{n})\), with \(n - 1 = 4\) degrees of freedom.

Plug in. \[t = \frac{12.3 - 12.35}{0.5/\sqrt{5}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{5} = 2.236\). Standard error \(= 0.5/2.236 = 0.2236\). Top \(= 12.3 - 12.35 = -0.05\). \(t = -0.05/0.2236 = -0.224\). The t table at row df = 4, upper-tail column 0.025, gives \(t[0.025,\,4] = 2.776\). Since \(|-0.224| = 0.224 < 2.776\), don't reject (the slide gives \(p = 0.83\)).

Interval. \(12.3 \pm 2.776 \times 0.2236 = 12.3 \pm 0.621\), so \((11.68, 12.92)\) KB.

Result. Fail to reject. The interval contains 12.35 and almost everything else nearby, so "no difference" here means "with five files we couldn't have seen one" (p.12).

Two proportions. An A/B test gives 800 users each variant. A converts 144 and B converts 176. Two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\hat{p}_1 - \hat{p}_2}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})(1/n_1 + 1/n_2)}}\). Here \(\hat{p}_1\) and \(\hat{p}_2\) are the two observed rates (successes over users in each group), \(n_1\) and \(n_2\) are the group sizes, and \(\hat{p}\) is the pooled rate, total successes over total users.

The pieces. \(\hat{p}_A = 144/800 = 0.18\). \(\hat{p}_B = 176/800 = 0.22\). Pooled \(\hat{p} = (144 + 176)/(800 + 800) = 320/1600 = 0.20\).

Plug in. \[Z = \frac{0.22 - 0.18}{\sqrt{0.20 \times 0.80 \times \left(\tfrac{1}{800} + \tfrac{1}{800}\right)}}\]

Work it out. \(0.20 \times 0.80 = 0.16\). \(1/800 + 1/800 = 2/800 = 0.0025\). \(0.16 \times 0.0025 = 0.0004\). \(\sqrt{0.0004} = 0.02\). Top \(= 0.04\). \(Z = 0.04/0.02 = 2.0\). Since \(2.0 > 1.96\), reject. For p, the normal table at row 2.0, column 0.00 gives cumulative 0.9772, so the upper tail is \(1 - 0.9772 = 0.0228\), and two-sided \(p = 2 \times 0.0228 = 0.0456\).

Result. Reject, \(p = 0.046\). B's 4-point higher conversion is unlikely to be noise at this sample size.

Compare study guide question 5, 520 vs 580 conversions out of 10,000 each. \(\hat{p}_1 = 0.052\), \(\hat{p}_2 = 0.058\), pooled \(\hat{p} = 1100/20000 = 0.055\). SE \(= \sqrt{0.055 \times 0.945 \times (2/10000)} = \sqrt{0.0519750 \times 0.0002} = \sqrt{0.0000103950} = 0.00322\). \(Z = 0.006/0.00322 = 1.86\). The table at row 1.8, column 0.06 gives cumulative 0.9686, so the tail is 0.0314 and \(p = 2 \times 0.0314 = 0.063\). It doesn't reject, because base rates near 5% need very large samples.

McNemar. Two classifiers on the same 500 items. A alone is right on 38, B alone on 20 (study guide question 6).

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{b - c}{\sqrt{b + c}}\), where \(b\) is the number of items only A got right and \(c\) is the number only B got right. Items they agree on are left out.

Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{38 - 20}{\sqrt{38 + 20}}\)

Work it out. Top \(= 18\). \(38 + 20 = 58\), and \(\sqrt{58} = 7.62\) (since \(7.6^2 = 57.76\)). \(Z = 18/7.62 = 2.36\). Since \(2.36 > 1.96\), reject. The normal table at row 2.3, column 0.06 gives cumulative 0.9909, so the tail is 0.0091 and \(p = 2 \times 0.0091 = 0.018\).

Result. Reject, \(p = 0.018\). A really is the more accurate classifier. A two-proportion test would be the wrong tool, since both classifiers answer the same items and their correctness is correlated (easy items are easy for both).

Easy to get wrong

  • Dividing by \(\sigma\) instead of \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). The test is about the mean, so you need the standard error of the mean.
  • Using the normal cutoff with small \(n\) and an estimated \(s\). Use \(t[\alpha/2,\,n-1]\), and remember the degrees of freedom are \(n-1\), not \(n\) (HT p.11).
  • Running an unpaired test on paired data. The between-unit spread swamps the effect and you throw away most of the power (study guide, question 4).
  • Forgetting that a rank-based test answers a somewhat different null hypothesis, not the same one more robustly (study guide).
  • Using the separate rates \(\hat{p}_1\) and \(\hat{p}_2\) in the two-proportion standard error. Under \(H_0\) the rates are equal, so you use the pooled \(\hat{p}\).
  • Putting the agreements into McNemar. Only the disagreements \(b\) and \(c\) enter the statistic.

The p-value, and what it isn't

The idea

The critical-value approach gives you one bit, reject or not. The p-value gives the same decision on a finer scale. It's the probability, computed assuming \(H_0\) is true, of getting a test statistic at least as extreme as the one you got (HT p.13). You reject when \(p \le \alpha\), which always matches comparing \(Z\) with the cutoff.

Reporting the actual value is better than writing "\(p < 0.05\)", because 0.049 and 0.0001 aren't the same evidence (p.13). But the p-value is a statement about the data given a hypothesis. It's never a statement about the hypothesis given the data, and most misuses come from flipping that around.

What to know

\[p = \Pr[\text{a statistic at least this extreme} \mid H_0 \text{ true}]\]

Two-sided, \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\). One-sided, \(p\) is the single tail in the direction of \(H_1\) (study guide, formula sheet). For a t statistic you usually can't get an exact p from a printed table, so you bracket it (for example "\(0.01 < p < 0.05\)") using the columns of the t table.

What a p-value is not (p.14).

MisreadingWhy it's wrong
The probability that \(H_0\) is trueThat would need a prior, and a test has none. The p-value is already conditioned on \(H_0\).
The probability your result was a flukeSame mistake in different words.
A measure of sizep depends on \(n\) at least as much as on the difference.
A sharp boundary0.06 and 0.04 are nearly the same evidence.
Support for \(H_0\) when largeWith \(n = 5\) you might not have been able to see anything. Absence of evidence isn't evidence of absence.
A claim about the world"Significant" is a word about the test.

One more from the study guide's true/false list. At \(\alpha = 0.05\) it's not true that 5% of significant published results are false positives. \(\alpha\) is the rate among true nulls, and the share of false claims among significant results also depends on power and on how many tested effects are real.

Worked example

Two-sided p, HW5 Problem 1.2. \(Z = 1.80\).

Formula. \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\), where \(z\) is the statistic you computed and \(\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\) is the upper-tail area of the standard normal past it. You double because a two-sided test counts both tails.

Table lookup. Normal table, row 1.8, column 0.00, gives cumulative \(\Phi(1.80) = 0.9641\). The upper tail is \(1 - 0.9641 = 0.0359\).

Plug in and work it out. \[p = 2 \times 0.0359 = 0.0718.\]

Result. Since \(0.0718 > 0.05\), fail to reject, the same verdict as part 1. In words, if the true mean really were 2.5 hours, a sample mean at least 0.24 hours away from 2.5 (either side) would turn up about 7% of the time.

One-sided p, HW5 Problem 2.2. \(Z = -2.50\) with \(H_1: \mu < 740\).

Formula. For a lower-tail alternative, \(p = \Pr[Z \le z]\), only the one tail in the direction of \(H_1\), no doubling.

Table lookup. By symmetry \(\Pr[Z \le -2.50] = \Pr[Z \ge 2.50]\). Row 2.5, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(2.50) = 0.9938\), so the tail is \(1 - 0.9938 = 0.0062\).

Result. \(p = 0.0062\). That's below \(\alpha = 0.01\), so reject, matching the critical-value test.

The slide example (HT p.10). \(n = 50\) files, \(\bar{x} = 12.3\) KB, \(\sigma = 0.5\), \(H_0: \mu = 12.5\), two-sided.

Formula. \(Z = (\bar{x} - \mu_0)/(\sigma/\sqrt{n})\), then \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\).

Plug in. \[Z = \frac{12.3 - 12.5}{0.5/\sqrt{50}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{50} = 7.071\). Standard error \(= 0.5/7.071 = 0.0707\). Top \(= -0.2\). \(Z = -0.2/0.0707 = -2.83\). Table row 2.8, column 0.03, gives \(\Phi(2.83) = 0.9977\), so the tail is \(1 - 0.9977 = 0.0023\). \(p = 2 \times 0.0023 = 0.0046 \approx 0.005\). Reject.

Interval. \(12.3 \pm 1.96 \times 0.0707 = 12.3 \pm 0.139\), so \((12.16, 12.44)\). It excludes 12.5.

Result. Reject, \(p \approx 0.005\). The difference is 0.2 KB, and whether that matters is a separate question the test doesn't ask.

Easy to get wrong

  • Forgetting to double the tail for a two-sided test, or doubling it for a one-sided test.
  • Writing "p = 0.03, so there's a 3% chance \(H_0\) is true". The correct sentence is "if \(H_0\) were true, data this extreme would occur about 3% of the time" (HT p.13-14).
  • Reading a small p as a big effect. The slide 17 table makes a 0.3 ms effect "highly significant" just by raising \(n\).
  • Reporting only "\(p < 0.05\)". Give the value, plus the effect size and interval (p.13, p.15).

Type I and Type II errors, power, and how many runs

The idea

A test can be wrong in two ways. It can reject a true \(H_0\) (a false positive, Type I) or fail to reject a false one (a missed effect, Type II). You pick the Type I rate directly, since it's \(\alpha\). You don't pick the Type II rate \(\beta\). It follows from \(\alpha\), from \(n\), from the noise \(\sigma\), and from how big the real effect is (HT p.6).

At a fixed \(n\), lowering \(\alpha\) raises \(\beta\), because a stricter cutoff means you also miss more real effects. The only way to lower both is more data (p.6). That's why choosing \(n\) is part of the design, done before you collect anything (p.15, p.19). A test that almost never gives false positives because it almost never rejects anything is useless, not careful (study guide).

What to know

The four outcomes, with your decision down the side and reality across the top (p.7).

\(H_0\) true\(H_0\) false
Do not reject \(H_0\)Correct, confidence \(= 1-\alpha\)Type II error, \(\Pr = \beta\)
Reject \(H_0\)Type I error, \(\Pr = \alpha\)Correct, power \(= 1-\beta\)

You never find out which column you're in. You control the left column with \(\alpha\), and the right column has to be bought with sample size (p.7). A published result is one cell of this table reported without the table. And \(\alpha = 0.05\) is a convention, not a law of nature, it's just the false-positive rate you agreed to (p.6).

Why power 0.80? It's Cohen's convention. Pairing \(\beta = 0.20\) with \(\alpha = 0.05\) treats a missed effect as a quarter as serious as a false alarm, since \(0.05/0.20 = 1/4\) (p.19).

Same table, other fields. The handout reads a membership inference attack as a test of \(H_0\) "this example wasn't in the training set". The false-positive rate is \(\alpha\) and the true-positive rate is power. Carlini et al. argue you should fix \(\alpha\) very small first and then ask about power (the Neyman-Pearson ordering), instead of quoting accuracy or AUC, which average over all thresholds (Hypothesis Testing in the Wild, entry 9, and study guide question 19).

Power for a two-sided one-sample Z test (study guide formula sheet), with \(\delta\) the true \(\mu - \mu_0\).

\[1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > z[\alpha/2] - \frac{|\delta|\sqrt{n}}{\sigma}\right]\]

Sample size for a target power (p.19).

\[n = \left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\,\sigma}{\delta}\right)^2\]
InputWhere it comes from
\(\delta\)The smallest difference you'd care about. A judgement, not a statistic.
\(\sigma\)A pilot run or published measurements.
Usual targetPower 0.80 at \(\alpha = 0.05\), so \((1.96 + 0.84)^2 \approx 7.8\) and \(n \approx 7.8(\sigma/\delta)^2\).

Halving \(\delta\) multiplies \(n\) by four. Power 0.90 needs about 34% more runs than 0.80, and 0.95 needs about 66% more (p.19). For two independent groups the study guide doubles it, \(n = 2((z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\sigma/\delta)^2\) per group, and for a one-sided test you use \(z[\alpha]\) in place of \(z[\alpha/2]\). Round \(n\) up.

Power computed after the fact from your own p-value tells you nothing, and an underpowered study doesn't only miss effects, the ones it finds come out exaggerated (p.19).

Worked example

Power. \(H_0: \mu = 50\), two-sided, \(\sigma = 10\), \(n = 25\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). The true mean is 54. (Study guide question 7.)

Formula. \(1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > z[\alpha/2] - \dfrac{|\delta|\sqrt{n}}{\sigma}\right]\). Here \(1 - \beta\) is the power (the chance you reject when the effect is real), \(\delta\) is the true \(\mu - \mu_0\), \(\sigma\) is the standard deviation, and \(n\) is the sample size. The fraction \(|\delta|\sqrt{n}/\sigma = |\delta|/(\sigma/\sqrt{n})\) is the effect measured in standard errors.

Plug in. \(\delta = 54 - 50 = 4\), \(\sigma = 10\), \(n = 25\), \(z[0.025] = 1.96\). \[1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > 1.96 - \frac{4 \times \sqrt{25}}{10}\right]\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{25} = 5\). \(4 \times 5 = 20\). \(20/10 = 2\), so the effect is 2 standard errors (same as \(4/(10/5) = 4/2 = 2\)). \(1.96 - 2 = -0.04\). \(\Pr[Z > -0.04] = \Pr[Z < 0.04]\) by symmetry, and the table at row 0.0, column 0.04 gives 0.5160. So power \(\approx 0.516\) and \(\beta = 1 - 0.516 = 0.484\). (The lower rejection tail, \(\Pr[Z < -1.96 - 2]\), is negligible.)

Result. Power is about 0.52. This experiment misses a real 4-unit effect about half the time, so a "no significant difference" from it says very little.

How many runs for power 0.80? Same \(\sigma = 10\) and \(\delta = 4\).

Formula. \(n = \left(\dfrac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\,\sigma}{\delta}\right)^2\). \(z[\alpha/2]\) is the two-sided cutoff and \(z[\beta]\) is the normal value with upper-tail area \(\beta\), the miss rate you'll accept.

Table lookup. Power 0.80 means \(\beta = 0.20\). The z with upper-tail area 0.20 (cumulative 0.80) is at row 0.8, column 0.04, so \(z[0.20] = 0.84\). And \(z[0.025] = 1.96\).

Plug in. \[n = \left(\frac{(1.96 + 0.84) \times 10}{4}\right)^2\]

Work it out. \(1.96 + 0.84 = 2.8\). \(10/4 = 2.5\). \(2.8 \times 2.5 = 7\). \(7^2 = 49\).

Check it. With \(n = 49\), the standard error is \(10/\sqrt{49} = 10/7 = 1.429\), and \(\delta\) is \(4/1.429 = 2.8\) standard errors. Power \(= \Pr[Z > 1.96 - 2.8] = \Pr[Z > -0.84] = 0.80\). (With the more precise \(z[0.20] = 0.842\) you get \(49.07\), which rounds up to 50.)

Result. 49 runs. To detect \(\delta = 2\) instead, \(\delta\) is halved, so \(n\) goes up by \(2^2 = 4\), giving \(4 \times 49 = 196\).

Paired versus two groups (study guide question 9). A pilot gives \(\sigma \approx 12\) minutes for job completion time. You want to detect a 5-minute reduction, two-sided, \(\alpha = 0.05\), power 0.80, with \(z[0.20] = 0.842\).

Formula. Same \(n = \left((z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\sigma/\delta\right)^2\) for the paired design. For two independent groups it's \(2\left((z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\sigma/\delta\right)^2\) per group.

Plug in. \[n = \left(\frac{(1.960 + 0.842) \times 12}{5}\right)^2\]

Work it out. \(1.960 + 0.842 = 2.802\). \(12/5 = 2.4\). \(2.802 \times 2.4 = 6.725\). \(6.725^2 = 45.2\). Round up to 46 jobs, each run under both schedulers. For two independent groups, \(2 \times 45.2 = 90.4\), round up to 91 jobs per group, 182 in total.

Result. 46 paired jobs or 182 unpaired. In practice the paired \(\sigma\) (of per-job differences) is far smaller than 12, because job-to-job variation cancels, so pairing needs even fewer runs than 46.

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking \(\beta = 1 - \alpha\). They're probabilities under different realities, and \(\beta\) depends on \(n\), \(\sigma\) and the true effect (HT p.6).
  • Swapping the labels. Type I is the false positive (rejecting a true null), Type II is the miss.
  • Using \(z[\beta] = 1.96\). For power 0.80, \(\beta = 0.20\) and \(z[0.20] = 0.84\).
  • Rounding \(n\) down. Always round up, or you fall short of the target power.
  • Calling a method "safe" because its false-positive rate is low. The handout's entry 1 (Bouthillier et al.) found that comparing average scores kept false positives under 5% but missed about 90% of real improvements. Low \(\alpha\) bought with no power is the useless test the study guide warns about.
  • "We ran it five times" isn't a sample size, it's the absence of one (p.19).

Effect size versus significance, and why intervals beat tests

The idea

The statistic is effect divided by \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\), so \(n\) is half the formula. Hold the effect fixed and raise \(n\), and Z grows like \(\sqrt{n}\), so quadrupling the data doubles Z for the same effect (study guide formula sheet). With enough data every non-zero effect becomes significant, and with too little data an important one won't be (HT p.17). Significance is partly a statement about \(n\), so it can't tell you whether a difference matters.

Jain's argument (p.16) is that a test gives you one bit, while an interval gives you the same bit plus the size of the effect and how precisely you know it. Intervals of \((-100, 100)\) and \((-1, 1)\) both contain 0, so both say "not significant". The first means you have no idea. The second means any effect is tiny. Only the interval tells those apart, so report the interval.

What to know

The p.17 table, effect 0.3 ms and \(\sigma = 50\) ms in every row.

\(n\)\(Z\)pVerdict
1,0000.190.85not significant
100,0001.900.058not significant
1,000,0006.00\(2 \times 10^{-9}\)"highly significant"

Ways to state how big an effect is (p.18).

MeasureFormula or formUsed in
Raw differenceOriginal units, e.g. 0.3 msSystems and networking
Relative changePercent or speedup ratioArchitecture, compilers (geometric mean over benchmarks)
Cohen's d\((\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2)/s\), with \(s\) pooled. Paired is \(\bar{d}/s_d\) (study guide)HCI, CS education, meta-analyses
Probability of superiority \(A_{12}\)\(\Pr[X_A > X_B] + \tfrac12\Pr[X_A = X_B]\), 0.5 means no difference (the tie term is from the study guide formula sheet)Fuzzing, test generation (ROC AUC is the same quantity)

Cohen's small, medium and large labels (0.2, 0.5, 0.8) come from behavioural science. With low-noise measurements \(d = 5\) can still be a negligible speedup, so don't lean on the labels (p.18). Before the experiment, decide the smallest effect that matters, since it's the \(\delta\) that sets \(n\). After it, report the effect with an interval.

Worked example

Same effect, bigger n. The effect is 0.3 ms and \(\sigma = 50\) ms (p.17).

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\text{effect}}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\), the effect divided by the standard error.

Plug in and work it out, \(n = 1{,}000{,}000\). \(\sqrt{1{,}000{,}000} = 1000\). Standard error \(= 50/1000 = 0.05\). \(Z = 0.3/0.05 = 6.0\).

Plug in and work it out, \(n = 1000\). \(\sqrt{1000} \approx 31.6\). Standard error \(= 50/31.6 = 1.58\). \(Z = 0.3/1.58 = 0.19\).

Result. The effect never changed, only \(n\) did. A 0.3 ms drop on a 250 ms page load is \(0.3/250 = 0.0012 = 0.12\%\), which nobody notices (p.17).

Significant but small. A scheduler is replayed against a baseline on 16 paired job traces. Mean completion time drops from 40.0 to 39.2 minutes. The 16 per-trace differences have \(\bar{d} = 0.8\) and \(s_d = 0.4\).

Formula. Paired t, \(t = \dfrac{\bar{d}}{s_d/\sqrt{n}}\) with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom. \(\bar{d}\) is the mean of the per-trace differences, \(s_d\) is their standard deviation, \(n\) is the number of pairs.

Plug in. \[t = \frac{0.8}{0.4/\sqrt{16}}\]

Work it out. \(\sqrt{16} = 4\). SE \(= 0.4/4 = 0.1\). \(t = 0.8/0.1 = 8.0\). Degrees of freedom \(= 16 - 1 = 15\). t table, row df = 15, upper-tail column 0.025, gives \(t[0.025,\,15] = 2.131\). Since \(8.0 > 2.131\), reject easily.

Now the size. Raw difference \(= 40.0 - 39.2 = 0.8\) minutes. Relative change \(= 0.8/40 = 0.02 = 2\%\). Cohen's \(d = \bar{d}/s_d = 0.8/0.4 = 2.0\), "large" by the labels, but only because replaying a trace is low-noise. The 95% interval is \(\bar{d} \pm t[0.025,\,15] \times \text{SE} = 0.8 \pm 2.131 \times 0.1 = 0.8 \pm 0.213\), so \((0.587, 1.013)\) minutes.

Result. Significant, but small. The sentence to write is "reduced mean completion time by 0.8 min (2%), 95% CI 0.59 to 1.01 min", not "a large effect".

Study guide question 21 is the same story with 12 replays, 42.0 to 41.2 minutes and \(s_d = 0.5\). SE \(= 0.5/\sqrt{12} = 0.5/3.464 = 0.144\). \(t = 0.8/0.144 = 5.54\). The t table at row df = 11, upper-tail column 0.025, gives 2.201, and \(5.54 > 2.201\), so you reject. The relative change is \(0.8/42.0 = 0.019 = 1.9\%\) and \(d = 0.8/0.5 = 1.6\), "large" only because replays are low-noise. What the authors should have fixed beforehand is the smallest improvement worth reporting, say 5%, since that \(\delta\) drives the power analysis and shows the real effect is well below it.

Computing \(A_{12}\). Fuzzer A covers 3, 5, 7 (thousand branches) in three runs and B covers 2, 4, 6.

Formula. \(A_{12} = \Pr[X_A > X_B] + \tfrac12\Pr[X_A = X_B]\). By hand, that's (A wins \(+\ \tfrac12\) ties) divided by the number of A-versus-B pairs.

Work it out. There are \(3 \times 3 = 9\) pairs. A's 3 beats only B's 2, so 1 win. A's 5 beats 2 and 4, so 2 wins. A's 7 beats 2, 4 and 6, so 3 wins. Total wins \(= 1 + 2 + 3 = 6\), ties \(= 0\). \(A_{12} = (6 + 0)/9 = 0.67\).

Result. \(A_{12} = 0.67\). A run of A beats a run of B about two times in three.

Easy to get wrong

  • Writing "highly significant" as if it meant "large". It means the test is confident the effect isn't zero (HT p.17).
  • Writing "not significant, so the two are equivalent". Look at the interval width first (p.16). Showing equivalence needs a narrow interval around zero or an equivalence test (study guide question 15c).
  • Quoting Cohen's labels for systems measurements. Give the raw and relative change too.
  • A table whose only number is p isn't reporting a result (p.14).

Independence, the experimental unit, and interference

The idea

The standard error \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\) assumes \(n\) independent observations (HT p.20). Real measurements often aren't independent. Consecutive latency samples share caches, queues and background load. Requests from one user share that user. Runs on one boot share that boot. If you treat 10,000 requests from one run of each system as \(n = 10{,}000\), the standard error comes out far too small and the test rejects far too often.

The fix is to count the right thing. The experimental unit is what's independently replicated (a run, a machine, a benchmark, a user), and \(n\) counts units, not measurements (p.20). Ten thousand requests from one run is one replicate per system.

Interference is worse than correlation. If treated units change the outcomes of control units, the test measures the wrong quantity, and more data narrows the interval around that wrong quantity (p.22). This is Spang et al., the paper from HW5 Problem 3.

What to know

Lag-1 autocorrelation is \(\rho = \text{Corr}(x_t, x_{t+1}) = \text{Cov}(x_t, x_{t+1})/\sigma^2\), and it lies between \(-1\) and \(+1\). At \(\rho = 0\) each run is unrelated to the one before it. With it (p.21),

\[\text{Var}(\bar{x}) \approx \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\cdot\frac{1+\rho}{1-\rho}, \qquad n_{\text{eff}} \approx n\,\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}.\]

For \(\rho > 0\) (drift, warm-up, caches) \(n_{\text{eff}} < n\), and the naive Z is too big by \(\sqrt{(1+\rho)/(1-\rho)}\). At \(\rho = 0.5\), \(n_{\text{eff}} = n/3\) and Z is too big by \(\sqrt{3}\). The actual Type I rate at a nominal 0.05 is about 11% at \(\rho = 0.2\), 26% at 0.5, 51% at 0.8 and 65% at 0.9 (p.21). For \(\rho < 0\) (high and low alternating, like garbage collection every other run) \(n_{\text{eff}} > n\).

Designing for independence (p.22).

MoveWhat you do
Analyse at the unitOne summary per run (mean, p99), then test across runs. For one long correlated series the study guide adds batch means, which average consecutive blocks and treat the block means as the observations
Replicate what variesSeparate runs, reboots, machines, days
RandomiseInterleave the order the alternatives are measured in
Block or pairSame benchmark, machine and time for both
CheckPlot in time order, look at the lag-1 correlation
InterferenceRandomise whole clusters, not individual units, or switch all units between treatments over time

Spang et al. (IMC 2021) on Netflix bitrate capping. Tested on a small fraction of traffic, capping looked like about 5% lower throughput. When most traffic was capped it gave about 12% higher throughput (p.22). The naive A/B test wasn't miscomputed. It measured the effect of treating 5% of traffic on a shared congested link, which isn't the effect of capping everyone. The handout calls this "a valid test of the wrong quantity". The assumption that broke has a name, SUTVA (Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption), meaning one unit's outcome doesn't depend on which treatment other units got. Their fix was paired links with different treatment allocations (one link mostly treated, a similar link mostly control, then compare links). A switchback design is the other fix, where a whole link or region flips between treatment and control over time so each period is all one or all the other (Hypothesis Testing in the Wild, entry 8, and study guide question 14).

The same unit question shows up in ML benchmarks. If you vary only the weight-initialization seed, your runs miss the variance from data splits, data order, augmentation and hyperparameter search. The standard error comes out too small and the Type I rate goes above \(\alpha\), so vary everything that would differ if someone else reran the pipeline (study guide question 13, handout entry 1).

Worked example

Two server configurations, 400 consecutive latencies from one run of each. The mean difference is 1.5 ms, and the usual formula gives a standard error of 0.5 ms (study guide question 22).

Naive. \(Z = \text{difference}/\text{SE} = 1.5/0.5 = 3.0\). Table row 3.0, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(3.00) = 0.99865\), so the tail is 0.00135 and \(p = 2 \times 0.00135 = 0.0027\). Looks strong.

Corrected for \(\rho = 0.6\).

Formula. \(n_{\text{eff}} = n\,\dfrac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}\) and \(\text{Var}(\bar{x}) \approx \dfrac{\sigma^2}{n}\cdot\dfrac{1+\rho}{1-\rho}\). Here \(n\) is the number of measurements, \(\rho\) is the lag-1 autocorrelation (how much each value tracks the one before it), and \(n_{\text{eff}}\) is how many independent values the series is really worth. The variance gets multiplied by \((1+\rho)/(1-\rho)\), so the standard error gets multiplied by its square root.

Plug in. \[n_{\text{eff}} = 400 \times \frac{1 - 0.6}{1 + 0.6}\]

Work it out. \(1 - 0.6 = 0.4\). \(1 + 0.6 = 1.6\). \(0.4/1.6 = 0.25\). \(400 \times 0.25 = 100\). The variance factor is \(1.6/0.4 = 4\), so the standard error is multiplied by \(\sqrt{4} = 2\), giving \(0.5 \times 2 = 1.0\) ms. Then \(Z = 1.5/1.0 = 1.5\). Table row 1.5, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(1.50) = 0.9332\), so the tail is 0.0668 and \(p = 2 \times 0.0668 = 0.134\).

Result. \(p = 0.134\), not significant. The 400 correlated values carry only as much information as 100 independent ones.

Even the corrected test only compares these two runs. The run is the unit, and there's one of each, so machine, boot and background load are tangled with the configuration. The real fix is many interleaved runs per configuration with one summary per run.

HW5 Problem 3 reading. The HW5 answer counted hourly cells as \(n\), not the roughly fourteen million sessions, because sessions in the same hour share the same congestion. That's the p.20 rule applied.

Easy to get wrong

  • Treating more samples from the same run as more evidence. They sharpen a wrong answer (HT p.21).
  • Thinking correlation only widens the interval. It invalidates it, because the Type I rate is no longer \(\alpha\) (p.21).
  • Believing a fancier test fixes interference. No choice of test repairs observations that influence each other (study guide).
  • Plugging \(\rho\) in upside down. Positive \(\rho\) shrinks \(n_{\text{eff}}\), and \((1-\rho)/(1+\rho)\) is below 1 when \(\rho > 0\).

Peeking and multiple comparisons

The idea

\(\alpha\) is the false-positive rate of one test, run once, at an \(n\) fixed in advance. Two common habits break that. The first is peeking, which means computing p after every batch and stopping as soon as it drops below \(\alpha\) (HT p.23). Every look is a fresh chance for noise to cross the line, so the procedure's Type I rate climbs even though each p is computed correctly.

The second is running many tests. A real evaluation compares several systems on several workloads and metrics. Each test has a 5% chance of a false positive, so a big table almost surely has some (p.26). These are the same failure, many chances to cross the same line (p.32). Both are fixed by deciding the stopping rule, the family of tests and the correction before you see any data.

What to know

Peeking costs (simulated A/A test, p.24). At a nominal 0.05 the actual Type I rate is 5% with 1 look, about 8% with 2, 14% with 5, 19% with 10, 25% with 20, 32% with 50 and 37% with 100. The study guide puts 14 daily looks at about 22%. Each p-value at the stopping point is computed correctly. It's the whole procedure whose Type I rate is no longer \(\alpha\) (p.23). Keep looking and you'll always reject. Rules that keep \(\alpha\) (p.25) are a fixed horizon (pick \(n\) by power analysis, look once), a group sequential design with a stricter boundary planned in advance (Pocock uses \(|Z| > 2.41\) at each of 5 looks, and the study guide gives 2.18 for 2 looks, while O'Brien-Fleming is very strict early and near 1.96 at the end), and always-valid p-values (sequential probability ratio tests, used in A/B platforms, which allow any stopping rule). Stopping for a deadline or budget is fine, since that isn't driven by the result. Stopping the moment you hit significance also inflates the effect you report, so state the stopping rule either way.

Counting tests. \(k\) systems compared pairwise give \(k(k-1)/2\) tests, times every benchmark and metric. The slide's example is 5 schedulers \(\times\) 8 workloads \(\times\) 3 metrics \(= 240\) tests, and with nothing real you'd expect \(240 \times 0.05 = 12\) "findings". The family is the set of tests whose conclusions are read together, and you have to define it yourself. Picking out the significant results afterwards and reporting only those doesn't keep the Type I rate at \(\alpha\) (p.26).

Family-wise error rate (p.27), the probability of at least one false positive among \(m\) independent tests with every null true.

\[\text{FWER} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^m, \qquad \text{FWER} \le m\alpha \text{ for any dependence.}\]

At \(\alpha = 0.05\) that's 23% for \(m = 5\), 40% for \(m = 10\), 64% for \(m = 20\), 92% for \(m = 50\) and 99% for \(m = 100\). Twenty tests is a coin flip on whether your table holds a false claim (p.27).

ProcedureRuleControls
BonferroniReject if \(p \le \alpha/m\)FWER, any dependence
HolmSort the p's. Reject \(p_{(k)}\) while \(p_{(k)} \le \alpha/(m-k+1)\), and stop at the first failureFWER, never fewer rejections than Bonferroni
Benjamini-HochbergFind the largest \(k\) with \(p_{(k)} \le k\alpha/m\), and reject \(p_{(1)}\) through \(p_{(k)}\)FDR, the expected fraction of rejections that are false

Use FWER when one false claim is costly (a headline result) and FDR when you're screening many candidates for follow-up (p.28). Once you have the p-values there's no reason to use plain Bonferroni over Holm. Bonferroni costs power, since at \(m = 10\) the two-sided cutoff rises from \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) to \(z[0.0025] = 2.81\). The same idea gives simultaneous intervals, where each of \(m\) intervals is built at level \(1 - \alpha/m\), so you use \(z[\alpha/(2m)]\) and all \(m\) cover together with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\) (study guide).

When you compare more than two systems at once, the study guide's route is one omnibus test first (ANOVA, or Friedman if the data aren't normal) and then a post-hoc procedure built for all pairs (Tukey HSD, Nemenyi). That machinery comes in the later ANOVA and comparing-alternatives lectures. Either way, report every test you ran, not only the significant ones.

Worked example

FWER by hand.

Formula. \(\text{FWER} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^m\), where \(\alpha\) is the per-test level and \(m\) is the number of independent tests. \((1-\alpha)^m\) is the chance all \(m\) tests stay quiet, so one minus it is the chance of at least one false positive.

Plug in. \(m = 20\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). \(\text{FWER} = 1 - 0.95^{20}\).

Work it out. With no calculator, square repeatedly. \(0.95^2 = 0.9025\). \(0.95^4 = 0.9025^2 = 0.8145\). \(0.95^5 = 0.8145 \times 0.95 = 0.7738\). \(0.95^{10} = 0.7738^2 = 0.5987\). \(0.95^{20} = 0.5987^2 = 0.3585\). So FWER \(= 1 - 0.3585 = 0.6415 \approx 0.64\).

Result. About a 64% chance of at least one false positive in 20 tests. The bound \(m\alpha = 20 \times 0.05 = 1.0\) is useless here, which is why you compute the exact form.

HW5 Problem 3 used this for Spang et al.'s 28 intervals. Split 28 as \(20 + 8\). \(0.95^{8} = 0.95^5 \times 0.95^2 \times 0.95 = 0.7738 \times 0.9025 \times 0.95 = 0.6634\). Then \(0.95^{28} = 0.95^{20} \times 0.95^8 = 0.3585 \times 0.6634 = 0.2378\), and FWER \(= 1 - 0.2378 = 0.762 \approx 76\%\).

Six p-values, four procedures (p.29). Sorted p-values 0.001, 0.008, 0.012, 0.030, 0.041, 0.200, with \(\alpha = 0.05\) and \(m = 6\).

Formulas. Bonferroni rejects any \(p \le \alpha/m\). Holm compares the \(k\)-th smallest p, \(p_{(k)}\), with \(\alpha/(m-k+1)\) and stops at the first failure. BH finds the largest \(k\) with \(p_{(k)} \le k\alpha/m\) and rejects \(p_{(1)}\) through \(p_{(k)}\). Here \(k\) is the rank after sorting from smallest to largest.

Plug in. Bonferroni threshold \(= 0.05/6 = 0.0083\) for every row. Holm thresholds are \(0.05/6 = 0.0083\), \(0.05/5 = 0.0100\), \(0.05/4 = 0.0125\), \(0.05/3 = 0.0167\), and so on. BH thresholds are \(1 \times 0.05/6 = 0.0083\), \(2 \times 0.05/6 = 0.0167\), \(3 \times 0.05/6 = 0.0250\), \(0.0333\), \(0.0417\), \(0.0500\).

\(k\)\(p_{(k)}\)Bonferroni \(0.05/6\)Holm \(0.05/(7-k)\)BH \(k \times 0.05/6\)
10.0010.0083, reject0.0083, reject0.0083, yes
20.0080.0083, reject0.0100, reject0.0167, yes
30.0120.0083, no0.0125, reject0.0250, yes
40.0300.0083, no0.0167, fail, stop0.0333, yes
50.0410.0083, nonot tested0.0417, yes
60.2000.0083, nonot tested0.0500, no

Result. No correction rejects 5 (everything under 0.05). Bonferroni rejects 2, Holm rejects 3, and BH rejects 5 because the largest \(k\) that passes is 5 (\(0.041 \le 0.0417\)). Same data, four answers, which is why you choose the procedure first.

Peeking with a plan. A student gets p = 0.07 at 10 trials, runs 10 more and gets p = 0.04. That's two looks, so from the p.24 peeking table the real Type I rate is about 8%, not 5%. Had they planned a 2-look Pocock design, each look would need \(|Z| > 2.18\). To turn that into a p cutoff, the normal table at row 2.1, column 0.08 gives \(\Phi(2.18) = 0.9854\), so the tail is \(1 - 0.9854 = 0.0146\) and the two-sided cutoff is \(p < 2 \times 0.0146 = 0.029\). Since \(0.04 > 0.029\), it wouldn't count (study guide question 24).

Simultaneous intervals (study guide question 25). Four schedulers against a baseline, reductions with large-sample SE of 1.0 min each, S1 3.1, S2 2.4, S3 1.2, S4 0.6.

Formula. Ordinary interval, estimate \(\pm z[\alpha/2] \times \text{SE}\). Bonferroni interval, estimate \(\pm z[\alpha/(2m)] \times \text{SE}\), where \(m\) is the number of intervals read together.

Ordinary, worked out. \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) and SE \(= 1.0\), so each half-width is 1.96. S1 is \(3.1 \pm 1.96 = (1.14, 5.06)\). S2 is \(2.4 \pm 1.96 = (0.44, 4.36)\). S3 is \(1.2 \pm 1.96 = (-0.76, 3.16)\). S4 is \(0.6 \pm 1.96 = (-1.36, 2.56)\). S1 and S2 exclude 0, S3 and S4 don't.

Bonferroni, worked out. \(m = 4\), so \(\alpha/(2m) = 0.05/8 = 0.00625\). The z with upper-tail area 0.00625 (cumulative 0.99375) falls between row 2.4, column 0.09 (0.9936) and row 2.5, column 0.00 (0.9938), very close to 2.50, so \(z[0.00625] = 2.498\). S1 is \(3.1 \pm 2.498 = (0.60, 5.60)\). S2 is \(2.4 \pm 2.498 = (-0.10, 4.90)\).

Chance of a false alarm without correction. \(1 - (1-\alpha)^m = 1 - 0.95^4 = 1 - 0.8145 = 0.185\).

Result. After correction only S1 stays significant. Without it, if all four true reductions were 0, you'd still see at least one interval excluding 0 about 18.5% of the time.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(m\alpha\) as the FWER. It's an upper bound and goes past 1 quickly. The exact value for independent tests is \(1 - (1-\alpha)^m\) (HT p.27).
  • Holm divides by \(m - k + 1\), so the first threshold is \(\alpha/m\) and the thresholds loosen. Stop at the first failure even if a later p would pass.
  • BH keeps going past failures. You want the largest \(k\) that passes, then reject everything up to it.
  • Reporting only the significant cells of a big table. A results table with one asterisk per benchmark is a family of tests (p.30).
  • "p = 0.07 after ten runs, let's run a few more" is peeking (p.23).

Six ways it goes wrong, and the papers in the wild

The idea

The second half of the lecture is a list of ways a correctly computed p-value stops meaning what it says. Slide 31 collects six of them, and five of the six are decided before any statistic is computed (HT p.31). So the fix is almost always in the design, not in a better formula.

The "Hypothesis Testing in the Wild" handout shows each idea in a real CS paper, and HW5 Problem 3 asked you to run its Part III checklist on one paper. For any paper it asks two things. What are \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) here, and which error is more expensive? Would an interval on the effect have told the reader more than the verdict?

What to know

The six ways (p.31). Choosing the direction of \(H_1\) after seeing the data. Reporting "not significant" as "equivalent" on \(n = 5\). Reporting p with no effect size, interval or sample size. Treating 10,000 requests from one run as 10,000 independent observations. Stopping the morning the dashboard turns green. Running 240 tests and reporting the twelve that cleared 0.05.

Four communities that hit multiple comparisons (p.30). FuzzBench uses per-benchmark Mann-Whitney tests plus Friedman/Nemenyi critical-difference diagrams across benchmarks. Otero et al. (IR) find unadjusted pairwise tests badly inflate Type I error and recommend Benjamini-Hochberg. Zhao et al. (data systems) point out every chart in an exploration tool is an implicit test, and 100 tests at 0.05 give about 13 "discoveries", roughly 40% bogus. Cockburn et al. (HCI) name p-hacking and HARKing.

Handout entryLecture idea it shows
1. Bouthillier et al., ML benchmarksAverage-score comparison had under 5% false positives but about 90% false negatives. Their test on \(P(A > B)\) needs the result to be significant and \(P(A > B) \ge 0.75\), and recommends 29 runs
2. Patterson et al., RL"5 runs is insufficient", skew breaks the t test at small n, and reporting the best of many tuned settings (maximization bias) inflates Type I error
3. Marie et al., machine translationSignificance testing fell after 2016, and about 40% of papers compared against copied scores computed differently, so the test compares two different things
4. Urbano et al., IRType I rate measured, not assumed. t and permutation tests hold \(\alpha\). Also Type III error, rejecting a two-sided \(H_0\) but concluding the wrong direction
5. FuzzBenchMann-Whitney U with the \(A_{12}\) effect size per benchmark, Friedman/Nemenyi across benchmarks
6. Daly et al., MongoDB regressionsA fixed 10% alert threshold ignores each test's variance, so noisy tests get high \(\alpha\) and quiet ones get high \(\beta\) at the same time
7. Johari et al., A/B testingContinuous monitoring can raise Type I error fivefold, and always-valid p-values fix it
8. Spang et al., NetflixInterference (SUTVA), a valid test of the wrong quantity (p.22)
9. Carlini et al., membership inferenceAn attack is a test, false-positive rate is \(\alpha\), true-positive rate is power
10. Cockburn et al., HCIHARKing (hypothesizing after the results are known) breaks step 1 of the recipe on p.15
11. Zhao et al., data explorationThe family isn't known in advance, so they use \(\alpha\)-investing to control false discoveries as tests arrive

The handout's eight pitfalls repeat slide 31 and add three. A p-value isn't the probability \(H_0\) is true, the test has to match how the data were collected (paired data need a paired test), and a valid test can still measure the wrong quantity.

Worked example

Counting FuzzBench's tests (handout entry 5).

Formula. Pairs among \(k\) systems \(= k(k-1)/2\). Tests \(m\) = pairs times benchmarks. Expected false positives with every null true \(= m\alpha\). Bonferroni threshold \(= \alpha/m\).

Plug in and work it out. \(k = 10\), so pairs \(= 10 \times 9/2 = 90/2 = 45\) per benchmark. Over 20 benchmarks, \(m = 45 \times 20 = 900\). Expected false positives \(= 900 \times 0.05 = 45\). Bonferroni threshold \(= 0.05/900 \approx 0.0000556\).

Result. 900 tests and about 45 false positives if nothing is real. That tiny threshold is why the platform uses Friedman/Nemenyi instead of 900 separate corrected tests.

Reconstructing Zhao et al.'s numbers (handout entry 11). Suppose 10 of the 100 correlations are real and the test has power 0.8.

Formula. True discoveries \(=\) (number of real effects) \(\times\) power. False discoveries \(=\) (number of nulls) \(\times\, \alpha\). Fraction false \(=\) false over (true plus false).

Plug in and work it out. True \(= 10 \times 0.8 = 8\). Nulls \(= 100 - 10 = 90\), so false \(= 90 \times 0.05 = 4.5\). Total \(= 8 + 4.5 = 12.5\), about 13 discoveries. Fraction false \(= 4.5/12.5 = 0.36 = 36\%\).

Result. About 13 discoveries and 36% of them false, close to the paper's 40%. This is why \(\alpha = 0.05\) doesn't mean 5% of significant results are false.

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking a better test fixes a design problem. Interference, copied baselines and maximization bias make the test answer a different question, and no test repairs that (handout pitfall 7).
  • Confusing Type III with Type II. Type III means you correctly rejected but named the wrong direction (handout entry 4).
  • Treating a single-threshold alarm as neutral. A fixed percentage cutoff sets a different \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) for every test, depending on its noise (handout entry 6).

Chi-square goodness of fit

The idea

The same test machinery works for checking whether data follow a claimed distribution. \(H_0\) says the data come from that distribution, and \(H_1\) says they don't. You bin the data, work out how many observations each bin should get under \(H_0\), and add up how far the observed counts are from those expected counts. A big total means the claimed distribution fits badly (chi-square goodness-of-fit handout). This isn't on the HT slides, so treat it as the handout's extension of the same logic.

The asymmetry from p.4 still applies. Failing to reject means the data are consistent with the distribution, not that the distribution is right.

What to know

\[\chi^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \frac{(o_i - e_i)^2}{e_i}, \qquad \text{degrees of freedom} = k - m\]

Here \(o_i\) and \(e_i\) are observed and expected counts, \(k\) is the number of bins, and \(m\) is the number of quantities taken from the data to get the expected counts. When only the total is used, \(m = 1\). Reject if \(\chi^2\) is bigger than the upper-tail critical value \(\chi^2_{\alpha}\) at \(k - m\) degrees of freedom. It's always a one-tailed (upper) test, since only large discrepancies count against \(H_0\). Merge neighbouring bins so that no expected count is below 5 (handout).

Worked example

A load balancer is claimed to spread requests evenly over 4 servers. Out of 120 requests the servers got 36, 24, 33, 27. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Formula. \(\chi^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \dfrac{(o_i - e_i)^2}{e_i}\), with \(k - m\) degrees of freedom. \(o_i\) is the observed count in bin \(i\), \(e_i\) is the count \(H_0\) predicts for it, \(k\) is the number of bins, and \(m\) is how many quantities you took from the data to get the \(e_i\).

Expected counts. Under \(H_0\) each server expects \(e_i = 120/4 = 30\). All expected counts are at least 5, so no merging.
