Plug in. \[\chi^2 = \frac{(36-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(24-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(33-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(27-30)^2}{30}\]

Work it out. The differences are \(6, -6, 3, -3\). Squared they're \(36, 36, 9, 9\). Their sum is \(36 + 36 + 9 + 9 = 90\). Since every \(e_i\) is 30, \(\chi^2 = 90/30 = 3.0\). Only the total was used to get the expected counts, so \(m = 1\) and the degrees of freedom are \(4 - 1 = 3\). In the chi-square table go to row df = 3 and the column for upper-tail area 0.05, which gives \(\chi^2_{0.05} = 7.815\). Since \(3.0 < 7.815\), fail to reject.

Result. \(\chi^2 = 3.0\), fail to reject. The counts are consistent with an even split.

In the handout's own example (radio messages vs a Poisson with \(\lambda = 4.6\)), 14 counts are merged into 10 bins, which gives \(\chi^2 = 6.749\) with \(10 - 1 = 9\) degrees of freedom, and \(H_0\) isn't rejected.

Easy to get wrong

  • Dividing by the observed count instead of the expected one.
  • Using \(k\) degrees of freedom. Subtract one for the total, and one more for each parameter you estimated from the data.
  • Leaving bins with expected counts under 5. Merge them first, then count \(k\).
  • The handout's example says \(\alpha = 0.01\) but quotes 16.919, which is the 0.05 value at 9 degrees of freedom. The 0.01 value is 21.666. The decision doesn't change because 6.749 is below both, but read your critical value from the right column.

Check yourself

1. You want to show a new cache cuts mean miss latency below the current 80 ns. Write \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), say one- or two-sided, and say why the claim doesn't go in \(H_0\).

\(H_0: \mu = 80\) (or \(\mu \ge 80\)) and \(H_1: \mu < 80\), and the test is one-sided. It's one-sided because only a decrease supports the claim. The claim goes in \(H_1\) because a test can only give evidence by rejecting \(H_0\). Failing to reject is never evidence for \(H_0\), so if the claim sat in \(H_0\), a small noisy experiment would "support" it automatically (HT p.4-5).

2. \(\sigma = 6\) ms is known. You sample \(n = 36\) requests and get \(\bar{x} = 102\) ms. Test \(H_0: \mu = 100\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\), give p, and give the 95% interval.

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\), where \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, \(\mu_0\) the mean under \(H_0\), \(\sigma\) the known standard deviation and \(n\) the sample size. Two-sided \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\). Interval \(\bar{x} \pm z[\alpha/2]\,\sigma/\sqrt{n}\).

Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{102 - 100}{6/\sqrt{36}}\).

Work it out. \(\sqrt{36} = 6\). SE \(= 6/6 = 1\). \(Z = 2/1 = 2.0\). Cutoff \(z[0.025] = 1.96\), and \(2.0 > 1.96\), so reject. Normal table row 2.0, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(2.00) = 0.9772\), so the tail is \(1 - 0.9772 = 0.0228\) and \(p = 2 \times 0.0228 = 0.0456\). Interval half-width \(= 1.96 \times 1 = 1.96\), so \(102 - 1.96 = 100.04\) and \(102 + 1.96 = 103.96\).

Result. Reject, \(p = 0.046\), 95% interval \((100.04, 103.96)\). The interval just excludes 100, matching the verdict (HT p.8, p.13).

3. Same data as question 2, but \(\alpha = 0.01\). What's the smallest \(\bar{x}\) above 100 that rejects, two-sided and one-sided (\(H_1: \mu > 100\))?

Formula. You reject when \(\bar{x} > \mu_0 + z_{\text{cut}} \times \sigma/\sqrt{n}\). This is just \(Z > z_{\text{cut}}\) solved for \(\bar{x}\). Two-sided uses \(z_{\text{cut}} = z[\alpha/2]\) and one-sided uses \(z[\alpha]\).

Table lookup. Two-sided, \(\alpha/2 = 0.005\), and the z with upper-tail area 0.005 (cumulative 0.995) is 2.576. One-sided, upper-tail area 0.01 (cumulative 0.99) gives 2.326.

Plug in and work it out. SE \(= 1\) from question 2. Two-sided, \(\bar{x} > 100 + 2.576 \times 1 = 102.576\). One-sided, \(\bar{x} > 100 + 2.326 \times 1 = 102.326\).

Result. About 102.58 two-sided and 102.33 one-sided. The observed 102 fails both at this \(\alpha\). This is the HW5 Problem 1.3 method (HT p.9).

4. Four runs give \(\bar{x} = 5.6\) s and \(s = 0.4\) s. Test \(H_0: \mu = 5\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\). \(t[0.025,\,3] = 3.182\).

Formula. \(t = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}\) with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom. You use t because \(\sigma\) is unknown (you only have the sample \(s\)) and \(n\) is small.

Plug in. \(t = \dfrac{5.6 - 5}{0.4/\sqrt{4}}\).

Work it out. \(\sqrt{4} = 2\). SE \(= 0.4/2 = 0.2\). Top \(= 0.6\). \(t = 0.6/0.2 = 3.0\). Degrees of freedom \(= 4 - 1 = 3\). The t table at row df = 3, upper-tail column 0.025, gives 3.182. Since \(3.0 < 3.182\), fail to reject.

Result. Fail to reject. A Z test would have rejected (\(3.0 > 1.96\)), which is exactly the mistake the t table protects you from (HT p.11-12).

5. A paper says "p = 0.02, so there's only a 2% chance our improvement is a fluke." What's wrong, and what should it say?

The sentence treats p as the probability that \(H_0\) is true, and it isn't. The p-value is computed assuming \(H_0\) is true, so it can't also be the probability that \(H_0\) is true or that the result is a fluke. That would need a prior, and a test has none. The correct reading is "if there were no improvement, a difference at least this large would show up about 2% of the time" (HT p.13-14).

6. A study reports "no significant difference (p = 0.30, n = 5), so the two systems are equivalent." Write the reviewer's objection.

Failing to reject isn't evidence that the systems are equivalent. With \(n = 5\) the test has little power, so a real difference could easily produce p = 0.30. Ask for the difference with a confidence interval. If the interval is wide, like \((-100, 100)\), the experiment can't say anything, and only a narrow interval around 0 supports "effectively equivalent" (HT p.14, p.16).

7. \(H_0: \mu = 20\), two-sided, \(\sigma = 8\), \(n = 16\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). The true mean is 24. Find the power, then the \(n\) needed for power 0.80 (use \(z[0.20] = 0.84\)).

Power formula. \(1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > z[\alpha/2] - \dfrac{|\delta|}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\right]\), where \(\delta\) is the true mean minus \(\mu_0\), and \(|\delta|/(\sigma/\sqrt{n})\) is that shift counted in standard errors.

Plug in. \(\delta = 24 - 20 = 4\). \(1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > 1.96 - \dfrac{4}{8/\sqrt{16}}\right]\).

Work it out. \(\sqrt{16} = 4\). SE \(= 8/4 = 2\). Shift \(= 4/2 = 2\) standard errors. \(1.96 - 2 = -0.04\). \(\Pr[Z > -0.04] = \Pr[Z < 0.04]\), and the table at row 0.0, column 0.04 gives 0.5160. So power \(\approx 0.516\) and \(\beta = 1 - 0.516 = 0.484\).

Sample-size formula. \(n = \left(\dfrac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\,\sigma}{\delta}\right)^2\), where \(z[\beta]\) is the normal value with upper-tail area \(\beta\).

Plug in and work it out. \(n = \left(\dfrac{(1.96 + 0.84) \times 8}{4}\right)^2\). \(1.96 + 0.84 = 2.8\). \(8/4 = 2\). \(2.8 \times 2 = 5.6\). \(5.6^2 = 31.36\). Round up to 32.

Result. Power is about 0.52, so this design misses the effect about half the time. You need 32 runs for power 0.80 (HT p.19).

8. A web company reports page load fell from 250.0 to 249.7 ms with \(p < 10^{-9}\) over a million loads and calls it "highly significant". What's right and what's misleading?

What's right is that the 0.3 ms drop is very likely not zero. What's misleading is "highly significant", which sounds large, but the tiny p comes from the huge \(n\).

Formula. \(Z = \text{effect}/(\sigma/\sqrt{n})\), and relative change \(=\) effect \(/\) baseline.

Plug in and work it out. Take \(\sigma = 50\) ms as on p.17. \(\sqrt{10^6} = 1000\), so SE \(= 50/1000 = 0.05\) and \(Z = 0.3/0.05 = 6\). Relative change \(= 0.3/250 = 0.0012 = 0.12\%\).

Result. Z is huge, but the change is 0.12%, which no user notices. They should report the raw and relative change with an interval and say what size would matter (HT p.17-18).

9. You record 900 consecutive latencies from one run of each of two systems, with lag-1 autocorrelation \(\rho = 0.8\). What's \(n_{\text{eff}}\) per series, by how much is the naive Z too big, and what's the experimental unit?

Formula. \(n_{\text{eff}} = n\,\dfrac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}\), where \(n\) is the number of measurements and \(\rho\) the lag-1 autocorrelation. The variance of the mean is too small by the factor \((1+\rho)/(1-\rho)\), so the naive Z is too big by \(\sqrt{(1+\rho)/(1-\rho)}\).

Plug in. \(n_{\text{eff}} = 900 \times \dfrac{1 - 0.8}{1 + 0.8}\).

Work it out. \(1 - 0.8 = 0.2\). \(1 + 0.8 = 1.8\). \(0.2/1.8 = 1/9\). \(900/9 = 100\). Variance factor \(= 1.8/0.2 = 9\), and \(\sqrt{9} = 3\).

Result. \(n_{\text{eff}} = 100\) per series, and the naive Z is 3 times too big. At \(\rho = 0.8\) the actual Type I rate is about 51% at a nominal 5%. The experimental unit is the run, and there's one per system, so you need many independent runs with one summary each (HT p.20-21).

10. A paper compares 5 systems pairwise on 4 workloads. How many tests is that, and what's the FWER bound if every null is true? Then apply Holm at \(\alpha = 0.05\) to the four p-values 0.004, 0.011, 0.020, 0.300.

Counting. Pairs \(= k(k-1)/2 = 5 \times 4/2 = 20/2 = 10\). Tests \(= 10 \times 4 = 40\).

FWER formulas. Bound \(m\alpha\), exact for independent tests \(1 - (1-\alpha)^m\). Plugging in, \(m\alpha = 40 \times 0.05 = 2\), which is useless since a probability can't pass 1. The independent-test value is \(1 - 0.95^{40}\). With \(0.95^{20} = 0.3585\), \(0.95^{40} = 0.3585^2 = 0.1285\), so FWER \(= 1 - 0.1285 \approx 0.87\).

Holm formula. Sort the p's. Compare the \(k\)-th smallest with \(\alpha/(m-k+1)\), and stop at the first failure.

Work it out with \(m = 4\). \(k = 1\), \(0.05/4 = 0.0125\), and \(0.004 \le 0.0125\), reject. \(k = 2\), \(0.05/3 = 0.0167\), and \(0.011 \le 0.0167\), reject. \(k = 3\), \(0.05/2 = 0.025\), and \(0.020 \le 0.025\), reject. \(k = 4\), \(0.05/1 = 0.05\), and \(0.300 > 0.05\), fail.

Result. 40 tests, FWER about 0.87. Holm rejects 3. Bonferroni at \(0.0125\) for all four would reject only 2, since \(0.020 > 0.0125\) (HT p.26-28).

11. Same four p-values, Benjamini-Hochberg at \(\alpha = 0.05\). How many rejections, and when would you prefer BH over Holm?

Formula. Find the largest \(k\) with \(p_{(k)} \le k\alpha/m\), where \(p_{(k)}\) is the \(k\)-th smallest p and \(m\) is the number of tests. Reject \(p_{(1)}\) through \(p_{(k)}\).

Plug in and work it out. Thresholds \(k \times 0.05/4\) are \(1 \times 0.0125 = 0.0125\), \(2 \times 0.0125 = 0.025\), \(3 \times 0.0125 = 0.0375\), \(4 \times 0.0125 = 0.05\). Check, \(0.004 \le 0.0125\) yes, \(0.011 \le 0.025\) yes, \(0.020 \le 0.0375\) yes, \(0.300 \le 0.05\) no. The largest passing \(k\) is 3.

Result. Reject 3. Use BH, which controls the false discovery rate, when screening many candidates for a follow-up study. Use Holm when a single false claim is costly, like a headline result (HT p.28).

12. A team checks its A/B dashboard every morning and plans to stop the first day p < 0.05. What goes wrong, and name two fixes.

The real false-positive rate climbs well past 5%. Each look is another chance for noise to cross 1.96, so the procedure's Type I rate is about 14% for 5 looks and 25% for 20 (HT p.24). The reported effect is also inflated, because you stop right when noise happens to push it up. Fixes are a fixed horizon with \(n\) from a power analysis and one look, a pre-planned group sequential design (Pocock \(|Z| > 2.41\) at each of 5 looks, or O'Brien-Fleming), or always-valid p-values (p.23-25).

13. A die is rolled 60 times and gives counts 8, 12, 9, 11, 6, 14. Is it fair at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? \(\chi^2_{0.05} = 11.070\) at 5 degrees of freedom.

Formula. \(\chi^2 = \sum \dfrac{(o_i - e_i)^2}{e_i}\) with \(k - 1\) degrees of freedom, where \(o_i\) is the observed count for face \(i\), \(e_i\) the expected count, and \(k\) the number of faces.

Expected counts. A fair die gives each face \(e_i = 60/6 = 10\).

Plug in. \(\chi^2 = \dfrac{(8-10)^2 + (12-10)^2 + (9-10)^2 + (11-10)^2 + (6-10)^2 + (14-10)^2}{10}\).

Work it out. Differences \(-2, 2, -1, 1, -4, 4\). Squares \(4, 4, 1, 1, 16, 16\). Sum \(= 42\). \(\chi^2 = 42/10 = 4.2\). Degrees of freedom \(= 6 - 1 = 5\). The chi-square table at row df = 5, upper-tail column 0.05, gives 11.070. Since \(4.2 < 11.070\), fail to reject.

Result. Fail to reject. The counts are consistent with a fair die, which isn't the same as proving it fair (chi-square goodness-of-fit handout).

14. An A/B test gives 200 users each variant. A converts 92 and B converts 108. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\) whether the rates differ.

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\hat{p}_B - \hat{p}_A}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})(1/n_A + 1/n_B)}}\), where \(\hat{p}_A\), \(\hat{p}_B\) are the two conversion rates, \(n_A\), \(n_B\) the group sizes, and \(\hat{p}\) the pooled rate (all conversions over all users).

The pieces. \(\hat{p}_A = 92/200 = 0.46\). \(\hat{p}_B = 108/200 = 0.54\). Pooled \(\hat{p} = (92 + 108)/(200 + 200) = 200/400 = 0.5\).

Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{0.54 - 0.46}{\sqrt{0.5 \times 0.5 \times (1/200 + 1/200)}}\).

Work it out. \(0.5 \times 0.5 = 0.25\). \(1/200 + 1/200 = 2/200 = 0.01\). \(0.25 \times 0.01 = 0.0025\). \(\sqrt{0.0025} = 0.05\). Top \(= 0.08\). \(Z = 0.08/0.05 = 1.6\). Since \(1.6 < 1.96\), fail to reject. Table row 1.6, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(1.60) = 0.9452\), so the tail is 0.0548 and \(p = 2 \times 0.0548 = 0.11\).

Result. Fail to reject, \(p = 0.11\). An 8-point difference isn't distinguishable from noise at this size, which is a power problem, not evidence of no effect (study guide formula sheet, question 5).

15. Two classifiers are run on the same 400 items. Only A is right on 25, only B is right on 11, and they agree on the rest. Do their accuracies differ at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? Why not use the two-proportion test?

Formula. McNemar, \(Z = \dfrac{b - c}{\sqrt{b + c}}\), where \(b\) is the number of items only A got right and \(c\) the number only B got right. Agreements drop out because they say nothing about which is better.

Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{25 - 11}{\sqrt{25 + 11}}\).

Work it out. Top \(= 14\). \(25 + 11 = 36\) and \(\sqrt{36} = 6\). \(Z = 14/6 = 2.33\). Since \(2.33 > 1.96\), reject. Table row 2.3, column 0.03, gives \(\Phi(2.33) = 0.9901\), so the tail is 0.0099 and \(p = 2 \times 0.0099 = 0.020\).

Result. Reject, \(p = 0.020\), so A is more accurate. The two-proportion test assumes two independent samples, but both classifiers answer the same items and their correctness is correlated, so it overstates the variance and loses power (study guide question 6).

16. A paper makes 10 comparisons. One has estimate 2.5 with SE 1.0. Is it significant with an ordinary 95% interval, and with a Bonferroni-adjusted one?

Formula. Ordinary interval, estimate \(\pm z[\alpha/2] \times \text{SE}\). Bonferroni, estimate \(\pm z[\alpha/(2m)] \times \text{SE}\), where \(m\) is the number of comparisons.

Ordinary. \(z[0.025] = 1.96\). \(2.5 \pm 1.96 \times 1.0\), so \(2.5 - 1.96 = 0.54\) and \(2.5 + 1.96 = 4.46\). The interval \((0.54, 4.46)\) excludes 0, so it's significant.

Bonferroni. \(m = 10\), so \(\alpha/(2m) = 0.05/20 = 0.0025\). The z with upper-tail area 0.0025 (cumulative 0.9975) is at row 2.8, column 0.01, so \(z[0.0025] = 2.81\). \(2.5 \pm 2.81\), so \(2.5 - 2.81 = -0.31\) and \(2.5 + 2.81 = 5.31\). The interval \((-0.31, 5.31)\) contains 0.

Result. Significant without correction, not significant after it. The interval is still mostly positive, so "not significant" isn't "no effect" (HT p.28, study guide).

17. A systems paper runs a t test for each of 8 workloads \(\times\) 3 metrics and highlights the two with p < 0.05, calling the other 22 "comparable". What's wrong?

Two hits out of 24 is about what noise alone gives, so the highlights aren't convincing and "comparable" is unsupported.

Formulas. Expected false positives \(= m\alpha\). Chance of at least one \(= 1 - (1-\alpha)^m\). Bonferroni threshold \(= \alpha/m\).

Plug in and work it out. \(m = 8 \times 3 = 24\). Expected false positives \(= 24 \times 0.05 = 1.2\). For the chance of at least one, \(0.95^{24} = 0.95^{20} \times 0.95^4 = 0.3585 \times 0.8145 = 0.292\), so \(1 - 0.292 = 0.71\). Bonferroni threshold \(= 0.05/24 \approx 0.0021\).

Result. With every null true you'd expect about 1.2 false positives and get at least one 71% of the time. Picking the hits afterwards is a selection effect (HT p.26), and the 0.0021 threshold would likely kill both. Ask whether those metrics were declared primary in advance, and ask for effect sizes and intervals for all 24. "Comparable" for the other 22 is an unsupported equivalence claim (study guide question 18).

18. A membership inference paper reports 62% accuracy. Another reports a 1.2% true-positive rate at a 0.1% false-positive rate. Put both in Type I / Type II language. Which is more alarming?

The second paper is more alarming. Take \(H_0\) as "this example wasn't in the training set". Then the false-positive rate is \(\alpha\) and the true-positive rate is power. The second paper fixes \(\alpha = 0.001\) and gets power 0.012. Per 1,000 members it flags \(0.012 \times 1000 = 12\), and per 1,000 non-members it falsely flags \(0.001 \times 1000 = 1\). So it can name specific training examples with confidence. Accuracy averages over everything and can come from a weak diffuse signal (HT p.6-7, handout entry 9).

19. A service's mean response time is specified as 100 ms with known \(\sigma = 8\) ms. After a change, 64 requests average 103.1 ms. Test \(H_0: \mu = 100\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\), with the p-value and the interval.

Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\), two-sided \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\), interval \(\bar{x} \pm 1.96\,\sigma/\sqrt{n}\).

Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{103.1 - 100}{8/\sqrt{64}}\).

Work it out. \(\sqrt{64} = 8\). SE \(= 8/8 = 1.0\) ms. Top \(= 3.1\). \(Z = 3.1/1.0 = 3.10\). Since \(3.10 > 1.96\), reject. Table row 3.1, column 0.00, gives \(\Phi(3.10) = 0.99903\), so the tail is 0.00097 and \(p = 2 \times 0.00097 = 0.0019\). Interval half-width \(= 1.96 \times 1.0 = 1.96\), so \(103.1 - 1.96 = 101.14\) and \(103.1 + 1.96 = 105.06\).

Result. Reject, \(p = 0.0019\), 95% interval about \((101.1,\ 105.1)\) ms. It excludes 100 and also tells you the change added roughly 1 to 5 ms, something the verdict alone doesn't (HT p.8, p.13, study guide question 1).

20. A storage benchmark is specified at 12.0 s. Eight runs on a new SSD give \(\bar{x} = 12.9\) s and \(s = 0.9\) s, roughly normal. Does the mean differ from 12.0 s at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? \(t[0.025,\,7] = 2.365\).

Formula. \(t = \dfrac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}\) with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom, because \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small.

Plug in. \(t = \dfrac{12.9 - 12.0}{0.9/\sqrt{8}}\).

Work it out. \(\sqrt{8} = 2.828\). SE \(= 0.9/2.828 = 0.318\). Top \(= 0.9\). \(t = 0.9/0.318 = 2.83\). Degrees of freedom \(= 8 - 1 = 7\). The t table at row df = 7, upper-tail column 0.025, gives 2.365. Since \(2.83 > 2.365\), reject. For p, look along row 7. The 0.025 column (2.365) is below 2.83 and the 0.005 column (3.499) is above it, so the one-tail area is between 0.005 and 0.025. Doubling gives \(0.01 < p < 0.05\) (the exact value is about 0.025).

Result. Reject. The SSD's mean differs from 12.0 s. Using 1.96 would give the same verdict here, but that cutoff is \(1 - 1.96/2.365 = 17\%\) too small, so a t near 2.1 would have been wrongly rejected (HT p.11-12, study guide question 3).

21. A new allocator and the baseline run six benchmarks. Baseline 12.1, 8.4, 15.3, 9.8, 20.2, 11.0 s. New 11.5, 8.1, 14.2, 9.6, 19.1, 10.4 s. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\), and explain why an unpaired t test on the two columns gives \(p \approx 0.79\).

Formula. Paired t, \(t = \dfrac{\bar{d}}{s_d/\sqrt{n}}\) with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom. \(d_i\) is new minus baseline on benchmark \(i\), \(\bar{d}\) is their mean, \(s_d = \sqrt{\sum (d_i - \bar{d})^2/(n-1)}\) is their standard deviation, and \(n\) is the number of benchmarks. You pair because the same benchmarks ran under both.

Differences. \(11.5 - 12.1 = -0.6\), \(8.1 - 8.4 = -0.3\), \(14.2 - 15.3 = -1.1\), \(9.6 - 9.8 = -0.2\), \(19.1 - 20.2 = -1.1\), \(10.4 - 11.0 = -0.6\). Sum \(= -3.9\), so \(\bar{d} = -3.9/6 = -0.65\).

Standard deviation. Deviations from \(-0.65\) are \(0.05, 0.35, -0.45, 0.45, -0.45, 0.05\). Squares are \(0.0025, 0.1225, 0.2025, 0.2025, 0.2025, 0.0025\), which add to 0.735. \(s_d^2 = 0.735/5 = 0.147\) and \(s_d = \sqrt{0.147} = 0.383\).

Plug in and work it out. \(t = \dfrac{-0.65}{0.383/\sqrt{6}}\). \(\sqrt{6} = 2.449\). SE \(= 0.383/2.449 = 0.157\). \(t = -0.65/0.157 = -4.15\). Degrees of freedom \(= 6 - 1 = 5\). The t table at row df = 5, upper-tail column 0.025, gives 2.571. Since \(|-4.15| = 4.15 > 2.571\), reject (\(p \approx 0.009\)).

Result. Reject. The new allocator wins on every benchmark, by 0.65 s on average.

Why unpaired fails. The unpaired test compares the column means \(76.8/6 = 12.80\) and \(72.9/6 = 12.15\) against column standard deviations of about 4.3 and 4.0 s, which measure how different the benchmarks are from each other. A 0.65 s effect disappears in 4 s of spread, so \(t \approx -0.27\). Pairing removes that spread, and ignoring it throws away almost all the power (study guide question 4).

22. You're comparing 5 fuzzers on 20 targets by coverage after 24 hours. How many trials per fuzzer-target pair, which test per target, how do you handle the number of comparisons, and what do you report besides p?

Pick the trial count from a power calculation, test each target with Mann-Whitney U, correct with Holm or BH (or Friedman plus Nemenyi), and report \(A_{12}\) with medians and intervals. For trials, use the recipe on (HT p.15) and the formula on (p.19). Run a pilot to estimate the run-to-run \(\sigma\) of coverage, pick the smallest effect worth detecting, compute \(n = ((z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\sigma/\delta)^2\) for power 0.80, and report all three so the choice can be checked. Coverage across trials is often skewed or bimodal and \(n\) is modest, so per target a two-sided Mann-Whitney U test is a sensible default (p.11). Five fuzzers give \(5 \times 4/2 = 10\) pairs per target, so \(10 \times 20 = 200\) tests, and with every null true you'd expect \(200 \times 0.05 = 10\) false positives (p.26). Either correct with Holm or Benjamini-Hochberg (p.28), or use Friedman plus Nemenyi across targets as FuzzBench does (p.30). Report the \(A_{12}\) effect size per pair, medians with intervals and the full distributions (p.18, study guide question 12).

23. To compare two GPU cluster schedulers, a labmate proposes one replay of a 500-job trace where a random half of the jobs use the new policy, then a two-sample t test on the 250 + 250 completion times. What's wrong, what's the unit, and what would you do instead?

The design has interference, correlated jobs and only one replay, and the unit is a whole replay, so you need many replays per scheduler. First, the halves share the same 16 nodes, so each policy's placements change the other's queueing delays. That's interference, and the test measures the new policy running beside the old one rather than deployed on its own (HT p.22). Second, jobs in one replay are correlated through shared queues, so 250 per group isn't 250 independent observations and the standard error is too small (p.20-21). Third, with one replay, run-to-run variation is never measured. The experimental unit is one complete replay of the trace under one scheduler. Instead, run each scheduler on the whole cluster for \(R\) replays, pair them back to back on the same testbed and alternate which goes first, spread the pairs over days, reduce each replay to one summary (mean JCT, plus p99 if the tail matters), and run a paired t test on the per-pair differences. Choose \(R\) in advance from a pilot and a power calculation (p.15, p.19, study guide question 23).

24. Would you control FWER or FDR, and with which procedure, for (a) a headline claim that your scheduler beats each of three published baselines, and (b) screening 200 configuration parameters to pick a few for a later experiment?

(a) FWER with Holm. It's a confirmatory headline claim, so a single false "beats baseline B" is the costly error, and with a family of three the power cost is small. (b) FDR with Benjamini-Hochberg. It's screening, so some false leads are fine because the follow-up experiment weeds them out. With \(m = 200\), Bonferroni's threshold would be \(\alpha/m = 0.05/200 = 0.00025\), which would miss most real effects. The follow-up must use fresh data, since retesting the selected parameters on the screening data reuses the same false positives (HT p.28, study guide question 26).

Comparing two alternatives

Slides: Comp (plus CI for the proportion interval), the comparing-alternatives study guide, and the Comparing Two Systems in the Wild handout. Homework: HW6 Problems 1, 2 and 3.

Paired or unpaired, and how to tell

The idea

Every question in this lecture is about a difference. "Is A better than B?" is answered by a confidence interval for A minus B, and the verdict is whether that interval contains 0 (Comp p.3). The interval also tells you how big the difference is, which a yes or no verdict never does.

Before any arithmetic you have to decide the design, because it decides which formula you use and how wide the interval comes out. The design is fixed when the data are collected. You can swap tests afterwards, but you can't turn unpaired data into paired data (Comp p.4).

Comp p.4 lists three things to settle before any test is chosen. The first is the response, such as mean latency, p99, hit ratio or energy per request, picked before measuring because a test on the wrong quantity can't support the claim. The second is the experimental unit, meaning one run, one benchmark or one user, because n counts units and not measurements. The third is the design, whether both systems can be measured on the same unit. The first two carry over unchanged from the hypothesis-testing lecture, and the third is this lecture's subject.

The test for the exam is simple. Ask whether each measurement of A has a natural partner measurement of B taken on the same unit, meaning the same benchmark, workload, trace, input, user or machine. If yes, the data are paired. If the two lists are just two independent samples, often with different lengths, they're unpaired (Comp p.5).

Pairing helps because units differ from each other far more than systems do. Benchmark b4 takes 64 seconds and b3 takes 7, and that spread has nothing to do with A versus B. When you subtract within a pair, the unit's own difficulty cancels, so only the system effect and a little noise are left (Comp p.6).

What to know

The variance of a difference is (Comp p.6)

\[ \mathrm{Var}(A-B) = \sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2 - 2\rho\,\sigma_A\sigma_B, \qquad \rho = \frac{\mathrm{Cov}(A,B)}{\sigma_A\sigma_B}. \]

On paired data \(\rho\) is usually close to 1, so the variance of the difference is far smaller than either variance. On unpaired data there is no correspondence, so \(\rho = 0\) and the between-unit spread stays in the standard error. The price of pairing is degrees of freedom, \(n-1\) instead of \(n_A+n_B-2\), and it's worth paying whenever \(\rho > 0\) (Comp p.6).

SituationDesignWhy
Same 9 workloads injected under two routing algorithms (HW6 Problem 1)PairedWorkload \(i\) gives \((A_i, B_i)\)
30 transactions on System A, 24 different ones on System B (HW6 Problem 2)UnpairedDifferent counts, no row of A goes with a row of B
Each user session sees only one schedulerUnpairedThe unit can't be measured twice
Measuring under A changes the unit for B (cache pollution, learning effects)Don't pairThe pair is no longer comparable (Comp p.5)

Two errors from Comp p.5 and p.27 are worth memorizing. A paired analysis of unpaired data is wrong, because it invents a correspondence that doesn't exist. An unpaired analysis of paired data is only wasteful, because it throws away the power you already paid for.

The lecture's demonstration (Comp p.7-8) ran eight benchmarks under both systems. The estimate was +1.83 s either way. Paired, the interval was (+0.65, +3.00) s with p = 0.008. Unpaired Welch on the same 16 numbers gave (−22.0, +25.7) s with p = 0.87, which is 20 times wider and the wrong conclusion.

Worked example

Five benchmarks, response time in seconds, each run on both systems.

Benchmark12345Mean
A12251840920.8
B10211535817.8
\(d = A-B\)243513.0

Same benchmark in each column, so this is paired. To see what pairing buys, compare the spread of each column with the spread of the differences.

  1. Formula. The sample variance is \(s^2 = \dfrac{\sum (x_i - \bar x)^2}{n-1}\). Here \(x_i\) is one value, \(\bar x\) is the mean of the column, \(x_i - \bar x\) is that value's deviation, and \(n\) is how many values there are (5 here, so you divide by 4).
  2. Plug in for A. The mean is \(\bar x_A = (12+25+18+40+9)/5 = 104/5 = 20.8\). The deviations are \(12-20.8 = -8.8\), \(25-20.8 = 4.2\), \(18-20.8 = -2.8\), \(40-20.8 = 19.2\), \(9-20.8 = -11.8\). \[ s_A^2 = \frac{(-8.8)^2 + 4.2^2 + (-2.8)^2 + 19.2^2 + (-11.8)^2}{4} \]
  3. Work it out for A. Square each one. \(8.8^2 = 77.44\), \(4.2^2 = 17.64\), \(2.8^2 = 7.84\), \(19.2^2 = 368.64\), \(11.8^2 = 139.24\). Add them, \(77.44 + 17.64 = 95.08\), then \(+ 7.84 = 102.92\), then \(+ 368.64 = 471.56\), then \(+ 139.24 = 610.8\). Divide, \(610.8/4 = 152.7\).
  4. Same steps for B. The mean is \(\bar x_B = (10+21+15+35+8)/5 = 89/5 = 17.8\). Deviations are \(-7.8, 3.2, -2.8, 17.2, -9.8\). Squares are \(60.84, 10.24, 7.84, 295.84, 96.04\), which add to 470.8. So \(s_B^2 = 470.8/4 = 117.7\).
  5. Same steps for d. The differences 2, 4, 3, 5, 1 add to 15, so \(\bar d = 15/5 = 3\). Deviations from 3 are \(-1, 1, 0, 2, -2\). Squares are 1, 1, 0, 4, 4, which add to 10. So \(s_d^2 = 10/4 = 2.5\).
  6. Result. \(s_A^2 = 152.7\), \(s_B^2 = 117.7\), but \(s_d^2 = 2.5\). The differences barely vary while each column varies a lot, because a hard benchmark is hard for both systems. That's \(\rho\) near 1 doing its job.

The next topic finishes the paired interval, and the Welch topic shows what the unpaired analysis of these same numbers would give.

Easy to get wrong

  • Equal sample sizes don't make data paired. Pairing comes from a shared unit, not from the counts matching.
  • n counts units, not measurements. Thirty iterations inside one run are one unit, because they share the same process state (Comp p.4).
  • The mean of the differences equals the difference of the means, so the estimate is identical under both designs. Only the standard error changes (Comp p.8).
  • Pairing doesn't create \(\rho\). It lets you use the correlation that's already in the data.
  • "A paired test always has more power than an unpaired one on the same data" is false. If \(\rho \approx 0\) and n is small, losing half the degrees of freedom can make the unpaired analysis slightly stronger (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 19b).
  • Pairing is the two-system version of blocking. The unit is the block, and its own difficulty cancels out (Comp p.6). The ANOVA lectures pick this idea up again.

The paired confidence interval

The idea

Once the data are paired, there's nothing new to learn. You turn the two columns into one column of differences and build the ordinary one-sample t interval on it (Comp p.9). If the interval contains 0, the data don't establish a difference. If it excludes 0, the sign of the interval tells you which system wins.

The interval is the finding, not just the verdict. An interval that straddles 0 but is wide says the experiment was too small to tell, which is different from saying the systems are the same (Comp p.11). The cache example's 90% interval (−0.041, +0.081) is compatible with B being 4 points better and with A being 8 points better, so the slide's reading is "six workloads told us very little", not "the policies are equivalent".

What to know

The five steps on Comp p.9 are to form \(d_i = A_i - B_i\), compute \(\bar d\) and \(s_d\), build the interval, check for 0, and report the difference with its interval.

\[ \bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n-1]}\,\frac{s_d}{\sqrt n}, \qquad t = \frac{\bar d}{s_d/\sqrt n}\ \text{with } n-1 \text{ df}. \]

Here n is the number of pairs, not the number of measurements. The slides use upper-tail notation, so \(t_{[0.025,\,8]} = 2.306\) is the value for a two-sided 95% interval on 8 degrees of freedom, and \(t_{[0.05,\,5]} = 2.015\) is the one for a two-sided 90% interval on 5 (Comp p.11).

The normality assumption is about the differences, not about A or B separately (Comp p.17). A and B can both be skewed as long as the differences are reasonably well behaved.

For speed with a hand calculation, use the shortcut \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2}{n-1}\).

Worked example

Small example. Continue the five benchmarks from the last topic, with \(\bar d = 3\) and \(s_d^2 = 2.5\). Build a 95% interval.

  1. Formula. \(\bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n-1]}\,\dfrac{s_d}{\sqrt n}\). Here \(\bar d\) is the mean of the differences, \(s_d\) is their standard deviation, \(n\) is the number of pairs, \(s_d/\sqrt n\) is the standard error, and \(t_{[\alpha/2,\,n-1]}\) is the t-table value for confidence \(1-\alpha\) with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom.
  2. Plug in. \(\bar d = 3\), \(s_d = \sqrt{2.5}\), \(n = 5\), and 95% means \(\alpha = 0.05\), so \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\) and df \(= 5 - 1 = 4\). \[ 3 \pm t_{[0.025,\,4]}\,\frac{\sqrt{2.5}}{\sqrt 5} \]
  3. Work it out. \(\sqrt{2.5} = 1.581\). \(\sqrt5 = 2.236\). Standard error \(= 1.581/2.236 = 0.707\). In the t table go to row df = 4 and column 0.025 to read 2.776. Half-width \(= 2.776 \times 0.707 = 1.963\). Lower end \(3 - 1.963 = 1.04\), upper end \(3 + 1.963 = 4.96\).
  4. Result. The 95% interval is (1.04, 4.96) s. Zero is outside, so A is slower than B by somewhere between about 1 and 5 seconds.

The same verdict as a test. The formula is \(t = \bar d/(s_d/\sqrt n)\), the mean difference measured in standard errors. Plug in \(t = 3/0.707\), which works out to 4.24. Compare with the table value 2.776. Since 4.24 is bigger, reject "no difference", which agrees with the interval.

The lecture's demonstration by hand (Comp p.7-8, study guide questions 2 and 3). The eight differences B − A are 0.8, 2.2, 0.5, 4.2, 1.5, 1.3, 3.6, 0.5.

  1. Formulas. Mean \(\bar d = \sum d_i / n\). The hand shortcut for the variance is \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2}{n-1}\), where \(\sum d_i^2\) means square every difference and add. Then standard error \(s_d/\sqrt n\), interval \(\bar d \pm t_{[0.025,\,n-1]}\, s_d/\sqrt n\), test statistic \(t = \bar d/(s_d/\sqrt n)\).
  2. Mean. Add the differences, \(0.8 + 2.2 = 3.0\), \(+0.5 = 3.5\), \(+4.2 = 7.7\), \(+1.5 = 9.2\), \(+1.3 = 10.5\), \(+3.6 = 14.1\), \(+0.5 = 14.6\). So \(\bar d = 14.6/8 = 1.825\) s.
  3. Sum of squares. \(0.8^2 = 0.64\), \(2.2^2 = 4.84\), \(0.5^2 = 0.25\), \(4.2^2 = 17.64\), \(1.5^2 = 2.25\), \(1.3^2 = 1.69\), \(3.6^2 = 12.96\), \(0.5^2 = 0.25\). These add to 40.52.
  4. Plug into the variance. \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{40.52 - 8(1.825)^2}{7}\). First \(1.825^2 = 3.3306\), then \(8 \times 3.3306 = 26.645\), then \(40.52 - 26.645 = 13.875\), then \(13.875/7 = 1.982\). So \(s_d = \sqrt{1.982} = 1.408\).
  5. Standard error and t. \(\sqrt8 = 2.828\), so SE \(= 1.408/2.828 = 0.498\). Then \(t = 1.825/0.498 = 3.67\) with \(8 - 1 = 7\) df. The table at row 7, column 0.025 gives 2.365, and 3.67 is past it.
  6. Interval. Half-width \(= 2.365 \times 0.498 = 1.18\). Lower end \(1.825 - 1.18 = 0.65\), upper end \(1.825 + 1.18 = 3.00\).
  7. Result. (0.65, 3.00) s. B is slower by 0.65 to 3.0 s on benchmarks averaging about 30 s, so roughly 2% to 10%.

The same numbers analyzed unpaired, to see the damage. The formula is the Welch one, \(s = \sqrt{s_A^2/n_A + s_B^2/n_B}\), where \(s_A, s_B\) are the standard deviations of each system's raw times and \(n_A = n_B = 8\). The lecture's raw SDs are 21.51 and 22.90.

  1. Plug in. \(s = \sqrt{21.51^2/8 + 22.90^2/8} = \sqrt{(21.51^2 + 22.90^2)/8}\).
  2. Work it out. \(21.51^2 = 462.7\) and \(22.90^2 = 524.4\). They add to 987.1, and \(987.1/8 = 123.4\). Then \(\sqrt{123.4} = 11.1\) s. So \(t = 1.825/11.1 = 0.16\). With about 14 df (row 14, column 0.025 gives 2.145), the half-width is \(2.145 \times 11.1 = 23.8\), and \(1.825 - 23.8 = -22.0\), \(1.825 + 23.8 = 25.6\). The slide prints +25.7 because it carries more decimals.
  3. Result. Same estimate, but the interval is about 20 times wider and contains 0. The between-benchmark spread is now in the denominator.

The lecture's cache example (Comp p.11, study guide question 1). Six workloads, \(\bar d = 0.020\), \(s_d = 0.0743\).

  1. Formula. \(\bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n-1]}\, s_d/\sqrt n\), with \(n = 6\) pairs, so df \(= 5\).
  2. Plug in. 90% has \(\alpha/2 = 0.05\) and 95% has \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\). So the two intervals are \(0.020 \pm t_{[0.05,\,5]}\,(0.0743/\sqrt6)\) and \(0.020 \pm t_{[0.025,\,5]}\,(0.0743/\sqrt6)\).
  3. Work it out. \(\sqrt6 = 2.449\), so SE \(= 0.0743/2.449 = 0.0303\). Table row 5, column 0.05 gives 2.015, so the 90% half-width is \(2.015 \times 0.0303 = 0.061\), and \(0.020 - 0.061 = -0.041\), \(0.020 + 0.061 = 0.081\). Table row 5, column 0.025 gives 2.571, so the 95% half-width is \(2.571 \times 0.0303 = 0.078\), and \(0.020 - 0.078 = -0.058\), \(0.020 + 0.078 = 0.098\).
  4. Result. 90% gives (−0.041, 0.081) and 95% gives (−0.058, 0.098). Both contain 0, so no difference is shown. The 95% one is wider because you asked for more confidence. The width is the real finding, since six workloads just can't pin this down.

HW6 Problem 1. The same workload went through both routing algorithms, so it's paired. Delays and differences \(d_i = A_i - B_i\) are

i123456789
A115.089.3102.194.3117.3120.398.987.6104.5
B125.397.685.3101.3112.787.9104.296.3113.2
d−10.3−8.316.8−7.04.632.4−5.3−8.7−8.7
  1. Formulas. \(\bar d = \sum d_i/n\), \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2}{n-1}\), interval \(\bar d \pm t_{[0.025,\,n-1]}\, s_d/\sqrt n\). Here \(n = 9\) pairs, so df \(= 8\).
  2. Mean. Keep a running sum of the d row, −10.3, then −18.6, −1.8, −8.8, −4.2, 28.2, 22.9, 14.2, 5.5. So \(\sum d_i = 5.5\) and \(\bar d = 5.5/9 = 0.611\).
  3. Sum of squares. \(10.3^2 = 106.09\), \(8.3^2 = 68.89\), \(16.8^2 = 282.24\), \(7.0^2 = 49.00\), \(4.6^2 = 21.16\), \(32.4^2 = 1049.76\), \(5.3^2 = 28.09\), \(8.7^2 = 75.69\), \(8.7^2 = 75.69\). They add to \(\sum d_i^2 = 1756.61\).
  4. Plug into the variance. \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{1756.61 - 9(0.611)^2}{8}\). Now \(0.611^2 = 0.3733\) and \(9 \times 0.3733 = 3.36\). Then \(1756.61 - 3.36 = 1753.25\) and \(1753.25/8 = 219.16\). So \(s_d = \sqrt{219.16} = 14.804\).
  5. Standard error. \(14.804/\sqrt9 = 14.804/3 = 4.935\).
  6. Table and half-width. Row 8, column 0.025 gives \(t_{[0.025,\,8]} = 2.306\). Half-width \(= 2.306 \times 4.935 = 11.38\).
  7. Interval. \(0.611 - 11.38 = -10.77\) and \(0.611 + 11.38 = 11.99\).
  8. Result. (−10.77, 11.99) ms. Zero is inside, so you can't say at 95% confidence that A has lower delay. Seven of nine workloads favor A, but workloads 3 and 6 favor B by a lot, and that spread makes the interval wide.

Easy to get wrong

  • Degrees of freedom are pairs minus 1. HW6 Problem 1 has 18 numbers but 8 df, not 16.
  • Write down which way you subtracted. With \(d = A - B\) on a delay, negative means A is better. Flip it and the interval flips sign.
  • "Contains 0" means "not shown", not "equal". The HW6 interval is compatible with A being 10 ms better and with B being 12 ms better.
  • Don't run the paired formula on HW6 Problem 2 just because you could line up the first 24 rows. There's no shared unit.

The unpaired interval with Welch degrees of freedom

The idea

When each system is measured on its own set of units, you can't subtract row by row. Instead you subtract the two sample means and ask how uncertain that difference is. Both means carry their own error, and those errors add in variance, so the standard deviation of the difference combines both samples (Comp p.12).

The catch is the degrees of freedom. With two variances that differ, there's no exact t distribution, so Welch's method computes an effective \(\nu\) from the data. It's usually fractional, and you either use it as is or round down before reading the t table.

What to know

From Comp p.12,

\[ s = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{n_B}}, \qquad (\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,\nu]}\, s, \qquad t = \frac{\bar x_A - \bar x_B}{s}, \] \[ \nu = \frac{\left(\dfrac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \dfrac{s_B^2}{n_B}\right)^2}{\dfrac{(s_A^2/n_A)^2}{n_A-1} + \dfrac{(s_B^2/n_B)^2}{n_B-1}}. \]

Facts about \(\nu\) from Comp p.12. It lies between \(\min(n_A,n_B)-1\) and \(n_A+n_B-2\), closer to the low end when the variances differ. Use it as is or round down, and never just use \(n_A - 1\). Rounding down gives a slightly larger t and so a slightly wider, more conservative interval, which is the safe direction with a printed table.

The hand procedure that avoids mistakes is to compute \(a = s_A^2/n_A\) and \(b = s_B^2/n_B\) once, then \(s = \sqrt{a+b}\) and \(\nu = (a+b)^2 / \big(a^2/(n_A-1) + b^2/(n_B-1)\big)\).

The lecture's example (Comp p.14) has \(n_A = 7\), \(\bar x_A = 0.2657\), \(s_A = 0.0934\) and \(n_B = 9\), \(\bar x_B = 0.4011\), \(s_B = 0.1447\). That gives \(s = 0.0598\), \(\nu = 13.6\), \(t_{[0.05,\,13.6]} = 1.765\) and the 90% interval (−0.241, −0.029). Zero is outside, so B has the higher hit ratio. The Welch statistic is \(t = -0.1354/0.0598 = -2.26\). At 95% the study guide's \(t_{[0.025,\,13.6]} = 2.150\) gives \(-0.135 \pm 0.129 = (-0.264,\ -0.007)\), which still excludes 0 but only just (study guide, answer 4). The size of the effect is 3 to 24 hit-ratio points, a wide range you should say out loud (Comp p.14).

Worked example

Small Welch example. System A on 5 independent runs gives 8, 8, 10, 12, 12 ms. System B on 6 other runs gives 9, 12, 15, 15, 18, 21 ms. Is there a difference at 95%?

  1. Formulas. \(a = s_A^2/n_A\) and \(b = s_B^2/n_B\), where \(s_A^2, s_B^2\) are the two sample variances and \(n_A, n_B\) the two sample sizes. Then \(s = \sqrt{a+b}\) is the standard error of the difference of means, \(\nu = \dfrac{(a+b)^2}{a^2/(n_A-1) + b^2/(n_B-1)}\) is the Welch degrees of freedom, and the interval is \((\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[0.025,\,\nu]}\, s\).
  2. Means. A adds to \(8+8+10+12+12 = 50\), so \(\bar x_A = 50/5 = 10\). B adds to \(9+12+15+15+18+21 = 90\), so \(\bar x_B = 90/6 = 15\). The estimate is \(10 - 15 = -5\).
  3. Variances. A's deviations from 10 are −2, −2, 0, 2, 2. Squares 4, 4, 0, 4, 4 add to 16, and \(n_A - 1 = 4\), so \(s_A^2 = 16/4 = 4\). B's deviations from 15 are −6, −3, 0, 0, 3, 6. Squares 36, 9, 0, 0, 9, 36 add to 90, and \(n_B - 1 = 5\), so \(s_B^2 = 90/5 = 18\).
  4. Plug into a, b, s. \(a = 4/5 = 0.8\) and \(b = 18/6 = 3.0\). So \(s = \sqrt{0.8 + 3.0} = \sqrt{3.8} = 1.949\).
  5. Plug into \(\nu\). \(\nu = \dfrac{(0.8 + 3.0)^2}{0.8^2/4 + 3.0^2/5}\). Numerator \(3.8^2 = 14.44\). Denominator \(0.64/4 + 9/5 = 0.16 + 1.80 = 1.96\). So \(\nu = 14.44/1.96 = 7.37\).
  6. Sanity check. \(\nu\) must sit between \(\min(5,6)-1 = 4\) and \(5+6-2 = 9\). 7.37 does.
  7. Table. Round 7.37 down to 7. Row 7, column 0.025 gives 2.365.
  8. Interval. Half-width \(= 2.365 \times 1.949 = 4.61\). Then \(-5 - 4.61 = -9.61\) and \(-5 + 4.61 = -0.39\).
  9. Result. (−9.61, −0.39) ms. Zero is outside, so A is faster by between about 0.4 and 9.6 ms. Say the range is wide, which is the honest reading of 11 runs.

The paired benchmarks analyzed the wrong way. Go back to the five benchmarks from the first topic and pretend they were unpaired. Use the same Welch formulas with \(s_A^2 = 152.7\), \(s_B^2 = 117.7\), \(n_A = n_B = 5\), and estimate \(20.8 - 17.8 = 3\).

  1. Plug in. \(a = 152.7/5\), \(b = 117.7/5\), \(\nu = (a+b)^2/\big(a^2/4 + b^2/4\big)\).
  2. Work it out. \(a = 30.54\) and \(b = 23.54\). Their sum is 54.08, so \(s = \sqrt{54.08} = 7.354\). For \(\nu\), the numerator is \(54.08^2 = 2924.6\). The denominator is \((30.54^2 + 23.54^2)/4 = (932.7 + 554.1)/4 = 1486.8/4 = 371.7\). So \(\nu = 2924.6/371.7 = 7.87\), rounded down to 7, and the table gives 2.365. Half-width \(= 2.365 \times 7.354 = 17.39\). Then \(3 - 17.39 = -14.39\) and \(3 + 17.39 = 20.39\).
  3. Result. (−14.39, 20.39). The paired interval was (1.04, 4.96). Same data, roughly nine times wider, and the opposite verdict.

HW6 Problem 2. The summary is \(n_A = 30\), \(\bar x_A = 1.8830\), \(s_A = 1.9017\) and \(n_B = 24\), \(\bar x_B = 2.2623\), \(s_B = 2.9108\). The question asks for 90%, so you need \(t_{[0.05,\,\nu]}\).

  1. Plug into a and b. \(a = 1.9017^2/30\) and \(b = 2.9108^2/24\). Since \(1.9017^2 = 3.6165\) and \(2.9108^2 = 8.4728\), you get \(a = 3.6165/30 = 0.12055\) and \(b = 8.4728/24 = 0.35303\).
  2. Standard error. \(a + b = 0.47358\), so \(s = \sqrt{0.47358} = 0.6882\).
  3. Plug into \(\nu\). \(\nu = \dfrac{0.47358^2}{0.12055^2/29 + 0.35303^2/23}\). Numerator \(= 0.22428\). The first denominator piece is \(0.014532/29 = 0.000501\) and the second is \(0.124630/23 = 0.005419\). They add to 0.005920. So \(\nu = 0.22428/0.005920 = 37.89\).
  4. Table. Round down to 37. Row 37, column 0.05 gives about 1.687 (software at 37.89 gives 1.686). If your printed table jumps from 30 to 40, use row 30, which gives 1.697 and a slightly wider interval.
  5. Interval. Estimate \(1.8830 - 2.2623 = -0.3793\). Half-width \(= 1.686 \times 0.6882 = 1.160\). Then \(-0.3793 - 1.160 = -1.540\) and \(-0.3793 + 1.160 = 0.781\).
  6. Result. (−1.540, 0.781) s. Zero is inside, so A isn't shown to be faster at 90%.

Easy to get wrong

  • Squares in the \(\nu\) denominator. It's \((s_A^2/n_A)^2\), the square of a variance-over-n, not \(s_A^2\) again.
  • The denominators inside \(\nu\) are \(n_A - 1\) and \(n_B - 1\), while the ones inside \(s\) are \(n_A\) and \(n_B\). Mixing them up is the most common slip.
  • A 90% two-sided interval uses \(t_{[0.05,\,\nu]}\), not \(t_{[0.10,\,\nu]}\). Half of \(\alpha\) goes in each tail.
  • If your table doesn't have \(\nu = 37\), use the next smaller row. Never jump up to a larger df, since that shrinks t.
  • s here is the standard deviation of the difference of means, already divided by n. Don't divide by \(\sqrt n\) again.

Pooled t or Welch

The idea

The older unpaired test assumes both populations have the same variance and pools the two sample variances into one estimate. That buys a few degrees of freedom. Welch drops the assumption and pays a little in df. The lecture's position is that Welch should be your default, chosen before you see the data, because it loses almost nothing when variances really are equal and protects the error rate when they aren't (Comp p.13).

The real danger is running both and reporting the one that clears 0.05. On the lecture's unpaired example the pooled test gives p = 0.0501 and Welch gives p = 0.0404, which are opposite verdicts at \(\alpha = 0.05\). Picking after the fact is p-hacking (Comp p.13).

What to know

The pooled version, from the study guide's formula sheet, is

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{(n_A-1)s_A^2 + (n_B-1)s_B^2}{n_A+n_B-2}, \qquad (\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n_A+n_B-2]}\; s_p\sqrt{\frac1{n_A} + \frac1{n_B}}. \]
Pooled tWelch t
Assumes\(\sigma_A^2 = \sigma_B^2\)Nothing about the variances
df\(n_A+n_B-2\)\(\nu\) from the formula
Old rulePool if one sample variance is under about 4 times the other
Current adviceWelch by default, decided in advance (Comp p.13). The source is Delacre, Lakens and Leys (2017), listed in the readings (Comp p.30)

Worked example

Use the Welch data from the last topic (\(n_A = 5\), \(s_A^2 = 4\), \(n_B = 6\), \(s_B^2 = 18\)).

  1. Formula. \(s_p^2 = \dfrac{(n_A-1)s_A^2 + (n_B-1)s_B^2}{n_A+n_B-2}\) is the pooled variance, a weighted average of the two sample variances with weights \(n-1\). The interval is \((\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[0.025,\,n_A+n_B-2]}\; s_p\sqrt{1/n_A + 1/n_B}\).
  2. Plug in. \(s_p^2 = \dfrac{(5-1)(4) + (6-1)(18)}{5+6-2} = \dfrac{4(4) + 5(18)}{9}\).
  3. Work it out. \(4 \times 4 = 16\) and \(5 \times 18 = 90\). They add to 106, and \(106/9 = 11.78\). So \(s_p = \sqrt{11.78} = 3.432\).
  4. Standard error. \(1/5 + 1/6 = 6/30 + 5/30 = 11/30 = 0.3667\), and \(\sqrt{0.3667} = 0.6055\). So SE \(= 3.432 \times 0.6055 = 2.078\).
  5. Table and interval. df \(= 5 + 6 - 2 = 9\). Row 9, column 0.025 gives 2.262. Half-width \(= 2.262 \times 2.078 = 4.70\). Then \(-5 - 4.70 = -9.70\) and \(-5 + 4.70 = -0.30\).
  6. Result. Pooled gives (−9.70, −0.30) ms. Welch gave (−9.61, −0.39).

Here they agree, but B's variance is 4.5 times A's, past the old rule's line, and the small group has the small variance. That makes the pooled test too conservative here (its interval is the wider one). With the variances the other way round, the small group having the big variance, it rejects too often. Either way its error rate isn't what it claims, which is why you commit to Welch up front.

The lecture's example pooled (Comp p.13-14, study guide question 5). With \(n_A = 7\), \(s_A = 0.0934\), \(n_B = 9\), \(s_B = 0.1447\),

  1. Plug in. \(s_p^2 = \dfrac{(7-1)(0.0934)^2 + (9-1)(0.1447)^2}{7+9-2} = \dfrac{6(0.0934)^2 + 8(0.1447)^2}{14}\).
  2. Work it out. \(0.0934^2 = 0.008724\), times 6 is 0.0523. \(0.1447^2 = 0.020938\), times 8 is 0.1675. Sum \(0.0523 + 0.1675 = 0.2198\), and \(0.2198/14 = 0.0157\). So \(s_p = \sqrt{0.0157} = 0.1253\).
  3. Standard error. \(1/7 + 1/9 = 9/63 + 7/63 = 16/63 = 0.254\), and \(\sqrt{0.254} = 0.504\). SE \(= 0.1253 \times 0.504 = 0.0632\).
  4. Test statistic. \(t = (\bar x_A - \bar x_B)/\text{SE} = (0.2657 - 0.4011)/0.0632 = -0.1354/0.0632 = -2.14\), with df \(= 7 + 9 - 2 = 14\).
  5. Result. Pooled gives \(t = -2.14\) on 14 df. Welch gave \(t = -2.26\) on 13.6 df.

Those are the p = 0.0501 and p = 0.0404 on the slide. You report Welch because it makes the weaker assumption, and the choice should have been made before either number existed.

Easy to get wrong

  • Pre-testing for equal variances and then choosing is a two-stage procedure with worse error control than always using Welch (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 19c).
  • The pooled weights are \(n-1\), not n, and the divisor is \(n_A+n_B-2\), the same number as the df.
  • Unequal sample sizes are fine for Welch, but ask why one side has fewer runs before trusting anything (Comp p.14).
  • A paper that writes "Student's t test assuming equal variances" with unequal group sizes and spreads is showing a red flag (Wild handout, reference table).

Do the error bars overlap?

The idea

Papers often plot each system's mean with its own interval and let the reader judge by eye. That picture answers a question about two systems, but your claim is about their difference (Comp p.15). The eyeball rule only works in one direction.

If the two intervals don't overlap, the systems differ at that level, and you can say so. If they overlap a little, they may still differ significantly, and you have to do the real test. If each mean sits inside the other's interval, there's no evidence of a difference, and that still isn't evidence they're equal (Comp p.15).

The reason overlap misleads is arithmetic. Two separate intervals have half-widths proportional to \(s_A/\sqrt{n_A}\) and \(s_B/\sqrt{n_B}\). For the bars to overlap the means can be up to the sum of those apart. But the interval on the difference uses \(\sqrt{s_A^2/n_A + s_B^2/n_B}\), which is always smaller than that sum, because the square root of a sum of squares is less than the sum. So the difference can exclude 0 while the bars still touch.

What to know

  • Non-overlap implies significant. Overlap implies nothing (Comp p.15).
  • For 95% intervals, an overlap of up to about half a margin of error can still be significant at 0.05 (Comp p.15).
  • The lecture's example (Comp p.16) has A's 90% interval (0.197, 0.334) and B's (0.311, 0.491). They overlap between 0.311 and 0.334, neither mean is inside the other interval, and the interval on the difference (−0.241, −0.029) excludes 0. Eyeballing says "no difference", the correct analysis says otherwise.
  • An error bar is meaningless unless the caption says what it is. A standard deviation, a standard error and a 95% interval differ by about a factor of \(\sqrt n\) and then another factor of about 2 (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 20). SD describes the spread of the data, SE and CI describe how precisely the mean is known.
  • The fix is to plot the difference and its interval. One picture, the right question (Comp p.16).

Worked example

Take the small Welch data (A = 8, 8, 10, 12, 12 and B = 9, 12, 15, 15, 18, 21) and draw individual 95% intervals.

  1. Formula. One system's own interval is \(\bar x \pm t_{[0.025,\,n-1]}\, s/\sqrt n\), where \(\bar x\) is that system's mean, \(s\) its standard deviation and \(n\) its number of runs.
  2. Plug in for A. \(\bar x_A = 10\), \(s_A = \sqrt4 = 2\), \(n_A = 5\), so \(10 \pm t_{[0.025,\,4]}\,(2/\sqrt5)\).
  3. Work it out for A. \(\sqrt5 = 2.236\), so SE \(= 2/2.236 = 0.894\). Row 4, column 0.025 gives 2.776. Half-width \(= 2.776 \times 0.894 = 2.48\). Then \(10 - 2.48 = 7.52\) and \(10 + 2.48 = 12.48\).
  4. Plug in and work it out for B. \(\bar x_B = 15\), \(s_B = \sqrt{18} = 4.243\), \(n_B = 6\). \(\sqrt6 = 2.449\), so SE \(= 4.243/2.449 = 1.732\). Row 5, column 0.025 gives 2.571. Half-width \(= 2.571 \times 1.732 = 4.45\). Then \(15 - 4.45 = 10.55\) and \(15 + 4.45 = 19.45\).
  5. Result. A's interval is (7.52, 12.48) and B's is (10.55, 19.45).

The bars overlap from 10.55 to 12.48. A's mean 10 isn't inside B's interval and B's mean 15 isn't inside A's, so this is case 2, "must do the test". The Welch interval on the difference was (−9.61, −0.39), which excludes 0. So the systems do differ, even though the bars overlap.

Notice why. Eyeballing overlap acts as if the two standard errors simply add, \(0.894 + 1.732 = 2.626\). The real standard error of the difference is \(\sqrt{\text{SE}_A^2 + \text{SE}_B^2}\). Plug in \(\sqrt{0.894^2 + 1.732^2}\). Since \(0.894^2 = 0.8\) and \(1.732^2 = 3.0\), that's \(\sqrt{3.8} = 1.949\), noticeably smaller than 2.626. So the difference can clear 0 while the bars still touch.

Easy to get wrong

  • "The error bars overlap, so the difference isn't significant" is false. This is the exact sentence the study guide asks you to criticize.
  • Heavy overlap means "no evidence", not "the systems are the same".
  • If the data are paired, separate intervals per system are even more misleading, since they include the between-unit spread that pairing would cancel.

Comparing two proportions

The idea

Sometimes the response is a yes or no per unit, such as a cache hit, a passed test question or a crash. Then each system has a proportion, and you want an interval on the difference of proportions. The Comp slides don't have a slide on this, so treat it as lower priority. It's built from the single-proportion interval in the CI deck (CI p.47-48) and the textbook's two-alternatives chapter (Lilja Ch. 5), and the comparing-alternatives study guide uses it in practice question 16.

The same paired-or-unpaired question applies. If both systems answered the same 400 questions, it's paired, and you can score each question as +1, 0 or −1 and run the paired interval on those numbers.

What to know

Single proportion, from CI p.48, with \(p\) the sample proportion and n large enough for the normal approximation,

\[ p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}. \]

The CI deck writes \(z_{1-\alpha/2}\) as a quantile, while Comp writes \(z[\alpha/2]\) as an upper-tail value. They're the same number, 1.960 for 95% and 1.645 for 90%.

Two independent proportions (unpaired). Variances add, just like Welch,

\[ (p_1 - p_2) \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p_1(1-p_1)}{n_1} + \frac{p_2(1-p_2)}{n_2}}. \]

Paired proportions. Score each unit \(d_i = +1\) if only A succeeded, \(-1\) if only B did, and 0 if they agreed. Then use \(\bar d \pm z\,s_d/\sqrt n\). Only the disagreeing units carry information, which is the idea behind McNemar's test (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 16).

Worked example

Unpaired. Model A gets 87% and model B gets 89% of 400 questions each, treated as independent samples.

  1. Formula. \((p_1 - p_2) \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\dfrac{p_1(1-p_1)}{n_1} + \dfrac{p_2(1-p_2)}{n_2}}\). Here \(p_1, p_2\) are the two observed proportions, \(n_1, n_2\) the number of trials behind each, and \(z_{1-\alpha/2}\) the normal-table value (1.96 for 95%).
  2. Plug in. \((0.87 - 0.89) \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{0.87 \times 0.13}{400} + \dfrac{0.89 \times 0.11}{400}}\).
  3. Work it out. \(0.87 \times 0.13 = 0.1131\), and \(0.1131/400 = 0.000283\). \(0.89 \times 0.11 = 0.0979\), and \(0.0979/400 = 0.000245\). Sum \(= 0.000528\). Square root \(= 0.0230\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.0230 = 0.045\). Estimate \(0.87 - 0.89 = -0.02\). Then \(-0.02 - 0.045 = -0.065\) and \(-0.02 + 0.045 = 0.025\).
  4. Result. (−0.065, 0.025). It contains 0, so treated as independent, the two models aren't shown to differ.

Paired, same data. Suppose both models got 344 questions right, only A got 4, only B got 12, and 40 were missed by both. Check, A has \(344 + 4 = 348\) right and \(348/400 = 87\%\), and B has \(344 + 12 = 356\) right and \(356/400 = 89\%\).

  1. Formulas. Score each question \(d_i = +1\) if only A got it, \(-1\) if only B got it, 0 otherwise. Then \(\bar d = \sum d_i/n\), \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2}{n-1}\), and the interval is \(\bar d \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\, s_d/\sqrt n\) with \(n = 400\) questions.
  2. Mean. The d values are four +1s, twelve −1s and 384 zeros. \(\sum d_i = 4 - 12 = -8\), so \(\bar d = -8/400 = -0.02\).
  3. Sum of squares. Each +1 and each −1 squares to 1, and zeros square to 0. So \(\sum d_i^2 = 4 + 12 = 16\).
  4. Plug into the variance. \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{16 - 400(0.02)^2}{399}\). Now \(0.02^2 = 0.0004\) and \(400 \times 0.0004 = 0.16\). Then \(16 - 0.16 = 15.84\) and \(15.84/399 = 0.0397\). So \(s_d = \sqrt{0.0397} = 0.199\).
  5. Interval. \(\sqrt{400} = 20\), so SE \(= 0.199/20 = 0.00996\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.00996 = 0.0195\). Then \(-0.02 - 0.0195 = -0.0395\) and \(-0.02 + 0.0195 = -0.0005\).
  6. Result. (−0.0395, −0.0005). It just excludes 0, so B is better. Pairing cut the half-width from 0.045 to 0.020, because the models agree on most questions and only the 16 disagreements carry information.

Easy to get wrong

  • Use z, not t, for proportions. The variance comes from the binomial formula, not from a sample standard deviation.
  • The normal approximation needs enough successes and failures in each group. With a handful of events, don't trust it.
  • Two leaderboard percentages from the same test set are paired. Comparing them as independent throws away the agreement.

Rank-based tests when normality fails

The idea

Runtimes and latencies are skewed and have outliers, and t intervals assume roughly normal data (the differences, for paired designs). The lecture gives four ways out, which are to analyze \(\log x\), use a rank-based test, bootstrap an interval, or compare the tail statistic directly if the claim is about p99 (Comp p.18). All of these must be chosen before you look at the results.

Rank tests replace values with their ranks, so one huge observation counts as "the largest" and nothing more. Each design has its own rank test. The decision chart on Comp p.17 is the one to memorize.

What to know

Design and dataUse
Paired, differences roughly normalPaired t on the differences
Paired, outliers but symmetricWilcoxon signed-rank
Paired, skewed differencesSign test or bootstrap
Unpaired, roughly normalWelch
Unpaired, skewed or ordinalMann-Whitney U, or bootstrap

Source is Comp p.17.

Mann-Whitney U (unpaired, also called Wilcoxon rank-sum, Comp p.19). Rank all \(n_1 + n_2\) values together, ties get the average of their ranks. With \(W_1\) the rank sum of sample 1,

\[ U_1 = W_1 - \frac{n_1(n_1+1)}{2}, \quad \mu_U = \frac{n_1 n_2}{2}, \quad \sigma_U^2 = \frac{n_1 n_2 (n_1+n_2+1)}{12}, \quad Z = \frac{U_1 - \mu_U}{\sigma_U}. \]

The normal approximation is fine once both samples exceed about 8. The null is that the populations are identical, \(P(X > Y) = 1/2\), not that the medians are equal. Report \(A_{12} = U_1/(n_1 n_2)\), the probability a random value from sample 1 beats one from sample 2.

Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired, Comp p.21). Drop zero differences, rank \(|d_i|\) from smallest, and sum the ranks of the positive and the negative ones. Then \(W^+ + W^- = n(n+1)/2\) and \(W = \min(W^+, W^-)\). For small n compute the exact p from all \(2^n\) sign patterns. Above about 20 pairs use \(\mu = n(n+1)/4\), \(\sigma^2 = n(n+1)(2n+1)/24\). Effect size is \(r = (W^+ - W^-)/(n(n+1)/2)\). It's robust to outliers but assumes symmetric differences, so it isn't safe under skew.

On the cache data the normal approximation gives \(\mu = 6(7)/4 = 10.5\), \(\sigma^2 = 6(7)(13)/24 = 22.75\), \(\sigma = 4.77\), and \(Z = (6 - 10.5)/4.77 = -0.94\) with p = 0.35. The exact p is 0.44 (14 of the 64 sign patterns give \(W^- \le 6\), so \(2 \times 14/64\)). The approximation is too rough at n = 6, so report the exact p (Comp p.22). The slide also gives the Hodges-Lehmann estimate of the shift, +0.02, as the rank test's companion to \(\bar d\). At n = 6 the smallest possible two-sided p is \(2/64 = 0.031\) (study guide, answer 8).

Sign test (paired, Comp p.23). Count the positive differences S among the n nonzero ones. Under the null \(S \sim \text{Binomial}(n, 1/2)\), and for large n, \(z = (S - n/2)/(\sqrt n/2)\). No symmetry assumption, but it's the least powerful because it ignores sizes. On the cache data S = 3 of 6, the most likely outcome under the null, so p = 1.00. That's far above the signed-rank's 0.44 because the three positive differences happen to be the three largest, which only the signed-rank sees (study guide, answer 9).

Transform before you rank (Comp p.18). Runtime skew is usually multiplicative, so take \(\log(A_i/B_i)\) per unit. Logs turn ratios into differences and are often close to symmetric, so a paired t (or signed-rank) on them is fine, and the result back-transforms into a geometric-mean speedup with an interval (study guide, answer 18). The slide also notes that t tests are fairly robust with equal-sized samples. The deeper question is whether the mean is the quantity your claim is about. If the claim is about p99, compare p99s (Comp p.18, p.27).

Software (Comp p.10). scipy's ttest_rel is the paired t, ttest_ind with equal_var=False is Welch, wilcoxon is signed-rank and mannwhitneyu is rank-sum. None of them returns a confidence interval by default, so you compute it or use stats.bootstrap, which gives an interval for any statistic, including a difference of medians or of p99s. In R, t.test(a, b, paired=TRUE) and wilcox.test() run the same tests and do print the interval. The slide's point is that you need the arithmetic to read the output, not to do it by hand in practice.

Readings (Comp p.30) are Jain Ch. 13 on paired and unpaired comparisons, Lilja Ch. 5 on comparing two alternatives, Delacre, Lakens and Leys (2017) on Welch by default, Cumming and Finch (2005) on reading pictures of data, and the scipy.stats docs. The slide's closing note is that Mann-Whitney U and the Wilcoxon rank-sum test are the same test, while the signed-rank test is a different one.

With 5 or fewer pairs, neither the signed-rank nor the sign test can reach p < 0.05 two-sided, since the smallest possible p is \(2/32 = 0.0625\) (Comp p.22-23).

Worked example

Mann-Whitney by hand. A gives 3.1, 4.5, 2.8, 6.0 and B gives 5.2, 7.1, 4.5, 8.3, 6.6. Sort all nine.

Value2.83.14.54.55.26.06.67.18.3
SampleAAABBABBB
Rank123.53.556789
  1. Formulas. \(U_1 = W_1 - \dfrac{n_1(n_1+1)}{2}\) and \(A_{12} = U_1/(n_1 n_2)\). Here \(W_1\) is the sum of sample 1's ranks in the combined sorted list, \(n_1\) and \(n_2\) are the two sample sizes, \(U_1\) counts the (A, B) pairs where A is larger (ties count half), and \(A_{12}\) turns that count into a probability.
  2. Ranks. The tied 4.5s would get ranks 3 and 4, so each gets the average \((3+4)/2 = 3.5\). A's ranks are 1, 2, 3.5 and 6.
  3. Plug in. \(W_A = 1 + 2 + 3.5 + 6\), \(n_1 = 4\), \(n_2 = 5\), so \(U_A = W_A - \dfrac{4 \times 5}{2}\) and \(A_{12} = U_A/(4 \times 5)\).
  4. Work it out. \(W_A = 12.5\). \(4 \times 5/2 = 10\). \(U_A = 12.5 - 10 = 2.5\). \(A_{12} = 2.5/20 = 0.125\).
  5. Check by counting. 6.0 beats B's 5.2 and 4.5, which is 2. A's 4.5 ties B's 4.5, which counts 0.5. Nothing else in A beats anything in B. Total 2.5, matching \(U_A\).
  6. Result. \(U_A = 2.5\) and \(A_{12} = 0.125\), meaning a random A value beats a random B value only 12.5% of the time. With samples this small, don't use the Z approximation.

Mann-Whitney with Z (the lecture's crash data, Comp p.20). \(n_1 = 15\), \(n_2 = 14\), \(W_1 = 162\).

  1. Formulas. \(U_1 = W_1 - n_1(n_1+1)/2\). Under "no difference" \(U_1\) has mean \(\mu_U = n_1 n_2/2\) and variance \(\sigma_U^2 = n_1 n_2(n_1+n_2+1)/12\). The test statistic is \(Z = (U_1 - \mu_U)/\sigma_U\), how many standard deviations \(U_1\) sits from where it would be by chance.
  2. Plug in. \(U_1 = 162 - \dfrac{15 \times 16}{2}\), \(\mu_U = \dfrac{15 \times 14}{2}\), \(\sigma_U^2 = \dfrac{15 \times 14 \times (15+14+1)}{12}\).
  3. Work it out. \(15 \times 16 = 240\), half is 120, so \(U_1 = 162 - 120 = 42\). \(15 \times 14 = 210\), half is 105, so \(\mu_U = 105\). \(210 \times 30 = 6300\) and \(6300/12 = 525\), so \(\sigma_U = \sqrt{525} = 22.91\). Then \(Z = (42 - 105)/22.91 = -63/22.91 = -2.75\).
  4. Table. For \(\alpha = 0.01\) two-sided, the normal table's cutoff is 2.576. \(|-2.75| = 2.75 > 2.576\), so reject.
  5. Result. The two systems differ at the 0.01 level. \(A_{12} = 42/210 = 0.20\), so System II runs longer between crashes four times out of five.

The medians, 0.43 against 0.885 weeks, say the same thing in the response's units, about a factor of two. Put both in the paper, because the p-value only says "different" (Comp p.20).

Signed-rank and sign test on the five benchmarks. The differences are 2, 4, 3, 5, 1, all positive.

  1. Formulas. Rank \(|d_i|\) from smallest (rank 1) to largest. \(W^+\) is the sum of ranks of positive differences and \(W^-\) the sum for negative ones, with \(W^+ + W^- = n(n+1)/2\). The exact two-sided p is \(2 \times \dfrac{\#\{\text{sign patterns at least this extreme}\}}{2^n}\), because under "no difference" each of the \(2^n\) ways to attach signs to the ranks is equally likely.
  2. Ranks. Sorted, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 get ranks 1 to 5, so the ranks are just the values.
  3. Plug in. \(W^+ = 1+2+3+4+5\), \(W^- = 0\) (no negatives), check sum \(5 \times 6/2\). There are \(2^5 = 32\) sign patterns.
  4. Work it out. \(W^+ = 15\), \(W^- = 0\), and \(5 \times 6/2 = 15\) matches. Only the all-plus pattern gives \(W^- = 0\), so one tail has probability \(1/32\). Two-sided p \(= 2 \times 1/32 = 2/32 = 0.0625\).
  5. Sign test, same data. S = 5 positives out of 5. Under the null \(S \sim \text{Binomial}(5, 1/2)\), so \(P(S = 5) = (1/2)^5 = 1/32\). Two-sided p \(= 2/32 = 0.0625\) again.
  6. Result. p = 0.0625 for both, which is above 0.05, so neither rejects. The paired t gave t = 4.24 with 4 df, well past 2.776. With five pairs, rank tests can't detect anything at 0.05 no matter how clear the data are.

Signed-rank on the lecture's cache data (Comp p.22). Six differences.

  1. Formulas. Same as above, plus the effect size \(r = (W^+ - W^-)/(n(n+1)/2)\), which runs from −1 (all negative) to +1 (all positive).
  2. Ranks. Sorted by size, ignoring sign, the differences are −0.03, −0.05, −0.06, +0.07, +0.09, +0.10, so they get ranks 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6.
  3. Plug in. \(W^+ = 4+5+6\), \(W^- = 1+2+3\), \(r = (W^+ - W^-)/(6 \times 7/2)\).
  4. Work it out. \(W^+ = 15\), \(W^- = 6\). Check, \(15 + 6 = 21\) and \(6 \times 7/2 = 21\). Then \(r = (15 - 6)/21 = 9/21 = 0.43\). For the exact p, count sign patterns of ranks 1 to 6 whose negative ranks sum to 6 or less. There are 14 of the \(2^6 = 64\), so p \(= 2 \times 14/64 = 28/64 = 0.4375\).
  5. Result. p ≈ 0.44, so no difference is shown. That agrees with the paired t's p = 0.54.

Easy to get wrong

  • Signed-rank is the paired test. Rank-sum (Mann-Whitney) is the unpaired one (Comp p.21, p.30).
  • Mann-Whitney doesn't test medians. It tests whether one distribution tends to produce larger values.
  • Signed-rank still assumes symmetry. For skewed differences use the sign test or a bootstrap.
  • Choosing the rank test because the t test missed 0.05 is p-hacking (Comp p.18).
  • HW6 Problem 2's CPU times are visibly skewed (values like 9.59 and 11.73 among mostly 0 to 4). By the Comp p.17 chart that's a Mann-Whitney or log-transform case, though the homework asked for the t interval.

Effect size, how many runs, and what to report

The idea

A p-value says whether a difference is distinguishable from noise, not whether it's big enough to matter. So report the raw difference with its interval in the response's units, which is usually the most useful statement (Comp p.24).

The number of runs should be planned before collecting data, from the smallest difference you'd care about. Pairing changes that number a lot, because the paired formula uses the small \(s_d\) and the unpaired one uses the large raw s (Comp p.25).

What to know

Effect sizes from Comp p.24 are the raw difference with its interval (+1.83 s, or −0.135 hit ratio), a ratio or speedup (B is \(31.61/29.79 = 1.06\times\) slower), a standardized \(d_z = \bar d/s_d\) for paired data or Cohen's \(d = (\bar x_A - \bar x_B)/s_p\) for unpaired, and the probability of superiority \(A_{12}\), where 0.5 means no difference. The standardized ones are for combining across studies. \(A_{12}\) needs no distributional assumption and, unlike a p-value, doesn't grow just because you added runs (Wild handout, entry 5).

Sample size from Comp p.25, with \(\delta\) the difference worth detecting and \(z[\beta] = 0.842\) for 80% power,

\[ \text{paired: } n = \left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s_d}{\delta}\right)^2, \qquad \text{unpaired: } n \text{ per group} = 2\left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s}{\delta}\right)^2. \]

Always round up. A post-hoc power calculation from your own p-value tells you nothing (Comp p.25).

Report the design (paired or not, what the unit was, how many), the test and why it was chosen in advance, the statistic with df and an exact p, the difference with its interval, and every comparison including non-significant ones (Comp p.26). If you measured three systems and report only one pair, say so, because the error rate isn't what the reader assumes (Comp p.26).

With c alternatives there are \(c(c-1)/2\) pairs. At c = 4 that's six tests and \(1 - 0.95^6 \approx 0.26\) chance of at least one false positive, which is why three or more systems need ANOVA first (Comp p.28). In general it's \(1 - (1-\alpha)^{c(c-1)/2}\) (study guide formula sheet). Only if the omnibus test rejects do you look for which pairs differ, with Tukey for all pairs, Dunnett against a baseline, or a correction. Friedman is the rank-based omnibus test when the data aren't normal (Comp p.28, study guide answer 25).

If you ran several tests on one comparison, report all of them and explain any disagreement. Quietly discussing only the one under 0.05 is the p-hacking the lecture warns about (study guide, answer 24).

Worked example

Lecture numbers (Comp p.25). \(s_d = 0.0743\), smallest difference worth detecting \(\delta = 0.05\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), 80% power.

  1. Formulas. Paired \(n = \left(\dfrac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s_d}{\delta}\right)^2\). Unpaired, per group, \(n = 2\left(\dfrac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s}{\delta}\right)^2\). Here \(z[\alpha/2]\) is the upper-tail normal value for the test level, \(z[\beta]\) the upper-tail value for the miss rate \(\beta = 1 - \text{power}\), \(s_d\) the SD of the differences, \(s\) the SD of raw values, and \(\delta\) the difference you want to catch.
  2. Table values. \(\alpha = 0.05\) two-sided gives \(z[0.025] = 1.960\). 80% power means \(\beta = 0.20\), and \(z[0.20] = 0.842\). Their sum is \(1.960 + 0.842 = 2.802\).
  3. Plug in, paired. \(n = (2.802 \times 0.0743/0.05)^2\).
  4. Work it out, paired. \(0.0743/0.05 = 1.486\). \(2.802 \times 1.486 = 4.164\). \(4.164^2 = 17.3\). Round up to 18.
  5. Plug in and work out, unpaired with \(s = 0.12\). \(n = 2(2.802 \times 0.12/0.05)^2\). \(0.12/0.05 = 2.4\). \(2.802 \times 2.4 = 6.725\). \(6.725^2 = 45.2\). \(2 \times 45.2 = 90.4\). Round up to 91.
  6. Result. 18 pairs against 91 per group (182 runs). Pairing cut the work by about a factor of five.

Five benchmarks again. Detect a 1-second difference at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with 80% power, using \(s_d = 1.581\). Plug into the paired formula, \(n = (2.802 \times 1.581/1)^2\). Then \(2.802 \times 1.581 = 4.430\) and \(4.430^2 = 19.6\), which rounds up to 20 benchmarks. In plain words, you'd need 20 benchmarks to have an 80% chance of catching a true 1-second gap.

Effect size for the small Welch example. The raw difference is −5 ms, 95% CI (−9.61, −0.39). As a share of B's mean, the formula is difference divided by \(\bar x_B\). Plug in \(5/15 = 0.33\), and for the ends \(0.39/15 = 0.026\) and \(9.61/15 = 0.64\). So A is about a third faster, with a range from about 3% to 64%. Report that, not just "significant".

Easy to get wrong

  • The unpaired formula has the factor 2 and uses the raw s, not \(s_d\).
  • \(z[\beta]\) for 80% power is 0.842, the upper 20% point. It's not 1.282 (that's 90% power).
  • "We ran it six times" isn't a sample size, it's the absence of one (Comp p.25). The six-workload example had about a one-in-five chance of detecting a 0.05 difference.
  • Not significant with tiny n is not "equivalent" (Comp p.27).

Building pairing into the experiment, and papers in the wild

The idea

The study guide's design questions all ask the same thing. Since the design can't be fixed afterwards, how do you get pairing, or something close to it, before you collect data? The answer is to make the two measurements share as much of their noise as possible (Comp p.5-6). The Wild handout is a set of real papers that do this well or badly, and it's the source for HW6 Problem 3.

What to know

SituationHow to pair or recover \(\rho\)Source
Cloud VMs whose speed driftsIn space, run A and B at the same time on the same machine (duet benchmarking). In time, interleave one run of A and one of B per round with the order randomized each round (RMIT)Study guide answer 15, Wild entries 1 and 2
Production schedulers, each session sees oneUnpaired, but stratify or block on request rate, region, device or time of day, use each user's pre-period behaviour as a covariate, or switch the whole system between A and B in alternating windowsStudy guide answer 12
Two LLMs on the same 400 questionsThe unit is the question, so it's paired by construction. You need per-question outcomes from the harness, not the two summary scoresStudy guide answer 16, Wild entry 3
p99 latency on 20 shared workloadsKeep the pairing, but make the response the per-workload p99, then pick paired t, signed-rank or sign test from the shape of the 20 differencesStudy guide answer 14
Iterations inside one executionThey don't add units. Spend runs at the level with the most variance, and pair at that levelWild entry 11, Comp p.4

With 20 runs to spend, 10 paired units almost always beat 10 + 10 unpaired. The answer flips when \(\rho \le 0\), meaning the units share nothing, or when measuring under A changes the unit for B (cache pollution, learning effects, leftover state). Then pairing buys no variance and adds carry-over bias (study guide, answer 17).

Papers from the Wild handout worth one line each.

PaperWhat it shows
Bulej et al., Duet Benchmarking (ICPE 2020)Running both variants together manufactures \(\rho\). Accuracy improved 2.3× to 12.5× on ScalaBench and DaCapo and 23.8× to 82.4× on SPEC CPU 2017. It fails if the two variants interfere, since they share the cores
Abedi and Brecht, RMIT (ICPE 2017)Consecutive batches of A then B reported up to 37.8% differences between identical alternatives. Interleaving pairs in time
Miller, Adding Error Bars to Evals (2024)The paired eval uses \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B)\) with the question as the unit, and the covariance shrinks it
Dror et al. (ACL 2018)A test-choice decision tree for NLP, with the paired bootstrap as the usual default. F1 isn't a mean, so a t test on it is hard to justify
Arcuri and Briand (ICSE 2011)Randomized algorithms need many runs, a rank test and \(A_{12}\)
FuzzBench (FSE 2021)Mann-Whitney plus \(A_{12}\) per benchmark, Friedman with Nemenyi across benchmarks, which is a blocked design
Bouthillier et al. (MLSys 2021)Compare by \(P(A > B)\), which is \(A_{12}\), with a threshold of 0.75 fixed in advance. In their simulations comparing average scores had a false-negative rate near 90%, while their test kept false positives near 5% and false negatives around 30%. They recommend 29 training runs
Georges et al. (OOPSLA 2007)The JVM protocol that compares by interval overlap, the Comp p.15 pitfall in a widely cited paper
Belia et al. (2005), Cumming and Finch (2005)Published researchers in psychology, behavioural neuroscience and medicine, asked to place two error bars so the difference was just significant, mostly couldn't, for both CI and SE bars, and often confused the two. Cumming and Finch is the source of the half-margin overlap rule
Card et al. (EMNLP 2020)Asks the power question backwards, finding the smallest effect a published n could detect
Kalibera and Jones (ISMM 2013)Repetition hierarchy of builds, executions and iterations. Repeats at the wrong level buy nothing

The handout's seven pitfalls are unpaired analysis of paired data, paired analysis of unpaired data, "not significant" read as "equivalent", two intervals instead of one, pooled t on unequal groups, the test chosen after the data, and a mean compared when the claim is about the tail. They match Comp p.27 plus the pooled-t item from p.13.

Worked example

Study guide question 17 with numbers. Suppose each system's runtime has \(\sigma_A = \sigma_B = 20\) s across units and you have 20 runs.

  1. Formulas. \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B) = \sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2 - 2\rho\,\sigma_A\sigma_B\), where \(\rho\) is the correlation between a unit's A and B times. Paired with \(n\) units, SE \(= \sigma_d/\sqrt n\) with \(n - 1\) df. Unpaired with \(n\) per side, SE \(= \sqrt{\sigma_A^2/n + \sigma_B^2/n}\) with \(2n - 2\) df. Half-width is the t value times SE.
  2. Strong correlation, plug in. \(\rho = 0.99\), \(\sigma_A = \sigma_B = 20\). \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B) = 20^2 + 20^2 - 2(0.99)(20)(20)\).
  3. Work it out. \(400 + 400 = 800\). \(2 \times 0.99 \times 400 = 792\). \(800 - 792 = 8\), so \(\sigma_d = \sqrt8 = 2.83\). Paired with 10 units, SE \(= 2.83/\sqrt{10} = 2.83/3.162 = 0.894\). Row 9, column 0.025 gives 2.262, so half-width \(= 2.262 \times 0.894 = 2.02\) s.
  4. Unpaired, same budget. 10 + 10 runs. SE \(= \sqrt{400/10 + 400/10} = \sqrt{40 + 40} = \sqrt{80} = 8.94\). df \(= 18\), and row 18, column 0.025 gives 2.101, so half-width \(= 2.101 \times 8.94 = 18.8\) s.
  5. Result for \(\rho = 0.99\). 2.02 s paired against 18.8 s unpaired. Pairing is about nine times narrower.
  6. No correlation, plug in and work out. \(\rho = 0\), so \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B) = 800 - 0 = 800\) and \(\sigma_d = \sqrt{800} = 28.3\). Paired SE \(= 28.3/3.162 = 8.94\), the same as unpaired. But paired uses 9 df (2.262) and unpaired 18 df (2.101). Paired half-width \(= 2.262 \times 8.94 = 20.2\) s, unpaired 18.8 s.
