  2. Stop at 100. The last 20 are iterations 81 to 100. Iterations 81 to 85 are at 10 ms, which is 5 of them. Iterations 86 to 100 are at 14 ms, which is 15. Plug in \(\dfrac{5 \times 10 + 15 \times 14}{20}\). Work it out, \(50 + 210 = 260\) and \(260/20 = 13.0\) ms.
  3. Stop at 90. The last 20 are 71 to 90. Iterations 71 to 85 are at 10 ms, which is 15. Iterations 86 to 90 are at 14 ms, which is 5. Plug in \(\dfrac{15 \times 10 + 5 \times 14}{20}\). Work it out, \(150 + 70 = 220\) and \(220/20 = 11.0\) ms.
  4. Compare. Relative difference \(= (13.0 - 11.0)/11.0 = 2/11 = 0.18\).
  5. Result. Same benchmark, an 18% difference that depends only on when you stopped.

The run-to-run spread inside each window looks small, so nothing in the numbers warns you. Plotting the iterations would show the two levels at once.

(b) Uncertainty of a speedup (entry 6). The baseline takes 10.0 s and your system 8.0 s, each with a 3% relative standard error.

  1. Formulas. Speedup \(R = T_{\text{base}}/T_{\text{new}}\). Relative error of a ratio \(\sigma_R/R \approx \sqrt{(\sigma_A/A)^2 + (\sigma_B/B)^2}\). Then \(\sigma_R = (\sigma_R/R) \times R\), and a rough 95% range is \(R \pm 1.96\,\sigma_R\).
  2. Plug in. \(R = 10.0/8.0\). \(\sigma_R/R \approx \sqrt{0.03^2 + 0.03^2}\).
  3. Work it out. \(R = 1.25\). \(0.03^2 = 0.0009\), twice is 0.0018, and \(\sqrt{0.0018} = 0.0424\). \(\sigma_R = 0.0424 \times 1.25 = 0.053\). \(1.96 \times 0.053 = 0.10\). \(1.25 - 0.10 = 1.15\) and \(1.25 + 0.10 = 1.35\).
  4. Result. Speedup 1.25, roughly 1.15 to 1.35.

The headline "25% faster" is really "somewhere between 15% and 35% faster", and that's before any systematic error.

(c) An interval on a success rate (the entry 17 discussion question). A policy succeeds in 18 of 20 trials. The same arithmetic applies to a VLM that answers 18 of 20 questions correctly.

  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\) (CI p.48), where \(p\) is the success share, \(n\) the number of trials and \(z = 1.96\) for 95%.
  2. Plug in. \(p = 18/20 = 0.9\), so \(0.9 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{0.9 \times 0.1}{20}}\).
  3. Work it out. \(0.9 \times 0.1 = 0.09\). \(0.09/20 = 0.0045\). \(\sqrt{0.0045} = 0.0671\). \(1.96 \times 0.0671 = 0.1315\). \(0.9 - 0.1315 = 0.769\) and \(0.9 + 0.1315 = 1.031\).
  4. Result. (0.769, 1.031). The top end is above 1, which no success rate can be, and that's the sign this interval is failing.

It's the Wald interval, and CI p.49 warns it's problematic and points to the Wilson interval for \(n < 40\). There are only 2 failures behind it, so the normal approximation to the binomial is poor. Wilson isn't worked on the slides, so you won't need it, but for the record here it is.

  1. Formula. Centre \(= \dfrac{p + z^2/(2n)}{1 + z^2/n}\) and half-width \(= \dfrac{z\sqrt{p(1-p)/n + z^2/(4n^2)}}{1 + z^2/n}\).
  2. Plug in. \(z^2 = 3.8416\), \(n = 20\), so \(z^2/(2n) = 3.8416/40\), \(z^2/n = 3.8416/20\), \(z^2/(4n^2) = 3.8416/1600\).
  3. Work it out. \(3.8416/40 = 0.0960\), so the top of the centre is \(0.9 + 0.0960 = 0.9960\). \(3.8416/20 = 0.1921\), so the bottom is 1.1921. Centre \(= 0.9960/1.1921 = 0.836\). Next \(3.8416/1600 = 0.0024\), and \(0.0045 + 0.0024 = 0.0069\), whose square root is 0.0831. Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.0831/1.1921 = 0.137\). \(0.836 - 0.137 = 0.70\) and \(0.836 + 0.137 = 0.97\).
  4. Result. About (0.70, 0.97), which stays inside 0 to 1.

Either way, "90% success" is really "somewhere from about 70% to 97%". And the interval assumes 20 independent trials (HT p.20). If the trials ran in one block after the operator tuned the lighting, or the object wasn't reset to a randomised pose each time, the trials share conditions and the true uncertainty is larger than any formula shows. That's why entry 17 asks you to report trial order, resets and conditions (Meas p.29).

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking a with-and-without comparison catches measurement bias. It catches perturbation. Bias needs the configuration itself varied, as in STABILIZER.
  • Calling time-of-day variation random by default. It's only random if your schedule spreads runs over it.
  • Reporting a speedup or efficiency with no uncertainty. It's a ratio of two noisy numbers, so it's noisier than either.
  • Treating the median as a way to hide bad runs. It's justified when interference is one-sided, and you should still say so.
  • Thinking a fixed seed makes an ML or VLM result trustworthy. It makes the result precise, not accurate, because every source of variance you held fixed is still a choice that could have gone another way (entry 16).
  • Treating a packet trace or a simulator scenario as ground truth. The capture has its own precision and drop behaviour (entry 12), and the scenario can decide the winner by never exercising the protocol (entry 13).
  • Reporting only a p-value for two randomized tools or configurations. With enough runs any difference is significant, so give an effect size such as \(\hat{A}_{12}\) (entry 14).
  • Using the slide proportion interval on a small success count like 18 of 20. It can run past 1, and CI p.49 says to use Wilson when \(n < 40\).

Check yourself

1. You profile a 10 second run at 1 kHz and function f gets 312 samples. Give the 95% interval for the share of time in f.
  1. Formula. \(p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\) (CI p.48). \(n\) is the number of samples, \(p\) the share that landed in f, \(z = 1.96\) for 95%.
  2. Get n and p. \(n = 1000 \text{ per second} \times 10 \text{ s} = 10{,}000\). \(p = 312/10{,}000 = 0.0312\).
  3. Plug in. \(0.0312 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{0.0312 \times 0.9688}{10{,}000}}\).
  4. Work it out. \(0.0312 \times 0.9688 = 0.03023\). Divide by 10,000 to get 0.00000302. Square root \(= 0.00174\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.00174 = 0.0034\). \(0.0312 - 0.0034 = 0.0278\) and \(0.0312 + 0.0034 = 0.0346\).
  5. Check. \(np = 312\) and \(n(1-p) = 9688\), both well above 10, so the normal approximation holds.
  6. Result. (0.0278, 0.0346), about 2.8% to 3.5%. The profiler printed "3.12%", but the honest statement is "about 3%, give or take 0.3 points" (Meas p.7). This is study guide question 1.
2. Your clock has 100 ns resolution and the operation takes about 180 ns. What can one measurement return, and what is the per-operation resolution if you time \(10^6\) iterations and divide?
  1. One reading. The clock only reports whole ticks of 100 ns, so 180 ns comes back as 100 ns or 200 ns and nothing else. The error can be up to 80 ns, almost half the quantity (CI p.10).
  2. Formula for the loop. Resolution \(= T_c/k\), where \(T_c\) is one tick and \(k\) the number of iterations sharing it.
  3. Plug in and work it out. \(100 \text{ ns}/10^6 = 0.0001\) ns.
  4. Result. 0.0001 ns per operation. That only works if the operation behaves the same when repeated. It often doesn't, because the loop warms the cache and branch predictor, and the compiler can drop a body whose result is unused (measurement study guide, question 3).
3. A clock read costs 22 ns and you bracket each operation with two reads. What share of the measured time is overhead for a 150 ns operation, and for a 15 ns one?
  1. Formula. Overhead share \(= \dfrac{2c}{T + 2c}\), with \(c\) one clock read and \(T\) the operation's true length.
  2. Plug in. \(2c = 2 \times 22 = 44\) ns. For 150 ns, \(44/(150 + 44)\). For 15 ns, \(44/(15 + 44)\).
  3. Work it out. \(150 + 44 = 194\), and \(44/194 = 0.227\). \(15 + 44 = 59\), and \(44/59 = 0.746\).
  4. Result. About 23% for the 150 ns operation and 75% for the 15 ns one, so the timer dominates the short one. The operation should be one to two orders of magnitude longer than a clock read, and if it isn't, time a loop (measurement study guide, question 4).
4. A 32-bit counter ticks every 10 ns. How long can you time before it wraps? A reading goes from 4,294,967,000 to 704. How long was the event?
  1. Formulas. \(T_{\max} = (2^n - 1)\,T_c\). After one wrap, ticks \(= \text{end} + 2^n - \text{start}\), and \(T_e = \text{ticks} \times T_c\).
  2. Plug in, rollover. \(T_{\max} = (2^{32} - 1) \times 10\) ns. \(2^{32} = 4{,}294{,}967{,}296 \approx 4.295 \times 10^9\).
  3. Work it out. \(4.295 \times 10^9 \times 10 = 4.295 \times 10^{10}\) ns. Times \(10^{-9}\) gives 42.95 s.
  4. Plug in, the reading. End 704 is smaller than start, so it wrapped once. Ticks \(= 704 + 4{,}294{,}967{,}296 - 4{,}294{,}967{,}000\).
  5. Work it out. \(4{,}294{,}967{,}296 - 4{,}294{,}967{,}000 = 296\). \(704 + 296 = 1000\) ticks. \(1000 \times 10 = 10{,}000\) ns.
  6. Result. You can time up to about 42.95 s, and this event took 10 µs. If an event could last longer than 42.95 s, you couldn't tell how many times it wrapped (Lilja ch. 6, interval timers).
5. An event is shorter than the 1 µs tick. Out of 2000 runs, 300 saw a tick. Estimate the event length with a 95% interval.
  1. Formulas. \(p = m/n\) and \(T_e \approx p\,T_c\), because a tick lands inside the event with probability \(T_e/T_c\). Interval \(p \pm 1.96\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\), then multiply by \(T_c\) (CI p.48).
  2. Plug in. \(p = 300/2000\), \(T_c = 1\) µs, \(n = 2000\).
  3. Estimate. \(p = 0.15\), so \(T_e \approx 0.15 \times 1 = 0.15\) µs \(= 150\) ns.
  4. Standard error. \(0.15 \times 0.85 = 0.1275\). \(0.1275/2000 = 0.00006375\). Square root \(= 0.00798\).
  5. Half-width and ends. \(1.96 \times 0.00798 = 0.0156\). \(0.15 - 0.0156 = 0.1344\) and \(0.15 + 0.0156 = 0.1656\). Times 1 µs gives 134 to 166 ns.
  6. Result. About 150 ns, with a 95% interval of 134 to 166 ns.
6. You have pilot data \(\sigma_p = 8\) ms between processes and \(\sigma_i = 2\) ms within a process, and a budget of 40 measurements. Compare 1 × 40, 8 × 5 and 40 × 1.
  1. Formula. \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \sigma_p^2/p + \sigma_i^2/(pk)\), SE \(= \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}}\), with \(p\) processes of \(k\) iterations (Meas p.25).
  2. Plug in. \(\sigma_p^2 = 64\), \(\sigma_i^2 = 4\), and \(pk = 40\) every time, so \(\operatorname{Var} = 64/p + 4/40 = 64/p + 0.1\).
  3. 1 × 40. \(64/1 + 0.1 = 64.1\). SE \(= \sqrt{64.1} = 8.01\) ms.
  4. 8 × 5. \(64/8 + 0.1 = 8 + 0.1 = 8.1\). SE \(= \sqrt{8.1} = 2.85\) ms.
  5. 40 × 1. \(64/40 + 0.1 = 1.6 + 0.1 = 1.7\). SE \(= \sqrt{1.7} = 1.30\) ms.
  6. Result. 40 × 1 wins, with an SE six times smaller than 1 × 40. Iterations can't reduce the between-process term, so the budget belongs at the process level (measurement study guide, question 5).
7. A benchmark runs 121.0 s untraced and 128.4 s traced. Its two probes cost 3.1 s and 2.9 s alone. Report the perturbation and explain the mismatch.
  1. Formula. Perturbation \(= (T_{\text{inst}} - T_{\text{base}})/T_{\text{base}}\).
  2. Plug in. \((128.4 - 121.0)/121.0\).
  3. Work it out. \(128.4 - 121.0 = 7.4\). \(7.4/121.0 = 0.061\).
  4. Result. 6.1%. The probes alone sum to \(3.1 + 2.9 = 6.0\) s, but together they cost 7.4 s.

That's not a contradiction, because perturbation isn't additive. Together their buffers can evict the workload's working set, making the program itself slower, which pushes the total above the sum. Sharing cached data or a log flush would push it below. Only the with-and-without measurement in the real configuration counts (Meas p.17).

8. A calibrated loop takes 2.00 s idle and 3.40 s now on a machine you can't inspect. What share are you getting, and how uncertain is the ratio if each timing is ±1%?
  1. Formulas. Share \(= T_{\text{idle}}/T_{\text{now}}\). For a ratio, relative errors add in quadrature, \(\sigma_R/R \approx \sqrt{(\sigma_A/A)^2 + (\sigma_B/B)^2}\).
  2. Plug in. Share \(= 2.00/3.40\). Error \(\approx \sqrt{1^2 + 1^2}\) percent.
  3. Work it out. \(2.00/3.40 = 0.59\), so something else uses about \(1 - 0.59 = 0.41\). \(\sqrt{1 + 1} = \sqrt2 = 1.41\).
  4. Result. You get about 59% of the machine, and the ratio is uncertain by about 1.4%, worse than either timing.

The inference could still fail if your loop and the neighbour stress different resources, or if the slowdown is throttling and not load (Meas p.18, study guide question 8).

9. Counting, tracing or sampling? (a) Total L3 misses in a 30 s run. (b) Which code path leads to the 1-in-a-million request that's 100 times slower. (c) A 40 minute job and no idea where time goes.

(a) Counting. You only need a total of a frequent event, and a hardware counter does that almost for free, while tracing would perturb the very cache behaviour you're measuring. (b) Tracing. You need context and the event is rare, so sampling would almost certainly miss it, and triggering the trace only above a latency threshold keeps the volume down. (c) Sampling. There's no hypothesis yet and you need overhead you can fix in advance, which is the flame graph case (Meas p.7-8).

10. True or false. (a) Running 1000 times instead of 10 makes the result more accurate. (b) Every measurement in a biased configuration is repeatable.

(a) False. More runs narrow the interval, which is precision, while accuracy is set by systematic error, and repetition doesn't touch that (Meas p.20). (b) True, and that's what makes bias dangerous. Every run agrees, the interval is tight, and nothing in the statistics warns you (Meas p.21).

11. A student reads the cycle counter before and after and divides by the CPU's rated clock frequency. Name three things wrong with this on current hardware.

The three problems are that the counter already counts time, there's no single frequency anyway, and the reads themselves aren't reliable. First, the counter is invariant, so it ticks at a fixed rate and dividing by rated MHz turns a right answer into a wrong one. Second, with frequency scaling and turbo there's no single frequency to divide by. Third, the read can be reordered by out-of-order execution unless serialised, and counters aren't guaranteed to agree across cores if the thread migrates. The lesson is that an instrument's error model expires (Meas p.13).

12. Two systems report 4.21 ± 0.08 s and 4.05 ± 0.09 s (95%). A reader says the second is faster because the intervals barely overlap. What's wrong, and does the conclusion survive?

The reasoning is wrong because overlap implies nothing (Meas p.27). You have to build the interval on the difference.

  1. Formula. Each SE is half-width/1.96. For unpaired results, \(\text{SE}_{\text{diff}} = \sqrt{\text{SE}_A^2 + \text{SE}_B^2}\), and the interval is \((\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm 1.96\,\text{SE}_{\text{diff}}\).
  2. Plug in. \(\text{SE}_A = 0.08/1.96\), \(\text{SE}_B = 0.09/1.96\).
  3. Work it out. \(\text{SE}_A = 0.041\) and \(\text{SE}_B = 0.046\). Squares are 0.00168 and 0.00212, which add to 0.00380. \(\sqrt{0.00380} = 0.062\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.062 = 0.12\). Difference \(= 4.21 - 4.05 = 0.16\). \(0.16 - 0.12 = 0.04\) and \(0.16 + 0.12 = 0.28\).
  4. Shortcut. The 1.96 cancels, so the half-width of the difference is just \(\sqrt{0.08^2 + 0.09^2} = \sqrt{0.0064 + 0.0081} = \sqrt{0.0145} = 0.120\).
  5. Result. (0.04, 0.28) s excludes 0, so the second system is faster. The conclusion survives, but by a different argument. If the same workloads were used for both, a paired interval would be better still (study guide question 22).
13. In question 1 (\(p = 0.0312\)) you want the share to within ±0.5 percentage points at 95%. How many samples, and how long at 1 kHz? What do you trade by sampling at 10 kHz instead?
  1. Formula. \(n = z^2\,p(1-p)/e^2\), with \(e\) the absolute half-width you want (measurement study guide, Part B).
  2. Plug in. \(n = 1.96^2 \times 0.0312 \times 0.9688/0.005^2\).
  3. Work it out. \(0.0312 \times 0.9688 = 0.030227\). \(1.96^2 = 3.8416\), and \(3.8416 \times 0.030227 = 0.116118\). \(0.005^2 = 0.000025\). \(0.116118/0.000025 = 4644.7\). Round up to 4645.
  4. Convert to time. \(4645/1000 = 4.6\) s, less than the 10 s you already ran, so ±0.5 points was already available.
  5. Tighter, ±0.1 points. Now \(e^2 = 0.001^2 = 0.000001\), so \(n = 0.116118/0.000001 = 116{,}118\), about 116 s or two minutes at 1 kHz.
  6. Result. 4645 samples (4.6 s) for ±0.5 points.

Going to 10 kHz collects samples ten times faster but raises the perturbation ten times, since sampling overhead is set by the rate. Samples 100 µs apart in the same call are also correlated, so they aren't independent (Meas p.7, measurement study guide, question 2).

14. The CI slides sample every 10 ms for a minute and find 658 of 6000 interrupts in the OS. How many interrupts for ±0.5% at 95% if the 0.5% is relative? If it's absolute?
  1. Formulas. Relative \(n = z^2\,p(1-p)/(e p)^2\) (CI p.54). Absolute \(n = z^2\,p(1-p)/e^2\) (measurement study guide). \(e = 0.005\) in both, \(z = 1.96\).
  2. p. \(p = 658/6000 = 0.1097\), so \(p(1-p) = 0.1097 \times 0.8903 = 0.09764\), and \(z^2\,p(1-p) = 3.8416 \times 0.09764 = 0.37510\).
  3. Relative, plug in and work out. \(e p = 0.005 \times 0.1097 = 0.0005485\). Squared, \(0.0005485^2 = 0.00000030085\). \(0.37510/0.00000030085 \approx 1{,}246{,}800\) interrupts. At 10 ms each that's \(1{,}246{,}800 \times 0.01 = 12{,}468\) s, and \(12{,}468/3600 = 3.46\) hours, the slide's answer (CI p.57).
  4. Absolute, plug in and work out. \(e^2 = 0.005^2 = 0.000025\). \(0.37510/0.000025 \approx 15{,}004\) interrupts. At 10 ms each that's about 150 s, or 2.5 minutes.
  5. Result. About 1.25 million interrupts (3.46 hours) if relative, about 15,000 (2.5 minutes) if absolute. The study guide's formula uses absolute \(e\) and the CI slides use relative, so read the question carefully.
15. For data set T a colleague wants to report the minimum, 7.2 ms, because timing noise only slows things down. Give the best case for it, two reasons against, and what to report instead.

The best case is that noise is one-sided. Interrupts, neighbours and migrations only add time, so what you observe is true cost plus non-negative contamination and the fastest run is the least polluted (Meas p.26). The first reason against is that the minimum is an extreme order statistic, so it's noisy, has no standard interval and keeps falling as you add runs, which makes papers with different \(n\) incomparable. The second is that it answers a best-case question, while most claims are about typical or tail behaviour that users actually feel. Instead, report all 30 points, the median of 13.0 ms with a quantile or bootstrap interval, and the minimum alongside with \(n\) stated if the best case matters (measurement study guide, question 21).

16. A paper says its system meets a 50 ms SLO written at p99, using a mean of 41 ms ± 0.2 ms over 100,000 requests. What's wrong, and which part is statistics and which is measurement?

The evidence doesn't touch the claim, because the mean and p99 are different quantities and right-skewed latency with a 41 ms mean can have a p99 far above 50 ms (Meas p.28). The statistics part is choosing the mean and its t interval when they should measure p99 with an order-statistic or bootstrap interval across repeated runs, and that's the easy half. The measurement part is bigger. One run's requests show only within-run variation, and a closed-loop harness can't overload the system, so its tail isn't the one the SLO is about. The fix is an open-loop generator driven by an arrival process, reporting the offered rate (measurement study guide, questions 14 and 24).

17. The companion handout asks what the practical difference is between perturbation and measurement bias, since both have unpredictable sign. Answer it.

The practical difference is how you fix them. Perturbation comes from your instrument, so you can measure it by running with and without the instrument and reporting the difference (Meas p.17). Measurement bias comes from a configuration choice like link order or environment size, and the instrument reports it faithfully and repeatably, so removing the instrument shows nothing (Meas p.21). The fix for bias is to vary the configuration across runs, as STABILIZER does with layout, which turns it into random error that repetition can then average (Meas p.22, measurement-in-practice handout, entries 1 and 2).

18. You compare two video encoder settings by timing a cloud VLM API. Setting A runs 200 requests Friday night and setting B runs 200 requests Monday morning, both with narrow intervals. What's the error, will more requests fix it, and what design change would?

The error is confounding, more requests won't fix it, and interleaving would. Setting and time slot change together, and shared cloud infrastructure shows real hour-of-day and day-of-week variation, so the difference includes whatever the service was doing at those times (Meas p.23). More requests only estimate Friday night and Monday morning more precisely, narrowing the interval around a biased difference. Interleaving A and B in randomised order across the same hours and days makes time shared spread instead of an offset (Meas p.22, measurement study guide, question 23, measurement-in-practice handout, entry 11).

19. Tool A finds a bug in 3 of 30 runs and tool B finds it in 1 of 30 (the entry 14 discussion question). Is A better? Give a 95% interval on the difference and the probability of superiority.

No, the data don't show A is better. Here's the work.

  1. Formula for the interval. \((p_A - p_B) \pm 1.96\sqrt{\dfrac{p_A(1-p_A)}{n_A} + \dfrac{p_B(1-p_B)}{n_B}}\). The runs are independent, so the variances add the same way as in Welch's test (Comp p.12).
  2. Plug in. \(p_A = 3/30 = 0.100\), \(p_B = 1/30 = 0.0333\), \(n_A = n_B = 30\).
  3. Work it out. \(0.100 \times 0.900/30 = 0.090/30 = 0.0030\). \(0.0333 \times 0.9667/30 = 0.0322/30 = 0.00107\). Sum \(= 0.00407\). Square root \(= 0.0638\). Half-width \(= 1.96 \times 0.0638 = 0.125\). Difference \(= 0.100 - 0.0333 = 0.0667\). \(0.0667 - 0.125 = -0.058\) and \(0.0667 + 0.125 = 0.192\).
  4. Interval result. (−0.058, 0.192) contains 0. With only 3 and 1 successes the normal approximation is weak too (CI p.49), so the real uncertainty is wider still.
  5. Formula for \(\hat A_{12}\). \(\hat A_{12} = P(\text{A beats B}) + \tfrac12 P(\text{tie})\) for one random run of each. A beats B when A finds the bug and B doesn't.
  6. Plug in and work it out. A wins with probability \(0.1 \times 29/30 = 0.0967\). B wins with \(0.9 \times 1/30 = 0.030\). Ties are the rest, \(1 - 0.0967 - 0.030 = 0.8733\), and half of that is 0.4367. So \(\hat A_{12} = 0.0967 + 0.4367 = 0.533\).
  7. Result. \(\hat A_{12} = 0.533\), barely above the 0.5 that means no difference (Comp p.24).

A reader needs both counts, the interval on the difference, the effect size, and how the number of runs was chosen before the experiment (HT p.19, measurement-in-practice handout, entry 14).

20. You compare two frame resolutions for a VLM streaming pipeline. Both run 200 questions over the same 50 clips, recorded once through one shaped bandwidth trace, and you repeat each with 3 seeds. The higher resolution wins by 2 points with a tight interval over seeds. What did the seeds sample, what did you hold fixed by accident, and what would entries 12, 13 and 16 have you change?

The seeds sample only the model's sampling randomness, and you held the clip set, the question set, the one bandwidth trace and the model version fixed by accident. So the tight interval is precision about this one setup, not accuracy about the method (Meas p.20, entry 16). The 2 points could come from those 50 clips or that trace and vanish on others, and repetition over seeds can't reveal that because it never varies them. Following entry 16, resample clips and questions in each run and run several traces, which turns those nuisance factors into spread you can see (p.22). Following entry 13, report what the trace contains, like its mean and minimum bandwidth, so a reader can tell whether it ever pushed either setting into trouble. Following entry 12, check the shaper against a separate throughput test before trusting that the link really ran at the rate you set. Since both settings see the same clips, analyse the per-clip differences as a paired comparison (Comp p.6), and treat the clip, not the seed, as the experimental unit (HT p.20).

21. A reviewer says your packet trace is ground truth, so your loss rate needs no error bar. Give two ways the trace could be wrong while looking complete, and say which one more repetition would reveal.

Capture drops and clock error can both fool you, and only the drops would show up under repetition. First, the capture host can drop packets when it falls behind during a burst, which is exactly when loss is interesting, and the trace still looks like a clean sequence of packets. Second, timestamps come from the capture host's clock, which has a finite resolution and can be offset or stepped, so inter-arrival times and delays can be wrong by a consistent amount (Meas p.12). Capture drops tend to vary from run to run with load, so repeated captures that disagree would reveal them. A clock offset or a bad vantage point is the same in every run, so it's systematic and repetition can't show it (p.20). The fix is Paxson's, which is to check the instrument against itself and against a redundant measurement, such as comparing the capture's packet count with the sender's count, before drawing any conclusion about the network (measurement-in-practice handout, entry 12).

Advisors, CS 701, publishing and ethics

Slides: Advisor, CS701, Pub, Ethics, Intro (course logistics), plus Sean Luke's tech report GMU-CS-TR-2013-8 on plagiarism (marked PLEASE READ on the schedule). Homework: none. These lectures have no homework, so expect short-answer or true/false questions that you can answer straight from the slides with the book open.

Choosing a PhD advisor

The idea

Every PhD student gets an academic advisor at admission, but that's not the same as your research advisor. You pick the research advisor yourself and declare it with a form. The deck calls this the most important decision of your PhD because it shapes whether you finish, how long it takes, and where your career goes afterward (Advisor p.3-4).

The core message is that you can't do this quickly. Most faculty want to work with you informally for a semester or more before they agree to advise you, so they can tell whether you're a good fit and you can tell whether they are. Prof. Aydin calls this informal stage the Preliminary Research Co-Operation Phase, or PRCP (p.8-9). CS 701 in the spring is a formal version of it (p.10).

What to know

FactDetailCite
Academic advisorAssigned at admission, usually someone with compatible interestsAdvisor p.3
Research advisor deadlineDeclared by paperwork within the first 24 credits, a hard deadlinep.3
Why it mattersThe most important decision of a PhD. It may determine success in the program, how long the PhD takes and your long-term career path. Choosing the advisor and choosing the research area go hand in handp.4
"Easy" casesYou were already in close contact with a faculty member during admission, so they're probably your academic advisor and maybe already your GRA boss. You still go through an initial research phase before declaring, and the checklist still appliesp.5
Common caseYou have a broad idea of your interests but should stay open-minded. Find faculty in those or closely related areas, read their recent projects and papers, and contact them only after doing that homeworkp.6
PRCPUnofficial early research involvement, one semester or more, so both sides judge fit. Faculty may skip it if they already know you from prior publications or class work. It's Aydin's term, not an official procedure, but nearly all faculty agree an initial phase mattersp.8-9
PRCP componentsWeekly or biweekly meetings, reading and discussing papers, group meetings, a limited-scope real project, and CS 701p.10
Fall timingUse Fall to plan your first research involvement. Attend research talks (including the ones in CS 700), request meetings with faculty, explain your background, ask how to get involved. Don't ask "can you be my advisor?" yetp.11
Why faculty say noDedicated students with good backgrounds and work ethic are a research-active professor's most important asset, but they may still decline. Either they're already over-committed with advisees (proper advising takes a lot of time) or they saw a mismatch during the PRCPp.12
Positive signs to facultyThe more the better. Good background in the area, willingness to learn by reading and discussing papers, being independent and open to brainstorming, proposing good new ideas, ability to join their actual research right away, even in a limited wayp.13
Your side of the PRCPLook for answers to the critical questions before committing, but don't expect them after one meeting or a few weeksp.14
Questions you should askIs their research style a match, and can you adapt if not (p.15). "Making progress" is vague and peer-reviewed research is the ultimate objective, so ask what submission or publication numbers and pace they expect, without underestimating how productive you'll become (p.16). How they interact (regular meetings, as needed, guidance by a postdoc), whether you want a hands-on or hands-off advisor, and funding odds if a GRA next year matters to you (p.17). How they pick students' projects and dissertation topics (p.18)p.15-18
Co-advisingPossible with two faculty, but only one is the official advisor. The thesis names both. The two professors' areas are usually related but complementary. Ask a potential advisor whether they're open to co-advising and whether they've done it before. It has pros and consp.19
Changing advisorTechnically possible and sometimes unavoidable, but a last resort. That's why the PRCP mattersp.20
Wrap-upMost faculty take their time before accepting new students. You should think carefully too, but do your homework and show you're a committed student once you start talking with someonep.21
More adviceTalks from the annual CRA-W Grad Cohort workshop for PhD students are online under "Resources from Past Years". The department may sponsor you to attend a future onep.22

The three misconceptions on p.7 are good exam fodder. You can't wait until your last semester before the 24-credit deadline, a faculty member you pick won't automatically agree, and they won't necessarily answer in a few weeks.

Worked example

Scenario. In week two of Fall, a first-year student emails a professor they've never met. The email says "Can you be my PhD advisor?" The student figures that since the deadline is 24 credits, they'll wait for a reply and won't contact anyone else in the meantime.

Judged against the slides.

Asking "can you be my PhD advisor?" this early is exactly what p.11 says to avoid. The recommended move is to read the professor's recent papers first, then ask for a meeting to explain your background and ask how you could get involved (p.6, p.11).

Treating the 24-credit deadline as room to delay is the first misconception on p.7. A PRCP can take a semester or more (p.8), so starting late can push you past the deadline.

Expecting a fast answer is the third misconception on p.7. Faculty take their time before accepting new students (p.21). Waiting on one person also wastes time, and the CS701 deck says the same thing about project supervisors, where you should contact several faculty in parallel (CS701 p.21).

Easy to get wrong

  • The academic advisor you get at admission isn't your research advisor. The research advisor needs separate paperwork within 24 credits (Advisor p.3).
  • PRCP is not an official GMU procedure. It's a name Prof. Aydin made up for the presentation (p.9).
  • With co-advising, both names go in the thesis, but only one is the declared official advisor (p.19).
  • The deck is based on Prof. Hakan Aydin's talk, not Setia's own material (p.2).

CS 701 requirements

The idea

CS 701, Research Experience in Computer Science, is the spring course where every first-year PhD student does a semester-long research project with one faculty supervisor. The goal is to give you real research experience and a reason to work closely with a faculty member (CS701 p.2-3). It doesn't have class meetings. Instead there's a three-way contract between you, the supervisor and the department, written on a registration form.

What to know

RuleDetailCite
Who takes itMandatory for first-year PhD CS students, spring only, B or better needed. Prerequisite is B or better in CS 700CS701 p.2
What it involvesEach student pairs with one CS faculty supervisor for a semester-long research project. The intent is to let first-year PhD students interact with CS faculty and get research experiencep.3
End of semesterThe student submits a report to the 701 supervisor, every 701 student gives a short (about 10-minute) public presentation to the entire department, and the supervisor submits the grade to the registrarp.3
RegistrationForm with project description and deliverables, signed by student and supervisor, sent to csphd@gmu.edu and aydin@cs.gmu.edu, approved by the department, which may ask for revisions. It's a 3-way contract among student, supervisor and department. Once approved you get a CRN from the department and register on PatriotWeb. The paperwork, revisions and CRN all take timep.4-5
Form deadlineOct 31, 2026. Email the form to csphd@gmu.edu with "[CS701 form]" in the subject line and cc aydin@cs.gmu.edu. The slide stresses that timely submission is criticalp.7
Who can superviseAny tenured or tenure-track CS faculty, so assistant, associate or full professors. On the CS faculty directory, look for people without "Teaching" or "Instructor" in their title. A co-supervisor (CS-affiliated faculty count too) can co-sign, but the registrar records one CS faculty member, the primary supervisorp.6, p.22
Co-supervisor detailsTheir name goes next to the primary supervisor's on the form and they co-sign. You can acknowledge their contributions in your report and oral presentationp.22
MeetingsNo class meetings. You and the supervisor meet periodically or as needed. Any required meeting pattern (weekly, biweekly) goes on the form. Periodic meetings keep you in touch and let you discuss progress and unclear pointsp.8
ScaleNeedn't be groundbreaking or immediately publishable, but the effort must match a 3-credit semester-long course for first-year PhD students. Much more than a term project in a regular course, and you can't finish by sprinting in the last few weeks. Timely planning and progress during spring is criticalp.9
Literature reviewQuite likely needed before the real work starts, and an essential part of many 701 projects, but the project can't consist only of a literature review or survey of papersp.10
Hands-on componentRequired. Software, simulations or experiments. For a theory project, working on a proof for a non-trivial open problem or a non-trivial novel complexity analysis countsp.11
Links to the literatureImplementing a set of techniques from possibly several papers and evaluating them thoroughly, maybe on new performance metrics, is fine. Proposing new techniques or extending existing ones is also reasonable. Work out details with the supervisorp.12
Description fieldMore than a title, about a paragraph, and it must state the hands-on partp.13
DeliverablesA project report is the default and mandatory deliverable, submitted to the supervisor at the end of spring. List any other requirements, like software or hardware artefacts or periodic meetings. Ask the supervisor about expected report format and lengthp.14
Paper submission as deliverableNot allowed. "A draft paper to be revised and submitted in the future" is fine, and the report can be that draftp.15

The misconceptions on p.16-26 each flip a wrong belief. Your 701 supervisor doesn't automatically become your PhD advisor (p.16). Your dissertation doesn't have to be in the 701 area, so you can do 701 in area X and the dissertation in area Y (p.17). A successful 701 can be the first step toward an advisor relationship, especially if the cooperation goes well, but that isn't its primary intent, and many students end up with a different area and advisor (p.18). Your first choice might not be available, for various reasons (p.19). There's no guarantee a meeting and a decision will happen quickly (p.20). Don't wait for one faculty member's answer before contacting the next. Contact several at once, both to save time and to learn about different kinds of 701 projects (p.21). You can have co-supervisors (p.22). Waiting until late October is a big mistake (p.23). Your GRA sponsor is the natural supervisor but isn't required (p.24). You can't defer 701 to next year (p.25). The department won't assign you a supervisor, because the faculty member has to agree (p.26).

The suggested search on p.27 has four steps. First identify a few CS faculty in your preferred areas. Then contact them and request meetings about possible 701 projects. Next collect information about projects from the faculty who are available. Finally decide, fill out the form, get the faculty signature and submit it to csphd@gmu.edu and aydin@cs.gmu.edu. If nobody in the first group is available, pick a second group, possibly in a different area, and repeat.

Worked example

Scenario. A student's draft 701 form reads like this. The supervisor is listed in the directory as "Assistant Professor, Teaching". Description is "Federated learning." Activities are reading and summarizing 15 recent papers. The deliverable is a paper submitted to INFOCOM by May.

Judged against the rules.

The supervisor is a problem because the primary supervisor must be tenured or tenure-track, and directory titles with "Teaching" or "Instructor" don't qualify (CS701 p.6). At most that person could be a co-supervisor who co-signs the form, with a tenured or tenure-track CS faculty member as the primary (p.22).

The description fails because it's just a title. It needs about a paragraph and has to name the hands-on component (p.13).

The activities fail because a literature review alone isn't allowed (p.10). The project needs implementation, simulation or experiments (p.11).

The deliverable fails because a conference submission by the end of spring can't be listed (p.15). Rewrite it as a project report in the form of a draft paper to be revised and submitted later.

A fixed version might say the student will implement two client-selection schemes from recent papers, run simulations on a standard dataset, and compare convergence time, with a final report written as a draft paper and weekly meetings. That matches p.12, which says implementing techniques from several papers and evaluating them, possibly on new metrics, is a fine project.

Easy to get wrong

  • "Not required to be publishable" doesn't mean "small". The effort must match a full 3-credit course and is much more than a term project (CS701 p.9).
  • Co-supervisors are allowed, but registration is under one CS tenure-line faculty member (p.6, p.22).
  • The 701 supervisor and the PhD advisor are separate decisions with separate forms (p.16).
  • If you can't find a 701 project, there's no "take it next year" option. That's a catalog rule (p.25).

CS 700 term project

The idea

The CS 700 term project lives in the same deck as CS 701. It's an individual project where you pick an experimental CS problem, measure something quantitatively and analyze it with the statistics from this course (CS701 p.28-29). It's where the CI and hypothesis-testing material gets used on your own data.

What to know

ItemRequirementCite
ScopeIndividual. Pick an experimental CS problem involving quantitative analysis of a computer system, technique, algorithm or method. Comparing several techniques quantitatively is fine. You run the experiments or analysis and write a term project reportCS701 p.28
Must includeExperiments and/or simulations, analyzed with class techniques such as confidence intervals and regressionp.29
Topic choiceIdeally in your research area or tied to your 701 work, but that isn't required and isn't gradedp.29
Not acceptableA literature survey of quantitative or statistical techniques, or applying statistics to polls or surveysp.29
ProposalOne page, PDF on Canvas. The slide says midnight Sunday Oct 25, but Setia moved it to Mon Nov 2. Briefly describe the problem or algorithms you'll analyze, the experimental techniques you'll use, and the methodology you'll followp.30
Status updateBy Nov 15, reporting any changes to the project and any unexpected problemsp.30
PresentationThe whole Dec 7 class, 5 minutes each. Cover the problem, the techniques you implemented or simulated, and preliminary resultsp.31
Report6-8 page technical report on your full findings, ACM or IEEE conference template, due Dec 7 on Canvas. More detail on the expected structure is promised laterp.32

Worked example

Scenario. Three students pitch proposals. Which are acceptable?

Proposal A surveys 40 classmates on which IDE they prefer and builds confidence intervals on the proportions. This is not acceptable, because applying statistics to polls or surveys is explicitly ruled out (CS701 p.29). The statistics are fine, but there's no computer system being measured.

Proposal B writes a review of how confidence intervals and regression are used in systems papers. This is not acceptable either, since a literature survey of quantitative or statistical techniques is the other banned option (p.29). There are no experiments.

Proposal C times two hash-table implementations over 30 runs at each of four load factors and compares them with confidence intervals and a regression of time on load factor. This one is acceptable. It measures a technique quantitatively, runs experiments, uses class methods and compares alternatives (p.28-29).

Easy to get wrong

  • The term project is individual (CS701 p.28).
  • Linking it to your 701 area is encouraged but isn't a grading factor (p.29).
  • The slide gives Oct 25 for the proposal (p.30), but Setia moved it to Nov 2. The 701 form is due Oct 31 (p.7). Don't mix them up.
  • Dec 7 is both the presentation day and the report deadline (p.31-32). The status update comes earlier, by Nov 15 (p.30).
  • The report uses a conference paper template, either ACM or IEEE, at 6-8 pages (p.32).

Peer review and publishing in CS

The idea

On top of years of graduate study, a PhD needs original research that ends in writing and defending a dissertation, and that research has to be worthy of publication in peer-reviewed journals or conference proceedings (Pub p.2). Peer review means other experts ("peers") read your paper before it's published, which protects the integrity of science by filtering out invalid or weak work. Reviewers write their comments and evaluations independently, and journal editors or conference chairs make the final call, sometimes after discussion (p.3). The deck lists three venue types but walks through only the conference process in detail, so that's what most questions will be about.

Most PhD students publish their first peer-reviewed papers during or after year three (p.7). It takes that long because you have to build a strong background, identify a problem, solve it, master the skills of scholarly writing, and win against other researchers. Knowing the process early helps you appreciate how stiff the competition is, so you can build the skills to read, evaluate, write and present papers alongside your research (p.8).

What to know

Why publish. Publication shows that outside experts, not just you or your advisor, accept the quality of your work. It makes the work accessible, and your publication record is your main track record as a researcher, and the slide flags "quality vs. quantity" (Pub p.4). Quality is what counts, since "only the number matters" is listed as a misconception (p.9). Long-term impact is often correlated with how many citations a paper gets from other researchers. Important papers can collect hundreds or even thousands over time, and citation numbers are used to gauge the impact of researchers and even of venues (p.5).

h-index. A metric added on top of total publications and total citations. It's the largest \(h\) such that an author (or a journal) has at least \(h\) papers each cited at least \(h\) times (p.6). Sort citation counts in decreasing order and find the last rank \(i\) where the count is still \(\ge i\). The slide adds that a PhD student's h-index usually won't be high even at graduation, and it grows as you keep publishing good papers.

Venue types. Conferences, journals and workshops (p.10). The deck only details conferences. The journal rules you need come from the Ethics deck, which covers extending a conference paper into a journal paper (Ethics p.12, p.14).

Conference stageWhat happensCite
Call for papersA conference solicits papers on a specific topic (robotics, computer vision, operating systems). The CFP gives dates, topics, formatting rules and the organizing committee. Authors submit by a deadline and wait for accept or reject, and the notification includes comments from anonymous reviewers who sit on the program committee (PC)Pub p.11
SubmissionFollow the page and font limits and the IEEE or ACM template. Deadlines are sometimes extended, but that's less likely at first-tier conferences, so never count on itp.12
Acceptance rateThe percentage of papers accepted for presentation and publication. Lower rate generally means more prestige. First-tier conferences accept 10-25%, sometimes under 10%. Your advisor can tell you how competitive venues are, since picking the right one for finished work isn't trivialp.13
ReviewsThe PC chair assigns papers to PC members, who are professors or industry researchers. Each submits an independent review scoring writing quality, organization, technical contribution and novelty, plus a final recommendation on a five-point scale (Strong Accept, Accept, Neutral, Reject, Strong Reject). Note the impact of non-technical factorsp.14
PC meetingThe committee discusses all papers and reviews and decides, which can take all day or several days. There's a fixed, pre-set number of slots (from about 30 to 1000+ depending on the conference), so reasonable above-threshold papers still get rejected, and the PC effectively orders the good papersp.15
Area chairsBig conferences have ACs, appointed by the PC chair, overseeing sub-areasp.16
RebuttalSome papers get reviews early so authors can clear up misunderstandings. Replies count in the decision. Many papers skip this and get a direct decisionp.17
Camera-readyAccepted papers are resubmitted by a new deadline with reviewer comments addressed when possible, then presented by the authors at the conference, which is also a chance to network and travelp.18
RejectionDon't take it personally or treat it as failing an exam. Treat the comments as a chance to improve, and talk with your advisor and co-authors about revising and resubmitting to another venue or the same conference's next editionp.19
AnonymityAuthors never know the reviewers. In double-blind review, which is increasingly common, reviewers don't know the authors either. The CFP then asks authors to leave off names and affiliations and to avoid indirect hints. A non-compliant paper is rejected right away. The slide asks why double-blind is spreading and leaves the answer open. The usual reason is that hiding the authors keeps a famous name or institution from swaying the scores, so the paper is judged on its contentp.21

The misconceptions slide (p.9) lists eight beliefs, all false. You should be able to reject each one.

  1. A publication is basically a graduate-level project report.
  2. If the results are correct, the paper will be accepted.
  3. If the results are correct, important and novel, it will be accepted.
  4. If my advisor says "it's good", it will be accepted.
  5. If my paper is rejected, the conference made an error or is biased.
  6. My paper was rejected, so all that time and effort was wasted.
  7. What matters is just publishing, and the venue isn't important.
  8. What matters is the number of publications, not quality.

Worked example

h-index by hand. An author has six papers with citations 3, 25, 0, 5, 8, 5.

  1. Formula. \(h = \max\{\, i : c_{(i)} \ge i \,\}\). Here \(c_{(i)}\) is the citation count of the paper in rank \(i\) after sorting from most cited to least, and \(h\) is the largest rank whose paper still has at least that many citations (Pub p.6).
  2. Plug in. Sort 3, 25, 0, 5, 8, 5 from largest to smallest to get 25, 8, 5, 5, 3, 0. So \(c_{(1)} = 25\), \(c_{(2)} = 8\), \(c_{(3)} = 5\), \(c_{(4)} = 5\), \(c_{(5)} = 3\), \(c_{(6)} = 0\).
  3. Work it out. Compare each count with its rank, as in the table below. \(25 \ge 1\), \(8 \ge 2\), \(5 \ge 3\), \(5 \ge 4\) all pass. \(3 \ge 5\) fails.
  4. Result. The last rank that passes is 4, so \(h = 4\).
Rank \(i\)CitationsCount \(\ge i\)?
125yes
28yes
35yes
45yes
53no, 3 < 5
60no

The last rank that passes is 4, so \(h = 4\). Four papers have at least 4 citations each, but there aren't five papers with at least 5 each, because the fifth paper has only 3. Notice that the 25-citation paper doesn't raise \(h\) above 4. One huge paper can't carry the index by itself.

Check with the slide's own example, citations 10, 9, 8, 6, 3, 1, which are already sorted. Compare each with its rank. \(10 \ge 1\), \(9 \ge 2\), \(8 \ge 3\), \(6 \ge 4\) all pass, and \(3 \ge 5\) fails. So \(h = 4\), matching Pub p.6.

Easy to get wrong

  • Correct results aren't enough, and correct, important and novel results still aren't a guarantee. The number of slots is fixed and writing quality and organization are scored too (Pub p.9, p.14-15).
  • Program committee members are anonymous to authors in every review model. Double-blind adds that reviewers don't know the authors (p.11, p.21).
  • Rebuttals don't happen for every paper (p.17).
  • When computing h, the answer is a count of papers, not a citation number. Don't report the citation count at the cutoff.

Research misconduct, self-plagiarism and dual submission

The idea

The goal of a PhD is original research that contributes to your field, but you have to get there ethically and with the highest standards of integrity (Ethics p.2). As a student, coursework falls under the GMU Honor Code, but your research, theses and dissertations fall under GMU Policy 4007, Misconduct in Research and Scholarship (p.3, p.16). IEEE, ACM and IFIP each have a Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct for their own submissions and publications (p.4). Much of the deck is adapted from the IEEE Author Ethics Guidelines (p.5). IEEE keeps a list of penalized authors who can't publish for extended periods, and it informs their institutions, which usually add their own sanctions (p.6). The three core offenses are fabrication, falsification and plagiarism.

What to know

TermPolicy 4007 definitionCite
FabricationMaking up data or results and recording or reporting themEthics p.20
FalsificationManipulating research materials, equipment or processes, or changing or omitting data or results, so the research isn't accurately representedp.20
PlagiarismAppropriating another person's ideas, processes, results or words without appropriate creditp.20
Not misconductHonest error or differences of opinionp.18

Policy 4007 covers fabrication, falsification or plagiarism in proposing, performing, reviewing or reporting research, and fabricated or falsified credentials on funding applications and manuscripts can count too (p.18). GMU's Office of Research Integrity and Assurance (ORIA) monitors and investigates misconduct (p.15).

Who Policy 4007 covers. All full-time and part-time university employees and anyone affiliated with GMU, graduate students doing research or scholarship that produces reports, conference papers, publications or creative works listing a GMU affiliation, undergraduates in sponsored research, and anyone at GMU who teaches, researches or does scholarship. For graduate students, academic misconduct goes solely through the Honor Code, with two exceptions that go through Policy 4007. Those are research activities, whether sponsored or not, and master's theses and doctoral dissertations (p.16).

How Policy 4007 runs. Every research-misconduct allegation is handled under it. The university aims for an impartial, unbiased proceeding, chooses investigators for relevant expertise, screens them beforehand for bias or personal, professional or financial conflicts of interest with anyone involved, and expects everyone to keep things confidential and cooperate (p.17). The policy sets rules to receive and review allegations, runs inquiries and investigations through committees and hearings, and communicates the results (p.19). GMU also offers in-person Responsible Conduct in Research (RCR) training through ORIA (p.21).

Citing sources. IEEE defines plagiarism as using another researcher's ideas, results or words without explicitly acknowledging the original author and source. It calls plagiarism in any form unacceptable and a serious breach of professional conduct, with potentially severe ethical and legal consequences (p.7). Verbatim text needs quotation marks plus a citation (p.8). Paraphrasing or summarizing with a citation is usually better (p.9). Copying the sentence with no quotes and no citation is plagiarism (p.10). Data, results, graphics and tables need a citation too (p.11).

Accurate reporting. The slide's short versions are fabrication as inventing data or results, and falsification as changing or omitting data or results or manipulating research equipment or software. Don't drop or tweak trends you can't explain yet, because another group probably has a similar implementation and will get the real results (p.13).

Self-plagiarism. The same rules apply to your own published work. Rephrase instead of pasting, except when extending your own conference paper into a journal submission (p.12).

Dual or concurrent submission. A submission can't be under review or already published elsewhere, and that includes papers with substantial overlap. People do it to raise their odds or to get two papers from one piece of work. It's not allowed, it's often caught, and authors get severe sanctions (Pub p.20, Ethics p.14). If you're unsure whether two papers overlap too much, check with the organizers and your advisor (Ethics p.14).

Conference to journal. The journal version needs substantially more material (typically 30% more for IEEE), and it must cite and attach the conference version with an explanation of the differences (Ethics p.14).

Worked example

Scenario. A student has four situations in one semester. Classify each.

(a) Of 30 timing runs, two are three times slower than the rest. The student can't explain them and quietly deletes them before computing the CI. This is falsification, because omitting data so the record isn't accurate is in the definition (Ethics p.20), and p.13 says not to drop trends you can't explain. The honest fix is to report all 30, or to report the exclusion and the reason.

(b) The student's simulator crashed for one configuration, so they fill that table cell with a value interpolated from neighbors and don't say so. That's fabrication, since a result that was never measured is being reported as one (p.20).

(c) The student pastes the related-work section from their own published workshop paper into a new conference paper. That's self-plagiarism (p.12). They need to rewrite it and cite the earlier paper.

(d) The student turns a 10-page conference paper into an IEEE journal submission. The slide's rule of thumb is about 30% more material (p.14).

  1. Formula. Journal size \(\approx L \times (1 + 0.30)\) and new material \(\approx L \times 0.30\), where \(L\) is the conference paper's length.
  2. Plug in. \(L = 10\) pages, so \(10 \times 1.3\) and \(10 \times 0.3\).
  3. Work it out. \(10 \times 1.3 = 13\) pages in total, and \(10 \times 0.3 = 3\) pages new.
  4. Result. Roughly 13 pages' worth of material, about 3 pages of it genuinely new.

They also must cite and attach the conference paper with a note on what changed (p.14). Reusing their own conference text here is the allowed exception (p.12).

Easy to get wrong

  • Graduate coursework misconduct goes through the Honor Code. Research activities and theses or dissertations go through Policy 4007 (Ethics p.16).
  • An honest mistake isn't research misconduct (p.18).
  • Falsification includes omission, not just changing numbers (p.20).
  • The conference-to-journal exception covers your own conference paper only, and it still requires citing and attaching it (p.12, p.14).
  • Submitting the same paper to two venues at once is banned even if you plan to withdraw one later (Pub p.20).

What counts as plagiarism, per Sean Luke

The idea

Luke's tech report walks through 19 examples built from one paragraph of his own paper and says which ones are plagiarism. His bottom line is that you can plagiarize two things, someone's words or their descriptive idea, and light rewording doesn't fix either one unless the ideas are well-known and general (Luke's tech report, opening section). He ranks plagiarism as the second worst thing you can do in academic research, after faking your experiments. People who plagiarize are often fired and their careers end, coauthors publicly denounce them to protect their own names, and PhD students are typically disowned by their advisors and thrown out of the program. Because it's that serious, he says you should avoid even the appearance of plagiarism.

He also separates plagiarism, which is an ethical offense, from copyright violation, which is a legal one you can be sued for. Some of his examples are cited and not plagiarism but are still copyright violations. His footnote 2 explains why he can reuse his own ACM-copyrighted papers as examples. ACM's policy lets authors reuse any portion of their own work without fee in their future works, provided the ACM citation and copyright notice are included, so even your own published material comes with a citation obligation.

What to know

Plagiarism hurts two groups, the source who loses credit and the readers who are misled about who wrote it. It can't appear anywhere in a paper, including the abstract, introduction, conclusion, tutorial or explanatory material and especially the previous-work section. Even if all your original contribution is in your own words, plagiarism anywhere else in the text is unacceptable (Luke's tech report, opening section). All the examples reuse one paragraph, one equation and one figure from page 3 of Luke and Panait's foraging paper, and Examples 18 and 19 use algorithms from a second Luke paper.

Luke's exampleWhat the writer didVerdict
1Pasted the source textPlagiarism in its purest form
2Pasted text, added [1]Plagiarism. Citing doesn't let you present their words as your own
3Pasted text, named the authors and citedPlagiarism. The idea is credited, but the words are still stolen
4Used less of their text, a bit over a phrase, citedDon't. Still open to accusation, because it's their invented text passed off as your speech. Under a phrase is relatively safe
5Lightly reworded, same structure, citedPlagiarism of the idea. Never reword "to avoid plagiarizing". If you're doing that you're probably still plagiarizing, so write in your own words from the start
6Rewrote the idea in own words without looking at the original, citedMuch better, but he says still not as good as quoting (Example 7)
7Block quote, offset, citedNot plagiarism and fine. But a very large quote without good reason makes reviewers wonder why you lean on someone else's text and can lead to copyright charges
8Inline quote in quotation marks, citedOK. Larger quotes should be offset as in Example 7 so the reader can't mistake them for your words
9Copied an equation with no attributionPlagiarism. Treat math as very dense text, so a moderate equation needs the care of several sentences
10"As Luke and Panait have shown [1], the update rule is" followed by the equationDangerously close. It credits the idea but doesn't make clear the equation text is theirs. Don't do it
11"The update rule (from Luke and Panait [1]) is"Acceptable. For a few equations say "This and the next four equations are from [1]". Standard folk notation like the Bellman equation needn't be cited, but be conservative and when in doubt cite
12Many equations, "cited, with permission"More than a few equations without permission looks like padding with the source's ideas. Ask permission from the author, and from the publisher too if it holds copyright. With permission and the source shown it's safe, but not for large pieces of text
13Copied a figure, no citationPlagiarism of both ideas and art
14Copied a figure with citationNot plagiarism, but almost as bad because it's copyright violation. Figures differ from text for two reasons. They stand apart from the text, so they look free-standing, and more importantly they're the author's creation and take much more effort
15Figure "reproduced with permission from [1]" in the captionCorrect. Needs author and possibly publisher permission, however small the figure. Permission must be stated in the caption and may also go in the body text
16Permitted figure but copied the original captionPlagiarism. Treat captions exactly like body text, and the citation doesn't let you reuse their words as yours
17Bibliography entry identical to another paper'sFine. Formats are rigid, so there's essentially one way to write it
18Small algorithm, clearly marked as from [2]OK, treated like a large equation. Better to rewrite it in your own words. Getting permission is wise but not necessary if it's very small
19Large algorithm, a third of a page or more, with permissionTreat like a figure, so you need author and publisher permission. Even then it looks like padding, which reviewers dislike, so think twice

Worked example

Is this paraphrase plagiarism? The source is the Smith et al. [10] sentence from Ethics p.8, which reads "Fault tolerance in autonomous driving systems is expected to become the most important research challenge in the years to come, especially with increased hardware complexity."

(a) "Fault tolerance in autonomous driving systems is expected to become the most important research challenge in the years to come, especially with increased hardware complexity." No quotes, no citation. Plagiarism. Ethics p.10 gives exactly this case, and it matches Luke's Example 1.

(b) The same sentence with [10] added at the end. Still plagiarism. Citing doesn't let you present the words as your own, which is Luke's Example 2.

(c) "In the coming years, fault tolerance in self-driving systems is predicted to become the biggest research challenge, particularly as hardware gets more complex [10]." Each phrase is swapped for a synonym, but the order and structure match the original. This is Luke's Example 5, light rewording with the same structure, so it's plagiarism even with the citation.

(d) Smith et al. [10] write, "Fault tolerance in autonomous driving systems is expected to become the most important research challenge in the years to come." Quotation marks plus citation, so it's fine (Ethics p.8, Luke's Example 8).

(e) "Fault tolerance is a very significant challenge for next generation autonomous driving systems [10]." This is the slide's own recommended paraphrase (Ethics p.9). It's a real summary in new words with a citation, like Luke's Example 6. Luke's tip is to write it from your own understanding without looking at the original, so you don't end up with (c).

Easy to get wrong

  • A citation doesn't make copied words OK. Luke's Examples 2 and 3 are plagiarism even though they cite.
  • Not plagiarism doesn't mean allowed. A cited figure without permission is copyright violation (Luke's Example 14).
  • Equations count as text. An uncited equation is plagiarism (Luke's Example 9).
  • Figure captions copied from the source are plagiarism even when the figure itself has permission (Luke's Example 16).
  • Matching bibliography entries are fine (Luke's Example 17).
  • Rewriting without looking at the original (Example 6) is "much better", but Luke ranks a properly marked quote (Example 7) above it.
  • Permission lets you reuse many equations, but Luke says it still doesn't cover large pieces of text (Luke's Example 12).

AI tools in research papers

The idea

Generative AI is generally allowed in papers as long as you disclose it in detail and take full responsibility for everything it produced, including any plagiarism or falsification that results (Ethics p.22). The catch is that every publisher and venue words its rules differently and the rules keep changing, so you have to check the current call for papers.

What to know

SourceRuleCite
IEEEDisclose AI content (text, figures, images, code) in the acknowledgments. Name the AI system, identify which sections used it and how much. Editing and grammar help is generally outside the policy, but disclosure is still recommendedEthics p.23
ACMGenerative AI such as ChatGPT can't be an author. Using it to create content is permitted but must be fully disclosed, for example with an acknowledgements statement like "ChatGPT was utilized to generate sections of this Work, including text, tables, graphs, code, data, citations". When unsure, err on the side of caution and disclose. Basic word processing that suggests replacement text, spelling or grammar checks, and translation tools are exempt and needn't be disclosed. The line between these and tools like MS-Word or Grammarly is blurring, so ACM says the policy will be updatedp.24
ElsevierAI only to improve readability and language, not to replace key authoring tasks like producing scientific insights, drawing conclusions or giving clinical recommendations. Disclose it in the manuscript, and a statement will appear in the published work. Don't list AI as an author or co-author or cite it as an author, since authorship carries responsibilities only humans can holdp.25
ICML 2024 CFPLLMs allowed as general-purpose writing aids. Authors are fully responsible, including for content that counts as plagiarism or fabrication. LLMs can't be authorsp.26

The takeaway on p.27 is to check the latest CFP or journal site first. If it says nothing, the organization's general rules (IEEE, ACM) apply. If you're unsure, ask the organizers and your advisor.

Worked example

Scenario. A student used ChatGPT to draft two paragraphs of related work and used a grammar checker on the whole paper. One of the ChatGPT paragraphs cites a paper that doesn't exist. The student lists "ChatGPT" as a co-author to be transparent. The CFP says nothing about AI.

Judged against the slides.

Since the CFP is silent, the publisher's general rules apply (Ethics p.27).

Listing ChatGPT as a co-author is wrong under ACM, Elsevier and ICML, which all say AI can't be an author (p.24-26). The right way to be transparent is a disclosure in the acknowledgments.

The drafted related-work paragraphs must be disclosed. IEEE wants the system named and the sections identified with how they were used (p.23), and ACM requires full disclosure (p.24).

The grammar checker is exempt under ACM (p.24). Under IEEE it's outside the policy, though disclosure is recommended (p.23).

The fake citation is the student's fault, not the tool's. Authors take full responsibility, including for fabrication (p.22, p.26), and making up a source is reporting something that doesn't exist.

Easy to get wrong

  • Disclosure doesn't shift responsibility. You own every AI error (Ethics p.22).
  • IEEE and ACM treat grammar tools slightly differently. ACM says no disclosure needed, IEEE says it's recommended (p.23-24).
  • The disclosure goes in the acknowledgments section, not in the author list (p.23-24).

Why CS 700 covers the research process, and course logistics

The idea

CS 700 used to be purely the required "systems" counterpart to the required theory courses CS 583 and CS 600, teaching experimental methods and quantitative techniques. In 2018 the PhD program was restructured to cut students' time to completion, so the course now also introduces the research process (Intro p.2). That's why lectures like the advisor, CS 701, publishing and ethics talks sit next to confidence intervals and hypothesis tests. One explicit goal is matching students with faculty for CS 701 (p.3).

What to know

ItemDetailCite
Course topicsAn overview of the research process (including meeting CS faculty and matching for CS 701), plus the "old CS 700" of experimental techniques (measurement, simulation) and statistical and quantitative methodsIntro p.3
PhD-process topicsWhat a PhD is and why get one, finding an advisor and working well with one, picking a dissertation topic, finding the right job (academia vs. industry), research ethics and integrity, how and when to use AI, and tools like LaTeX, plotting and scripting. Reading, reviewing papers and giving talks are left to advanced CS 800 seminarsp.4
Class formatEarly weeks spend the second half of class discussing one of those topics, with pointers to CRA workshop talks. Faculty research presentations run across the semester to help you find a 701 match. Guest lectures near the end cover experimental techniques in specific areas like software engineering and MLp.5
Grading40% homework, 35% midterm, 25% term projectp.8
RulesAll assignments individual under the Mason Honor Code. Lost points can be won back by resubmitting within two weeks. The term project should ideally use experimental methods tied to your 701 researchp.8
LogisticsMaterial on Canvas, Piazza for assignment discussion, office hours Wednesday 1:30-2:30 PM in Nguyen 5305, GTA Mohammadreza Noeip.8
LaTeXRequired for all homework submissionsp.26

Worked example

Course grade by hand. Suppose you average 90 on homework, score 80 on the midterm and 85 on the term project. Weight each by its share from Intro p.8.

  1. Formula. Grade \(= w_H H + w_M M + w_P P\), where \(H, M, P\) are the homework, midterm and project scores and \(w_H, w_M, w_P\) their weights.
  2. Plug in. \(0.40 \times 90 + 0.35 \times 80 + 0.25 \times 85\).
  3. Work it out. \(0.40 \times 90 = 36\). \(0.35 \times 80 = 28\). \(0.25 \times 85 = 21.25\). Then \(36 + 28 = 64\) and \(64 + 21.25 = 85.25\).
  4. Result. 85.25. The weights sum to \(0.40 + 0.35 + 0.25 = 1\), so this is a weighted average of the three scores, which is why it has to land between 80 and 90.

Easy to get wrong

  • The midterm is 35%, not the largest share. Homework is 40% (Intro p.8).
  • The resubmission window for lost homework points is two weeks (p.8).
  • Linking the term project to 701 is "ideally" on p.8 and explicitly not graded on CS701 p.29, so it's encouraged, never required.

Check yourself

1. True or false. A PhD student must declare their research advisor before taking CS 701.

False. The research advisor is declared with separate paperwork within the first 24 credits (Advisor p.3), and the 701 supervisor doesn't automatically become your advisor (CS701 p.16). Many students end up with a different advisor and area than their 701 project (CS701 p.18).

2. What is the PRCP, and why does the advisor lecture say it matters?

It's the Preliminary Research Co-Operation Phase, an informal semester or more of working together before a professor agrees to advise you. It lets both sides check for a good fit (Advisor p.8). It's not an official procedure (Advisor p.9), and it matters because changing advisors later is a last resort (Advisor p.20).

3. A student proposes a CS 701 project that is a careful survey of 20 papers on GPU scheduling. Will the department approve it? What deliverable is mandatory?

No. A 701 project can't consist only of a literature review (CS701 p.10) and needs a hands-on part like implementation, simulation or experiments (CS701 p.11). The mandatory deliverable is a project report (CS701 p.14).

4. Can "submit a paper to a conference by May" be listed as a CS 701 deliverable? What's the allowed alternative?

No. A conference or journal submission completed by the end of spring can't be a deliverable. "A draft paper to be revised and submitted for publication in the future" is fine, and the report can take that form (CS701 p.15).

5. Name the two kinds of CS 700 term project that are explicitly not acceptable, and give the page count and template for the final report.

A literature survey of quantitative or statistical techniques, and applying statistics to polls or surveys (CS701 p.29). The report is 6-8 pages in an ACM or IEEE conference template, due Dec 7 on Canvas (CS701 p.32).

6. Compute the h-index of an author whose papers have 12, 2, 7, 4, 9, 1, 4 citations.
  1. Formula. \(h\) is the largest rank \(i\) such that the \(i\)-th most cited paper has at least \(i\) citations, \(h = \max\{\, i : c_{(i)} \ge i \,\}\) (Pub p.6).
  2. Plug in. Sort from largest to smallest to get 12, 9, 7, 4, 4, 2, 1. So ranks 1 to 7 hold 12, 9, 7, 4, 4, 2, 1.
  3. Work it out. Rank 1, \(12 \ge 1\) passes. Rank 2, \(9 \ge 2\) passes. Rank 3, \(7 \ge 3\) passes. Rank 4, \(4 \ge 4\) passes. Rank 5, \(4 \ge 5\) fails.
  4. Result. \(h = 4\). Four papers have at least 4 citations each, but there aren't five with at least 5 each.
7. True or false. If a paper's results are correct, important and novel, a good conference will accept it.

False. That's listed as a misconception (Pub p.9). Conferences have a fixed number of slots, so reasonable above-threshold papers still get rejected (Pub p.15), and reviewers also score writing quality and organization (Pub p.14).

8. In double-blind review, what must authors do, and what happens if they don't?

They leave their names and affiliations off the paper and avoid indirect hints about who they are. A paper that doesn't comply is rejected right away (Pub p.21).

9. Define fabrication, falsification and plagiarism as GMU Policy 4007 does. Is an honest mistake in an analysis research misconduct?

Fabrication is making up data or results and reporting them. Falsification is manipulating materials, equipment or processes, or changing or omitting data, so the record isn't accurate. Plagiarism is taking someone's ideas, processes, results or words without proper credit (Ethics p.20). An honest error is explicitly not misconduct (Ethics p.18).

10. Is reusing text from your own published paper ever allowed? What must a journal extension of a conference paper include?

Normally no. Self-plagiarism follows the same rules, so you rephrase. The exception is extending your conference paper into a journal paper (Ethics p.12). The journal version needs substantially more material (typically 30% more for IEEE) and must cite and attach the conference version with an explanation of the differences (Ethics p.14).

11. Per Sean Luke, a student copies a paragraph word for word, writes "As Luke and Panait have pointed out" before it and puts [1] after it. Plagiarism or not?

Plagiarism. That's his Example 3. Naming and citing the authors credits their idea, but the words are still theirs presented as yours. It would be fine only in quotation marks or as an offset block quote (Examples 7 and 8). The slide rule is the same, verbatim text needs quotes plus a citation (Ethics p.8).

12. Per Luke, you include another paper's figure with a citation in the caption. Is it plagiarism? Is it acceptable?

It isn't plagiarism, but it's not acceptable because it's copyright violation (his Example 14). Figures take real effort to create, so you need the author's permission and possibly the publisher's, stated in the caption, for example "reproduced with permission from [1]" (Example 15). Copying the original caption word for word would still be plagiarism (Example 16).

13. Can ChatGPT be listed as a co-author on an ACM paper? Does a grammar checker need to be disclosed?

No, generative AI can't be listed as an author, and its use to create content must be fully disclosed, for example in the acknowledgements. Basic spelling and grammar tools are exempt from disclosure under ACM (Ethics p.24). IEEE also puts grammar help outside its policy but recommends disclosing it anyway (Ethics p.23).

14. True or false. If you already know a professor well from prior publications or their class, they will probably still require a full PRCP before advising you.

False. The PRCP slide says many faculty want an initial unofficial phase unless they already know the student from prior publications or class work (Advisor p.8). Note the easy-case slide still expects some initial research involvement before you officially declare (Advisor p.5).

15. True or false. The department will assign you a CS 701 supervisor if you can't find one, and you can take 701 the following year instead.

False on both counts. The faculty member has to agree to supervise, so the department won't assign one (CS701 p.26), and deferring 701 to next year isn't possible under the catalog (CS701 p.25). The fix is to contact a second group of faculty, possibly in another area (CS701 p.27).

16. Name the four scoring criteria a PC member uses and the five possible final recommendations.

Writing quality, organization, technical contribution and novelty. The recommendation is Strong Accept, Accept, Neutral, Reject or Strong Reject (Pub p.14). Two of the four criteria are non-technical, which is why the slide warns about non-technical factors.

17. True or false. "A publication is essentially a graduate-level project report."

False. It's the first misconception on Pub p.9. A peer-reviewed paper has to beat other researchers' work for a fixed number of slots (Pub p.15) and be judged worthy by independent experts (Pub p.3), which a project report never faces.

18. True or false. A graduate student accused of misconduct in their dissertation research is handled under the Honor Code.

False. Graduate academic misconduct goes through the Honor Code only, except for research activities (sponsored or not) and master's theses and doctoral dissertations, which go through Policy 4007 (Ethics p.16). ORIA handles those investigations (Ethics p.15).

19. What are the steps Policy 4007 sets out for handling an allegation, and how does it protect fairness?

It receives and reviews allegations, conducts inquiries and investigations through committees and hearings, and communicates the results (Ethics p.19). Investigators are chosen for relevant expertise and screened beforehand for bias and personal, professional or financial conflicts of interest, and everyone is expected to keep the proceeding confidential (Ethics p.17).

20. Per Sean Luke, why is copying a cited figure treated differently from copying cited text, and what's the fix?

A cited figure is still copyright violation because a figure is the author's own creation, and the fix is to get permission and say so in the caption. Luke gives two reasons figures differ from text. A figure is set off from the text, so it looks free-standing, and more importantly it takes far more effort to make than a sentence. So a citation alone isn't enough (his Example 14). The fix is author permission, plus publisher permission if needed, stated in the caption, however small the figure (Example 15).

21. True or false. Under Elsevier's policy, you may use AI to draw scientific conclusions as long as you disclose it.

False. Elsevier allows AI only to improve readability and language, not to replace key authoring tasks like producing insights or drawing scientific conclusions. Disclosure is required on top of that limit, not instead of it (Ethics p.25).

22. What weight does the midterm carry in CS 700, and what happens if you lose points on a homework?

The midterm is 35%, with homework 40% and the term project 25%. Lost homework points can be recovered by resubmitting within two weeks (Intro p.8).
