    Meaning. Inside the BS-only column, 17 of the 23 started in Fall, so about 3 in 4 BS-only students are Fall starters.

Final answer. 0.75, 0.425, 0.235 and 0.739.

How this could show up on the exam

The conditional-probability idea comes back in the p-value. A p-value is \(P[\text{data this extreme} \mid H_0]\), not \(P[H_0 \mid \text{data}]\) (HT p.13-14). The cohort shows the two directions differ. \(P[\text{Spring} \mid \text{MS}] = \frac{4/40}{17/40} = 4/17 = 0.235\), counted inside the MS column. \(P[\text{MS} \mid \text{Spring}] = \frac{4/40}{10/40} = 4/10 = 0.4\), counted inside the Spring row. Same top number, different group on the bottom.

Problem 7. \(X\) is uniform on [10, 90]. Find the mean and standard deviation, and \(P[X \lt 20]\), \(P[X \gt 60]\), \(P[20 \lt X \lt 30]\).

Question. Assume a random variable has uniform distribution between 10 and 90. What is the mean? What is the standard deviation? What is the probability that the random variable will take a value
  • Less than 20?
  • More than 60?
  • Between 20 and 30?
In plain words. Uniform means every value between 10 and 90 is equally likely, so the density is a flat rectangle. Any probability is just the width of the stretch you care about divided by the full width 80.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Mean. Formula. For uniform on \([a, b]\), \(\mu = \dfrac{a + b}{2}\), the midpoint, where \(a\) is the low end and \(b\) the high end.
    Plug in. \(a = 10\), \(b = 90\), so \(\mu = (10 + 90)/2\).
    Arithmetic. \(100/2 = 50\).
    Meaning. The average value is the middle of the range, 50.
  2. Standard deviation. Formula. \(\sigma^2 = \dfrac{(b - a)^2}{12}\), so \(\sigma = \dfrac{b - a}{\sqrt{12}}\).
    Plug in. \(\sigma^2 = (90 - 10)^2/12 = 80^2/12\).
    Arithmetic. \(80^2 = 6400\), and \(6400/12 = 533.33\). For the root, \(\sqrt{12} \approx 3.464\) (since \(3.464^2 = 12.00\)), so \(\sigma = 80/3.464 \approx 23.09\).
    Meaning. Values are typically about 23 away from 50.
  3. Probabilities. Formula. The density is \(f(x) = \dfrac{1}{b - a}\), so \[ P[c \lt X \lt d] = \frac{d - c}{b - a}, \] where \([c, d]\) is the stretch you want (clipped to stay inside \([a, b]\)).
  4. \(P[X \lt 20]\). The stretch is from 10 to 20. Plug in \(\frac{20 - 10}{80} = \frac{10}{80} = 0.125\). One eighth of the range.
  5. \(P[X \gt 60]\). The stretch is from 60 to 90. Plug in \(\frac{90 - 60}{80} = \frac{30}{80} = 0.375\).
  6. \(P[20 \lt X \lt 30]\). The stretch is from 20 to 30. Plug in \(\frac{30 - 20}{80} = \frac{10}{80} = 0.125\).

Final answer. Mean 50, standard deviation 23.09, then 0.125, 0.375 and 0.125.

How this could show up on the exam

If a single reading is rounded to the nearest tick, the rounding error is uniform between \(-\Delta\) and \(+\Delta\) with \(\Delta\) half a tick. For 1 ms resolution, \(a = -0.5\) and \(b = 0.5\). Mean \((-0.5 + 0.5)/2 = 0\). Standard deviation \((0.5 - (-0.5))/\sqrt{12} = 1/3.464 \approx 0.29\) ms. An interval timer is different, since both ends are quantized and the error can reach a full tick either way (CI p.9-10).

Problem 8. \(Z\) is standard normal. Find \(P[0.9 \lt Z \lt 1.3]\), \(P[Z \gt 0.9]\) and \(P[Z \gt -0.9]\).

Question. Assume a random variable has standard normal distribution N(0,1). What is the probability that this random variable will take on a value
  • Between 0.9 and 1.3
  • Greater than 0.9
  • Greater than −0.9
Explain how you obtained your results.
In plain words. The normal table only gives the area to the left of a number, \(\Phi(z)\). Every other area is built from that by subtracting two table values, taking 1 minus a value, or using the fact that the bell curve is symmetric around 0.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Table lookups. \(\Phi(z) = P[Z \lt z]\). Row 1.3, column 0.00 gives \(\Phi(1.30) = 0.9032\). Row 0.9, column 0.00 gives \(\Phi(0.90) = 0.8159\).
  2. Between. Formula. \(P[a \lt Z \lt b] = \Phi(b) - \Phi(a)\), the area left of \(b\) minus the area left of \(a\).
    Plug in. \(\Phi(1.3) - \Phi(0.9) = 0.9032 - 0.8159\).
    Arithmetic. \(0.9032 - 0.8159 = 0.0873\).
    Meaning. About 9% of the area sits between 0.9 and 1.3.
  3. Upper tail. Formula. \(P[Z \gt a] = 1 - \Phi(a)\), since the total area is 1.
    Plug in. \(1 - 0.8159\).
    Arithmetic. \(1.0000 - 0.8159 = 0.1841\).
    Meaning. About 18% of values are above 0.9.
  4. Above a negative number. Formula. By symmetry \(P[Z \gt -a] = P[Z \lt a] = \Phi(a)\). The area right of \(-0.9\) is the mirror image of the area left of \(+0.9\).
    Plug in. \(\Phi(0.9) = 0.8159\).
    Meaning. About 82% of values are above \(-0.9\).

Final answer. 0.0873, 0.1841 and 0.8159.

How this could show up on the exam

This is exactly the lookup for a p-value. In HW5 the statistic was \(Z = 1.80\). Row 1.8, column 0.00 gives \(\Phi(1.80) = 0.9641\). The upper tail is \(1 - 0.9641 = 0.0359\), and the two-sided p-value doubles it to \(0.0718\) (HT p.13).

Problem 9. Daily August highs are normal with mean 85 and standard deviation 6. Using z-scores, find \(P[83 \lt X \lt 88]\), \(P[X \gt 90]\) and \(P[X \lt 82]\).

Question. Over the course of several years, in a certain city, it is observed that the highest daily temperature in August, in Fahrenheit, is normally distributed with mean = 85 and standard deviation = 6. What is the probability that a randomly selected August day’s highest temperature is
  • Between 83 and 88
  • Greater than 90
  • Smaller than 82
Solve problem 9 using z scores. Show your work.
In plain words. The table only covers the standard normal (mean 0, standard deviation 1). So you first convert each temperature into "how many standard deviations from the mean" (its z-score), then do Problem 8 again.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Formula. \(z = \dfrac{x - \mu}{\sigma}\), where \(x\) is the temperature, \(\mu = 85\) the mean and \(\sigma = 6\) the standard deviation.
  2. Between 83 and 88.
    Plug in. \(z_{83} = (83 - 85)/6\) and \(z_{88} = (88 - 85)/6\).
    Arithmetic. \(z_{83} = -2/6 = -0.333 \approx -0.33\). \(z_{88} = 3/6 = 0.50\).
    Table. \(\Phi(0.50)\) is row 0.5, column 0.00, which is 0.6915. \(\Phi(-0.33)\) isn't in most tables, so use symmetry, \(\Phi(-0.33) = 1 - \Phi(0.33)\). \(\Phi(0.33)\) is row 0.3, column 0.03, which is 0.6293. So \(\Phi(-0.33) = 1 - 0.6293 = 0.3707\).
    Area. \(P = \Phi(0.50) - \Phi(-0.33) = 0.6915 - 0.3707 = 0.3208\).
    Meaning. About 32% of August days top out between 83 and 88.
  3. Greater than 90.
    Plug in. \(z = (90 - 85)/6 = 5/6\).
    Arithmetic. \(5/6 = 0.833 \approx 0.83\). Table row 0.8, column 0.03 gives \(\Phi(0.83) = 0.7967\). Upper tail \(1 - 0.7967 = 0.2033\).
    Meaning. About 1 day in 5 goes above 90.
  4. Less than 82.
    Plug in. \(z = (82 - 85)/6 = -3/6 = -0.5\).
    Arithmetic. By symmetry \(\Phi(-0.5) = 1 - \Phi(0.5) = 1 - 0.6915 = 0.3085\).
    Meaning. About 31% of days stay below 82.

Final answer. About 0.321, 0.203 and 0.309. Rounding \(z\) to two decimals moves the last digit slightly (the unrounded answers are 0.322 and 0.202).

How this could show up on the exam

Run it backwards. "Above what temperature are the hottest 5% of days?" Formula, solve \(z = (x - \mu)/\sigma\) for \(x\) to get \(x = \mu + z\sigma\). You need the \(z\) with 5% above it, which is \(z[0.05] = 1.645\). Plug in \(x = 85 + 1.645 \times 6\). Arithmetic \(1.645 \times 6 = 9.87\), so \(x = 94.87\). So the hottest 5% of days are above about 94.9 degrees. That's the same move as rescaling a normal quantile with \(x = m + s z\) in a Q-Q plot (Summ p.69).

HW2. Beyond performance metrics

A short report that evaluates metrics from your own research area against the six criteria from the metrics lecture (Metrics p.7-13, assignment on Metrics p.33).

The assignment. Identify one or more metrics common in your area, evaluate them against the lecture's criteria, and propose extra criteria your area needs.

Question.
  • Identify one or more metrics commonly used in your area of research.
    • If you haven’t yet decided on your research area, pick one of the areas you might be interested in.
    • Look at one or more seminal research articles in your area and see what metrics were used.
  • Evaluate the metric(s) based on the criteria discussed in today’s lecture.
    • What additional criteria might be useful in evaluating metrics in your research area?
  • Submit a short typed report (at most 4 pages), formatted in LaTeX.
In plain words. Take a number your field uses to say "this system is better" and stress-test it. For each of the six criteria, ask whether the number could go up while the thing you actually care about gets worse. Then say what the six criteria miss for your field.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Learn the six criteria (Metrics p.7-12). Linear means twice the metric is twice the performance. Reliable means if A scores higher then A really performs better. Repeatable means the same experiment gives the same value. Easy to use means it's easy to measure correctly. Consistent means the units and definition are the same on every system. Independent means outside pressure, such as vendors, can't bend it.
  2. Know which are required. Reliable, repeatable, consistent and independent are required, while linear and easy to use are nice to have (Metrics p.13).
  3. Know the means-versus-ends split. A means-based metric counts what was done, useful or not, and an ends-based metric counts only what was accomplished (Metrics p.27-28). MIPS is means-based, and execution time is ends-based.
  4. Pick your metrics. Your report used average throughput and the QoE score from video streaming, because they fail in almost opposite ways.
  5. Grade throughput on all six. Linear, yes, twice the bits is twice the throughput. Easy to use, yes. Reliable, no, because a sender can inflate it by filling a deep queue (bufferbloat), and everyone else waits. Independent, no, because ISPs advertise it. Repeatable and consistent only partly, because Pantheon found rankings shift with the network path. It's also means-based, counting bits moved and not whether the video stalled.
  6. Grade QoE on all six. Consistent, no, because each paper picks its own weights (Yin et al. price 1 s of stall at 3000 kbps of bitrate). Linear, no, because it adds unlike units through a chosen weight. Reliable, no, because the linear form was never validated against subjective tests (Peroni and Gorinsky). Independent, no, because the people proposing an algorithm set the weights it's judged under. Easy to use, yes. Repeatable, partly.
  7. Propose extra criteria. You added externality (what the flow does to others sharing the link, which Jain's index hides), reporting the distribution and not only the mean (tail latency decides usability), and stating the range of conditions the metric was tested over.

Final answer (your report). Throughput is linear and easy to use, partly repeatable and consistent, but not reliable or independent. QoE is easy to use and partly repeatable but fails linearity, reliability, consistency and independence.

How this could show up on the exam

"Evaluate MIPS against the six criteria." MIPS is repeatable, easy to measure and independent, but it isn't linear, reliable or consistent, since instructions do different amounts of work on CISC and RISC machines (Metrics p.16-17).

Another version asks for precision and recall (Metrics p.32). A search returns 30 pages, 20 relevant, and misses 40 more relevant pages. Formulas, \(\text{precision} = \dfrac{\text{relevant pages returned}}{\text{all pages returned}}\) and \(\text{recall} = \dfrac{\text{relevant pages returned}}{\text{all relevant pages that exist}}\). Plug in, precision \(= 20/30\) and recall \(= 20/(20 + 40) = 20/60\). Arithmetic, \(20/30 = 0.667\) and \(20/60 = 0.333\). Meaning, two thirds of what it returned was useful, but it found only one third of the useful pages.

HW3. Choosing a mean, quartiles and Q-Q plots

Question 1 picks and computes the right mean. Question 2 summarizes two data sets with the course conventions and checks normality with a Q-Q plot. This maps to the summarizing-data lecture (Summ p.20-32 and Summ p.39-73).

Problem 1(a). Five features are each built on top of the previous one and improve quality by 10%, 50%, 70%, 85% and 15%. What's the mean improvement per feature, and which mean?

Question. For each of the following sub-questions, i) indicate what type of mean (arithmetic, geometric, or harmonic) should be used, with explanation, ii) compute the mean value based on your choice.

a. A researcher considers adding five new features to her optimization project to improve the quality of the solution. Each feature is implemented on top of the previous one (i.e., Feature 2 is implemented on top of Feature 1, Feature 3 is implemented on top of Feature 2, and so on). She observes the following quality improvements after adding each feature. Feature 1, 10%. Feature 2, 50%. Feature 3, 70%. Feature 4, 85%. Feature 5, 15%. What is the mean improvement per feature? What type of mean did you use and why?

In plain words. Each feature multiplies the quality you already had, so the gains compound like interest. You want the one constant growth factor that, applied five times, gives the same total. That's the geometric mean.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which mean. The effects compound (multiply), so use the geometric mean (Summ p.30, Summ p.32).
  2. Formula. \[ \bar x_G = \left( \prod_{i=1}^{n} x_i \right)^{1/n} = \sqrt[n]{x_1 x_2 \cdots x_n}, \] where \(x_i\) is each growth ratio and \(n\) is how many there are (Summ p.23). A 10% gain is the ratio 1.10.
  3. Plug in. \(n = 5\), ratios 1.10, 1.50, 1.70, 1.85, 1.15. So \(\bar x_G = (1.10 \times 1.50 \times 1.70 \times 1.85 \times 1.15)^{1/5}\).
  4. Multiply. \(1.10 \times 1.50 = 1.65\). Then \(1.65 \times 1.70 = 2.805\). Then \(2.805 \times 1.85 = 5.18925\). Then \(5.18925 \times 1.15 = 5.9676\).
  5. 5th root by hand. Try 1.42 and 1.43. \(1.42^2 = 2.0164\), \(1.42^4 = 2.0164^2 = 4.0659\), \(1.42^5 = 4.0659 \times 1.42 = 5.7735\). \(1.43^2 = 2.0449\), \(1.43^4 = 2.0449^2 = 4.1816\), \(1.43^5 = 4.1816 \times 1.43 = 5.9797\). Our product 5.9676 sits between 5.7735 and 5.9797, at \((5.9676 - 5.7735)/(5.9797 - 5.7735) = 0.1941/0.2062 \approx 0.94\) of the way. So the root is about \(1.42 + 0.0094 = 1.4294\).
  6. Convert back. \(1.4294 - 1 = 0.4294\), so 42.94%.

Final answer. Geometric mean 1.4294, so about 42.94% improvement per feature, matching main.tex. Five features at 42.94% each give the same total as the real five.

How this could show up on the exam

Without a calculator the numbers will be friendly. Three successive versions give 100%, 300% and 700% gains. Ratios are 2, 4 and 8. Plug in \(\bar x_G = (2 \times 4 \times 8)^{1/3}\). Arithmetic, \(2 \times 4 = 8\), \(8 \times 8 = 64\), and \(\sqrt[3]{64} = 4\) since \(4 \times 4 \times 4 = 64\). So the mean ratio is 4, a 300% gain per version. The arithmetic mean of the ratios would be \((2 + 4 + 8)/3 = 14/3 = 4.67\), and \(4.67^3 \approx 102\) overshoots the true total of 64. That overshoot is the reason the arithmetic mean is wrong for compounding.

Problem 1(b). A compiler's running times on six similar programs are 150, 200, 120, 300, 270 and 180 ms. Mean, and which one?

Question. For each of the following sub-questions, i) indicate what type of mean (arithmetic, geometric, or harmonic) should be used, with explanation, ii) compute the mean value based on your choice.

b. The running time of a new compiler is measured with source programs of comparable size. In 6 experiments, the running times are measured as 150 ms, 200 ms, 120 ms, 300 ms, 270 ms, and 180 ms. What is the mean execution time? What type of mean did you use and why?

In plain words. These are times. Times add up to a total time, so the right average is the ordinary one, total divided by count.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which mean. The mean of times should be directly proportional to total time, which is the arithmetic mean (Summ p.25, Summ p.27).
  2. Formula. \(\bar x = \dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i\), where \(x_i\) is each time and \(n\) is the number of runs (Summ p.11).
  3. Plug in. \(n = 6\), so \(\bar x = (150 + 200 + 120 + 300 + 270 + 180)/6\).
  4. Arithmetic. \(150 + 200 = 350\), \(+ 120 = 470\), \(+ 300 = 770\), \(+ 270 = 1040\), \(+ 180 = 1220\). Then \(1220/6 = 203.33\).

Final answer. Arithmetic mean 203.33 ms, matching main.tex. A typical program takes about 203 ms to compile.

How this could show up on the exam

"If every time doubled, what should happen to the mean?" It should double, and only the arithmetic mean of times does that (Summ p.25). Another version adds weights (Summ p.33). Formula \(\bar x_W = \sum w_i x_i\) with weights \(w_i\) adding to 1. Tasks of 2, 5 and 10 s with frequencies 0.5, 0.3, 0.2. Plug in \(0.5(2) + 0.3(5) + 0.2(10)\). Arithmetic \(1 + 1.5 + 2 = 4.5\) s.

Problem 1(c). A database ran at 2500, 3500, 3700, 1800 and 2000 transactions per second in five experiments. Mean, and which one?

Question. For each of the following sub-questions, i) indicate what type of mean (arithmetic, geometric, or harmonic) should be used, with explanation, ii) compute the mean value based on your choice.

c. A new database system is implemented and its performance is tested. In five different experiments, the following performance (speed) values are obtained. 2500 transactions per second, 3500 transactions per second, 3700 transactions per second, 1800 transactions per second, 2000 transactions per second. What is the mean performance (in terms of transactions per second)? What type of mean did you use and why?

In plain words. These are rates, "per second", so time is on the bottom. Averaging rates directly overweights the fast runs. Flip each rate into time per transaction, average those, and flip back. That's the harmonic mean.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which mean. The mean of rates should be inversely proportional to total time, which is the harmonic mean (Summ p.25, Summ p.28).
  2. Formula. \[ \bar x_H = \frac{n}{\sum_{i=1}^{n} 1/x_i}, \] where \(x_i\) is each rate and \(n\) the number of runs (Summ p.22).
  3. Plug in. \(n = 5\), so \(\bar x_H = \dfrac{5}{1/2500 + 1/3500 + 1/3700 + 1/1800 + 1/2000}\).
  4. Reciprocals. \(1/2500 = 0.000400\), \(1/3500 = 0.000286\), \(1/3700 = 0.000270\), \(1/1800 = 0.000556\), \(1/2000 = 0.000500\).
  5. Sum. \(0.000400 + 0.000286 = 0.000686\), \(+ 0.000270 = 0.000956\), \(+ 0.000556 = 0.001512\), \(+ 0.000500 = 0.002012\). With more digits it's 0.0020115.
  6. Divide. \(5/0.0020115 = 5{,}000{,}000/2011.5 \approx 2485.7\). Check, \(2011.5 \times 2485.7 \approx 5{,}000{,}000\).

Final answer. Harmonic mean 2485.66 transactions per second, matching main.tex. The arithmetic mean would say 2700, which is too high.

How this could show up on the exam

Friendly numbers. Rates of 2, 3 and 6 jobs/s. Plug in \(\bar x_H = 3/(1/2 + 1/3 + 1/6)\). Arithmetic, put everything over 6, \(3/6 + 2/6 + 1/6 = 6/6 = 1\), so \(\bar x_H = 3/1 = 3\) jobs/s. The arithmetic mean would say \((2 + 3 + 6)/3 = 11/3 = 3.67\). A conceptual follow-up is "when does the harmonic mean equal total work divided by total time?" Only when every run did the same amount of work \(F\) (Summ p.26-28). If the work differs, use the weighted harmonic mean or just total work over total time.

Problem 2(a) and (b). For normal.txt (1000 values) and not-normal.txt (870 values), find the mean and variance, then the median and the first, second and third quartiles.

Question. Consider the two data sets, normal.txt and not-normal.txt. For each data set, do the following exercises.

a. Find the mean and variance of the sample data.
b. Find the median, first, second, and third quartile of the sample data.

In plain words. Summarize each file with its center (mean, median) and spread (variance, quartiles). The quartiles are the values one quarter, half and three quarters of the way up the sorted list, found with the course's plotting position \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/n\).
Step-by-step solution
  1. Mean. Formula. \(\bar x = \dfrac{1}{n}\sum x_i\) (Summ p.11).
    Plug in. For normal.txt the values add to \(\sum x_i = 2014.155\) with \(n = 1000\). For not-normal.txt, \(\sum x_i = 181.248\) with \(n = 870\).
    Arithmetic. \(2014.155/1000 = 2.0142\). \(181.248/870 = 0.2083\).
  2. Variance. Formula. The one-pass form (Summ p.39) is \[ s^2 = \frac{\sum x_i^2 - \left(\sum x_i\right)^2/n}{n - 1}. \] Here \(\sum x_i^2\) is the sum of the squared values and \((\sum x_i)^2\) is the square of the plain sum. The slide writes it as \(\frac{n\sum x_i^2 - (\sum x_i)^2}{n(n-1)}\), which is the same thing with top and bottom multiplied by \(n\). Divide by \(n - 1\) because it's a sample.
    Plug in, normal.txt. \(\sum x_i^2 = 5101.507\). So \(s^2 = \dfrac{5101.507 - 2014.155^2/1000}{999}\).
    Arithmetic. \(2014.155^2 = 4{,}056{,}820\), divided by 1000 is 4056.820. Then \(5101.507 - 4056.820 = 1044.687\). Divide by 999 to get 1.0457.
    Plug in, not-normal.txt. \(\sum x_i^2 = 76.007\). So \(s^2 = \dfrac{76.007 - 181.248^2/870}{869}\).
    Arithmetic. \(181.248^2 = 32{,}850.8\), divided by 870 is 37.760. Then \(76.007 - 37.760 = 38.248\). Divide by 869 to get 0.0440.
  3. Quartiles. Rule. Sort the data. The \(i\)-th sorted value \(x_{(i)}\) sits at \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/n\) (Summ p.59). To find the value at fraction \(p\), solve \(p = (i - 0.5)/n\) for the position, \[ i = np + 0.5. \] If \(i\) is a whole number, the answer is \(x_{(i)}\). If not, interpolate between the two neighbors (Summ p.59, Summ p.42).
  4. normal.txt, \(n = 1000\). For \(Q_1\), \(i = 1000(0.25) + 0.5 = 250.5\), halfway between \(x_{(250)} = 1.26463\) and \(x_{(251)} = 1.27142\), so \(Q_1 = (1.26463 + 1.27142)/2 = 1.2680\). For the median, \(i = 500.5\), halfway between 2.03199 and 2.03237, so 2.0322. For \(Q_3\), \(i = 750.5\), halfway between 2.69049 and 2.69483, so 2.6927.
  5. not-normal.txt, \(n = 870\). For \(Q_1\), \(i = 870(0.25) + 0.5 = 218\), a whole number, so \(Q_1 = x_{(218)} = 0.0587\). For the median, \(i = 435.5\), halfway between 0.1426 and 0.1432, so 0.1429. For \(Q_3\), \(i = 870(0.75) + 0.5 = 653\), so \(Q_3 = x_{(653)} = 0.2865\).
  6. What it shows. The second quartile is the median. In not-normal.txt the mean 0.2083 sits well above the median 0.1429. Also \(Q_3 - Q_2 = 0.2865 - 0.1429 = 0.1436\) is much larger than \(Q_2 - Q_1 = 0.1429 - 0.0587 = 0.0842\). A long upper half like that is what a right-skewed shape looks like (Summ p.48).

By-hand version. Data 7, 1, 11, 5, 9, 3.

  1. Sort. 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11 with \(n = 6\).
  2. Mean. \(\sum x_i = 1 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 9 + 11 = 36\), so \(\bar x = 36/6 = 6\).
  3. Variance. \(\sum x_i^2 = 1 + 9 + 25 + 49 + 81 + 121 = 286\). \((\sum x_i)^2/n = 36^2/6 = 1296/6 = 216\). So \(s^2 = (286 - 216)/(6 - 1) = 70/5 = 14\), and \(s = \sqrt{14} \approx 3.742\) (since \(3.74^2 = 13.99\)).
  4. Plotting positions. \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/6\) gives \(0.5/6 = 0.083\), \(1.5/6 = 0.250\), \(2.5/6 = 0.417\), \(3.5/6 = 0.583\), \(4.5/6 = 0.750\), \(5.5/6 = 0.917\).
  5. \(Q_1\). \(i = 6(0.25) + 0.5 = 2\), so \(Q_1 = x_{(2)} = 3\). \(Q_3\). \(i = 6(0.75) + 0.5 = 5\), so \(Q_3 = x_{(5)} = 9\).
  6. Median. \(i = 6(0.5) + 0.5 = 3.5\), halfway between \(x_{(3)} = 5\) and \(x_{(4)} = 7\). Interpolate, \(5 + 0.5(7 - 5) = 5 + 1 = 6\).

Final answer. normal.txt has mean 2.0142, variance 1.0457, \(Q_1 = 1.2680\), median \(Q_2 = 2.0322\), \(Q_3 = 2.6927\). not-normal.txt has mean 0.2083, variance 0.0440, \(Q_1 = 0.0587\), median \(Q_2 = 0.1429\), \(Q_3 = 0.2865\). These match main.tex. The small version gives mean 6, \(s^2 = 14\), \(Q_1 = 3\), median 6, \(Q_3 = 9\).

How this could show up on the exam

With \(n = 10\), \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/10\) are 0.05, 0.15, ..., 0.95. For \(Q_1\), \(i = 10(0.25) + 0.5 = 3\), so \(Q_1 = x_{(3)}\). For \(Q_3\), \(i = 10(0.75) + 0.5 = 8\), so \(Q_3 = x_{(8)}\). That's the layout in Summ p.60. Expect a follow-up on which spread measure to report. For skewed data the SIQR beats \(s\), for the same reason the median beats the mean (Summ p.45, Summ p.49). Formula \(\text{SIQR} = (Q_3 - Q_1)/2\). For not-normal.txt, plug in \((0.2865 - 0.0587)/2 = 0.2278/2 = 0.1139\).

Problem 2(c). Plot a histogram of each data set.

Question. Consider the two data sets, normal.txt and not-normal.txt. For each data set, do the following exercises.

c. Plot the sample data using a histogram.

In plain words. Chop the range into equal bins, count how many values land in each, and draw bars. The only number you have to compute is how many bins, and the course uses Sturges' rule for that.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Method. Divide the range (max minus min) into equal bins, count the points in each, and plot relative frequency, which is count divided by \(n\) (Summ p.54).
  2. Formula. Sturges' rule, written on the slide with floor brackets, \[ k = \lfloor 1 + \log_2 n \rfloor, \] where \(k\) is the number of bins, \(n\) the number of values, and \(\lfloor \cdot \rfloor\) means round down (Summ p.54).
  3. normal.txt. Plug in \(n = 1000\). \(\log_2 1000\) is the power of 2 that gives 1000. Since \(2^9 = 512\) and \(2^{10} = 1024\), it's just under 10, about 9.97. Then \(1 + 9.97 = 10.97\), and rounding down gives \(k = 10\).
  4. not-normal.txt. Plug in \(n = 870\). It's between \(2^9 = 512\) and \(2^{10} = 1024\), about \(\log_2 870 \approx 9.76\). Then \(1 + 9.76 = 10.76\), rounded down to \(k = 10\).
  5. Bin width. width \(=\) (max \(-\) min)\(/k\). For normal.txt, max 5.2128 and min \(-1.6554\), so \((5.2128 + 1.6554)/10 = 6.8682/10 = 0.687\). For not-normal.txt, \((1.2957 - 0.0007)/10 = 1.295/10 = 0.1295\).
  6. Shape. normal.txt gives a symmetric bell. not-normal.txt piles up near 0 with a long tail to the right.

Final answer. Per the slide, 10 bins for both files. Your main.tex says 11 bins for both, because analyze.py used round(1 + log2 n) rather than the floor. It's a small slip that doesn't change any conclusion, but on the exam use the floor as the slide shows.

By-hand version. With the six values 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, \(\log_2 6 \approx 2.58\) (between \(2^2 = 4\) and \(2^3 = 8\)), so \(k = \lfloor 1 + 2.58 \rfloor = \lfloor 3.58 \rfloor = 3\) bins. The range is \(11 - 1 = 10\), so each bin is \(10/3 = 3.33\) wide. The bins are [1, 4.33), [4.33, 7.67), [7.67, 11] with 2, 2 and 2 points, so each bar has height \(2/6 = 0.33\).

How this could show up on the exam

"Why does the bin size matter?" The same 12 values look different with 7 bins and with 12 bins (Summ p.55-56), so a histogram's shape partly reflects the analyst's choice. That's why the Q-Q plot, which has no bins, is the better check of shape.

Problem 2(d). Draw the quantile plot of each data set.

Question. Consider the two data sets, normal.txt and not-normal.txt. For each data set, do the following exercises.

d. Draw the quantile plot for the sample data.

In plain words. Sort the data and plot each value against its position fraction \(f_i\). It's the sorted data drawn as a curve, so steep parts mean values are spread out and flat parts mean values are bunched together.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Rule. A quantile plot puts \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/n\) on the x-axis and the \(i\)-th sorted value \(x_{(i)}\) on the y-axis (Summ p.59).
  2. normal.txt. The x-values are \(0.5/1000 = 0.0005\), \(1.5/1000 = 0.0015\), up to \(999.5/1000 = 0.9995\). The curve is an S shape, steep at both ends and flat in the middle where most values sit.
  3. not-normal.txt. The x-values run from \(0.5/870 = 0.0006\) to \(869.5/870 = 0.9994\). The curve stays low and flat for most of \(f\), then shoots up near \(f = 1\), which is the long right tail.

Final answer. These are the two quantile-plot figures in main.tex.

By-hand version. For 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, plot (0.083, 1), (0.25, 3), (0.417, 5), (0.583, 7), (0.75, 9), (0.917, 11). The points fall on a straight line because the values are evenly spaced.

How this could show up on the exam

Given a 10-row table like Summ p.60, you could be asked to read off the 80th percentile. Formula, position \(i = np + 0.5\). Plug in \(i = 10(0.80) + 0.5 = 8.5\), so it's halfway between \(x_{(8)} = 44.5\) and \(x_{(9)} = 50.3\). Interpolate, \(Q(0.80) = 44.5 + 0.5(50.3 - 44.5) = 44.5 + 0.5(5.8) = 44.5 + 2.9 = 47.4\). So 80% of the values are below about 47.4.

Problem 2(e). Compare each data set with the normal distribution using a Q-Q plot.

Question. Consider the two data sets, normal.txt and not-normal.txt. For each data set, do the following exercises.

e. Compare the distribution of the sample data against the theoretical normal distribution using a Q-Q plot.

Note. You can use a program such as Microsoft Excel, OpenOffice Calc, Matlab or Mathematica for doing these exercises, or you can do them without the help of any program.

In plain words. For each sorted data value, work out what that value "should" be if the data were normal with the same mean and standard deviation. Plot real against expected. If the data are normal the points hug the 45° line, and the way they bend away tells you the shape.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Normal quantile. For each \(f_i\), find \(z_i\) with \(\Phi(z_i) = f_i\), the standard normal value with fraction \(f_i\) below it. Use the normal table backwards, or the approximation \(z_i = 4.91[f_i^{0.14} - (1 - f_i)^{0.14}]\) (Summ p.69).
  2. Rescale. Formula. \(x_i = m + s z_i\), where \(m\) is the sample mean and \(s\) the sample standard deviation (Summ p.69). This puts the theoretical values in the data's units.
  3. Plot. \((x_i, x_{(i)})\), theoretical against observed (Summ p.63). Points on the 45° line mean the data are normal. Without rescaling, the intercept estimates the mean and the slope estimates the standard deviation (Summ p.70).
  4. normal.txt. \(m = 2.0142\), \(s = \sqrt{1.0457} = 1.0226\). The points follow the line, with correlation 0.9993. The few that drift are far out in the tails, where there are very few values.
  5. not-normal.txt. \(m = 0.2083\), \(s = \sqrt{0.0440} = 0.2098\). The points sit above the line at both ends and below it in the middle, with correlation 0.904. At the left end, the smallest \(z\) is about \(-3.25\), so the normal predicts \(0.2083 + 0.2098(-3.25) \approx -0.47\), but the data can't go below 0. At the right end, the data's largest values (up to 1.30) are far bigger than a normal would give. That's the asymmetric, right-skewed pattern (Summ p.71-73).

By-hand version. Data 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11 with \(m = 6\), \(s = 3.742\).

  1. \(z_i\) from the table, read backwards. For \(f = 0.917\) find the \(z\) with \(\Phi(z) = 0.917\). That's \(z = 1.38\), since row 1.3, column 0.08 gives 0.9162. For \(f = 0.75\), \(z = 0.67\) (row 0.6, column 0.07 gives 0.7486). For \(f = 0.583\), \(z = 0.21\) (row 0.2, column 0.01 gives 0.5832). The lower three \(f\) values (0.083, 0.25, 0.417) are the mirror images, so \(z = -1.38, -0.67, -0.21\).
  2. Rescale with \(x_i = 6 + 3.742\,z_i\). \(3.742 \times 1.38 = 5.16\), \(3.742 \times 0.67 = 2.51\), \(3.742 \times 0.21 = 0.79\). So the six theoretical values are \(6 - 5.16 = 0.84\), \(6 - 2.51 = 3.49\), \(6 - 0.79 = 5.21\), \(6 + 0.79 = 6.79\), \(6 + 2.51 = 8.51\), \(6 + 5.16 = 11.16\).
  3. Pair with the data. (0.84, 1), (3.49, 3), (5.21, 5), (6.79, 7), (8.51, 9), (11.16, 11). Every point is within about 0.5 of the line \(y = x\), so the data look consistent with a normal.

Final answer. normal.txt is consistent with a normal (correlation 0.9993), and not-normal.txt is right-skewed and not normal (correlation 0.904), matching main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

A skewed six-point version. Data 1, 1, 1, 2, 3, 10. Mean \(m = 18/6 = 3\). Deviations from 3 are \(-2, -2, -2, -1, 0, 7\), squares \(4, 4, 4, 1, 0, 49\), sum 62, so \(s^2 = 62/5 = 12.4\) and \(s = \sqrt{12.4} \approx 3.52\). Same \(z\) values as above, \(\pm 1.38\), \(\pm 0.67\), \(\pm 0.21\). Rescale, \(3.52 \times 1.38 = 4.86\), \(3.52 \times 0.67 = 2.36\), \(3.52 \times 0.21 = 0.74\). Theoretical values \(3 - 4.86 = -1.86\), \(3 - 2.36 = 0.64\), \(3 - 0.74 = 2.26\), \(3 + 0.74 = 3.74\), \(3 + 2.36 = 5.36\), \(3 + 4.86 = 7.86\). Against the data, the first point (\(-1.86\), 1) is above the line, the middle points (2.26, 1), (3.74, 2), (5.36, 3) are below, and the last (7.86, 10) is above. That's the same bowed shape as not-normal.txt, so you'd answer "right-skewed, not normal".

HW4. Confidence intervals

Intervals for a mean with \(z\) and \(t\), what sample size and the critical value do to the width, and the fixes when normality fails (batching and bootstrap). This is the confidence-interval lecture (CI p.27-38 and CI p.63-70).

Problem 1.1. normal.txt is the whole population. Find the population mean and variance.

Question. Problem 1, normally distributed data. Use the population data in the file normal.txt. This data is normally distributed.

1. Find the population mean and variance.

In plain words. Here the file isn't a sample, it's everything. So you compute the true mean \(\mu\) and true variance \(\sigma^2\), dividing by \(N\) instead of \(N - 1\). These are the targets the intervals in the next parts try to capture.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Mean. Formula. \(\mu = \dfrac{1}{N}\sum x_i\), where \(N\) is the population size. Plug in. \(2014.155/1000\). Result. \(\mu = 2.0142\).
  2. Variance. Formula. \(\sigma^2 = \dfrac{1}{N}\sum (x_i - \mu)^2\). Divide by \(N\), not \(N - 1\), because the \(n - 1\) correction is only for estimating a population variance from a sample (Summ p.39).
  3. Short-cut from HW3. HW3 got \(s^2 = 1044.687/999 = 1.0457\). The population version uses the same top, \(1044.687/1000 = 1.0447\). Same thing as \(s^2 \times \frac{N-1}{N} = 1.0457 \times 0.999 = 1.0457 - 0.0010 = 1.0447\).

Final answer. \(\mu = 2.0142\), \(\sigma^2 = 1.0447\), matching main.tex. For not-normal.txt (Problem 2.1), \(\mu = 181.248/870 = 0.2083\) and \(\sigma^2 = 38.248/870 = 0.0440\) (the \(N\) versus \(N - 1\) change only shows in the fifth decimal). These are the same population values shown on CI p.32.

How this could show up on the exam

"Your HW3 and HW4 variances differ in the fourth digit. Which is right?" Both are. HW3 treated the file as a sample and divided by \(n - 1 = 999\), and HW4 treated it as the population and divided by \(N = 1000\).

Problem 1.2. Draw 50 values from normal.txt and give 90%, 95% and 99% intervals for the mean. Do they contain \(\mu\)?

Question. Problem 1, normally distributed data. Use the population data in the file normal.txt. This data is normally distributed.

2. Generate a random sample of size 50, that is, randomly select 50 numbers from the population. Find 90%, 95% and 99% confidence intervals for the mean. Is the population mean within the intervals?

In plain words. From 50 random values, give a range that you're 90%, 95% or 99% confident contains the true mean. The range is "sample mean plus or minus a critical value times the standard error". With \(n \gt 30\) you use \(z\).
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which formula. \(n = 50 \gt 30\), so \(s\) is a good stand-in for \(\sigma\) and the normal applies (CI p.27).
  2. Formula. \[ \bar x \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\,\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} \] (CI p.31). Here \(\bar x\) is the sample mean, \(s\) the sample standard deviation, \(n\) the sample size, \(s/\sqrt{n}\) the standard error, and \(z_{1-\alpha/2}\) the normal critical value (the same number as \(z[\alpha/2]\) in upper-tail notation).
  3. Critical values from the table. 90% means \(\alpha = 0.10\), so \(\alpha/2 = 0.05\) and \(z = 1.645\). 95% means \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\) and \(z = 1.960\). 99% means \(\alpha/2 = 0.005\) and \(z = 2.576\).
  4. Plug in. Your sample had \(\bar x = 2.2093\), \(s = 0.9443\), \(n = 50\).
  5. Standard error. \(\sqrt{50} \approx 7.071\) (since \(7.071^2 = 50.0\)). So \(0.9443/7.071 = 0.1335\).
  6. 90%. Half-width \(1.645 \times 0.1335 = 0.2197\). Lower \(2.2093 - 0.2197 = 1.9896\). Upper \(2.2093 + 0.2197 = 2.4290\).
  7. 95%. Half-width \(1.960 \times 0.1335 = 0.2617\). Lower \(2.2093 - 0.2617 = 1.9476\). Upper \(2.2093 + 0.2617 = 2.4710\).
  8. 99%. Half-width \(2.576 \times 0.1335 = 0.3440\). Lower \(2.2093 - 0.3440 = 1.8653\). Upper \(2.2093 + 0.3440 = 2.5533\).
  9. Contains \(\mu\)? \(\mu = 2.0142\) is above every lower end and below every upper end, so yes for all three.

Final answer. (1.9897, 2.4290), (1.9476, 2.4710), (1.8653, 2.5533), all containing \(\mu\), matching main.tex. The hand version's 1.9896 differs in the last digit only from rounding the standard error.

How this could show up on the exam

"\(n = 100\), \(\bar x = 10\), \(s = 2\). Give the 95% interval." Standard error \(s/\sqrt{n} = 2/\sqrt{100} = 2/10 = 0.2\). Half-width \(1.96 \times 0.2 = 0.392\). Interval \(10 \pm 0.392 = (9.61, 10.39)\).

A follow-up asks how many measurements make the 95% half-width \(\pm 5\%\) of the mean. Formula (CI p.44) \[ n = \left( \frac{z_{1-\alpha/2}\, s}{\bar x\, e} \right)^2, \] where \(e\) is the allowed error as a fraction of the mean. Plug in \(z = 1.96\), \(s = 2\), \(\bar x = 10\), \(e = 0.05\), so \(n = (1.96 \times 2/(10 \times 0.05))^2\). Arithmetic, \(1.96 \times 2 = 3.92\), \(10 \times 0.05 = 0.5\), \(3.92/0.5 = 7.84\), and \(7.84^2 = 61.5\). Always round up, so 62 runs.

Problem 1.3. Draw 16 values and give the three intervals again. Do they contain \(\mu\)?

Question. Problem 1, normally distributed data. Use the population data in the file normal.txt. This data is normally distributed.

3. Generate a random sample of size 16. Find 90%, 95% and 99% confidence intervals for the mean. Is the population mean within the intervals?

In plain words. Same job with only 16 values. Now \(s\) is a shaky estimate of \(\sigma\), so you swap \(z\) for the bigger \(t\) value with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom. That's allowed because the population is normal.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which formula. \(n = 16\) is small and the population is normal, so use \(t\) with \(n - 1 = 15\) degrees of freedom (CI p.37).
  2. Formula. \[ \bar x \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\,\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}, \] where \(t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\) is the t value with upper-tail area \(\alpha/2\) and \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom (CI p.37).
  3. Critical values. t table, row 15. Column 0.05 gives 1.753 (90%). Column 0.025 gives 2.131 (95%). Column 0.005 gives 2.947 (99%).
  4. Plug in. \(\bar x = 2.2911\), \(s = 1.1332\), \(n = 16\).
  5. Standard error. \(\sqrt{16} = 4\), so \(1.1332/4 = 0.2833\).
  6. 90%. Half-width \(1.753 \times 0.2833 = 0.4966\). Interval \(2.2911 - 0.4966 = 1.7945\) to \(2.2911 + 0.4966 = 2.7877\).
  7. 95%. Half-width \(2.131 \times 0.2833 = 0.6037\). Interval \(2.2911 - 0.6037 = 1.6874\) to \(2.2911 + 0.6037 = 2.8948\).
  8. 99%. Half-width \(2.947 \times 0.2833 = 0.8349\). Interval \(2.2911 - 0.8349 = 1.4562\) to \(2.2911 + 0.8349 = 3.1260\).
  9. Contains \(\mu\)? 2.0142 is inside all three, so yes.

Final answer. (1.7944, 2.7877), (1.6872, 2.8949), (1.4562, 3.1259), all containing \(\mu\), matching main.tex to within rounding.

How this could show up on the exam

"\(n = 9\), \(\bar x = 20\), \(s = 6\), 95% interval?" Standard error \(6/\sqrt{9} = 6/3 = 2\). Degrees of freedom \(9 - 1 = 8\), so row 8, column 0.025 gives \(t = 2.306\). Half-width \(2.306 \times 2 = 4.612\). Interval \(20 - 4.61 = 15.39\) to \(20 + 4.61 = 24.61\). If they give \(\sigma\) instead of \(s\), use \(z\) even at small \(n\) (CI p.27).

Problem 1.4. Compare the widths from 1.2 and 1.3 and explain the difference using both the sample size and the distribution.

Question. Problem 1, normally distributed data. Use the population data in the file normal.txt. This data is normally distributed.

4. Compare the widths of the intervals from parts 2 and 3. Explain the difference in terms of both the sample size and the distribution you used for the critical value.

In plain words. The 16-value interval came out much wider. Width is "critical value times \(s/\sqrt{n}\)", so you can split the ratio of widths into three pieces, one from \(\sqrt{n}\), one from \(t\) versus \(z\), and one from the two samples having different \(s\).
Step-by-step solution
  1. Formula. Width \(= 2 \times \text{crit} \times s/\sqrt{n}\), twice the half-width.
  2. Plug in at 95%. For \(n = 16\) the width is \(2 \times 0.6037 = 1.207\). For \(n = 50\) it's \(2 \times 0.2617 = 0.523\).
  3. Ratio. \(1.207/0.523 \approx 2.31\).
  4. Split the ratio. \[ \frac{\text{width}_{16}}{\text{width}_{50}} = \frac{t}{z} \times \frac{s_{16}}{s_{50}} \times \sqrt{\frac{50}{16}}. \]
  5. Sample size piece. \(\sqrt{50/16} = \sqrt{3.125} \approx 1.77\) (since \(1.77^2 = 3.13\)). Width shrinks with \(\sqrt{n}\) (CI p.39).
  6. Critical value piece. \(2.131/1.960 \approx 1.09\). \(t\) has heavier tails than \(z\), the price of estimating \(\sigma\) from 16 values (CI p.35).
  7. Luck piece. \(1.1332/0.9443 \approx 1.20\). This sample of 16 just happened to have a larger \(s\).
  8. Check. \(1.77 \times 1.09 = 1.93\), and \(1.93 \times 1.20 = 2.31\). It matches the ratio.

Final answer. The 16-sample interval is about 2.3 times as wide, mostly from \(\sqrt{n}\), a bit from \(t\) versus \(z\), and some from a larger sample standard deviation. This matches main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

"How many times more data to halve an interval's width?" Width goes as \(1/\sqrt{n}\). To make \(1/\sqrt{n}\) half as big, \(\sqrt{n}\) must double, so \(n\) must be multiplied by \(2^2 = 4\). Also "what happens to the width as the confidence level goes up?" It grows, because the critical value grows (CI p.32).

Problem 2.1 to 2.3. Repeat 1.1 to 1.3 with not-normal.txt, samples of 50 and 16.

Question. Problem 2, data that is not normally distributed. Repeat parts 1 to 3 of Problem 1 for the population data in the file not-normal.txt. This data is not normally distributed.

Reminder of parts 1 to 3. (1) Find the population mean and variance. (2) Random sample of size 50, 90%, 95% and 99% confidence intervals for the mean, is the population mean within them? (3) The same with a random sample of size 16.

In plain words. Same formulas, but now the population is skewed. At \(n = 50\) the central limit theorem still makes the sample mean close to normal, so \(z\) is fine. At \(n = 16\) the \(t\) interval is shakier, which sets up the fixes in 2a and 2b.
Step-by-step solution
  1. 2.1, population. \(\mu = 0.2083\), \(\sigma^2 = 0.0440\), dividing by \(N = 870\) (see Problem 1.1 above).
  2. 2.2, why \(z\) still works. The data aren't normal, but the CLT makes the sample mean close to normal at this size, so the \(z\) interval is still fine (CI p.29, CI p.67).
  3. 2.2, plug in. \(\bar x = 0.2168\), \(s = 0.2278\), \(n = 50\). Standard error \(0.2278/7.071 = 0.03222\).
  4. 2.2, 90%. \(1.645 \times 0.03222 = 0.0530\). Interval \(0.2168 - 0.0530 = 0.1638\) to \(0.2168 + 0.0530 = 0.2698\).
  5. 2.2, 95%. \(1.960 \times 0.03222 = 0.0631\). Interval \(0.2168 - 0.0631 = 0.1537\) to \(0.2168 + 0.0631 = 0.2799\).
  6. 2.2, 99%. \(2.576 \times 0.03222 = 0.0830\). Interval \(0.2168 - 0.0830 = 0.1338\) to \(0.2168 + 0.0830 = 0.2998\).
  7. 2.3, plug in. \(\bar x = 0.2406\), \(s = 0.2040\), \(n = 16\), \(t\) with 15 degrees of freedom. Standard error \(0.2040/4 = 0.0510\).
  8. 2.3, 90%. \(1.753 \times 0.0510 = 0.0894\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.0894 = 0.1512\) to \(0.2406 + 0.0894 = 0.3300\).
  9. 2.3, 95%. \(2.131 \times 0.0510 = 0.1087\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.1087 = 0.1319\) to \(0.2406 + 0.1087 = 0.3493\).
  10. 2.3, 99%. \(2.947 \times 0.0510 = 0.1503\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.1503 = 0.0903\) to \(0.2406 + 0.1503 = 0.3909\).
  11. Contains \(\mu\)? 0.2083 is inside all six intervals.

Final answer. 50-sample intervals (0.1638, 0.2698), (0.1537, 0.2800), (0.1339, 0.2998). 16-sample intervals (0.1512, 0.3300), (0.1319, 0.3493), (0.0903, 0.3909). All contain \(\mu\). These match main.tex (the last digit can differ by 1 from rounding the standard error by hand).

How this could show up on the exam

"Why is the \(t\) interval at \(n = 16\) suspect for this data but fine at \(n = 50\)?" With skewed data and small \(n\), the CLT hasn't kicked in. The slide's example is exactly \(n = 16\) from a population with skew 1.8, where a nominal 95% interval covers only about 91% of the time, and it's consistently too narrow (CI p.67).

Problem 2a. Batch the 16 values into 4 batches of 4 in draw order, and build the intervals from the 4 batch means with \(t\) on 3 degrees of freedom.

Question. Then, for the smaller sample only (size 16), compute the 90%, 95% and 99% intervals two further ways. These correspond to the first and third responses on the lecture slides for when the normality assumption fails.

2a. Batching. Group your 16 observations into 4 batches of 4 and compute the mean of each batch. You now have 4 numbers. Compute the confidence intervals from those 4 batch means, using the t distribution with 3 degrees of freedom.

Batch the observations in the order you drew them. Do not sort the sample first, since sorting would group the small values together and the large values together, and the batch means would no longer be four draws from the same distribution.

In plain words. Averaging a few skewed values gives something closer to normal. So you average each group of 4, pretend those 4 averages are your data, and build an ordinary \(t\) interval from them. You pay for it with only 3 degrees of freedom.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Why. Batching averages groups of at least four, which pushes the values toward normal. It's the CLT applied by hand (CI p.68). Keep draw order, since sorting would make each batch a different slice of the distribution.
  2. The 16 values in draw order. 0.6280, 0.3864, 0.5279, 0.1426 | 0.5905, 0.1637, 0.1448, 0.0480 | 0.0704, 0.1905, 0.0519, 0.0488 | 0.0747, 0.3559, 0.0713, 0.3538.
  3. Batch means. Batch 1, \(0.6280 + 0.3864 + 0.5279 + 0.1426 = 1.6849\), divided by 4 is 0.4212. Batch 2, \(0.5905 + 0.1637 + 0.1448 + 0.0480 = 0.9470\), so 0.2368. Batch 3, \(0.0704 + 0.1905 + 0.0519 + 0.0488 = 0.3616\), so 0.0904. Batch 4, \(0.0747 + 0.3559 + 0.0713 + 0.3538 = 0.8557\), so 0.2139.
  4. Mean of the batch means. \(\bar x = (0.4212 + 0.2368 + 0.0904 + 0.2139)/4 = 0.9623/4 = 0.2406\). It's the same as the raw sample mean, as it has to be.
  5. Variance. Formula. \(s^2 = \dfrac{\sum x_i^2 - (\sum x_i)^2/n}{n - 1}\) with \(n = 4\) batch means (Summ p.39).
    Squares. \(0.4212^2 = 0.1774\), \(0.2368^2 = 0.0561\), \(0.0904^2 = 0.0082\), \(0.2139^2 = 0.0458\). Sum \(0.1774 + 0.0561 + 0.0082 + 0.0458 = 0.2874\) (0.28742 unrounded).
    Plug in. \((\sum x_i)^2/n = 0.9623^2/4 = 0.9260/4 = 0.2315\). So \(s^2 = (0.2874 - 0.2315)/3\).
    Arithmetic. Carrying the unrounded sums, \(0.28742 - 0.23151 = 0.05591\), and \(0.05591/3 = 0.01864\). Then \(s = \sqrt{0.01864} \approx 0.1365\) (since \(0.1365^2 = 0.01863\)).
  6. Standard error. \(s/\sqrt{4} = 0.13652/2 = 0.06826\), about 0.0683. Keep the extra digit for the next steps.
  7. Critical values. t table, row 3. Columns 0.05, 0.025 and 0.005 give 2.353, 3.182 and 5.841.
  8. 90%. \(2.353 \times 0.06826 = 0.1606\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.1606 = 0.0800\) to \(0.2406 + 0.1606 = 0.4012\).
  9. 95%. \(3.182 \times 0.06826 = 0.2172\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.2172 = 0.0234\) to \(0.2406 + 0.2172 = 0.4578\).
  10. 99%. \(5.841 \times 0.06826 = 0.3987\). Interval \(0.2406 - 0.3987 = -0.1581\) to \(0.2406 + 0.3987 = 0.6393\).

Final answer. (0.0799, 0.4012), (0.0233, 0.4578), (−0.1581, 0.6393), all containing \(\mu = 0.2083\), matching main.tex to rounding.

How this could show up on the exam

"16 observations become 4 batches. What happens to the 95% critical value?" It goes from \(t[0.025, 15] = 2.131\) to \(t[0.025, 3] = 3.182\). Ratio \(3.182/2.131 = 1.49\), so about 50% wider (CI p.68).

A twist. "Your 12 observations become 3 batches of 4. Batch means are 2, 4, 6." Mean \((2 + 4 + 6)/3 = 12/3 = 4\). Deviations \(-2, 0, 2\), squares \(4, 0, 4\), so \(s^2 = (4 + 0 + 4)/(3 - 1) = 8/2 = 4\) and \(s = 2\). Standard error \(2/\sqrt{3} = 2/1.732 = 1.155\). Degrees of freedom \(3 - 1 = 2\), so \(t[0.025, 2] = 4.303\). Half-width \(4.303 \times 1.155 = 4.97\). The 95% interval is \(4 \pm 4.97 = (-0.97, 8.97)\). That's huge, which shows why batching needs plenty of data.

Problem 2b. Bootstrap the 16 values with \(B = 10{,}000\) resamples and report the percentile intervals.

Question. 2b. Bootstrap. Resample your 16 observations with replacement, recompute the mean, and repeat many times. The percentiles of those resampled means give the interval. The script below does this. Save it next to not-normal.txt and run it with Python 3 (it needs only NumPy).
import numpy as np

data = np.loadtxt("not-normal.txt")
rng = np.random.default_rng(12345)                    # record your seed

sample = rng.choice(data, size=16, replace=False)

B = 10000
resamples = rng.choice(sample, size=(B, len(sample)), replace=True)
means = resamples.mean(axis=1)
for level in (0.90, 0.95, 0.99):
    a = (1 - level) / 2
    lo, hi = np.quantile(means, [a, 1 - a])
    print(f"{level:.0%}: ({lo:.4f}, {hi:.4f})")
If you are working in Excel or R instead, you may use any equivalent implementation, but report B, your seed, and the tool you used.
In plain words. Pretend your 16 values are the whole population. Draw fake samples of 16 from them with replacement, take each fake sample's mean, and see how much those means bounce around. The middle 95% of the bounce is your 95% interval.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Procedure. Draw 16 values from your sample with replacement, compute the mean, and repeat \(B\) times (CI p.69).
  2. Percentile rule. Sort the \(B\) means. For confidence level \(1 - \alpha\), the interval runs from the \(\alpha/2\) percentile to the \(1 - \alpha/2\) percentile. In positions, the lower end is about the \((\alpha/2) \times B\)-th sorted mean and the upper end about the \((1 - \alpha/2) \times B\)-th.
  3. Plug in. \(B = 10{,}000\). For 95%, \(0.025 \times 10{,}000 = 250\) and \(0.975 \times 10{,}000 = 9750\), so the 250th and 9750th sorted means. For 90%, \(0.05 \times 10{,}000 = 500\) and \(0.95 \times 10{,}000 = 9500\). For 99%, \(0.005 \times 10{,}000 = 50\) and \(0.995 \times 10{,}000 = 9950\).
  4. Replacement is the whole trick. Without it every resample is the same 16 values and every mean is identical (CI p.69).
  5. Result. Your run with seed 12345 gave (0.1618, 0.3242) at 90%, (0.1493, 0.3407) at 95%, and (0.1230, 0.3705) at 99%.

By-hand version. Sample {2, 4, 9}. Four resamples might be {2, 2, 9} with mean \(13/3 = 4.33\), {4, 9, 9} with mean \(22/3 = 7.33\), {2, 4, 4} with mean \(10/3 = 3.33\) and {9, 2, 4} with mean \(15/3 = 5\). With thousands of these, you'd sort the means and read off the percentiles. The computer is only needed because \(B\) is large, and nothing in the method needs a formula.

Final answer. The three bootstrap intervals above, all containing 0.2083, matching main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

"Name a statistic you can get an interval for by bootstrap but not by the \(t\) formula." The median, a quantile, a ratio or a correlation (CI p.69). "What's the catch at small \(n\)?" Percentile intervals under-cover, and if your 16 values missed the tail, every resample misses it too (CI p.69-70).

Problem 2c. (1) Put raw, batched and bootstrap intervals in one table and say whether each contains \(\mu\). (2) Which is widest and narrowest, and did you expect that? (3) Why does batching widen the interval and what did it cost? (4) Does the 99% batched interval contain impossible values? (5) Why doesn't one sample's coverage tell you a method's coverage?

Question. 2c. Compare.
  1. Put the three sets of intervals for the sample of 16 (raw, batched, and bootstrapped) into a single table, at all three confidence levels. State for each whether it contains the population mean.
  2. Which method gives the widest intervals, and which the narrowest? Was that what you expected before computing them?
  3. The batched interval is meant to correct for the data not being normal. Explain why the correction makes the interval wider rather than narrower, and say what you gave up to obtain it.
  4. Look at your 99% batched interval. Does it contain any values that are impossible for this quantity? What does that tell you about the method at this sample size?
  5. Your sample either did or did not contain the population mean. Explain why that single outcome does not, on its own, tell you whether a method’s coverage is correct.
In plain words. Line up the three methods, measure each interval's width (upper minus lower), and explain the ranking. Then two conceptual points. Batching trades precision for safety, and "my interval caught \(\mu\)" is one coin flip, not proof the method works.
Step-by-step solution
  1. (1) The table. Width is upper minus lower.
    LevelMethodIntervalWidthContains 0.2083
    90%Raw \(t\)(0.1512, 0.3300)\(0.3300 - 0.1512 = 0.1788\)yes
    Batched(0.0799, 0.4012)\(0.4012 - 0.0799 = 0.3213\)yes
    Bootstrap(0.1618, 0.3242)\(0.3242 - 0.1618 = 0.1624\)yes
    95%Raw \(t\)(0.1319, 0.3493)\(0.3493 - 0.1319 = 0.2174\)yes
    Batched(0.0233, 0.4578)\(0.4578 - 0.0233 = 0.4345\)yes
    Bootstrap(0.1493, 0.3407)\(0.3407 - 0.1493 = 0.1914\)yes
    99%Raw \(t\)(0.0903, 0.3909)\(0.3909 - 0.0903 = 0.3006\)yes
    Batched(−0.1581, 0.6393)\(0.6393 + 0.1581 = 0.7974\)yes
    Bootstrap(0.1230, 0.3705)\(0.3705 - 0.1230 = 0.2475\)yes
  2. (2) Ranking. Batching is widest and bootstrap narrowest at every level. That's what the slides predict, batching "about 50% wider" (CI p.68) and bootstrap the narrowest (CI p.70). Here batching came out \(0.4345/0.2174 \approx 2.0\) times as wide as raw at 95%, more than 1.5, for the reason in the next step.
  3. (3) Why batching widens. Half-width is \(t \times s/\sqrt{n}\), so look at both pieces. The degrees of freedom fall from 15 to 3, so the critical value jumps from 2.131 to 3.182, a factor of \(3.182/2.131 = 1.49\). The standard error also grew, from 0.0510 to 0.0683, a factor of \(0.0683/0.0510 = 1.34\), because these four batch means happened to be spread out. Together \(1.49 \times 1.34 = 2.0\). The cost is precision, since the interval rests on 4 numbers instead of 16, and you lose any statement about individual observations (CI p.68).
  4. (4) Impossible values. The 99% batched interval goes down to \(-0.1581\). Every value in the file is positive, so a negative mean is impossible. With only 4 batch means the method is too blunt at high confidence.
  5. (5) One sample isn't coverage. Coverage is a long-run property of the procedure. About 95 of 100 repeated 95% intervals should contain \(\mu\) (CI p.33). One sample containing \(\mu\) is consistent with 95% coverage and also with 91%, so you'd have to repeat the draw many times and count.

Final answer. Batching widest, bootstrap narrowest, all nine intervals contain 0.2083, and the 99% batched interval includes impossible negative values. This matches main.tex, where batching came out about twice as wide as raw at 95%, more than the slide's "about 50%" because this sample's batch means happened to be spread out.

How this could show up on the exam

"Your measurements are consecutive request latencies from one run. Which of the three responses should you use?" Batching, because it's the only one that handles correlated observations (CI p.70). "You want the median." Transform or bootstrap. And remember the transform trap, where back-transforming the endpoints of a log interval gives an interval for the median, not the mean (CI p.68).

HW5. Hypothesis testing

A \(z\) test with known \(\sigma\), its p-value, the rejection threshold, and choosing \(H_0\) for a one-sided claim. This is the hypothesis-testing lecture (HT p.4-15).

Problem 1.1. Neural network training time has known \(\sigma = 0.8\) h. Test \(H_0\colon \mu = 2.5\) h at \(\alpha = 0.05\) when 36 systems average 2.74 h.

Question. Problem 1, testing a mean with \(\sigma\) known. For a certain class of a new neural network architecture, we would like to test the hypothesis that the mean training time \(\mu\) is equal to \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) hours. The standard deviation is known to be 0.8 hours.

1. Suppose we run experiments with 36 such systems and observe that the mean training time in our experiments is 2.74 hours. Test the null hypothesis \(H_0\colon \mu = 2.5\) hours at the level of significance \(\alpha = 0.05\). Do you reject \(H_0\) or fail to reject it?

In plain words. Someone claims the true mean is 2.5 h. You saw 2.74 h. Is that gap big enough that it's unlikely to be just noise? Measure the gap in standard errors (that's \(Z\)) and compare it with 1.96.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Hypotheses. \(H_0\colon \mu = 2.5\) and \(H_1\colon \mu \ne 2.5\). Nothing in the question names a direction, so it's two-sided (HT p.5).
  2. Formula. \(\sigma\) is known, so use \[ Z = \frac{\bar x - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \] (HT p.8). Here \(\bar x\) is the observed mean, \(\mu_0\) the value \(H_0\) claims, \(\sigma\) the known standard deviation, and \(n\) the number of systems.
  3. Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{2.74 - 2.5}{0.8/\sqrt{36}}\).
  4. Arithmetic. \(\sqrt{36} = 6\). Standard error \(0.8/6 = 0.1333\). Top \(2.74 - 2.5 = 0.24\). So \(Z = 0.24/0.1333 = 1.80\).
  5. Rule. Two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\), reject if \(|Z| \gt z[0.025] = 1.960\) (HT p.8).
  6. Compare. \(|1.80| = 1.80 \lt 1.96\), so don't reject.
  7. Same verdict from the interval. \(2.74 \pm 1.96 \times 0.1333\). \(1.96 \times 0.1333 = 0.261\). Interval \(2.74 - 0.261 = 2.479\) to \(2.74 + 0.261 = 3.001\), which contains 2.5 (HT p.8-9).

Final answer. \(Z = 1.80\), fail to reject \(H_0\), matching main.tex. The observed mean is 1.8 standard errors above 2.5, not quite far enough to rule out noise at the 5% level.

How this could show up on the exam

Smaller numbers. \(n = 16\), \(\sigma = 0.8\), \(\bar x = 2.9\). Standard error \(0.8/\sqrt{16} = 0.8/4 = 0.2\). \(Z = (2.9 - 2.5)/0.2 = 0.4/0.2 = 2.0\). Since \(2.0 \gt 1.96\), reject at 0.05. Or the slide's example (HT p.10), \(n = 50\), \(\bar x = 12.3\), \(\sigma = 0.5\), \(\mu_0 = 12.5\). Standard error \(0.5/7.071 = 0.0707\). \(Z = (12.3 - 12.5)/0.0707 = -0.2/0.0707 = -2.83\). Since \(|-2.83| = 2.83 \gt 1.96\), reject.

Problem 1.2. Find the p-value and restate the conclusion.

Question. Problem 1, testing a mean with \(\sigma\) known. For a certain class of a new neural network architecture, we would like to test the hypothesis that the mean training time \(\mu\) is equal to \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) hours. The standard deviation is known to be 0.8 hours.

2. For the same experiments, find the p-value. Using the p-value you computed, restate the conclusion you reached in part 1.

In plain words. The p-value asks "if \(H_0\) were true, how often would I see a result at least this far from 2.5?" Small p means surprising under \(H_0\). Two-sided means you count both tails.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Definition. The p-value is the probability, if \(H_0\) is true, of a statistic at least as extreme as the one seen (HT p.13).
  2. Formula. Two-sided, \(p = 2\,P[Z \ge |z_{\text{obs}}|] = 2\,(1 - \Phi(|z_{\text{obs}}|))\), where \(z_{\text{obs}}\) is the statistic you got.
  3. Plug in. \(z_{\text{obs}} = 1.80\), so \(p = 2(1 - \Phi(1.80))\).
  4. Table. Row 1.8, column 0.00 gives \(\Phi(1.80) = 0.9641\).
  5. Arithmetic. \(1 - 0.9641 = 0.0359\). Then \(2 \times 0.0359 = 0.0718\).
  6. Decide. Reject if \(p \le \alpha\). \(0.0718 \gt 0.05\), so fail to reject, the same verdict as before. That has to happen, since the p-value and critical-value rules always agree (HT p.13).

Final answer. \(p = 0.0718\), fail to reject, matching main.tex. If the true mean really were 2.5, you'd see a gap this large about 7% of the time.

How this could show up on the exam

For \(Z = 2.0\), row 2.0 gives \(\Phi(2.0) = 0.9772\), so the two-sided \(p = 2(1 - 0.9772) = 2(0.0228) = 0.0456\). That rejects at 0.05 but not at 0.01. Also expect a "true or false" on what a p-value means. "p = 0.07 means there's a 7% chance \(H_0\) is true" is false (HT p.14).

Problem 1.3. At \(\alpha = 0.01\) with 36 systems, what's the smallest observed mean above 2.5 that rejects \(H_0\)?

Question. Problem 1, testing a mean with \(\sigma\) known. For a certain class of a new neural network architecture, we would like to test the hypothesis that the mean training time \(\mu\) is equal to \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) hours. The standard deviation is known to be 0.8 hours.

3. Suppose we reduce the level of significance to \(\alpha = 0.01\). If we run experiments on 36 systems to test the null hypothesis that \(\mu = 2.5\) hours, what is the minimum mean time (larger than 2.5 hours) we would need to observe to reject the hypothesis?

In plain words. Run the test backwards. Instead of computing \(Z\) from a mean, set \(Z\) equal to the critical value and solve for the mean. That gives the cutoff the observed mean has to beat.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Critical value. Keep the test two-sided, so \(\alpha/2 = 0.005\) and the critical value is \(z[0.005] = 2.576\).
  2. Formula. Reject when \(\dfrac{\bar x - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \gt z[\alpha/2]\). Solve for \(\bar x\), \[ \bar x \gt \mu_0 + z[\alpha/2]\,\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}. \]
  3. Plug in. \(\bar x \gt 2.5 + 2.576 \times 0.8/6 = 2.5 + 2.576 \times 0.1333\).
  4. Arithmetic. \(2.576 \times 0.1333 = 0.343\). Then \(2.5 + 0.343 = 2.843\).
  5. One-sided version. If \(H_1\) were \(\mu \gt 2.5\), you'd put all of \(\alpha\) in one tail and use \(z[0.01] = 2.326\). Then \(2.326 \times 0.1333 = 0.310\), and \(2.5 + 0.310 = 2.810\) (HT p.5).

Final answer. About 2.84 h (two-sided), or 2.81 h one-sided, matching main.tex. Any observed mean above 2.84 h would reject at the 1% level.

How this could show up on the exam

"With \(\sigma = 0.8\), how many systems do you need to detect a 0.2 h difference with 80% power at \(\alpha = 0.05\)?" Formula (HT p.19) \[ n = \left( \frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\,\sigma}{\delta} \right)^2 \approx 7.8\left(\frac{\sigma}{\delta}\right)^2, \] where \(\delta\) is the smallest difference you care about and \(\beta = 0.20\) is the miss rate. The 7.8 comes from \((1.96 + 0.84)^2 = 2.8^2 = 7.84\). Plug in \(7.8 \times (0.8/0.2)^2\). Arithmetic, \(0.8/0.2 = 4\), \(4^2 = 16\), \(7.8 \times 16 = 124.8\). Round up to 125 systems.

Problem 2.1. A group suspects the mean time to first sensor fault is below 740 h. What should \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) be?

Question. Problem 2, choosing the null hypothesis. A research group develops an autonomous driving system. Because of the specific sensor model adopted, they suspect that the mean time \(\mu\) to experience the first fault in the sensor subsystem is less than \(\mu_0 = 740\) hours.

1. If we use hypothesis testing to confirm this assertion, what should be the null hypothesis \(H_0\)? What should be the alternative hypothesis \(H_1\)? Carefully justify your choices for \(H_0\) and \(H_1\).

In plain words. A test can only "prove" something by rejecting \(H_0\). So whatever you want to show goes in \(H_1\), and \(H_0\) is the boring opposite.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Rule. To establish a claim, make its negation the null (HT p.4). The claim is "less than 740", so it goes in \(H_1\).
  2. Hypotheses. \(H_0\colon \mu = 740\) (or \(\mu \ge 740\)) and \(H_1\colon \mu \lt 740\).
  3. Sidedness. It's one-sided, since only a mean below 740 supports the claim (HT p.5).
  4. Reason. Rejecting \(H_0\) is evidence for \(H_1\), but failing to reject is not evidence for \(H_0\) (HT p.4). If the claim sat in \(H_0\), a small noisy experiment would "support" it just by failing to reject.

Final answer. \(H_0\colon \mu = 740\), \(H_1\colon \mu \lt 740\), one-sided, matching main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

"A vendor claims its new disk has mean latency under 5 ms. Set up the test." \(H_0\colon \mu = 5\), \(H_1\colon \mu \lt 5\). The trap version picks the direction after seeing the data, which turns a one-sided test into a disguised two-sided test at \(\alpha = 0.10\) (HT p.5).

Problem 2.2. 50 systems give mean 706 h and \(s = 96.1\) h. At \(\alpha = 0.01\), can you confirm \(\mu \lt 740\)?

Question. Problem 2, choosing the null hypothesis. A research group develops an autonomous driving system. Because of the specific sensor model adopted, they suspect that the mean time \(\mu\) to experience the first fault in the sensor subsystem is less than \(\mu_0 = 740\) hours.

2. Suppose we test 50 such systems and obtain a mean time to first fault of 706 hours, with a standard deviation of 96.1 hours. If the target level of significance is \(\alpha = 0.01\), can we confirm, based on these experiments, the assertion that the mean time to first fault is less than 740 hours?

In plain words. 706 is below 740, but is it far enough below to rule out noise at the strict 1% level? Compute how many standard errors below 740 it is, and since it's one-sided, compare with \(-2.326\).
Step-by-step solution
  1. Which test. \(\sigma\) is unknown but \(n = 50\) is large, so \(s\) can stand in for \(\sigma\) and \(Z\) and \(t\) are nearly the same (HT p.11).
  2. Formula. \(Z = \dfrac{\bar x - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}\), where \(\bar x = 706\), \(\mu_0 = 740\), \(s = 96.1\), \(n = 50\).
  3. Plug in. \(Z = \dfrac{706 - 740}{96.1/\sqrt{50}}\).
  4. Arithmetic. \(\sqrt{50} = 7.071\). Standard error \(96.1/7.071 = 13.59\). Top \(706 - 740 = -34\). So \(Z = -34/13.59 = -2.50\).
  5. Rule. One-sided (lower tail) at 0.01, reject if \(Z \lt -z[0.01] = -2.326\).
  6. Compare. \(-2.50 \lt -2.326\), so reject \(H_0\).
  7. p-value. Formula. One-sided lower tail, \(p = P[Z \le z_{\text{obs}}] = \Phi(-2.50) = 1 - \Phi(2.50)\). Table. Row 2.5, column 0.00 gives 0.9938. Arithmetic. \(1 - 0.9938 = 0.0062\). Since \(0.0062 \lt 0.01\), the p-value agrees.

Final answer. Reject \(H_0\), so the data confirm the mean is below 740 h at the 0.01 level, with \(p = 0.0062\). This matches main.tex. Using \(t\) with 49 degrees of freedom instead (critical value about 2.405) gives the same verdict.

How this could show up on the exam

Friendlier numbers. \(n = 36\), \(\bar x = 716\), \(s = 60\). Standard error \(60/\sqrt{36} = 60/6 = 10\). \(Z = (716 - 740)/10 = -24/10 = -2.4\). That's below \(-2.326\), so reject at 0.01. The p-value is \(1 - \Phi(2.4) = 1 - 0.9918 = 0.0082\). With \(n = 9\) and the same mean and \(s\), you'd need \(t[0.01, 8]\) and the population roughly normal (HT p.11).

HW6. Comparing two alternatives

A paired interval for two routing algorithms and an unpaired Welch interval for two systems. This is the comparing-alternatives lecture (Comp p.5-14).

Problem 1. Two routing algorithms ran the same 9 workloads. Delays for A are 115.0, 89.3, 102.1, 94.3, 117.3, 120.3, 98.9, 87.6, 104.5 and for B are 125.3, 97.6, 85.3, 101.3, 112.7, 87.9, 104.2, 96.3, 113.2. Is A better than B with 95% confidence?

Question. Two packet routing algorithms are being evaluated. Experiments were conducted by injecting the same workload in the network and measuring the average end-to-end packet delay. The results of the various experiments are shown in Table 1. Can you say with 95% confidence that routing algorithm A is better than algorithm B?
ExperimentAB
1115.0125.3
289.397.6
3102.185.3
494.3101.3
5117.3112.7
6120.387.9
798.9104.2
887.696.3
9104.5113.2
In plain words. Each workload ran under both algorithms, so compare them workload by workload. Take the 9 differences A minus B and build an ordinary \(t\) interval for their mean. If the interval includes 0, you can't say either one is better.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Design. The same workload ran under both, so it's paired. Analyse the 9 differences \(d_i = A_i - B_i\) (Comp p.5, Comp p.9). Lower delay is better, so a negative \(d\) favours A.
  2. Differences. \(115.0 - 125.3 = -10.3\), \(89.3 - 97.6 = -8.3\), \(102.1 - 85.3 = 16.8\), \(94.3 - 101.3 = -7.0\), \(117.3 - 112.7 = 4.6\), \(120.3 - 87.9 = 32.4\), \(98.9 - 104.2 = -5.3\), \(87.6 - 96.3 = -8.7\), \(104.5 - 113.2 = -8.7\).
  3. Mean. Formula. \(\bar d = \dfrac{1}{n}\sum d_i\) with \(n = 9\) pairs.
    Arithmetic. Positives \(16.8 + 4.6 + 32.4 = 53.8\). Negatives \(10.3 + 8.3 + 7.0 + 5.3 + 8.7 + 8.7 = 48.3\). So \(\sum d_i = 53.8 - 48.3 = 5.5\), and \(\bar d = 5.5/9 = 0.611\).
  4. Standard deviation. Formula. \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - (\sum d_i)^2/n}{n - 1}\) (Summ p.39).
    Squares. \(10.3^2 = 106.09\), \(8.3^2 = 68.89\), \(16.8^2 = 282.24\), \(7.0^2 = 49.00\), \(4.6^2 = 21.16\), \(32.4^2 = 1049.76\), \(5.3^2 = 28.09\), \(8.7^2 = 75.69\), \(8.7^2 = 75.69\).
    Sum. \(106.09 + 68.89 = 174.98\), \(+ 282.24 = 457.22\), \(+ 49.00 = 506.22\), \(+ 21.16 = 527.38\), \(+ 1049.76 = 1577.14\), \(+ 28.09 = 1605.23\), \(+ 75.69 = 1680.92\), \(+ 75.69 = 1756.61\).
    Plug in. \(s_d^2 = (1756.61 - 5.5^2/9)/8\).
    Arithmetic. \(5.5^2 = 30.25\), \(30.25/9 = 3.36\). \(1756.61 - 3.36 = 1753.25\). \(1753.25/8 = 219.16\). Then \(s_d = \sqrt{219.16} \approx 14.80\) (since \(14.8^2 = 219.04\)).
  5. Standard error. \(s_d/\sqrt{n} = 14.80/\sqrt{9} = 14.80/3 = 4.935\).
  6. Critical value. Degrees of freedom are pairs minus 1, \(9 - 1 = 8\). t table row 8, column 0.025 gives 2.306.
  7. Interval. Formula. \(\bar d \pm t[\alpha/2, n-1]\, s_d/\sqrt{n}\) (Comp p.9).
    Plug in. \(0.611 \pm 2.306 \times 4.935\).
    Arithmetic. \(2.306 \times 4.935 = 11.38\). Lower \(0.611 - 11.38 = -10.77\). Upper \(0.611 + 11.38 = 11.99\).
  8. Decide. Zero is inside \((-10.77, 11.99)\), so the data don't establish a difference.

Final answer. (−10.77, 11.99), you can't say A is better at 95%, matching main.tex. Note that this isn't evidence the two are equal. With 9 workloads the interval is consistent with A being 10 units better or 12 units worse (Comp p.11).

How this could show up on the exam

A 4-pair version. Differences 2, 4, 3, 3. Mean \(\bar d = 12/4 = 3\). Deviations from 3 are \(-1, 1, 0, 0\), squares \(1, 1, 0, 0\), so \(s_d^2 = 2/3 = 0.667\) and \(s_d = \sqrt{0.667} = 0.816\). Standard error \(0.816/\sqrt{4} = 0.816/2 = 0.408\). Row 3, column 0.025 gives \(t = 3.182\). Half-width \(3.182 \times 0.408 = 1.30\). Interval \(3 - 1.30 = 1.70\) to \(3 + 1.30 = 4.30\), which excludes 0.

A rank-based follow-up on the HW6 data (Comp p.23). The sign test counts the positive differences, \(S = 3\) out of 9. Under \(H_0\), \(S \sim \text{Binomial}(9, 1/2)\). Formula \(P[S \le 3] = \sum_{k=0}^{3}\binom{9}{k}(1/2)^9\). Plug in \(\binom{9}{0} = 1\), \(\binom{9}{1} = 9\), \(\binom{9}{2} = \frac{9 \times 8}{2} = 36\), \(\binom{9}{3} = \frac{9 \times 8 \times 7}{6} = 84\), and \(2^9 = 512\). Arithmetic \((1 + 9 + 36 + 84)/512 = 130/512 = 0.254\). Two-sided, double it, \(p = 260/512 \approx 0.51\). Same conclusion.

Problem 2. CPU times for 30 transactions on System A and 24 on System B. Is A faster at 90% confidence?

Question. Two computer systems are being evaluated for their performance. Data corresponding to the CPU time for running some transactions on System A and System B are reported in Table 2. Is System A faster than System B at the 90% confidence level?

Table 2, CPU time (sec). System A, 30 values. 1.3779, 0.0457, 1.4415, 1.3589, 0.6898, 0.7076, 3.9083, 2.2081, 0.9244, 6.5770, 4.4775, 4.6199, 0.1775, 0.3354, 0.3687, 0.0956, 1.2116, 3.8814, 0.2922, 3.0970, 3.2150, 1.2880, 3.0046, 6.8230, 0.7478, 0.3845, 0.2354, 0.9951, 0.3045, 1.6950.

System B, 24 values. 0.9584, 0.5726, 1.3915, 0.8075, 0.1344, 0.1277, 1.8032, 1.2048, 9.5949, 4.9107, 0.2834, 0.3841, 0.0812, 2.1362, 4.6396, 1.0127, 2.9434, 1.0844, 0.7860, 2.1536, 1.4948, 11.7346, 0.9436, 3.1111.

In plain words. Different transactions ran on each system, so you can't pair them up. Compare the two means with Welch's interval, which lets the two systems have different spreads and sizes. If the interval for \(\bar x_A - \bar x_B\) includes 0, you can't say A is faster.
Step-by-step solution
  1. Design. Different transactions on each system and unequal sizes, so there's no pairing. Use Welch's unpaired interval by default (Comp p.12-13).
  2. Summary statistics. From the 54 values in data/table2.txt. For A, \(n_A = 30\), \(\sum x = 56.4889\), \(\sum x^2 = 211.2488\). For B, \(n_B = 24\), \(\sum x = 54.2944\), \(\sum x^2 = 317.6984\).
  3. Means. \(\bar x_A = 56.4889/30 = 1.8830\). \(\bar x_B = 54.2944/24 = 2.2623\).
  4. Variances. Formula. \(s^2 = \dfrac{\sum x^2 - (\sum x)^2/n}{n - 1}\).
    A, \(56.4889^2 = 3191.00\), \(/30 = 106.367\), \(211.249 - 106.367 = 104.882\), \(/29 = 3.6166\). So \(s_A = \sqrt{3.6166} = 1.9017\).
    B, \(54.2944^2 = 2947.88\), \(/24 = 122.828\), \(317.698 - 122.828 = 194.870\), \(/23 = 8.4726\). So \(s_B = \sqrt{8.4726} = 2.9108\).
  5. Difference. \(\bar x_A - \bar x_B = 1.8830 - 2.2623 = -0.3793\). Negative means A's mean time is smaller.
  6. Standard deviation of the difference. Formula. \[ s = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{n_B}} \] (Comp p.12).
    Plug in. \(s = \sqrt{3.6166/30 + 8.4726/24}\).
    Arithmetic. \(3.6166/30 = 0.12055\). \(8.4726/24 = 0.35303\). Sum \(0.12055 + 0.35303 = 0.47358\). Root \(\sqrt{0.47358} = 0.6882\) (since \(0.6882^2 = 0.4736\)).
  7. Degrees of freedom. Formula. \[ \nu = \frac{\left(s_A^2/n_A + s_B^2/n_B\right)^2}{\dfrac{(s_A^2/n_A)^2}{n_A - 1} + \dfrac{(s_B^2/n_B)^2}{n_B - 1}} \] (Comp p.12).
    Plug in. \(\nu = \dfrac{0.47358^2}{0.12055^2/29 + 0.35303^2/23}\).
    Arithmetic. Top \(0.47358^2 = 0.22428\). Bottom, \(0.12055^2 = 0.014532\) and \(/29 = 0.000501\). \(0.35303^2 = 0.124630\) and \(/23 = 0.005419\). Sum \(0.000501 + 0.005419 = 0.005920\). So \(\nu = 0.22428/0.005920 = 37.89\).
  8. Critical value. Round \(\nu\) down to 37 (Comp p.12). 90% two-sided means upper-tail 0.05, so row 37, column 0.05 gives \(t[0.05, 37] = 1.687\).
  9. Interval. Formula. \((\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t[\alpha/2, \nu]\, s\).
    Plug in. \(-0.3793 \pm 1.687 \times 0.6882\).
    Arithmetic. \(1.687 \times 0.6882 = 1.161\). Lower \(-0.3793 - 1.161 = -1.540\). Upper \(-0.3793 + 1.161 = 0.782\).
  10. Decide. Zero is inside \((-1.540, 0.782)\), so A isn't shown to be faster.

Final answer. (−1.540, 0.781), so A isn't shown to be faster at 90%, matching main.tex (which used \(\nu = 37.89\) directly with \(t = 1.686\), hence 0.781).

How this could show up on the exam

A small Welch problem. A is 2, 4, 6, 8 and B is 6, 8, 10, 12, 14.

  1. Means. \(\bar x_A = 20/4 = 5\). \(\bar x_B = 50/5 = 10\). Difference \(5 - 10 = -5\).
  2. Variances. A deviations \(-3, -1, 1, 3\), squares \(9, 1, 1, 9\), sum 20, so \(s_A^2 = 20/3 = 6.667\). B deviations \(-4, -2, 0, 2, 4\), squares \(16, 4, 0, 4, 16\), sum 40, so \(s_B^2 = 40/4 = 10\).
  3. Pieces. \(s_A^2/n_A = 6.667/4 = 1.667\). \(s_B^2/n_B = 10/5 = 2\). Sum 3.667, so \(s = \sqrt{3.667} = 1.915\).
  4. \(\nu = 3.667^2/(1.667^2/3 + 2^2/4) = 13.44/(2.778/3 + 4/4) = 13.44/(0.926 + 1) = 13.44/1.926 = 6.98\). Round down to 6.
  5. Row 6, column 0.05 gives \(t = 1.943\). Half-width \(1.943 \times 1.915 = 3.72\). The 90% interval is \(-5 \pm 3.72 = (-8.72, -1.28)\).

That excludes 0, so A is smaller. Never use \(n - 1\) of one sample as the degrees of freedom (Comp p.12). A related trap is reading overlap of two separate intervals as "no difference" when the interval on the difference excludes 0 (Comp p.15-16).

HW4 to HW6, Problem 3. Auditing a paper's statistics

Each of HW4, HW5 and HW6 ended with a paper from the "in practice" handouts, read for its setup and results only. The exam can ask the same questions about an experiment it describes, so learn the questions and the checklist rather than your specific papers. Every handout question is listed below with your answer.

HW4 Problem 3 (confidence intervals in the wild). Eight questions from Part III of the confidence-intervals in-practice handout.

Question. Work through the homework exercise in Part III of the companion handout Confidence Intervals in the Wild. Read only the experimental setup and results sections of the paper you choose, and answer all eight questions there. Two or three sentences per question is enough. Give the full citation at the top.

Choosing the paper is part of the exercise. Pick the entry from Part I of the handout that sits closest to your own research area, or any peer-reviewed paper from that area, ideally one you would cite anyway, that reports experimental results with confidence intervals, error bars, or any other statement of uncertainty. Papers that report results with no statement of uncertainty are also fair game, and often more revealing.

If you do not have an area yet, or your area is not represented in Part I, use entry 4 (Georges, Buytaert and Eeckhout, Statistically Rigorous Java Performance Evaluation, OOPSLA 2007) or entry 13 (Henning et al., When Should I Run My Application Benchmark?, FSE Companion 2025) as your default. Entry 13 is the better choice if you want a reported experiment to audit. Entry 4 is the better choice if you want the mechanics of Problems 1 and 2 drilled on a real evaluation. Either is an acceptable answer to question 1, so say which you picked and why you defaulted to it.

In plain words. Take a real paper's error bars and ask whether they mean what they seem to. What's being estimated, from how many truly independent pieces, under what assumption, and what errors the bars can't see.
Step-by-step solution
  1. (1) What you picked and why. Handout question 1. Say what you picked and why. Give the full citation and one or two sentences on how the paper relates to your own work. Puffer (Yan et al., NSDI 2020). Your research is low-latency video streaming, no Part I entry covered it, and the paper reports intervals throughout.
  2. (2) Locate the interval. Handout question 2. Locate the interval. Quote the sentence or identify the figure where an interval or error bar first appears. If you cannot find one, say so, that is a finding, and the rest of the questions still apply to whatever the paper reports in its place. Section 3.4 says the 95% interval on a scheme's stall ratio is between ±13% and ±21% of the mean. The first figure with intervals is Figure 9.
  3. (3) Identify the estimator. Handout question 3. Identify the estimator. Mean, proportion, variance, quantile, geometric mean, or something else? Which formula from lecture applies, if any? The headline is the stall ratio, total stall time over total watch time. That's a ratio, not a plain mean, so no lecture formula fits and they bootstrapped it (CI p.69). Average SSIM is a time-weighted mean with a \(z\) interval.
  4. (4) Find \(n\). Handout question 4. Find n. How many measurements? Is the choice justified, or asserted? 637,189 streams from 54,612 IP addresses, about 2.5 to 2.8 stream-years per scheme. It's whatever eight months collected, not planned. The "2 stream-years to separate a 15% difference" claim isn't derived.
  5. (5) Check the assumption. Handout question 5. Check the assumption. What distributional assumption does their interval require, and do the authors address it? If the response is plainly not normal and n is small, which of the three responses on slide 68 did they use, and if none, which would you have used? Stalls are heavily skewed (only about 4% of streams stalled), which is why the intervals stay wide (CI p.67). Bootstrap was the right response, but the number of resamples and the interval type aren't stated.
  6. (6) Check independence. Handout question 6. Check the independence. What is the unit being treated as an independent draw, a run, a request, a benchmark, a user, a seed? Are consecutive units plausibly independent, or is this an entry-15 situation dressed up as an entry-1 situation? Randomization was by session, but the bootstrap resampled streams, and streams in one session share a connection. Resampling the wrong unit makes intervals too narrow (CI p.66, HT p.20). Resampling sessions would match the randomization.
