  7. (7) What the interval excludes. Handout question 7. Check what the interval excludes. Name one source of error in their setup that the interval does not cover (pitfall 3). Systematic error, such as serving everything from one Stanford server to self-selected viewers. A narrow interval is a claim about precision, not accuracy (CI p.17).
  8. (8) Interpretation. Handout question 8. Check the interpretation. Do the authors state a conclusion that requires a credible interval while computing a confidence interval? They never say "95% probability the true value is in here", which would need a credible interval, since a confidence interval is a statement about the procedure (CI p.33). They do compare schemes by overlapping error bars, which isn't a test.

Final answer. Your full answers are in homework4/main.tex. The general lesson is that the estimator, the unit of resampling and the excluded error sources matter as much as the formula.

How this could show up on the exam

"A paper reports mean latency 4.2 ms ± 0.1 ms (95% CI) from 10,000 requests in one run. What's wrong?" The requests aren't independent, so \(n\) isn't 10,000 and the interval is too narrow. The fix is several separate runs with one summary per run, or batching (HT p.20-22, CI p.70).

HW5 Problem 3 (hypothesis testing in the wild). Seven questions (numbered 0 to 6) from Part III of the hypothesis-testing in-practice handout.

Question. Work through the homework exercise in Part III of the companion handout Hypothesis Testing in the Wild. Read only the experimental setup and results sections of the paper you choose, and answer all seven questions there. Two or three sentences per question is enough, except for the last question, which asks you to rewrite the paper’s key result as an effect size with a confidence interval. Give the full citation at the top.

Choosing the paper is part of the exercise. Pick the entry from Part I of the handout that sits closest to your own research area, or any peer-reviewed paper from that area, ideally one you would cite anyway, that makes a claim of the form “A is better than B”. Papers that make that claim with no test and no statement of uncertainty are also fair game, and often more revealing. Several entries (1, 3, 4, 7, 9 and 10) are about statistical practice rather than about one system beating another. If you pick one of them, apply the questions to the paper’s own central empirical claim, for entry 3, for example, that the share of machine translation papers using significance tests fell after 2016.

If you do not have an area yet, or your area is not represented in Part I, use one of two default papers. Computer architecture and compilers, Mytkowicz, Diwan, Hauswirth and Sweeney, Producing Wrong Data Without Doing Anything Obviously Wrong!, ASPLOS 2009 (a harmless setup detail changes the measured speedup of -O3 over -O2, and the paper ends with a single 95% confidence interval on that speedup). Computer vision, Musgrave, Belongie and Lim, A Metric Learning Reality Check, ECCV 2020 (re-runs 14 methods with 10 training runs each, reports means with 95% confidence intervals, and finds that years of claimed improvements shrink to almost nothing). Either is an acceptable answer to question 0, so say which you picked and why you defaulted to it.

In plain words. Take a paper that says "A beats B" and rebuild the test it should have run. What are \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), what's an independent unit, could it have detected a real effect, how many tests did it run, and what's the effect in real units with an interval.
Step-by-step solution
  1. (0) What you picked and why. Handout question 0. Say what you picked and why. Give the full citation and one or two sentences on how the paper relates to your own work. Spang et al. (IMC 2021) on Netflix bitrate capping, entry 8, the closest to video streaming. Your own comparisons share a bottleneck link too.
  2. (1) \(H_0\) and \(H_1\). Handout question 1. Write down H0 and H1. For the paper’s central claim, state the null and alternative hypotheses, and whether the test is one- or two-sided. If the paper never states them, reconstruct them, and say so. Never stated, so you reconstructed them. \(H_0\), the total treatment effect is 0. \(H_1\), it isn't 0. Two-sided because the paper uses two-sided 95% intervals (HT p.4-5).
  3. (2) Test, assumptions and unit. Handout question 2. Identify the test and its assumptions. Which test was used, if any? What does it assume (normality, independence, pairing, equal variances)? Do the authors check or address those assumptions? What is the experimental unit, and does n count units or measurements (slide 20)? No named test. A regression on hourly means with Newey-West standard errors, which assumes the hourly means are roughly normal. The unit measured is the session, but they counted hour cells as \(n\), because sessions in the same hour share congestion and aren't independent (HT p.20).
  4. (3) \(\alpha\), \(n\) and power. Handout question 3. Find α, n, and β. What significance level? How many observations, and what is an observation (a run, a seed, a benchmark, a user)? Is there any power analysis? If not, estimate roughly what effect size the experiment could have detected. \(\alpha = 0.05\) implied by the 95% intervals. Only a few dozen hour cells per estimate and no power analysis. Detectable effect, formula \(\delta \approx (z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta]) \times \text{SE}\) (HT p.19). Plug in \((1.96 + 0.84) \times 2.6\). Arithmetic, \(1.96 + 0.84 = 2.8\), and \(2.8 \times 2.6 = 7.28\). So about 7%.
  5. (4) Count the tests. Handout question 4. Count the tests. How many comparisons does the paper make in total? What is the family-wise error rate if none were corrected (slide 27)? Is there a correction, and does the choice between family-wise error and false discovery rate control fit the paper’s claims (slide 28)? 28 intervals (7 metrics times 4 estimates) with no correction. Formula \(\text{FWER} = 1 - (1 - \alpha)^m\) (HT p.27). Plug in \(1 - 0.95^{28}\). Arithmetic by repeated squaring, \(0.95^2 = 0.9025\), \(0.95^4 = 0.9025^2 = 0.8145\), \(0.95^8 = 0.8145^2 = 0.6634\), \(0.95^{16} = 0.6634^2 = 0.4401\). Then \(0.95^{28} = 0.95^{16} \times 0.95^{8} \times 0.95^{4} = 0.4401 \times 0.6634 \times 0.8145 = 0.238\). So \(\text{FWER} = 1 - 0.238 = 0.76\). False discovery rate control fits the claim better (HT p.28).
  6. (5) Stopping rule and interference. Handout question 5. Check the stopping rule and the estimand. Was n fixed in advance, or could the authors have kept running until the result was significant (slides 23 to 25)? Could units in the experiment have interfered with each other (slide 22)? Does the test measure the quantity the paper’s claim is about (pitfall 7)? Five days, but not said to be fixed in advance. Interference is the paper's point. Capped and uncapped sessions shared a link, so the naive A/B test said −5% while capping nearly all traffic gave +12% (HT p.22).
  7. (6) Rewrite the result. Handout question 6. Rewrite the result. Rewrite the paper’s key sentence as an effect size with a confidence interval, as slides 16 and 18 recommend. Is the effect large enough to matter? Does the claim become stronger, weaker, or different? Capping nearly all traffic raised throughput by about 12% (95% CI roughly 7% to 17%), while the naive test estimated −5% (95% CI about −8% to −2%). The claim gets stronger, since the intervals don't overlap, but the true gain could be as small as 7%.

Final answer. Your full answers are in homework5/main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

"A paper runs 10 independent tests at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with every \(H_0\) true. What's the chance of at least one false positive?" Formula \(1 - (1 - \alpha)^m\). Plug in \(1 - 0.95^{10}\). Arithmetic, \(0.95^{10} = 0.95^8 \times 0.95^2 = 0.6634 \times 0.9025 = 0.599\), so \(1 - 0.599 = 0.40\) (HT p.27). With Bonferroni each test uses \(\alpha/m = 0.05/10 = 0.005\) (HT p.28). Or "the team checked the dashboard every morning and stopped when p dropped below 0.05". That's peeking, and with 20 looks the real Type I error is about 25% (HT p.23-24).

HW6 Problem 3 (comparing two systems in the wild). Eight questions from Part III of the comparing-alternatives in-practice handout.

Question. Work through the homework exercise in Part III of the companion handout Comparing Two Systems in the Wild. Read only the experimental setup and results sections of the paper you choose, and answer all eight questions there. Two or three sentences per question is enough, except for the last question, which asks you to rewrite the paper’s headline comparison as an effect with a confidence interval and to say what the experiment could not have detected. Give the full citation at the top.

Choosing the paper is part of the exercise. Pick the entry from Part I of the handout that sits closest to your own research area, or any peer-reviewed paper from that area, ideally one you would cite anyway, that compares exactly two systems, models, algorithms or configurations. “Ours” versus a baseline is ideal. Papers that make that comparison with no test and no statement of uncertainty are also fair game, and often more revealing. Several entries (4, 5, 9, 10 and 11) are guides to, or surveys of, statistical practice rather than a comparison of two systems. If you pick one of them, apply the questions to the paper’s own central empirical claim, for entry 9, for example, that most published researchers could not position two error bars so that the difference was just significant.

If you do not have an area yet, or your area is not represented in Part I, use one of three default papers, each comparing two alternatives with a test from the lecture (unpaired t, paired t, or rank-based), and each turning on what counts as an independent observation. Systems and compilers, Curtsinger and Berger, STABILIZER, ASPLOS 2013 (an unpaired two-sample t test of -O3 against -O2 on each SPEC CPU2006 benchmark, check which version of the t test it names and whether its fallback test for non-normal benchmarks matches its design). Machine learning, Dietterich, Approximate Statistical Tests for Comparing Supervised Classification Learning Algorithms, Neural Computation 1998 (the paired t test on error rates over the same splits or folds, ask what the unit is and why differences on overlapping training sets aren’t independent). Software security and testing, Klees et al., Evaluating Fuzz Testing, CCS 2018 (AFLFast against AFL with at least 30 trials of 24 hours, compared with the Mann-Whitney U test, ask why a rank test suits this response, what the unit is, and whether trials on each target could have been paired). Any of the three is an acceptable answer to question 1, so say which you picked and why you defaulted to it.

In plain words. Take a paper comparing two systems and check the design. Was it paired, which test, does the response fit that test's assumptions, are error bars being read correctly, and how big a difference could the experiment actually have seen.
Step-by-step solution
  1. (1) What you picked and why. Handout question 1. Say what you picked and why. Full citation and a sentence on its relation to your work. Miller (2024), "Adding Error Bars to Evals", entry 3. Your research compares vision-language models on the same benchmark questions.
  2. (2) Design and unit. Handout question 2. Identify the design. Paired or unpaired? What is the unit, a benchmark, a test-set example, a run, a user? How many units per system? Paired, with the question as the unit (the cluster for MGSM), since both models answer the same questions (Comp p.5). 5,000 MATH, 164 HumanEval and 2,500 MGSM questions.
  3. (3) Could it be paired, and what does pairing buy? Handout question 3. If it is unpaired, could it have been paired? If yes, estimate what that would have bought. How much of the observed variance is between units rather than between systems? It already is. Formula \(\text{Var}(A - B) = \sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2 - 2\rho\sigma_A\sigma_B\) (Comp p.6). In Miller's example \(\rho = 0.5\) cuts the variance from 1/6 to 1/9. Standard error scales with the square root, so the ratio is \(\sqrt{(1/9)/(1/6)} = \sqrt{6/9} = \sqrt{2/3} \approx 0.82\). That's \(1 - 0.82 = 0.18\), about 18% smaller.
  4. (4) The comparison. Handout question 4. Find the comparison. Which test, if any? Is the statistic reported with its degrees of freedom and an exact p? If there is no test, what is offered instead? A paired \(z\) test on per-question differences, interval \(= \bar d \pm 1.96\,\text{SE}\). No degrees of freedom or p-values, significance means the interval excludes 0.
  5. (5) Assumptions. Handout question 5. Check the assumptions. Is the response plausibly normal, or is it a bounded score, a ratio, or a quantile? Would a rank-based test or a bootstrap have been more defensible (slides 17 to 18)? Scores are 0 or 1, so not normal. The CLT covers 5,000 questions, but for 164 a paired bootstrap or sign test is safer (Comp p.17-18).
  6. (6) The figure. Handout question 6. Look at the figure. If there are error bars, does the caption say what they are? Is any conclusion drawn from their overlap (slides 15 to 16)? No figures, only standard errors in tables, and no conclusion from overlap. Single-model intervals on MATH overlap even though the paired interval excludes 0, which is the overlap trap (Comp p.15).
  7. (7) Effect size. Handout question 7. Find the effect size. What is the difference, in the units of the response, and does the paper give an interval for it? Is the difference large enough to matter, and who decides? Galleon beats Dreadnought by 2.5 points on MATH, 95% CI (1.2, 3.8). Whether that matters is left to whoever runs the eval.
  8. (8) Rewrite and what it couldn't detect. Handout question 8. Rewrite the claim. Restate the paper’s headline comparison as an effect with an interval, and add the sentence about what the experiment could not have detected. HumanEval's interval half-width was about 4.1 points. Formula \(\text{SE} = \text{half-width}/1.96\). Plug in \(4.1/1.96 = 2.09 \approx 2.1\). Smallest reliably detectable difference \(\approx 2.8 \times \text{SE}\) (Comp p.25, same 2.8 as above). Plug in \(2.8 \times 2.1 = 5.9\), about 6 points. Rewritten, Galleon scored 2.5 points higher on MATH (95% CI 1.2 to 3.8). HumanEval (−3.1, CI −7.2 to 1.0) and MGSM (−2.7, CI −6.1 to 0.7) are consistent with no difference, and HumanEval couldn't detect differences under about 6 points.

Final answer. Your full answers are in homework6/main.tex.

How this could show up on the exam

"Two compilers were each run on 8 benchmarks, and the paper used an unpaired test. Comment." It should have been paired, since every benchmark ran under both. On the slide example the paired interval is (0.65, 3.00) s and the unpaired one is (−22.0, 25.7) s, the same data with the opposite conclusion (Comp p.7-8).

The general checklist. Use this order on any experiment the exam describes.

  1. Claim and hypotheses. What exactly is claimed, what are \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), and was the direction picked before the data (HT p.4-5)?
  2. Unit and \(n\). What is independently replicated, a run, a benchmark, a user? Does \(n\) count units or measurements (HT p.20)?
  3. Design. Paired or unpaired, and could it have been paired (Comp p.5)?
  4. Test and assumptions. \(z\), \(t\), Welch, rank-based or bootstrap, and is the response plausibly normal or is it a ratio, a bounded score or a tail quantile (Comp p.17-18, CI p.67)?
  5. \(\alpha\), power and \(n\). Was \(n\) planned from a power calculation? If not, what effect could it have detected (HT p.19)?
  6. Multiple tests and peeking. How many comparisons, any correction, a fixed stopping rule (HT p.23-28)?
  7. Effect size with an interval. Is the difference reported in real units with an interval, and is it big enough to matter (HT p.16-18, Comp p.24)?
  8. What the interval misses. Systematic error, interference, a narrow setup (CI p.17, HT p.22).
  9. Reading of the result. No "equivalent" from a non-significant result, no conclusions from overlapping error bars, no "95% probability" for a confidence interval (HT p.14, Comp p.15, CI p.33).
How this could show up on the exam

"A paper says our scheduler cuts mean latency from 12.1 to 11.4 ms, p < 0.05, from one run of each system with 10,000 requests, after trying three workloads and reporting the one that worked." Going down the list, the unit is the run, so \(n\) is 1 per system and the p-value means nothing. The workload choice is a hidden family of 3 tests, so the real false-positive chance is up to \(1 - 0.95^3 = 1 - 0.857 = 0.14\), not 0.05. There's no interval on the \(12.1 - 11.4 = 0.7\) ms difference, and "p < 0.05" throws away the exact \(p\). A fix would be several independent runs per system on all three workloads, paired by workload, with the difference and its interval reported for each.

Printable notes

The exam is open notes, so there's no page limit like a cheatsheet would have. Print CS700_Midterm_Notes.pdf one-sided at 100% scale. The first pages hold every formula and decision rule from lectures 2 to 11, in the order the guide covers them. The last page holds the tables you'd normally get from a calculator, which are t critical values, the standard normal table, chi-square quantiles and square roots from 2 to 100.

Bring your own homework printouts too, since the exam questions will look like them. Click a page to open it full size.
